Uniformly rectifiable metric spaces: Lipschitz images, Bi-Lateral Weak Geometric Lemma and Corona Decompositions Thanks: D. Bate and M. Hyde are supported by the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (Grant agreement No. 948021). R. Schul is supported by the National Science Foundation under Grant No. DMS-2154613
David Bate, Matthew Hyde, Raanan Schul

Abstract
. In their 1991 and 1993 foundational monographs, David and Semmes characterized uniform rectifiability for subsets of Euclidean space in a multitude of geometric and analytic ways. The fundamental geometric conditions can be naturally stated in any metric space and it has long been a question of how these concepts are related in this general setting. In this paper we prove their equivalence. Namely, we show the equivalence of Big Pieces of Lipschitz Images, Bi-lateral Weak Geometric Lemma and Corona Decomposition in any Ahlfors regular metric space. Loosely speaking, this gives a quantitative equivalence between having Lipschitz charts and approximations by nicer spaces. En route, we also study Reifenberg parametrizations.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
28A12,28A75,28A78

Contents

1.
Introduction

A set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
\mathcal{H}^{n}(E)<\infty
is said to be

n
-rectifiable
if it can be covered, up to a set of
\mathcal{H}^{n}

measure zero, by countably many Lipschitz images of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Here and
throughout,
\mathcal{H}^{n}
denotes the
n
-dimensional Hausdorff measure
. Rectifiable sets generalize the notion of
C^{1}
manifolds
while still maintaining many of their nice geometric properties (in some
suitable measure theoretic sense). This notion goes back to
Besicovitch
[
Bes28
]
and
has many nice properties.
For example, a consequence of
Rademacher’s Theorem is that rectifiable sets admit
approximate
tangent planes
at
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-a.e. point. Conversely, the existence of
approximate tangent planes at

\mathcal{H}^{n}
-a.e. point implies rectifiability, see
[
Mat95
]
.
An idea that we will return to later is that the notion of tangent may
be weakened to allow the approximating tangent planes at a point to

rotate
depending on the scale, as was shown by Marstrand

[
Mar61
]
and Mattila
[
Mat75
]
. Since their inception, rectifiable sets have appeared in many areas of
analysis and geometry and unify many different topics
[
Mat23
]
.

This article concerns itself with quantitative notions of rectifiability
and approximation by tangent planes. In the late 80s and early 90s there
was particular interest in studying the
L^{2}
boundedness of certain
singular integral operators in connection with Vitushkin’s conjecture
concerning geometric characterizations of
removable sets
(see

[
Tol14
]
for an introduction). In

[
Cal77
]
, Calderón proved the Cauchy transform is
bounded on
L^{2}(\Gamma)
for
\Gamma
a Lipschitz graph with small
constant. The small constant assumption was later removed by Coifman,
McIntosh and Meyer
[
CMM82
]
. Key to the full solution
of Vitushkin’s conjecture was the development of multi-scale techniques
for rectifiability, initiated by Jones. In
[
Jon90
]

Jones proves his Travelling Salesman Theorem, which characterizes
rectifiable curves in the plane via
\beta
-numbers, a coefficient
measuring how well a set can be approximated by a line, at each scale
and location. Okikiolu
[
Oki92
]
later
extended this characterization to rectifiable curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Building
on the work of Jones, David and Semmes

[
DS91
,
DS93a
]
developed a rich theory of
quantitative rectifiability for higher dimensional subsets of Euclidean
space, formulating a framework within which to consider

L^{2}
-boundedness of
n
-dimensional singular integral operators. It was
in these works that they introduced the notion of uniform rectifiability.

Definition 1.0.1
.

A set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
is said to be
uniformly

n
-rectifiable
if it is
Ahlfors
n
-regular
i.e. there exists

C_{0}\geq 1
such that

(1.0.1)

\displaystyle C_{0}^{-1}r^{n}\leq\mathcal{H}^{n}(E\cap B(x,r))\leq C_{0}r^{n}

for all
x\in E
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E)
, and has
Big Pieces of
Lipschitz images (BPLI) of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
i.e. there exist constant
\theta,L\geq 0
such that for each
x\in E
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E)
there is an

L
-Lipschitz map
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that

(1.0.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(E\cap B(x,r)\cap f(B_{n}(0,r)))\geq\theta r^{n},

where
B_{n}(0,r)
denotes the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
centred at the origin of
radius
r.

In
[
DS93a
]
, David and Semmes proved many
characterizations of uniformly rectifiable sets. To state a characterization in terms of tangents, we need some
notation. For a set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
, a point
x\in E
and a radius

0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E),
define

(1.0.3)

\displaystyle b\beta_{E}(x,r)=\frac{1}{r}\inf_{L}\left(\sup_{y\in E\cap B(x,r)}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,L)+\sup_{y\in L\cap B(x,r)}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,E)\right),

where the infimum is taken over all
n
-dimensional affine subspaces
L

in
\mathbb{R}^{m}.
Thus,
b\beta_{E}
measures how well
E
can be
approximated by an
n
-dimensional affine subspace in a ball, with respect to
Hausdorff distance; how “flat” the set is. We will use it together with the following condition which quantifies an
event
occurring at
most
scales
r>0
and locations
x\in E.

Definition 1.0.2
.

Let
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be Ahlfors
n
-regular. A non-negative Borel
measure
\mu
on
E\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E))
is called a
Carleson
measure
if there exists
C>0
such that
\mu(B(x,r)\times(0,r))\leq Cr^{n}
for all
x\in E
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E).
The smallest such

C
is called the
Carleson norm
of
\mu
. A set
\mathscr{A}\subseteq E\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E)
is a
Carleson set
if

\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{A}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)\tfrac{dr}{r}
is a Carleson measure.

Note, if
\mathscr{A}=E\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E)),
then for
\mu=\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{A}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}\tfrac{dr}{r}
we have
\mu(B(x,r)\times(0,r))=\infty
for all
x
and
r.
Thus, roughly speaking,
\mathscr{A}
is a Carleson
set if set of scales
r
and locations
x
such that
(x,r)\in\mathscr{A}
is
small.

Definition 1.0.3
.

Let
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be Ahlfors
n
-regular. We say
E
satisfies the

Bi-lateral Weak Geometric Lemma
(BWGL) if for each
\varepsilon>0

the set
\{(x,r)\in E\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}b\beta_{E}(x,r)>\varepsilon\}
is a
Carleson set.

The BWGL condition is a quantitative version of having a
rotating tangent
i.e. a tangent that can depend on the scale. Marstrand

[
Mar61
]
and Mattila
[
Mat75
]

showed that the existence of a rotating tangent plane at

\mathcal{H}^{n}
-a.e. point (together with a mild dimensional assumption)
ensures rectifiability. The following result of David and Semmes characterizes uniform rectifiability in terms of the BWGL.

Theorem 1.0.4
.

Let
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be Ahlfors
n
-regular. Then
E
is uniformly

n
-rectifiable if and only if it satisfies the
BWGL
.

With the recent interest in studying analysis on metric spaces,
questions regarding the rectifiability of such spaces naturally arise.
The definition of rectifiability in metric spaces differs slightly from
the Euclidean one above. A metric space
X
is
n
-rectifiable
if it
can be covered, up to a set of
\mathcal{H}^{n}
measure zero, by countably many
Lipschitz images of
subsets
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
One of the first to
consider rectifiability in general metric spaces was Kirchheim

[
Kir94
]
, who proved that Lipschitz functions

f\colon A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to X
are differentiable (in a suitable metric
sense) almost everywhere, and consequently derived a local structure
theory of rectifiable metric spaces. Using Kirchheim’s metric Rademacher
theorem, it is possible to define a notion of an approximate tangent
spaces in terms of local approximation, in terms of the
Gromov–Hausdorff distance, to an
n
-dimensional Banach space. The
Gromov–Hausdorff distance is an
intrinsic
property of the metric
space and hence these tangent spaces do not require additional structure
of the ambient metric space. Recently the first named author proved the
converse direction, that is, the existence of Gromov–Hausdorff
approximate tangent spaces implies rectifiability

[
Bat22
]
. Since the Gromov–Hausdorff distance is
an isometric invariant of metric spaces, this result generalizes those
of Marstrand and Mattila.

Correct notions of quantitative rectifiability in metric spaces have
been sought for some time. With the notable exception of G. C. David’s
[
Dav16
]
, for arbitrary metric spaces the story so far
has been entirely focussed on 1-dimensional spaces. The papers

[
Hah05
,
Hah08
,
Sch07a
,
DS21
]
prove results analogous to the Travelling Salesman Theorem of Jones for general metric spaces. In
[
Hah08
,
Sch07a
]

the authors must assume Ahlfors regularity of the spaces while in

[
Hah05
,
DS21
]
no such assumption is needed.
Instead of measuring flatness via local approximations by lines,
flatness is measured using Menger curvature. See also
[
LW09
,
LW11
]
for higher dimensional sets in Hilbert space.

For specific metric space or by assuming to ambient structure, more
progress has been made. In the Heisenberg group and for more general
Carnot groups, Travelling Salesman type theorems have been proven in

[
CLZ19
,
FFP07
,
Jui10
,
LS16a
,
LS16b
]
with a complete solution given by S. Li in
[
Li22
]
. In these spaces a version of uniform rectifiability defined in terms of
big pieces of
intrinsic Lipschitz graphs
seems to yield
positive results, see for example

[
CFO19
,
CLY22
,
CLY23
,
FOR20
,
Rig19
]
.
The correct notion of uniform rectifiability in parabolic space was
introduced in
[
HLN03
,
HLN04
]
. See

[
BHH+23
]
and the reference therein for results in this
direction. A Travelling Salesman Theorem for subsets of Hilbert space
was proven in
[
Sch07b
]
, an analogue for higher
dimensional subsets can be found in
[
Hyd22a
]
. A combination of
the papers
[
BM23a
,
BM23b
]
provides a
Travelling Salesman Theorem for subsets of Banach spaces. See also

[
ENV19
]
, who prove higher dimensional analogues for
measures in Hilbert and Banach spaces.

1.1.
New results

In this paper we prove a version of Theorem
1.0.4
for general metric spaces (Theorem
A
). As in the case of going from Euclidean rectifiability to rectifiability in metric spaces, we must slightly modify the definition of UR and we must change the way in which we measure local flatness. To go from Euclidean UR to UR in metric spaces we again look at Lipschitz images of
subsets
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see Definition
1.2.1
). A significant difference is that our definition of BWGL uses the Gromov-Hausdorff distance to measure closeness to an
n
-dimensional Banach space. That is, one replaces
b\beta
in Definition
1.0.3
with
\xi_{X}
, a suitable Gromov-Hausdorff version of
b\beta
. See Definition
3.1.3
for the definition of
\xi_{X}
and Definition
3.1.5
for the explicit definition of BWGL in metric spaces. This intrinsic notion of flatness allows us to rid ourselves of requiring ambient structure in the metric space.

Theorem A
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space. Then
X
is
UR
if and only if
X
satisfies the
BWGL
.

One could be tempted to prove Theorem
A
by quantifying the techniques from
[
Bat22
]
. Indeed, this was our first attempt. In short, this fails because the Carleson condition in BWGL is not
strong enough to construct the Hölder mapping in

[
Bat22
]
(we discuss this a little more in Section

1.5
below). An alternate approach to prove Theorem
A
would be to translate the classical proof of Theorem
1.0.4
to metric spaces. However, the known proofs rely significantly on either singular integral techniques, Lipschitz graphs or the ambient Euclidean structure.

In pursuit of Theorem
A
, we pass via two more conditions inspired by Euclidean uniform rectifiability. The first of these is related to the
Corona Decomposition
, introduced by Semmes and David-Semmes for subsets of Euclidean space in
[
Sem90
,
DS91
]
. We introduce the
Corona Decomposition by normed spaces
which is a “Gromov-Hausdorff” version of the Euclidean corona decomposition that can be defined for any metric space.
As in the Euclidean case, this serves as a key bridge between UR and the BWGL. We also rely on a
Corona Decomposition by Ahlfors regular Reifenberg flat spaces
and notions of quantitative differentiation of Lipschitz functions on metric spaces. Theorem
B
connects all these notions and others (which we define momentarily).

Theorem B
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space. Then, the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

X
is UR.

(2)

X
satisfies a Carlson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients.

(3)

X
satisfies the
BWGL
.

(4)

X
admits a corona decomposition by Ahlfors regular Reifenberg flat metrics on
\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(5)

X
admits a corona decomposition by normed spaces.

Crucial to the proof of Theorem
B
is the analysis of Reifenberg flat metric spaces, which we return to below, see Theorem
C
.

1.2.
Uniformly
n
-rectifiable (UR)

A metric variant of Definition
1.0.1
is the following.

Definition 1.2.1
.

A metric space
X
is said to be
uniformly
n
-rectifiable
(UR) if it is
Ahlfors
n
-regular
(see Definition
3.1.1
) and has
Big Pieces of Lipschitz Images (BPLI) of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
i.e. there exist constant
\theta,L\geq 0
such that for each
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E)
there is a set
F\subset B_{n}(0,r)
and an
L
-Lipschitz map
f\colon F\to X
such that

(1.2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(X\cap B(x,r)\cap f(F))\geq\theta r^{n},

where
B_{n}(0,r)
denotes the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
centered at the origin of radius
r.

Definition 1.2.2
.

A metric space
X
is said to have
Big Pieces of Bi-Lipschitz Images (BPBI) of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

if it is
Ahlfors
n
-regular
there exist constant
\theta,L\geq 0
such that for each
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E)
there is a set
F\subset B_{n}(0,r)
and an
L
-bi-Lipschitz map
f\colon F\to X
such that

(1.2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(X\cap B(x,r)\cap f(F))\geq\theta r^{n},

where
B_{n}(0,r)
denotes the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
centered at the origin of radius
r.

The above list of equivalences can be further extended using
[
Sch09
]
. Specifically, in
[
Sch09
, Corollary 1.2]
it is shown that UR is equivalent to having BPBI. It follows from the results in Section
4.2
that BPBI is equivalent to having
\text{BP}^{2}
(BI), that is, sets which have big of sets which have BPBI (see
[
KS22
]
for a precise definition of
\text{BP}^{j}
and related results). Indeed, if
X
has
\text{BP}^{2}
(BI) then it has a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients (Proposition
4.2.1
) and is therefore UR by Theorem
B
. Thus, we can add the following to the list in Theorem
B
.

(6)

X
has
\text{BP}^{j}
(BI) for some
j\geq 1.

(7)

X
has
\text{BP}^{j}
(BI) for all
j\geq 1.

1.3.
The
\gamma
-coefficients and the BWGL

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space. For
\varepsilon>0
we say
X
satisfies the
Bi-lateral Weak Geometric Lemma with parameter
\varepsilon
(BWGL
(\varepsilon)
) if the set
\{(x,r)\in E\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\xi_{X}(x,r)>\varepsilon\}
is a Carleson set (see Definition
2.6.4
, the metric analogue of Definition
1.0.2
). The coefficient
\xi_{X}
is a suitable Gromov-Hausdorff version of
b\beta
(see Definition
3.1.3
, the metric analogue of (
1.0.3
)). In particular,
\xi_{X}(x,r)
measures how close (in Gromov-Hausdorff distance) the ball
B(x,r)\subseteq X
is to a ball of the same radius in a
n
-dimensional Banach space. We emphasize that the Banach space, in particular the norm, can change depending on both
r
and
x
. We say
X
satisfies the
Bi-lateral Weak Geometric Lemma
(BWGL) if for each
\varepsilon>0
it satisfies the BWGL
(\varepsilon)
. In fact, in both Theorem
A
and Theorem
B
, the proof shows that if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small then the condition BWGL may be replaced by the condition BWGL
(\varepsilon)
. Explicitly, one may add to Theorem
B
the following.

(8)

X
satisfies the BWGL
(\varepsilon)
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0.

In order to quantify Rademacher’s Theorem, it is natural to approximate a Lipschitz map
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
on a ball
B(x,r)
by an affine function
A\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},
see
[
Dor85
]
. David and Semmes generalized this quantitative differentiation to
f\colon E\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
where
E
is an Ahlfors
n
-regular set in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. This generalization was called the WALA (Weak Approximation of Lipschitz functions by Affine functions) condition, see
[
DS93a
, Definition I.2.47]
, and is stated in terms of Carleson sets.

For a Lipschitz
f\colon X\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|),
we introduce the coefficient
\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|}^{K}(x,r)
which in addition to measuring how bi-laterally flat
B_{X}(x,r)
is (in the sense of
\xi_{X}(x,r)
), also measures how well
f
can be approximated by a
K
-Lipschitz affine function on the best approximating tangent space to
B_{X}(x,r)
(Definition
3.1.6
). A Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients is a type of quantitative differentiation for arbitrary metric spaces, see Definition
3.1.7
. We will be more precise about what we mean by this in Section
4
.

At first glance the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients is stronger than the BWGL. Indeed, for every ball
\gamma_{X,f}^{K}\geq\xi_{X}
. Theorem
B
says that the Carleson conditions for these respective quantities are equivalent.

An
L^{p}
version of the
\gamma
coefficients has been defined recently in
[
AMV23
]
where they prove quantitative differentiability estimates for Sobolev functions on UR subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}.

1.4.
Corona Decompositions

The corona decomposition for subsets of Euclidean space was introduced in
[
Sem90
,
DS91
]
. Very roughly speaking, for a set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
this means that there is a subset of bad pairs
(x,r)\in E\cap B(z,R)\times(0,R)
satisfying a Carleson condition such that if we remove the corresponding balls from
E\cap B(z,R)
, then the remainder can be well-approximated by a Lipschitz graph. Here, well-approximated at a point
x
means approximation up to the smallest
r
such that
B(x,r)
is disjoint from a fixed dilation of balls in the Carleson set. We make use of two analogous definitions using the Gromov-Hausdorff distance to measure approximations. In one case the approximating spaces are Reifenberg flat metrics on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see below). In the other case, they are
n
-dimensional Banach spaces. See Section
6
. While these conditions look quite different, it turns out they are equivalent by Theorem
B
.

We mention that corona decompositions have been introduced in various specific metric spaces. In the context of Heisenberg and Carnot groups such objects have been considered in
[
DDF22
]
and
[
NY22
]
, respectively. In parabolic space, see
[
BHH+22
,
BHH+23
]
. The graphs used in each of these contexts differ from those used in the Euclidean setting, but both rely on some ambient structure to define the approximations. As for our other conditions, our use of the Gromov-Hausdorff distance avoids us relying on such structure.

1.5.
Reifenberg flat metric spaces

Definition 1.5.1
.

A metric space
X
is said to be
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat if
\xi_{X}(x,r)\leq\delta
for all
x\in X
and
0<r<\infty.
At various points we may simply write
RF
.

Reifenberg
[
Rei60
]
first studied these sets in Euclidean space (defined as in Definition
1.5.1
except with
\xi
replaced by
b\beta
), where he shows that such sets are locally bi-Hölder equivalent to a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
A vast generalization of this result was given more recently by David and Toro in
[
DT12
]
, to allow for sets with holes. Reifenberg flat metric spaces have been studied in
[
CC97
]
and
[
DT99
]
. In
[
CC97
]
it is shown that Reifenberg flat metric spaces are locally bi-Hölder equivalent to a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, generalising the result of Reifenberg to metric spaces. In
[
DT99
]
, the authors construct interesting embeddings of such spaces into lower dimensional Euclidean spaces. In all instances above, the definition of Reifenberg flat is slightly different to that given in Definition
1.5.1
. Namely, they assume the approximating tangent spaces are Euclidean.

In Theorem
5.1.1
we prove a Reifenberg type theorem for metric spaces that lies somewhere between that of Cheeger-Colding
[
CC97
]
and David-Toro
[
DT12
]
. We do not assume bi-lateral approximations at all scales as in
[
CC97
]
, but we do not have as general assumptions as those contain in
[
DT12
]
. Our result differs fundamentally in the fact that we allow non-Euclidean tangents, namely
n
-dimensional Banach spaces, which may vary at different scales and locations. Roughly speaking, we show that if
X
is a metric space and for each
x\in X
there exists a scale
0<r_{x}<\infty
(with the compatibility between the scales of nearby points) such that
\xi_{X}(x,r)\leq\delta
for all
r_{x}<r<\infty
then we can find a metric
\rho
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is bi-Hölder equivalent to the Euclidean metric, such that
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
approximates
X
up to the scales determined by the
r_{x}.
The result in its full generality is rather complicated to state. Theorem
C
below is a simplified version of Theorem
5.1.1
where the Reifenberg condition is assumed to hold up to some uniform scale across
X
. Theorem
5.1.1
is well-suited to stopping-time constructions.

Theorem C
.

For each
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that the following holds. Let
X
be a metric space that is
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat up to scale
s_{0}>0
, that is,
\xi_{X}(x,r)\leq\delta
for all
x\in X
and
s_{0}<r<\infty.
Then, for each
x\in X
and
r>0
there exists a metric
\rho
which is bi-Hölder equivalent to
|\cdot|
, the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and a map
\bar{g}\colon B_{X}(x,r)\to B_{\rho}(0,2r)
such that

(1.5.1)

\displaystyle|\rho(\bar{g}(y),\bar{g}(z))-d_{X}(y,z)|\leq\varepsilon(d_{X}(y,z)+s_{0})

for all
y,z\in B_{X}(x,r)
and

(1.5.2)

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in B_{\rho}(0,(1-\varepsilon)r)}\inf_{\bar{g}(B_{X}(x,r))}\rho(u,\cdot)\leq\varepsilon s_{0}.

If additionally
X
is Ahlfors
n
-regular, then
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
is Ahlfors
n
-regular with regularity constant depending on
n
and the regularity constant for
X
.

Theorem
C
is related to
[
Bat22
, Theorem
3.7]
which constructs a Hölder map

\iota\colon B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)\to\tilde{X}
into a metric space
containing
X
, whose image is mostly contained in
X
. This is achieved
under the assumption that
B(x,r)
is well approximated by tangent
planes for many
x\in X
and all scales
r<s_{0}
.
This final hypothesis is crucial for the construction, which iteratively
constructs the Hölder map, scale by scale.
As alluded to above, this hypothesis does not follow from the BWGL;
Indeed, it is possible that all locations may have many consecutive
scales that are not approximated by tangent planes where the iteration
cannot continue.
To the best of our knowledge, this situation is unworkable.

Instead, we resort to the weaker conclusion from Theorem

C
to gain the advantage that we only require
approximations at scales
r>s_{0}
. This hypothesis can be
obtained from the BWGL, after a decomposition into stopping time regions.
More precisely, we obtain a corona decomposition by Reifenberg flat
metric spaces (see Section
7
). In order to prove UR, we show that RF metric spaces have corona
decompositions by normed spaces (see Section
6
).
By combining these two results, we see that the BWGL implies a corona
decomposition by sets with corona decompositions by normed spaces.
With some more work, UR follows from this conclusion (see Sections

8
and
9
).

We give a more detailed overview of our proof strategy in Section

1.7
below.

1.6.
Other remarks

The Ribe program (originally formulated by Bourgain
in
[
Bou86
]
) asks for a dictionary between results in
metric geometry and Banach space geometry, see

[
Nao12
]

and

[
Nao18
]
.
A standard question related to this program (see e.g. Naor’s

[
Nao18
, Problem 2]
) is the question of which metric spaces bi-Lipschitz embed into which
others, with specific focus given to Euclidean targets

[
Hei03
,
Ost13
,
Sem99
]
.
In Theorem
A
and Theorem
B
we give a
characterization
of metric spaces which have, up to controlled
distortion, uniformly big pieces which have linear structure, that is,
uniformly big pieces which bi-Lipschitzly map into a Euclidean space of the same dimension.
This characterization is in terms of intrinsic metric quantities.

One naturally wants to ask whether this can be used to map a whole
(Uniformly Rectifiable) metric space
X
bi-Lipschitzly into Euclidean
space.
Semmes, in his 1999 paper
[
Sem99
, Proposition 2.10]
, showed that one may deform the distance on
X
by an

A_{1}
weight and map the resulting metric space bi-Lipschitzly into
Euclidean space. In fact, his result is proven in the more general
category of
Doubling
metric spaces which have
Big Pieces of Euclidean
Space
. We will not define these concepts here, but rather refer to the
beautiful exposition of
[
Sem99
]
and note that

[
Sem99
, Section 4]
is devoted to UR metric spaces. Thus, for an Ahlfors regular space
X
satisfying BWGL, one has the conclusion of
[
Sem99
, Proposition 2.10]
.

Another natural question is to what extent can the quantitative nature be salvaged if one does not assume Ahlfors regularity and the dimension
is larger than 1 (we have already discussed the one dimensional case in
the first part of the introduction).
In the Euclidean case, there are plenty of results in this direction (see e.g.
[
DT12
,
AT15
,
Tol15
,
AS18
,
ENV19
,
Vil19
,
AV21
,
Hyd22b
]
) and a natural question is if these can
be transported to the metric setting.
We note that Theorem
C
and Theorem
5.1.1
as well
as the stopping time style of the proof of Theorem
B
suggest that a discrete version of Theorem
B
holds and
gives some hope for further generalizations. See Section
10
for an example in this direction.

The expert will note that we do not have, in Theorem
B
, a condition on the square summability of some suitable variant of the Jones
\beta
-number i.e. that
\beta^{2}(x,r)d\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)\tfrac{dr}{r}
is a Carleson measure, as in
[
DS93a
]
. We leave this as an open question. Generalising the main result in
[
AS14
]
would be a first step towards such a condition.

1.7.
Structure of the paper and outline of the proof of Theorem
B

We took care in formatting the table of contents so that it may serve the reader as a guide through the proof
. Here, we give a more detailed outline.

In Section
2
we discuss general preliminaries needed for the rest of the paper. Section
3
focusses of preliminaries related to the theory of uniform rectifiability. It is there that we introduce the
\xi
and
\gamma
coefficients and study their fundamental properties. The implication (1) implies (2) is proven in Section
4
and is carried out in two stages. We first show that the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients is
stable under big pieces
(Proposition
4.2.1
) which, roughly speaking, means if
X
is a metric space,
\mathcal{F}
is a family of metric spaces which satisfies the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients, and
X
has big pieces of
\mathcal{F}
(see Definition
3.1.2
) then
X
also satisfies the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients. The second stage consist of showing that metric spaces which are bi-Lipschitz equivalent to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfy a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients (see Definition
3.1.7
and Proposition
4.4.1
). If
X
is a UR metric space, we can show that
X
has big pieces of bi-Lipschitz images of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(after embedding in some suitable Banach space
\mathcal{B}
). Thus, by combining the above two stages we complete the proof of this implication.

Section
5
is independent of the work in Sections
3
and
4
. In Section
5
we prove (the more general statement of) Theorem
C
inspired by the scheme described in
[
CC97
]
. We need to construct the metric
\rho
and the map
\bar{g}.
We obtain
\rho
by first constructing a sequence
M_{\ell}
of smooth connected manifolds,
f_{\ell}\colon M_{\ell}\to M_{\ell+1}
of diffeomorphisms and
\phi_{\ell}
of semi-metrics on
M_{\ell}
. Pulling the
\phi_{\ell}
back to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
via the
f_{\ell}
and taking a limit gives a semi-metric
\phi_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(Lemma
5.6.3
). We then obtain the metric
\rho
from
\phi_{\infty}
(Definition
5.6.7
). The
M_{\ell}
are constructed in two stages. In the first stage we construct a sequence
W_{\ell}
of (possibly) non-connected manifolds (Section
5.3
) and a sequence of smooth mappings
h_{\ell}\colon W_{\ell}\to W_{\ell+1}
(Section
5.4
). In the second stage we glue the
W_{\ell}
using the maps
h_{\ell}
to construct the connected manifolds
M_{\ell}
(Section
5.5
). The maps
f_{\ell}
are also constructed in Section
5.5
and the semi-metrics in Section
5.6
. In Section
5.7
and Section
5.8
respectively we construct the metric
\rho
and the map
\bar{g}
as in Theorem
C
and prove the desired properties. In Section
5.9
, we consider the Ahlfors regular case. In Section
5.10
, we show how Theorem
C
follows from Theorem
5.1.1
.

We then return to proving the rest of Theorem
B
. The implication (2) implies (3) is immediate from the fact that
\gamma_{X,f}^{K}\geq\xi_{X}
(see the paragraph before Theorem
B
or Lemma
3.1.8
).

In Section
6
we show that Ahlfors regular Reifenberg flat metric spaces admit a corona decomposition by normed spaces (see Theorem
6.1.1
and Definition
6.4.2
). This is carried out in two stages. In stage one we show, using Theorem
5.1.1
and a result of Guy C. David
[
Dav16
]
on bi-Lipschitz decomposition of Lipschitz maps on metric spaces, that Ahlfors regular Reifenberg flat metric spaces are UR (Proposition
6.3.6
). A short description of this step is given at the beginning of Section
6.3
. The implication (1) implies (2) tells us now that our space satisfies a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients. In stage two we leverage this fact to construct a corona decomposition for Reifenberg flat sets. This strategy is explained after the statement of Proposition
6.5.1
.

The implication (8) implies (4), and hence (3) implies (4), is proven in Section
7
(see Proposition
7.0.1
). The argument is an application of Theorem
5.1.1
inside a stopping-time argument.

The implication (4) implies (5) is based on Corollary
7.0.2
and the main result in Section
8
. We showed in Section
6
that Ahlfors regular Reifenberg flat metric spaces admit a corona decomposition by normed spaces. Thus, any space satisfying (4) has a corona decomposition by spaces which admit corona decompositions by normed spaces (
\text{CD}^{2}(\text{normed spaces})
), see Corollary
7.0.2
. Then, based on original arguments by David and Semmes, we show that any space satisfying
\text{CD}^{2}(\text{normed spaces})
also has a corona decomposition by normed spaces (Proposition
8.0.1
).

The implication (5) implies (1) is carried out in Section
9
. In fact, we show that (5) implies BPBI which implies immediately UR. The proof follows closely the arguments in
[
DS91
, Section 16-17]
which give the analogous implication in Euclidean space. We have included the details for completeness.

1.8.
Acknowledgments

We thank Matthew Badger for comments on a preliminary version of the introduction to the paper.

2.
Preliminaries

Contents of this section

2.1.
Notation

Throughout the paper
C
will denote some absolute constant (depending perhaps on
n
) which may change from line to line. If there exists
C\geq 1
such that
a\leq Cb,
then we shall write
a\lesssim b.
If the constant
C
depends on a parameter
t
, we shall write
a\lesssim_{t}b.
We shall write
a\sim b
if
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a,
similarly we define
a\sim_{t}b.
We shall not keep track of how the constants depend on
n
.

Suppose
(X,d)
is a metric space. Whenever we want to emphasize the metric on
X
we will write
d_{X}.
We denote by
B_{X}(x,r)
or
B_{d}(x,r)
the closed ball in
X
centred at
x
of radius
r
. If the context is clear we may simply write
B(x,r).
If
B
is a ball in
X
we may write
x_{B}
to denote its centre and
r_{B}
to denote its radius. For sets
E,F\subseteq X
let

\displaystyle\text{dist}(E,F)

\displaystyle=\inf\{d(x,y)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x\in E,\ y\in F\};

\displaystyle\text{diam}(E)

\displaystyle=\sup\{d(x,y)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x,y\in F\}

The
n
-dimensional Hausdorff measure of
E
, is defined as

(2.1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(E)=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\inf\left\{\sum_{i}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(U_{i})^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}A\subseteq\bigcup_{i}U_{i}\ \text{and}\ \mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}U_{i}\leq\delta\right\}.

At various point throughout the paper we will write
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{d}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}_{d}
and
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{d}
when we wish to emphasize the metric with which these quantities are defined. If the metric is induced by a norm
\|\cdot\|,
we may also write
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\|\cdot\|},

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}_{\|\cdot\|}
and
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\|\cdot\|}.

Suppose
f\colon X\to Y
is a map between metric spaces,
L>0
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
. We say
f
is
L
-
Lipschitz
if

(2.1.2)

\displaystyle d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))\leq Ld_{X}(x,y)

for all
x,y\in X
and
L
-
bi-Lipschitz
if

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle L^{-1}d_{X}(x,y)\leq d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))\leq Ld_{X}(x,y).

for all
x,y\in X.
If there exists
C\geq 1
such that

d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))\leq Cd_{X}(x,y)^{\alpha}

for all
x,y\in X
then we say
f
is
Hölder with exponent

\alpha.
If there is
C\geq 1
such that

(2.1.4)

\displaystyle C^{-1}d_{X}(x,y)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\leq d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))\leq Cd_{X}(x,y)^{\alpha}.

for all
x,y\in X
then we say
f
is
bi-Hölder with exponent
\alpha.
We say two metric spaces
X
and
Y
are
bi-Hölder equivalent with exponent
\alpha
if there exists a surjective map
f\colon X\to Y
which is bi-Hölder with exponent
\alpha.

2.2.
Normed space and Banach-Mazur distance

Let
Q(n)
denote the set of all
n
-dimensional normed spaces. Define the
Banach-Mazur distance
between
X,Y\in Q(n)
by

d_{\mathrm{BM}}(X,Y)=\inf\left\{\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|T^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\right\},

where the infimum is taken over all isomorphisms
T
from
X
onto
Y
.

Remark 2.2.1
.

It is known by a result of John
[
Joh48
]
that
d_{\mathrm{BM}}(X,Y)\leq n
for all
X,Y\in Q(n).
Furthermore, if
I(n)
denotes the space of isometry classes of
Q(n),
then
(I(n),\log d_{\mathrm{BM}})
defines a compact metric space.

For a pair of normed spaces
X
and
Y
, let
\mathcal{B}(X,Y)
denote the space of bounded linear operators from
X
to
Y
. We equip
\mathcal{B}(X,Y)
with the operator norm defined by

\|T\|_{\mathcal{B}(X,Y)}=\sup_{\|x\|_{X}=1}\|T(x)\|_{Y}.

In the case that
X=Y
we will write
\mathcal{B}(X)
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{B}(X)}
for short. If the context is clear, we will simply write
\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}.
Let
U\subseteq X
be open. A mapping
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
is in
C^{1}(U)
if its Fréchet derivative
Df
exists and is continuous at each
x\in U.
We say
f\in C^{2}(U)
if
f,Df\in C^{1}(U)
. For such a map, let
D^{2}f
denote Fréchet derivate of
Df.
If
x\in U
, then
Df(x)\in\mathcal{B}(X,Y)
and
D^{2}f(x)\in\mathcal{B}(X,\mathcal{B}(X,Y)).
For
r>0
and
m\in\{1,2\},
we define

(2.2.1)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{m}(U),r}=\sup_{x\in U}\left\{r^{-1}\|f(x)\|_{F}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}r^{k-1}\|D^{k}f(x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\right\},

where
D^{1}f(x)=Df(x).

2.3.
Gromov-Hausdorff approximations

In this section we define the notion of Gromov-Hausdorff approximations and prove some useful properties concerning them.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
be compact metric spaces and
\delta>0
. We say a map
\phi\colon X\to Y
is a
\delta
-
Gromov-Hausdorff approximation
(
\delta
-GHA) if it is a
\delta
-
isometry
, meaning

(2.3.1)

\displaystyle\sup_{x,y\in X}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d_{X}(x,y)-d_{Y}(\phi(x),\phi(y))\right\rvert}\leq\delta,

and if
\phi(X)
is
\delta
-
dense in

Y
, meaning

(2.3.2)

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in Y}{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{Y}(u,\phi(X))\leq\delta.

Remark 2.3.2
.

We do not impose that
\phi
be Lipschitz. Note however that if
\mathcal{N}
is a well-separated collection of points in
X
then
\phi|_{\mathcal{N}}
is Lipschitz. Under certain assumptions on
X
and
Y
, we can always
C(n)\delta
-GHA
\varphi\colon X\to Y
which well-approximates
\phi
(see Lemma
2.3.5
).

Suppose
B
is a ball in some
n
-dimensional normed space and
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to B
is a
\delta
-GHA. While
\phi
is not necessarily Lipschitz, it is possible to find a nearby
C\delta
-GHA which is Lipschitz. Before proving this we needs some auxiliary results.

Lemma 2.3.3
.

Suppose
(Y,\|\cdot\|)
is a normed space,
z\in Y
and
\lambda>0
. Define a map
p_{z,\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to B_{Y}(z,\lambda)
by

p_{z,\lambda}(y)=z+\frac{y-z}{\max\{1,\|y-z\|/\lambda\}}.

Then
p_{z,\lambda}
is 2-Lipschitz and

(2.3.3)

\displaystyle\|p_{z,\lambda}(y)-y\|\leq\max\{0,\|y-z\|-\lambda\}

for all
y\in Y.

Proof.

By translating it suffices to prove the case
z=0.
Let us write
p_{\lambda}=p_{0,\lambda}.
It is easy to check that
\|p_{\lambda}(y)\|\leq\lambda
for all
y\in Y
so
p_{\lambda}
takes values in
B_{Y}(0,\lambda)
. We now consider (
2.3.3
). Let
y\in Y
and, to ease notation, let
s(y)=\max\{1,\|y\|/\lambda\}.
If
\|y\|\leq\lambda
then
s(y)=1
so that
p_{\lambda}(y)=y
and (
2.3.3
) follows immediately. Suppose instead that
\|y\|>\lambda.
Then
s(y)=\|y\|/\lambda
and

\|p_{\lambda}(y)-y\|=\bigg\|\frac{\lambda y}{\|y\|}-y\bigg\|=\big|\lambda-\|y\|\big|=\|y\|-\lambda.

Finally, we check that
p_{\lambda}
is 2-Lipschitz. Let
x,y\in Y
and suppose without loss of generality that
\|x\|\leq\|y\|.
If
\|y\|\leq\lambda
then
p_{\lambda}(x)=x
and
p_{\lambda}(y)=y
so there is nothing to show. Suppose instead that
\|y\|>\lambda.
In this case, we have
1\leq s(x)\leq\|y\|/\lambda
and
\|x\|\leq\lambda s(x).
Combining this with the triangle inequality, we get

(2.3.4)

\displaystyle\|p_{\lambda}(x)-p_{\lambda}(y)\|

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\frac{x}{s(x)}-\frac{\lambda y}{\|y\|}\bigg\|=\frac{1}{s(x)}\bigg\|x-\frac{\lambda s(x)y}{\|y\|}\bigg\|\leq\|x-y\|+\bigg|1-\frac{\lambda s(x)}{\|y\|}\bigg|\|y\|

(2.3.5)

\displaystyle\leq\|x-y\|+\bigg(1-\frac{\|x\|}{\|y\|}\bigg)\|y\|\leq 2\|x-y\|,

which finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.3.4
.

Let
L\geq 0,

X
be a metric space and
Y
an
n
-dimensional normed space. Suppose
A\subseteq X
and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}A\to Y
is
L
-Lipschitz, then there exists an
nL
-Lipschitz extension
F\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
of
f.

Proof.

Let
\ell^{n}_{\infty}=(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}).
By Remark
2.2.1
, we have
d_{\mathrm{BM}}(Y,\ell_{\infty}^{n})\leq n
so there exists an isomorphism
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to\ell^{n}_{\infty}
satisfying
\|T\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
and
\|T^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq n.
Let
g=T\circ f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}A\to\ell^{n}_{\infty}.
By applying the McShane Extension Theorem
[
Hei01
, Theorem 6.2]
to each component of
T\circ f,
we obtain an
L
-Lipschitz extension
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to\ell^{n}_{\infty}
of
T\circ f.
It is then easy to check that
T^{-1}\circ g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
is the required
nL
-Lipschitz extension of
f.

∎

Lemma 2.3.5
.

Let
0<\delta<1,

(X,d)
a compact metric space and
(Y,\|\cdot\|)
an
n
-dimensional normed space. Suppose
B\subseteq Y
is a ball of radius
r>0
and
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to B
is a
\delta r
-GHA. Then, there exists a
20n\delta r
-GHA
\varphi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to B
which is
20n
-Lipschitz such that

(2.3.6)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in X}\|\phi(x)-\varphi(x)\|\leq 20n\delta r.

Proof.

By translating and scaling we may assume
x_{B}=0
and
r=1.
Let
\mathcal{N}
be a maximal net in
X
such that
d(x,y)\geq\delta
for all distinct
x,y\in\mathcal{N}.
Since
\phi
is a
\delta
-GHA, if
x,y\in\mathcal{N}
are distinct then

\|\phi(x)-\phi(y)\|\leq\delta+d(x,y)\leq 2d(x,y).

It follows that
\phi|_{\mathcal{N}}
is
2
-Lipschitz. By Lemma
2.3.4
, we may extend
\phi|_{\mathcal{N}}
to a
2n
-Lipschitz map
\tilde{\varphi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
. The map
\tilde{\varphi}
is almost what we need, however, it may still take values outside
B
. We instead define

\varphi=p_{0,1}\circ\tilde{\varphi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to B,

where
p_{0,1}
is the map from Lemma
2.3.3
. Since
p_{0,1}
is 2-Lipschitz we know
\varphi
is
4n
-Lipschitz. Next, let us check (
2.3.6
). Suppose
x\in X
and choose
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
d(x,x^{\prime})\leq\delta.
Using that
\tilde{\varphi}
is
2n
-Lipschitz and
\tilde{\varphi}(x^{\prime})=\phi(x^{\prime})
, we have

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\varphi}(x)-\phi(x)\|\leq\|\tilde{\varphi}(x)-\tilde{\varphi}(x^{\prime})\|+\|\phi(x^{\prime})-\phi(x)\|\leq(2n+1)d(x,x^{\prime})+\delta\leq 4n\delta.

Since
\phi
takes values in
B
we must have
\|\tilde{\varphi}(x)\|\leq 1+4n\delta
(recall that
B
is centred at the origin with unit radius). With the definition of
\varphi,
(
2.3.3
) implies
\|\varphi(x)-\tilde{\varphi}(x)\|\leq 4n\delta
. Combined with (
2.3.7
), this gives

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle\|\varphi(x)-\phi(x)\|\leq\|\varphi(x)-\tilde{\varphi}(x)\|+\|\tilde{\varphi}(x)-\phi(x)\|\leq 8n\delta

and completes the proof of (
2.3.6
). It only remains for us to check that
\varphi
defines a
20n\delta
-GHA. By (
2.3.6
), the triangle inequality and the fact that
\phi
is a
\delta
-GHA, if
x,y\in X
then

(2.3.9)

\displaystyle\left|d(x,y)-\|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\|\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|d(x,y)-\|\phi(x)-\phi(y)\|\right|+\|\varphi(x)-\phi(x)\|

(2.3.10)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|\varphi(y)-\phi(y)\|\leq 20n\delta,

so
\varphi
defines a
20n\delta
-isometry. Furthermore, if
u\in B
then there exists
x\in X
such that
\|\phi(x)-u\|\leq\delta.
By (
2.3.6
) we have
\|\varphi(x)-u\|\leq 9n\delta
and so
\varphi(X)
is
9n
-dense in
B
.
∎

If one of
X
or
Y
is a normed space,
B\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
are balls, and
\phi\colon B\to B^{\prime}
is a
\delta
-GHA, the next lemma tells us that
\phi(x_{B})
is mapped close to
x_{B^{\prime}}.
Additionally, for another ball
B^{\prime\prime}\subseteq B
, by slightly modifying the restriction of
\phi
to
B^{\prime\prime}
we are able to construct a
C\delta
-GHA from
B^{\prime\prime}
to a ball of the same radius in
Y
.

Lemma 2.3.6
.

Let
0<2\delta<\alpha<1.
Suppose
(X,d_{X})
and
(Y,d_{Y})
are metric spaces and that one of
X
or
Y
is a normed space whose metric is induced by the norm. Let
B\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
be balls of common radius
r>0
and suppose
\phi\colon B\to B^{\prime}
is a
\delta r
-GHA. First, we have

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle\phi(x_{B})\in 10\delta B^{\prime}

and

(2.3.12)

\displaystyle d_{Y}(\phi(x),x_{B^{\prime}})\leq d_{X}(x,x_{B})+11\delta r

for all
x\in B.
Additionally, if
z\in B
is such that
B_{X}(z,\alpha r)\subseteq B,
there exists a map
\varphi\colon B_{X}(z,\alpha r)\to B_{Y}(\phi(z),\alpha r)
which is a
50\delta r
-GHA such that

(2.3.13)

\displaystyle d_{Y}(\phi(x),\varphi(x))\leq\delta r

for all
x\in B_{X}(z,\alpha r).

Proof.

We will prove the case when
Y
is a normed space with norm
\|\cdot\|.
The proof in the other case is very similar and we omit the details. By translating and scaling we may assume
x_{B^{\prime}}=0
and
r=1.
We start with (
2.3.11
). Suppose towards a contradiction that
\|\phi(x_{B})\|>10\delta
. Let
z=-\phi(x_{B})/\|\phi(x_{B})\|
so that
\|z-\phi(x_{B})\|\geq 1+10\delta
and let
x\in B
such that
\|\phi(x)-z\|\leq\delta.
Then,

(2.3.14)

\displaystyle d(x,x_{B})\geq\|\phi(x)-\phi(x_{B})\|-\delta\geq\|\phi(x)-z\|+\|z-\phi(x_{B})\|-\delta\geq 1+9\delta.

However, since
x\in B
we have
d(x,x_{B})\leq 1,
which is a contradiction. Equation (
2.3.12
) follows immediately from (
2.3.11
) and the fact that
\phi
is a
\delta
-GHA since for any
x\in B
we have

(2.3.15)

\displaystyle\|\phi(x)\|\leq\|\phi(x)-\phi(x_{B})\|+10\delta\leq d(x,x_{B})+11\delta.

Fix now some
z\in B
such that
B(z,\alpha)\subseteq B
and let us define
\varphi\colon B_{X}(z,\alpha)\to B_{Y}(\phi(z),\alpha)
. The map
\phi|_{B_{X}(z,\alpha)}
is almost what we need, however it may take values outside
B_{Y}(\phi(z),\alpha)
. To remedy this we instead define

(2.3.16)

\displaystyle\varphi=p_{\phi(z),\alpha}\circ\phi|_{B_{X}(z,\alpha)}\to B_{Y}(\phi(z),\alpha),

where
p_{\phi(z),\alpha}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to\alpha B_{Y}(\phi(z),\alpha)
is the map from Lemma
2.3.3
. Since
\phi
is a
\delta
-GHA, if
x\in B_{X}(z,\alpha)
then
\|\phi(x)-\phi(z)\|\leq\alpha+\delta
and (
2.3.13
) follows from this, (
2.3.3
) and the definition of
\varphi
. It remains to check that
\varphi
is a
50\delta
-GHA. By (
2.3.13
), if
x,y\in B_{X}(z,\alpha)
then

(2.3.17)

\displaystyle\left|\|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\|-d(x,y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\|\phi(x)-\phi(y)\|-d(x,y)\right|+\|\varphi(x)-\phi(x)\|

(2.3.18)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|\varphi(y)-\phi(y)\|\leq 3\delta

and so
\varphi
defines a
3\delta
-isometry. Suppose now that
u\in B_{Y}(\phi(z),\alpha)
. Let
\tilde{u}\in B_{Y}(\phi(z),\alpha-2\delta)
such that
\|u-\tilde{u}\|\leq 2\delta
(such a point exists for example by Lemma
2.3.3
) and choose a point
x\in B
such that
\|\phi(x)-\tilde{u}\|\leq\delta
. By the triangle inequality and using that
\phi
is a
\delta
-GHA we have

(2.3.19)

\displaystyle d(x,z)\leq\|\phi(x)-\phi(z)\|+\delta\leq\|\phi(x)-\tilde{u}\|+\|\tilde{u}-\phi(z)\|+\delta\leq\delta+\alpha-2\delta+\delta=\alpha.

This gives
x\in B_{X}(z,\alpha).
Now, after combining the above observations with (
2.3.13
) we get

(2.3.20)

\displaystyle\|\varphi(x)-u\|\leq\|\varphi(x)-\phi(x)\|+\|\phi(x)-\tilde{u}\|+\|\tilde{u}-u\|\leq 3\delta.

and conclude that
\varphi(B_{X}(z,\alpha))
is
3\delta
-dense.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.3.7
.

While this result holds for all choices of
0<2\delta<\alpha<1
it is only really interesting for
\delta
much smaller than
\alpha.

Lemma 2.3.8
.

Let
X,Y
and
Z
be compact metric spaces and
0<\delta,\varepsilon<1.
Suppose
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
is a
\delta
-GHA and
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Z
is an
\varepsilon
-GHA. Then,
g\circ f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Z
is an
(\delta+2\varepsilon)
-GHA.

Proof.

Set
h=g\circ f
. Since
f
is a
\delta
-isometry and
g
is an
\varepsilon
-isometry, if
x,y\in X
then

(2.3.21)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d_{X}(x,y)-d_{Z}(h(x),h(y)\right\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d_{X}(x,y)-d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))\right\rvert}

(2.3.22)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))-d_{Z}(h(x),h(y))\right\rvert}\leq\delta+\varepsilon

and so
h
define a
(\delta+\varepsilon)
-isometry. Now suppose
z\in Z.
Since
g(Y)
is
\varepsilon
-dense in
Z
, there exists
y\in Y
such that
d_{Z}(g(y),z)<\varepsilon.
Since
f(X)
is
\delta
-dense in
X,
there exists
x\in X
such that
d_{Y}(f(x),y)\leq\delta.
Using this with the fact that
g
is an
\varepsilon
-isometry, we have

(2.3.23)

\displaystyle d_{Z}(h(x),z)

\displaystyle\leq d_{Z}(h(x),g(y))+d_{Z}(g(y),z)\leq d_{Z}(f(x),y)+\varepsilon+d_{Z}(g(y),z)

(2.3.24)

\displaystyle\leq\delta+2\varepsilon.

We conclude that
h(X)
is
(\alpha+2\varepsilon)
-dense in
Z,
and this finishes the proof.
∎

In the final part of this section we show that, for
\delta
small enough, if there exists a
\delta r
-GHA between balls of radius
r
in two norms spaces then actually there exists a global affine map between the spaces which is bi-Lipschitz with small constant i.e. the balls in two normed spaces are close then the spaces are small perturbations of each other.

We need several preliminary lemmas. The first of these is a useful topological observation. A proof in the case
r=1
and
\|\cdot\|=|\cdot|
(the Euclidean norm) can be found in
[
Bat20
, Lemma 7.3]
. The proof is easily modified to give the more general statement.

Lemma 2.3.9
.

Let
0<\delta<\tfrac{1}{2}
. Suppose
\|\cdot\|
is a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
B=B_{\|\cdot\|}(x,r)
is a ball and
f\colon B\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a continuous function such that
\|f(x)-x\|\leq\delta r
for all
x\in\partial B
then
f(B)\supseteq B(x,(1-\delta)r).

Corollary 2.3.10
.

Let
\|\cdot\|
be a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
H\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
a continuous function. Suppose there exists
R>0
such that
H(x)=x
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying
\|x\|>R.
Then
H
is surjective.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and fix
r>2\max\{\|x\|,R\}.
Our hypotheses on
H
gives
H(y)=y
for all
y\in\partial B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)
. Apply Lemma
2.3.9
with
\delta=\tfrac{1}{4}
implies
B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,\|x\|)\subseteq B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,\tfrac{3r}{4})\subseteq H(B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r))
which proves the result.
∎

Corollary 2.3.11
.

Let
0<\delta<\tfrac{1}{10}.
Suppose
\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}
are two norms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
T\colon(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1})\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{2})
is affine and
(1+\delta)
-bi-Lipschitz. Then,

T(B_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(x,r))\supseteq B_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}(T(x),(1-C\delta)r)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r>0.

Proof.

By scale invariance we may assume
r=1.
Let
p=p_{x,1-\delta}\colon(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1})\to B_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(x,1)
be the map from Lemma
2.3.3
and define
f\coloneqq T\circ p\circ T^{-1}.
Let
y=T(x)
. Then,
f
maps
B_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(y,1)
to itself and satisfies
\|f(z)-z\|_{2}\leq 4\delta
for all
z\in B_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}(y,1)
. Lemma
2.3.9
now gives
f(B_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}(y,r))\supseteq B_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}(y,1-4\delta).
Since
p\circ T^{-1}(B_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}(y,1))\subseteq B_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(x,1),
this proves the result.
∎

The following is due to Mankiewicz
[
Man72
]
.

Lemma 2.3.12
.

Suppose
X
and
Y
are normed spaces and
E\subseteq X
and
F\subseteq Y
are closed convex sets with non-empty interior. If
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}E\to F
is a surjective isometry then there exists an affine isometry
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y
such that
f|_{E}=T|_{E}.

We find the affine map discussed above by a compactness argument, thus, it is useful to know what happens if there exist
\delta
-GHA with arbitrary small
\delta
. The following is sufficient for our needs.

Lemma 2.3.13
.

Let
\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}
be norms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and suppose
\phi_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(0,1)\rightarrow B_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}(0,1)
is a sequence of
\delta_{i}
-
GHA
with
\delta_{i}\to 0.
There exists a subsequence
\phi_{i_{k}}
such that the following holds. For all
\varepsilon>0
there is
K\in\mathbb{N}
, depending only on
\varepsilon
, and a linear map
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

(2.3.25)

\displaystyle\|T(x)-T(y)\|_{2}=\|x-y\|_{1}\mbox{ for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and

\sup_{x\in B_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(0,1)}\|\phi_{i_{k}}(x)-T(x)\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon\mbox{ for all }k\geq K.

Proof.

Denote
B_{1}=B_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(0,1)
and
B_{2}=B_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}(0,1)
. By Lemma
2.3.5
, we may as well assume that each
\phi_{i}
is
20n
-Lipschitz. Then, by Arzelà-Ascoli, we can find a subsequence
\{\phi_{i_{k}}\}
, which converge uniformly to a function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{1}\to B_{2}.
If we can show that
f
is the restriction of a linear isometry
T\colon(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1})\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{2})
then we are done. To see that this holds, first notice that

(2.3.26)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\|f(x)-f(y)\|_{2}-\|x-y\|_{1}\right\rvert}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\|\phi_{i_{k}}(x)-\phi_{i_{k}}(y)\|_{2}-\|x-y\|_{1}\right\rvert}

(2.3.27)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\delta_{i_{k}}=0

for all
x,y\in B_{1}.
Hence,
f
is an isometry. Additionally, if
u\in B_{2}
then there exist points
x_{i_{k}}\in B_{1}
such that
\|\phi_{i_{k}}(x_{i_{k}})-u\|\leq\delta_{i_{k}}.
Since
B_{1}
is compact we find a further subsequence
x_{i_{k_{j}}}
which converges to a point
x\in B_{1}.
Since the sequence
\phi_{i_{k_{j}}}
converges uniformly to
f
on
B_{1},
we have

(2.3.28)

\displaystyle\|f(x)-u\|=\lim_{j\to\infty}\|\phi_{i_{k_{j}}}(x_{i_{k_{j}}})-u\|=0,

so
f
is a surjective isometry. Now, by Lemma
2.3.12
there exists an affine map
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying (
2.3.25
) and such that
T|_{B_{1}}=f|_{B_{1}}
. By (
2.3.11
) we know
\|\phi_{i_{k}}(0)\|\leq 10\delta_{i_{k}}
which implies
T(0)=f(0)=0,
so
T
is in fact linear.
∎

Lemma 2.3.14
.

For all
0<\varepsilon<1
there exists
0<\delta<1
such that the following holds. Let
\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}
be two norms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and suppose
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(x_{1},r)\rightarrow B_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}(x_{2},r)
a
\delta r
-
GHA
. Then, there exists an affine map
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

(2.3.29)

\displaystyle(1-C\varepsilon)\|x-y\|_{1}\leq\|T(x)-T(y)\|_{2}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|x-y\|_{1}\mbox{ for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and

(2.3.30)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(x_{1},r)}\|\phi(x)-T(x)\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon r.

Proof.

By translating, scaling and composing
\phi
with an affine transformation, it is enough find a linear map in the case where
r=1
and both balls are centred at the origin. Suppose the lemma is false (with these extra assumption), then there exists
0<\varepsilon<1
such that the following holds. For each
i\in\mathbb{N},
there are norms
\|\cdot\|_{1,i},\|\cdot\|_{2,i}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a
\tfrac{1}{i}
-GHA
\phi_{i}\colon B_{1}^{i}\to B_{2}^{i}
, where
B_{1}^{i}=B_{\|\cdot\|_{1,i}}(0,1)
and
B_{2}^{i}=B_{\|\cdot\|_{2,i}}(0,1)
, such that

(2.3.31)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B_{1}^{i}}\|\phi_{i}(x)-T(x)\|_{2,i}>\varepsilon

for all linear maps
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying

(2.3.32)

\displaystyle(1-C\varepsilon)\|x-y\|_{1,i}\leq\|T(x)-T(y)\|_{2,i}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|x-y\|_{1,i}\mbox{ for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Recalling that
(I(n),\log d_{\text{BM}})
is compact (see Remark
2.2.1
), we may suppose there are norms
\|\cdot\|_{1}
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
such that
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1,i})\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1})
and
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{2,i})\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{2})
in
I(n).
Let
B_{1}=B_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(0,1)
and
B_{2}=B_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}(0,1).

We define a sequence of maps
\varphi_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{1}\to B_{2}
as follows. For
i\in\mathbb{N}
let

\delta_{1}^{i}=2\log d_{\mathrm{BM}}((\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1}),(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1,i}))\mbox{ and }\delta_{2}^{i}=2\log((\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{2}),(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{2,i})).

By definition, there exist linear isomorphisms
T_{1,i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1})\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1,i})
and
T_{2,i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{2,i})\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{2})
such that

(2.3.33)

\displaystyle\|T_{j,i}\|\leq 1\mbox{ and }\|T_{j,i}^{-1}\|\leq e^{\delta_{j}^{i}/2}\leq 1+\delta_{j}^{i},\quad j=1,2.

Then, let

\varphi_{i}\coloneqq T_{2,i}\circ\phi_{i}\circ T_{1,i}\colon B_{1}\to B_{2}.

We claim

(2.3.34)

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}\mbox{ is a }C(\delta_{i}^{1}+\delta_{i}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{i})\mbox{-GHA}.

Indeed, suppose first that
x,y\in B_{1}.
By (
2.3.33
) and since
\phi_{i}
is a
\tfrac{1}{i}
-GHA, we have

(2.3.35)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{i}(x)-\varphi_{i}(y)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi_{i}\circ T_{1,i}(x)-\phi_{i}\circ T_{1,i}(y)\|_{2,i}\leq\|T_{1,i}(x)-T_{1,i}(y)\|_{1,i}+\tfrac{1}{i}

(2.3.36)

\displaystyle\leq\|x-y\|_{1}+\tfrac{1}{i}

and

(2.3.37)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{i}(x)-\varphi_{i}(y)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq(1-C\delta_{2}^{i})\|\phi_{i}\circ T_{1,i}(x)-\phi_{i}\circ T_{1,i}(y)\|_{2,i}

(2.3.38)

\displaystyle\geq(1-C\delta_{2}^{i})\left(\|T_{1,i}(x)-T_{1,i}(y)\|_{1,i}-\tfrac{1}{i}\right)

(2.3.39)

\displaystyle\geq(1-C\delta_{1}^{i})(1-C\delta_{2}^{i})\left(\|x-y\|_{1}-\tfrac{1}{i}\right)

(2.3.40)

\displaystyle\geq\|x-y\|_{1}-C\delta_{1}^{i}-C\delta_{2}^{i}-\tfrac{1}{i},

where we used the fact that
\|x-y\|_{1}\leq 2
in the final inequality. Since
x,y\in B_{1}
were arbitrary, combining the above two estimates we have that
\varphi_{i}
is a
C(\delta_{1}^{i}+\delta_{2}^{i}+\tfrac{1}{i})
-isometry.

Now, let
u\in B_{2}.
By (
2.3.33
) and Corollary
2.3.11
we have
T_{2,i}(B_{2}^{i})\supseteq B_{\|\cdot\|_{2}}(0,1-C\delta_{2}^{i}).
Hence, there exists
z\in B_{2}^{i}
such that
\|T_{2,i}(z)-u\|_{2}\leq C\delta_{2}^{i}.
Since
\phi_{i}
is a
\tfrac{1}{i}
-GHA, there exists
y\in B_{1}^{i}
such that
\|\phi_{i}(y)-z\|_{2,i}\leq\tfrac{1}{i}.
Again, by (
2.3.33
) and Corollary
2.3.11
we have
T_{1,i}(B_{1})\supseteq B_{\|\cdot\|_{1,i}}(0,1-C\delta_{1}^{i})
, so there exists
x\in B_{1}
such that
\|T_{1,i}(x)-y\|_{1,i}\leq C\delta_{1}^{i}.
Combining the above, with more applications of (
2.3.33
), gives

(2.3.41)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{i}(x)-u\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|T_{2,i}\circ\phi_{i}\circ T_{1,i}(x)-T_{2,i}\circ\phi_{i}(y)\|_{2}+\|T_{2,i}\circ\phi_{i}(y)-T_{2,i}(z)\|_{2}

(2.3.42)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|T_{2,i}(z)-u\|_{2}

(2.3.43)

\displaystyle\leq\|T_{1,i}(x)-y\|_{1,i}+\tfrac{1}{i}+\|\phi_{i}(y)-z\|_{2,i}+\|T_{2,i}(z)-u\|_{2}

(2.3.44)

\displaystyle\leq C(\delta_{1}^{i}+\delta_{2}^{i}+\tfrac{1}{i}).

Since
u\in B_{2}
was arbitrary, it follows that
\varphi_{i}(B_{1})
is
C(\delta_{1}^{i}+\delta_{2}^{i}+\tfrac{1}{i})
-dense in
B_{2}.
This now finishes the proof of (
2.3.34
).

Since each
\varphi_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}
is a
C(\delta_{1}^{i}+\delta_{2}^{i}+\tfrac{1}{i})
-GHA and
\delta_{1}^{i}+\delta_{2}^{i}+\tfrac{1}{i}\to 0
, we can apply Lemma
2.3.13
to find a subsequence of the
\varphi_{i}
(which we do not relabel), an integer
K\geq 0
depending on
\varepsilon,
and a linear map
\tilde{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

(2.3.45)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{T}(x)-\tilde{T}(y)\|_{2}=\|x-y\|_{1}\mbox{ for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and

(2.3.46)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B_{1}}\|\varphi_{i}(x)-\tilde{T}(x)\|_{2}<\tfrac{\varepsilon}{4}\mbox{ for all }i\geq K.

Suppose
i\geq K
is such that

(2.3.47)

\displaystyle C(\delta_{1}^{i}+\delta_{2}^{i}+\tfrac{1}{i})\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}

and set
T\coloneqq T_{2,i}^{-1}\circ\tilde{T}\circ T_{1,i}^{-1}.
Then,
T
is a linear map from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to itself and satisfies (
2.3.32
) by (
2.3.33
), (
2.3.45
) and (
2.3.47
). Now, let
x\in B_{1}^{i}
and choose
x^{\prime}\in B_{\|\cdot\|_{1,i}}(0,1-C\delta_{1}^{i})
such that
\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{1,i}\leq C\delta_{1}^{i}.
It follows that
T^{-1}(x^{\prime})\in B_{1}
by (
2.3.33
). By (
2.3.32
), (
2.3.47
) and since
\phi_{i}
is a
\tfrac{1}{i}
-GHA, we have

(2.3.48)

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}(x)-T(x)\|_{2,i}

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi_{i}(x^{\prime})-T(x^{\prime})\|_{2,i}+\|\phi_{i}(x^{\prime})-\phi_{i}(x)\|_{2,i}+\|T(x^{\prime})-T(x)\|_{2,i}

(2.3.49)

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi_{i}(x^{\prime})-T(x^{\prime})\|_{2,i}+C(\delta_{1}^{i}+\delta_{2}^{i}+\tfrac{1}{i})

(2.3.50)

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi_{i}(x^{\prime})-T(x^{\prime})\|_{2,i}+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Since
T^{-1}(x^{\prime})\in B_{1},
(
2.3.33
) and (
2.3.46
) give

(2.3.51)

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}(x^{\prime})-T(x^{\prime})\|_{2,i}

\displaystyle=\|T_{2}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{i}\circ T_{1}^{-1}(x^{\prime})-T_{2}^{-1}\circ\tilde{T}\circ T_{1}^{-1}(x^{\prime})\|_{2,i}

(2.3.52)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta_{2}^{i})\|\varphi_{i}\circ T_{1}^{-1}(x^{\prime})-\tilde{T}\circ T_{1}^{-1}(x^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Combining the above two estimates we get

\|\phi_{i}(x)-T(x)\|_{2,i}\leq\varepsilon.

Since
x\in B_{1,i}
was arbitrary this contradict (
2.3.31
) and we finish the proof of the lemma. ∎

2.4.
Maps between normed spaces

In this section we include various result concerning mappings between normed spaces which will be important in the next section.

Lemma 2.4.1
.

Let
0<\varepsilon,r<1/2
,
m\in\{1,2\}
and
(Y,\|\cdot\|)
be an
n
-dimensional normed space. Suppose
H\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Y
is in
C^{m}(Y)
, surjective and satisfies
\|H-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{m}(Y),r}\leq\varepsilon.
Then
H
is invertible with
H^{-1}\colon Y\to Y
in
C^{m}(Y)
. Moreover,

(2.4.1)

\displaystyle\|H^{-1}-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{m}(Y),r}\lesssim\varepsilon.

Proof.

By surjectivity and the Inverse function Theorem, to verify that
H
is invertible with inverse
H^{-1}\in C^{m}(Y),
it suffices to check that
DH(x)
is injective (hence invertible) for each
x\in Y.
To see this, suppose
v,w\in Y
. Then,

(2.4.2)

\displaystyle\|DH(x)(v)-DH(x)(w)\|

\displaystyle=\|DH(x)(v-w)\|

(2.4.3)

\displaystyle\geq\|v-w\|-\|DH(x)(v-w)-(v-w)\|

(2.4.4)

\displaystyle\geq\|v-w\|/2.

It now only remains to prove (
2.4.1
). The above inequality implies

\|DH(x)^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}\leq 2

for all
x\in Y.
Thus,
\|D[H^{-1}](z)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}\leq 2
for all
z\in Y
by the Inverse Function Theorem. So, for any
z\in Y,
the estimate
\|H-\text{Id}\|_{C^{m}(Y),r}\leq\varepsilon
then implies

(2.4.5)

\displaystyle\|H^{-1}(z)-z\|=\|H^{-1}(z)-H(H^{-1}(z))\|\leq\varepsilon r

and

(2.4.6)

\displaystyle\|D[H^{-1}](z)-\text{Id}\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

\displaystyle=\|D[H^{-1}](z)-D[H\circ H^{-1}](z)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

(2.4.7)

\displaystyle=\|D[H^{-1}](z)-DH(H^{-1}(z))D[H^{-1}](z)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

(2.4.8)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\|D[H^{-1}](z)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}\leq 2\varepsilon.

Since
z
was arbitrary, this completes the proof in the case
m=1.
If
m=2
we proceed as follows. Define a map
G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to B(Y)
by setting
G(x)=DH(H^{-1}(x))
. In this way
D[H^{-1}](x)=G(x)^{-1}
(here we identify
\mathcal{B}(Y)
with the set of all
n\times n
matrices and
G(x)^{-1}
denotes the inverse matrix of
G(x)
). By the derivative formula for inverse matrices, the chain rule and our estimates for
H
and
H^{-1}
, we have

(2.4.9)

\displaystyle\|D^{2}[H^{-1}](z)(y)\|_{\mathcal{B}(E)}

\displaystyle=\|D[H^{-1}](x)\circ DG(z)(y)\circ D[H^{-1}](x)\|_{\mathcal{B}(E)}

(2.4.10)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|[D^{2}H(H^{-1}(x))\left(D[H^{-1}](x)(y)\right)\|_{\mathcal{B}(E)}

(2.4.11)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\|D[H^{-1}](x)(y)\|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\|y\|

for all
y\in Y.
It now follows that
\|D^{2}[H^{-1}](z)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y,\mathcal{B}(Y))}\lesssim\varepsilon/r
and this finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.4.2
.

For each
\eta>0
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that the following holds. Let
m\in\{1,2\},

r>0,

(Y,\|\cdot\|)
be an
n
-dimensional normed space and
U_{1}\subseteq U_{2}\subseteq Y
be open bounded sets. Suppose that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(U_{1},U_{2}^{c})\geq\eta r
and
H\colon U_{2}\to Y
is in
C^{m}(U_{2})
and satisfies

(2.4.12)

\displaystyle\|H-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{m}(U_{2}),r}\leq\varepsilon.

Then, there exists a map surjective map
\hat{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Y
in
C^{m}(Y)
such that

(2.4.13)

\displaystyle\hat{H}|_{U_{1}}

\displaystyle=H|_{U_{1}},

(2.4.14)

\displaystyle\hat{H}|_{U_{2}^{c}}

\displaystyle=\emph{Id},

(2.4.15)

\displaystyle\hat{H}(x)

\displaystyle=H(x)\mbox{ whenever }H(x)=x.

Furthermore,
\hat{H}
is invertible with inverse
\hat{H}^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Y
in
C^{m}(Y)
and

(2.4.16)

\displaystyle\|\hat{H}-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{m}(Y),r}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\eta}\varepsilon\mbox{ and }\|\hat{H}^{-1}-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{m}(Y),r}\lesssim_{\eta}\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
\eta>0
be fixed. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(U_{1},U_{2}^{c})\geq\eta r,
there exists a smooth bump function
\varphi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\varphi\equiv 1
on
U_{1},

\varphi\equiv 0
on
U_{2}^{c}
and
\|D^{k}\varphi\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim_{\eta}r^{-k}
for each
k\in\{1,2\}.
Let
\hat{H}=(H-\mbox{Id})\varphi+\mbox{Id}.
Then,
\hat{H}\in C^{m}(Y)
and it is simple to check that (
2.4.13
), (
2.4.14
) and (
2.4.15
) hold with this definition. If
x\in Y
then by (
2.4.12
), we have

(2.4.17)

\displaystyle\|\hat{H}(x)-x\|=\|(H(x)-x)\varphi(x)\|\leq\|H(x)-x\|\,|\varphi(x)|\lesssim\varepsilon r

and

(2.4.18)

\displaystyle\|D\hat{H}(x)-I_{n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\|D[\hat{H}-\mbox{Id}](x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}

(2.4.19)

\displaystyle\leq\|D[H-\mbox{Id}](x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}|\varphi(x)|+\|H(x)-x\|\,\|D\varphi(x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim_{\eta}\varepsilon,

where
I_{n}
is the identity matrix on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
m=1
this proves the first estimate in (
2.4.16
). If
m=2
and
y\in Y
is such that
\|y\|=1,
then we have the further estimate

(2.4.20)

\displaystyle\|D^{2}[\hat{H}-\text{Id}](x)(y)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

\displaystyle\leq\|D^{2}[H-\text{Id}](x)(y)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}|\varphi(x)|

(2.4.21)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+2\|D[H-\text{Id}](x)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}|D\varphi(x)(y)|

(2.4.22)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\|H(x)-x\|\|D^{2}\varphi(x)(y)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}\lesssim_{\eta}\varepsilon r^{-1}

so that
\|D^{2}[\hat{H}-\text{Id}](x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim_{\eta}\varepsilon r^{-1}.
This now proves the first half (
2.4.16
) in this case. Since
\hat{H}
is continuous and equal to the identity outside a large ball (by (
2.4.14
)), it is surjective by Corollary
2.3.10
. Then, supposing
\varepsilon
is small enough with respect to
\eta,
the first part of (
2.4.16
) and Lemma
2.4.1
imply that
\hat{H}
is invertible with inverse
\hat{H}^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Y
in
C^{m}(Y)
satisfying the second half of (
2.4.16
).
∎

Lemma 2.4.3
.

Let
(Y,\|\cdot\|)
be a normed space and fix parameters
0<\varepsilon<1
,
r>0
,
k\geq 1
and
m\in\{1,2\}.
For each
1\leq i\leq k
let
U_{i}\subseteq Y
be an open set,
f_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}U_{i}\to Y
a map in
C^{m}(U_{i})
,
g_{i}\colon Y\to Y
a
(1+\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz affine map, and suppose that
\|f_{i}-g_{i}\|_{C^{m}(U_{i}),r}\leq\varepsilon
. If
U\subseteq Y
is open and
f_{k}\circ\dots\circ f_{1}
is defined on
U
then

(2.4.23)

\displaystyle\|f_{k}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}-g_{k}\circ\cdots g_{1}\|_{C^{m}(U),r}\lesssim_{k}\varepsilon.

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on
k
. The base case
k=1
holds by assumption. Now, fix some
k\geq 1
for which the result holds and consider the case
k+1.
Let
F_{k}=f_{k}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}
and
G_{k}=g_{j}\circ\dots\circ g_{1}.
We proceed with (
2.4.23
). Fix
U\subseteq Y
on which
f_{k+1}\circ F_{k}
is well-defined and let
x\in U.
Using the estimate

(2.4.24)

\displaystyle\|f_{k+1}-g_{k+1}\|_{C^{m}(U_{i}),r}\leq\varepsilon

with (
2.4.23
) for
k
and the fact that
g_{k+1}
is
(1+\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, we get

(2.4.25)

\displaystyle\|f_{k+1}(F_{k}(x))-g_{k+1}(G_{k}(x))\|

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{k+1}(F_{k}(x))-g_{k+1}(F_{k}(x))\|

(2.4.26)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|g_{k+1}(F_{k}(x))-g_{k+1}(G_{k}(x))\|\lesssim\varepsilon r.

Since
g_{k+1}
and
G_{k}
are affine, there exists linear maps
L_{1},L_{2}\colon Y\to Y
such that
Dg_{k+1}(y)=L_{1}
and
DG_{k}(y)=L_{1}
for all
y\in Y.
Since each
g_{i}
is
(1+\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, we have that
L_{1}
is
(1+\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz and both
G_{k}
and
L_{2}
are
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz for some
C
depending on
k
. Since
G_{k}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz and
\|F_{k}-G_{k}\|_{C^{m}(U),r}\lesssim_{k}\varepsilon
by induction, it follows that
\|DF_{k}(x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim_{k}\varepsilon
. Using the above with (
2.4.23
) for
k
and (
2.4.24
), we have

(2.4.27)

\displaystyle\|D[f_{k+1}\circ F_{k}](x)-L_{1}L_{2}\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

\displaystyle\leq\|Df_{k+1}(F_{k}(x))DF_{k}(x)-L_{1}DF_{k}(x)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

(2.4.28)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|L_{1}DF_{k}(x)-L_{1}L_{2}\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

(2.4.29)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\|DF_{k}(x)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}+(1+\varepsilon)\|DF_{k}(x)-L_{2}\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

(2.4.30)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k}\varepsilon.

If
m=1
this completes the proof. Suppose then that
m=2.
Since
DG_{k}(y)=L_{1}
and
Dg_{k+1}(y)=L_{2}
for all
y\in Y
, we have
D^{2}G_{k}(y)=D^{2}g_{k+1}(y)=0
for all
y\in Y.
Using (
2.4.23
) for
k
with (
2.4.24
) now implies
\|D^{2}f_{k+1}(y)(z)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{r}\|z\|
and
\|D^{2}F_{k}(y)(z)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}\lesssim_{k}\tfrac{\varepsilon}{r}\|z\|
for all
y,z\in Y.
Hence,

(2.4.31)

\displaystyle\|D^{2}[f_{k+1}\circ F_{k}](x)(y)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

\displaystyle\leq\|D^{2}f_{k+1}(F_{k}(x))(DF_{k}(x)(y))DF_{k}(x)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

(2.4.32)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\|Df_{k+1}(F_{k}(x))D^{2}F_{k}(x)(y)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

(2.4.33)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k}\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\|DF_{k}(x)(y)\|\,\|DF_{k}(x)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}

(2.4.34)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\|Df_{k+1}(F_{k}(x))\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y)}\|y\|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{r}\|y\|.

This implies
\|D^{2}[f_{k+1}\circ F_{k}](x)\|_{\mathcal{B}(Y,\mathcal{B}(Y))}\lesssim_{k}\tfrac{\varepsilon}{r}
and completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.4.4
.

Let
\varepsilon,r>0
and
\Lambda\geq 1
. Suppose
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is in
C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\|f-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),r}\leq\varepsilon
and
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is invertible such that
g,g^{-1}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

(2.4.35)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\|Dg(x)\|+\|D[g^{-1}](x)\|+r\|D^{2}g(x)\|+r\|D^{2}[g^{-1}](x)\|\right)\leq\Lambda.

Then,

(2.4.36)

\displaystyle\|g\circ f\circ g^{-1}-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),r}\lesssim_{\Lambda}\varepsilon

Proof.

Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Applying the Mean Value Theorem with (
2.4.35
) and the estimate on
\|f-\text{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),r}
, we first have

(2.4.37)

\displaystyle\|g\circ f\circ g^{-1}(x)-x\|

\displaystyle=\|g\circ f\circ g^{-1}(x)-g\circ g^{-1}(x)\|

(2.4.38)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\|f\circ g^{-1}(x)-g^{-1}(x)\|\leq L\varepsilon r.

To estimate the derivatives, we first note that
\|D[g\circ f\circ g^{-1}](x)-I_{n}\|
is at most

(2.4.39)

\displaystyle\|D[g](f(g^{-1}(x)))-D[g](g^{-1}(x)\|\,\|D[f](g^{-1}(x))\|\,\|D[g^{-1}](x)\|

(2.4.40)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|D[g](g^{-1}(x))D[f](g^{-1}(x))D[g^{-1}](x)-I_{n}\|

By the Mean Value Theorem, (
2.4.35
) and the estimate on
\|f-\text{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),r}
, the first term is at most

(2.4.41)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{3}r^{-1}\|f(g^{-1}(x))-g^{-1}(x)\|\leq\varepsilon\Lambda^{3}.

Since
D[g](g^{-1}(x))D[g^{-1}](x)=I_{n},
the second term is as most

(2.4.42)

\displaystyle\|D[g](g^{-1}(x))(D[f](g^{-1}(x))-\text{Id})D[g^{-1}](x)\|\leq\varepsilon\Lambda^{2},

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.4.5
.

Let
\|\cdot\|
be a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\varepsilon,r>0.
Let
H\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an affine function such that
\|H(x)-x\|\leq\varepsilon r
for all
x\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(z,r)
. Then,

(2.4.43)

\displaystyle\|DH\cdot v-v\|\lesssim\varepsilon\|v\|

for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we shall assume
z=0
and
r=1.
Since
H
is affine it is of the form
H=y+L
for some linear map
L\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and some point
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Evaluating
H
at the origin and using (
2.4.43
) gives
\|y\|_{k}\leq\varepsilon.
Then, for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
we have

(2.4.44)

\displaystyle\|DH\cdot v-v\|_{k}

\displaystyle=\|v\|\bigg\|L\left(\frac{v}{\|v\|}\right)-\frac{v}{\|v\|}\bigg\|_{k}\leq\|v\|\left(\bigg\|H\left(\frac{v}{\|v\|}\right)-\frac{v}{\|v\|}\bigg\|_{k}+\|y\|_{k}\right)

(2.4.45)

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon\|v\|

∎

2.5.
Cyclic maps

As in
[
GXS12
]
and
[
Vio21
]
, it will be convenient for us to introduce the following compatibility condition for functions defined on normed spaces.

Definition 2.5.1
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be normed space and
r>0.
Suppose
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y,g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Z
and
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Z
are bijective. We say the maps
f,g,h
are
r
-
cyclic
if the following holds. Set

(2.5.1)

\displaystyle I_{2,1}=f,\ I_{1,2}=f^{-1},\ I_{3,2}=g,\ I_{2,3}=g^{-1},\ I_{3,1}=h,\ I_{1,3}=h^{-1}

and denote
B_{1}=B_{X}(0,r),\ B_{2}=B_{Y}(0,r)
and
B_{3}=B_{Z}(0,r).
For each
\sigma\in S_{3}
(the symmetric group) we have that

(2.5.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mbox{ for all }x\in B_{\sigma(1)}\mbox{ such that }I_{\sigma(2),\sigma(1)}(x)\in B_{\sigma(2)}\mbox{ and }I_{\sigma(3),\sigma(2)}\circ I_{\sigma(2),\sigma(1)}(x)\in B_{\sigma(3)}\\
&\mbox{ we have }I_{\sigma(3),\sigma(1)}(x)=I_{\sigma(3),\sigma(2)}\circ I_{\sigma(2),\sigma(1)}(x).\end{split}

Remark 2.5.2
.

The reason for introducing such a notion will become more clear in later sections. For now, observe the following. Suppose
(U_{i},\varphi_{i}),

i=1,2,3
, are coordinate patches in a manifold
M
satisfying
\varphi_{i}(U_{i})=B_{i}
. If
I_{i,j}=\varphi_{i}\circ\varphi_{j}^{-1}
are their transition maps, then (
2.5.2
) must hold. In Section
5.3
we will construct a manifold from transition maps satisfy the condition in (
2.5.2
).

Remark 2.5.3
.

It turns out we do not need to check (
2.5.2
) for every
\sigma\in S_{3}.
Indeed, suppose
\sigma\in S_{3}
satisfies (
2.5.2
). If
\sigma^{\prime}\in S_{3}
is such that
\sigma^{\prime}(1)=\sigma(3),\sigma^{\prime}(2)=\sigma(2)
and
\sigma^{\prime}(3)=\sigma(1)
then (
2.5.2
) (with
\sigma^{\prime}
) also holds. In particular, one only needs to consider the permutations
(1,2,3),(3,1,2)
and
(1,3,2).

The following lemma gives us a way to check the cyclic condition. Roughly speaking it says that if we know (
2.5.2
) is satisfied for at least one permutation and approximately satisfied for all remaining permutation, each with parameter
r
, then in fact (
2.5.2
) is satisfies for all permutation with a slightly smaller parameter.

Lemma 2.5.4
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be normed spaces and
r>0.
Suppose
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Y,g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\to Z
and
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to Z
are bijective. Let
I_{i,j}
and
B_{i}
as in Definition
2.5.1
. Additionally, let
\|\cdot\|_{1},\,\|\cdot\|_{2}
and
\|\cdot\|_{3}
denote the norms on
X,Y
and
Z
respectively. Let
L\geq 1
and
\varepsilon>0
such that
L\varepsilon<1
and assume that each
I_{i,j}
is
L
-bi-Lipschitz and for each
\sigma\in S_{3}
and each
x\in B_{\sigma(1)}
that

(2.5.3)

\displaystyle\|I_{\sigma(3),\sigma(2)}\circ I_{\sigma(2),\sigma(1)}(x)-I_{\sigma(3),\sigma(1)}(x)\|_{\sigma(3)}\leq\varepsilon r.

Moreover, suppose (
2.5.2
) is satisfied for
\sigma=(1,2,3).
Then the maps
I_{1,2},I_{2,3}
and
I_{1,3}
are
(1-L\varepsilon)r
-cyclic.

Proof.

As mentioned in Remark
2.5.3
, we only need to check (
2.5.2
) for the permutations
(1,2,3),(3,1,2)
and
(1,3,2).
The case
(1,2,3)
holds by hypothesis. Consider next
(3,1,2).
Let
\lambda=(1-L\varepsilon)
and let
x\in\lambda B_{3}
such that
y=I_{1,3}(x)\in\lambda B_{1}
and
I_{2,1}(y)=I_{2,1}(I_{1,3}(x))\in\lambda B_{2}.
Using that
I_{3,2}
is
L
-bi-Lipschitz with (
2.5.3
), we have

(2.5.4)

\displaystyle\|I_{3,2}(I_{2,1}(y))-x\|_{3}\leq L\|I_{2,1}(y)-I_{2,3}(x)\|_{2}=L\|I_{2,1}(I_{1,3}(x))-I_{2,3}(x)\|_{3}\leq L\varepsilon r.

By our choice of
\lambda,
this implies
I_{2,3}(I_{2,1}(y))\in B_{3}.
Using (
2.5.2
) in the case of
(1,2,3)
then gives

(2.5.5)

\displaystyle I_{2,3}(x)=I_{2,3}(I_{3,1}(y))=I_{2,3}(I_{3,2}(I_{2,1}(y)))=I_{2,1}(I_{1,3}(x))).

The proof for
(1,3,2)
is almost identical, we omit the details.
∎

2.6.
Christ-David cubes and Carleson conditions

At various points in the paper It will be convenient for us to work with a version of “dyadic cubes” tailored to
X
. These are the so-called Christ-David cubes, which were first introduced by David
[
Dav88
]
and generalized in
[
Chr90
]
and
[
HM12
]
. The following is a combination of the formulation in
[
Chr90
]
and
[
HM12
]
.

Lemma 2.6.1
.

Let
X
be a doubling metric space and
X_{k}
be a sequence of maximal
\varrho^{k}
-separated nets, where
\varrho=1/1000
and let
c_{0}=1/500.
Then, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a collection
\mathcal{D}_{k}
of “cubes” such that the following conditions hold.

(1)

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z},\ X=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}}Q.

(2)

If
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{k}\mathcal{D}_{k}
and
Q_{1}\cap Q_{2}\not=\emptyset,
then
Q_{1}\subseteq Q_{2}
or
Q_{2}\subseteq Q_{1}.

(3)

For
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
set
\ell(Q)=5\varrho^{k}.
Then there is
x_{Q}\in X_{k}
such that

\displaystyle B_{X}(x_{Q},c_{0}\ell(Q))\subseteq Q\subseteq B_{X}(x_{Q},\ell(Q)).

(4)

If
X
is Ahlfors
n
-regular, then there exists
C\geq 1
such that
\mathcal{H}^{n}(\{x\in Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,X\setminus Q)\leq\eta\varrho^{k}\})\lesssim\eta^{\frac{1}{C}}\ell(Q)^{d}
for all
Q\in\mathcal{D}
and
\eta>0.

Given a collection of cubes
\mathcal{D}
and
Q\in\mathcal{D},
define

(2.6.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(Q)=\{R\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\subseteq Q\}.

Let
\text{Parent}(Q)
be the unique cube
R\in\mathcal{D}
such that
R\supseteq Q
and
\ell(Q)=\varrho\ell(Q).
Let
\text{Child}(Q)
denote the collection of cubes
R\subseteq Q
such that
Q=\text{Parent}(R).
Let
\text{Sibling}(Q)
be the collection of cubes
R
such that
\text{Parent}(R)=\text{Parent}(Q).

Remark 2.6.2
.

If
A>2
and
Q,R\in\mathcal{D}
are such that
Q\subseteq R
it is easy to see that
AB_{Q}\subseteq AB_{R}.
For a proof see
[
AS18
, Lemma 2.10]
.

Definition 2.6.3
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space and
\mathcal{D}
a system of Christ-David cubes for
X
. A collection of cubes
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
satisfies a
Carleson packing condition
if there exists
C>0
such that

(2.6.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{B}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{n}\leq C\ell(Q_{0})^{n}

for all
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}.

The Carleson packing condition is a natural measure of smallness. If
\mathcal{B}
satisfies a Carleson packing condition then roughly speaking there are not too many cubes in
\mathcal{B}.
The Carleson packing condition is a discretized version of the usual Carleson type conditions defined below.

Definition 2.6.4
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space. A non-negative Borel measure
\mu
on
X\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X))
is called a
Carleson measure
if there exists
C>0
such that
\mu(B(x,r)\times(0,r))\leq Cr^{n}
for all
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X).
The smallest such
C
is called the
Carleson norm
of
\mu
. A set
\mathscr{A}\subseteq X\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X))
is a
Carleson set
if
\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{A}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)\tfrac{dr}{r}
is a Carleson measure.

It is easy to show the following, which allows us to pass between the conditions and discrete Carleson conditions.

Lemma 2.6.5
.

Let
K>1
and
X
be Ahlfors
(C_{0},n)
-regular. Let
f
a real valued function on
X\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X))
and suppose there exists
C\geq 1
such that
rf(x,r)\leq Csf(y,s)
for all
x,y\in X
and
0\leq r\leq s<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)
which satisfy
B(x,r)\subseteq B(y,s).
Let
\mathcal{D}
be a system of dyadic cubes for
X
. For each
Q\in\mathcal{D},
set
f(Q)\coloneqq f(x_{Q},K\ell(Q)).
Then,
\{(x,r)\in X\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f(x,r)>\varepsilon\}
is a Carleson set for each
\varepsilon>0
if and only if
\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}f(Q)>\varepsilon\}
satisfies a Carleson packing condition of each
\varepsilon>0.
The Carleson norms depending quantitatively on each other and
C_{0}.

Finally, we define what we mean by stopping-time region.

Definition 2.6.6
.

Suppose
X
is a metric space and
\mathcal{D}
is a system of Christ-David cubes on
X
. A collection
