S\subseteq\mathcal{D}
is called a
stopping-time region
if the following conditions hold.

(1)

There is a
top cube

Q(S)\in S
such that
Q(S)
contains all cubes in
S
.

(2)

If
Q\in S
and
Q\subseteq R\subseteq Q(S),
then
R\in S
.

(3)

If
Q\in S
, then all siblings of
Q
are also in
S
.

Let
\min(S)
denote the
minimal cubes
of
S
i.e. those cubes in
S
whose children are not in
S
.

3.
UR and BWGL in metric spaces and the
\gamma
-coefficients

Contents of this section

3.1.
Main definitions

Although we have explained roughly each of the conditions (1), (2) and (3) in the introduction, we shall define them explicitly here. We shall also prove some useful properties concerning them.

Definition 3.1.1
.

A metric space
X
is said to be
Ahlfors
n
-regular
if there exists a constant
C_{0}\geq 1
such that for each
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)
we have

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle C_{0}^{-1}r^{n}\leq\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{X}(x,r))\leq C_{0}r^{n}.

If only the first inequality holds we say
X
is
Ahlfors lower
n
-regular
. If only the second inequality holds we say
X
is
Ahlfors upper
n
-regular
. We call
C_{0}
the
regularity constant
. If the context is clear we may simply write Ahlfors regular, Ahlfors lower regular or Ahlfors upper regular.

Definition 3.1.2
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space and
\mathcal{F}
a family of Ahlfors
n
-regular metric spaces with uniform regularity constant. We say that
X
has
big pieces of

\mathcal{F}
if there exists a constant
\theta>0
such that for each
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)
there exists
F\in\mathcal{F}
which satisfies

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(F\cap B_{X}(x,r))\geq\theta r^{n}.

Both the BWGL and the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients are stated in terms of Carleson sets, see Definition
2.6.4
. The relevant coefficient for defining the BWGL is
\xi_{X}
, which is a Gromov-Hausdorff version of
b\beta
, measuring local approximations by
n
-dimensional Banach spaces.

Definition 3.1.3
.

Let
(X,d)
be a metric space,
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X).
For a norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
\Phi(x,r,\|\cdot\|)
denote the collection of all mappings of the form
\varphi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B(x,r)\rightarrow B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r).
For
\varphi\in\Phi(x,r,\|\cdot\|)
, define

(3.1.3)

\displaystyle\zeta_{X}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sup_{y,z\in B(x,r)}\left|\,d(y,z)-\|\varphi(y)-\varphi(z)\|\,\right|;

(3.1.4)

\displaystyle\eta_{X}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sup_{u\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)}{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\|\cdot\|}(u,\varphi(B(x,r)));

(3.1.5)

\displaystyle\xi_{X}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{X}(x,r,\varphi,\|\cdot\|)+\eta_{X}(x,r,\varphi,\|\cdot\|),

and

\xi_{X}(x,r)=\inf_{\|\cdot\|}\inf_{\varphi\in\Phi(x,r,\|\cdot\|)}\xi_{X}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi).

Remark 3.1.4
.

If
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\|\cdot\|
is the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
then the coefficient
\zeta_{X}
behaves in some sense like
\beta^{2}
, where
\beta
is the Jones
\beta
-number, as defined in
[
Jon90
]
. This behaviour was suggested in
[
DT99
]
and a rigorous statement was proven in
[
Vio22
]
. See also
[
BM23a
]
and
[
ENV19
]
.

Definition 3.1.5
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space. For
\varepsilon>0
we say
X
satisfies the
Bi-lateral Weak Geometric Lemma with parameter
\varepsilon
(BWGL
(\varepsilon)
) if the set
\{(x,r)\in E\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(E))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\xi_{X}(x,r)>\varepsilon\}
is a Carleson set. We say
X
satisfies the
Bi-lateral Weak Geometric Lemma
(BWGL) if for every
\varepsilon>0
it satisfies the BWGL
(\varepsilon)
.

To define the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients we of course need to define the
\gamma
-coefficients. For a metric space
X,
a ball
B_{X}(x,r)
, a norm
\|\cdot\|
and a Lipschitz
f\colon X\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|),
we introduce the coefficient
\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|}^{K}(x,r)
which as well as measuring how bi-laterally flat
B_{X}(x,r)
is (in the sense of
\xi_{X}(x,r)
), also measures how well
f
is approximated by
K
-Lipschitz “affine functions on
B_{X}(x,r)
”. Since
X
is an arbitrary metric space, it doesn’t make sense to talk about affine functions. The coefficient really measures how well
f
is well-approximated by affine functions defined on the best approximating tangent space to
B_{X}(x,r)
and is defined below.

Definition 3.1.6
.

Let
(X,d)
be a metric space,
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X).
Let
\|\cdot\|_{0}
and
\|\cdot\|
be norms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For
K\geq 1
and mappings
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
and
\varphi\colon B(x,r)\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)
, define

\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)=\frac{1}{r}\inf_{A}\sup_{y\in B(x,r)}\|f(y)-A(\varphi(y))\|_{0},

where the infimum is taken over all affine mappings
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\text{Lip}(A)\leq K
when viewed as a function from
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)
to
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
. Then, define

\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r)=\inf_{\|\cdot\|}\inf_{\varphi\in\Phi(x,r,\|\cdot\|)}[\xi_{X}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)+\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)].

The Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients is a sort of quantitative differentiation condition for Lipschitz functions on a space
X
. It states that, at most scales and locations, the space
X
is approximately bi-laterally flat and Lipschitz functions are well-approximated by affine functions with uniform gradient on
X
.

Definition 3.1.7
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space. We say
X
satisfies a
Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients
if there exists
K\geq 1
and for each
\varepsilon>0
a constant
C=C(\varepsilon)
such that if
\|\cdot\|_{0}
is a norm and
f\colon X\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
is 1-Lipschitz, then

(3.1.6)

\displaystyle\{(x,r)\in\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma_{X,f}^{K}(x,r)>\varepsilon\}

is Carleson set with Carleson norm
C
.

The following is immediate from the definitions.

Lemma 3.1.8
.

Let
X
be a metric space,
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)
. Let
K\geq 1,

\|\cdot\|_{0}
a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
f\colon X\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
a 1-Lipschitz function. Then,
\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r)\geq\xi_{X}(x,r).
In particular, a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients implies the
BWGL
.

Remark 3.1.9
.

In
[
DS93a
]
, David and Semmes introduced the
Weak Approximation of Lipschitz functions by Affine functions
(WALA) for subsets of Euclidean space. Roughly speaking,
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfies the WALA condition if for any given 1-Lipschitz function
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and for most
(x,r)\in X\times\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)
there exists an affine function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
which well-approximates
f
in
L^{\infty}(B_{X}(x,r))
. By most, we mean the complement of these pairs is a Carleson set. While it is tempting to think of the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients as a metric version of the WALA (
f
is well-approximated by affine functions at most scales and locations), this is not really the case. It is more accurately a metric version of BWGL + WALA. We need to include the BWGL condition so that the geometry of
X
is well-represented in the tangent space. Roughly speaking, this allows us to view the affine function on the tangent space as being an affine function on
X
. The Carleson condition on the
\gamma
-coefficients is seemingly much stronger than the WALA. As we will see from Theorem
B
, a Carleson condition on the
\gamma
-coefficients is equivalent to UR. In Euclidean space, it is still an open problem to determine whether or not WALA implies UR.

3.2.
Preliminaries with the
\gamma
-coefficient

Let us start by proving some basic properties regarding the coefficients
\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
X
be a metric space,
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)
. If
\|\cdot\|
is a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\varphi\in\Phi(x,r,\|\cdot\|)
then
\|\varphi(x)\|\leq 10\xi_{X}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)r.

Proof.

The proof very similar to (
2.3.11
), we omit the details.
∎

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Let
K\geq 1
,
X
be a metric space,
\|\cdot\|_{0}
a norm and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
be
1
-Lipschitz. Then,

(3.2.1)

\displaystyle\xi_{X}(x,r)\leq\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r)\lesssim 1

for all
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X).
Furthermore, if
y\in X
and
0<s<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)
are such that
B(x,r)\subseteq B(y,s),
then

(3.2.2)

\displaystyle\xi_{X}(x,r)\lesssim\frac{s}{r}\xi_{X}(y,s)

and

(3.2.3)

\displaystyle\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r)\lesssim_{K}\frac{s}{r}\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(y,s).

Proof.

Let
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X).
We start with (
3.2.1
). The first inequality is immediate from Lemma
3.1.8
. For the second inequality, let
|\cdot|
denote the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n},
let
\varphi\in\Phi(x,r,|\cdot|)
such that
\varphi(y)=0
for all
z\in B(x,r)
and let
A
be a translation of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
A(0)=f(x).
Notice that
A
is an affine map and 1-Lipschitz from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to itself. With these choices, it is easy to see that
\zeta_{X}(x,r,|\cdot|,\varphi)\leq 2
and
\eta_{X}(x,r,|\cdot|,\varphi)\leq 1.
Since
f
is 1-Lipschitz,
f(z)\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(A(0),r)
for all
z\in B(x,r).
Thus,
\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|}(x,r,|\cdot|,\varphi)\leq 1
and (
3.2.1
) follows.

Now let
y\in X
and
0<s<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)
such that
B(x,r)\subseteq B(y,s).
We prove (
3.2.2
). Let
\varepsilon>0
,
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}
and
\phi\in\Phi(y,s,\|\cdot\|)
such that

(3.2.4)

\displaystyle\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)+\eta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)\leq\xi_{X}(y,s)+\varepsilon.

Recall the properties of
p_{z,\lambda}
from Lemma
2.3.3
and let
\tilde{\phi}=p_{\phi(x),r}\circ\phi.
Then
\tilde{\phi}
maps
B(x,r)
to
B_{\|\cdot\|}(\phi(x),r)
and if
z\in B(x,r)
then

(3.2.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\tilde{\phi}(z)-\phi(z)\|&\leq\max\{0,\|\phi(z)-\phi(x)\|-r\}\\
&\leq\max\{0,d(z,x)+s\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)-r\}\\
&\leq s\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi).\end{split}

Let us estimate
\xi_{X}(x,r),
starting with
\zeta_{X}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\tilde{\phi}).
By the triangle inequality, (
3.2.5
) and the definition of
\zeta_{X}
, we have

(3.2.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}r\zeta_{X}(x,r,\|\cdot\|_{,}\tilde{\phi})&=\sup_{w,z\in B(x,r)}|d(w,z)-\|\tilde{\phi}(w)-\tilde{\phi}(z)\|\,|\\
&\leq\sup_{w,z\in B(x,r)}|d(w,z)-\|\phi(w)-\phi(z)\||+2s\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)\\
&\leq 3s\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi).\end{split}

To estimate
\eta_{X}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\tilde{\phi}),
let us fix some
u\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(\phi(x),r).
Then, let
\tilde{r}=r-s\xi_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)
and set

\tilde{u}=p_{\phi(x),\tilde{r}}(u)\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(\phi(x),\tilde{r}).

Observe that

(3.2.7)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{u}\|\leq\|\tilde{u}-\phi(x)\|+\|\phi(x)\|\leq\tilde{r}+d(x,y)+s\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)\leq r+(s-r)=s,

so that
\tilde{u}\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,s).
Then, by definition, there exists a point
z\in B(y,s)
such that

\|\tilde{u}-\phi(z)\|\leq s\eta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi).

In fact we have
z\in B(x,r)
since

(3.2.8)

\displaystyle d(x,z)

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi(x)-\phi(z)\|+s\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)

(3.2.9)

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi(x)-\tilde{u}\|+\|\tilde{u}-\phi(z)\|+s\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)

(3.2.10)

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{r}+s\eta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)+s\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)=r.

From this inequality we also get
\phi(y)\in B(\phi(x),r)
, hence,
\tilde{\phi}(y)=p_{\phi(x),r}(\phi(y))=\phi(y).
This then gives

(3.2.11)

\displaystyle\|u-\tilde{\phi}(y)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|u-\tilde{u}\|+\|\tilde{u}-\phi(y)\|\leq s\xi_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)+s\eta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)

(3.2.12)

\displaystyle\leq 2s\xi_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi).

Since
u\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(\phi(x),r)
is arbitrary, this implies

(3.2.13)

\displaystyle r\eta_{X}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\tilde{\phi})\leq 2s\xi_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi).

Since
\varepsilon
was arbitrary, combining (
3.2.6
) and (
3.2.13
) finishes the proof of (
3.2.2
).

We now prove (
3.2.3
). Let
\tau>0
,
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}
and
\phi\in\Phi(y,s,\|\cdot\|)
such that

(3.2.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)&+\eta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)+\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)\\
&\leq\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(y,s)+\varepsilon.\end{split}

Define
\tilde{\phi}
as below (
3.2.4
) so that the estimates (
3.2.5
), (
3.2.6
) and (
3.2.13
) still hold. It only remains to estimate
\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\tilde{\phi}).
For this, let
\delta>0
and
A\colon(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
be affine and
K
-Lipschitz such that

(3.2.15)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s}\sup_{z\in B(y,s)}\|f(z)-A(\phi(z))\|_{0}\leq\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)+\delta.

Using (
3.2.5
), (
3.2.15
) and the fact that
A
is
K
-Lipschitz, we have

(3.2.16)

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in B(x,r)}\|f(z)-A(\tilde{\phi}(z))\|_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{z\in B(x,r)}\|f(z)-A(\phi(z))\|_{0}+\|A(\phi(z))-A(\tilde{\phi}(z))\|_{0}

(3.2.17)

\displaystyle\leq s[\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)+\delta]+Ks\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi).

Since
\delta
is arbitrary, this implies

(3.2.18)

\displaystyle r\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\tilde{\phi})\leq s\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi)+Ks\zeta_{X}(y,s,\|\cdot\|,\phi).

Then, since
\tau
was arbitrary, this, (
3.2.14
), (
3.2.6
) and (
3.2.13
) imply (
3.2.3
).
∎

Definition 3.2.3
.

Let
(Z,d)
be a metric space and suppose
X,Y\subseteq Z
. For
x\in X
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)
define

I_{X,Y}(x,r)=\frac{1}{r}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in E\cap B(x,r)\\
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y,Y)\leq r\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,Y),

where we set the supremum over the empty set to be zero.

Lemma 3.2.4
.

Let
(Z,d)
be a metric space and suppose
X_{1},X_{2}\subseteq Z
are such that
X_{1}\cap X_{2}\neq\emptyset.
Let
x\in X_{1}\cap X_{2}
,
0<r<\min\{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X_{1}),\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X_{2})\}
and suppose
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
is 1-Lipschitz. Write
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
for the restrictions of
f
to
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, respectively. Then

\gamma_{X_{1},f_{1},\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r)\lesssim_{K}\gamma_{X_{2},f_{2},\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,3r)+I_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x,r)+I_{X_{2},X_{1}}(x,3r).

Proof.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
and let
\|\cdot\|
be a seminorm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\varphi_{2}\in\Phi(x,3r,\|\cdot\|)
such that

(3.2.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega_{X_{2},f_{2},\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,3r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi_{2})&+\zeta_{X_{2}}(x,3r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi_{2})+\eta_{X_{2}}(x,3r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi_{2})\\
&\leq\gamma_{X_{2},f_{2},\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,3r)+\varepsilon.\end{split}

For brevity, in the remainder of the proof we will write
\Omega_{2}\coloneqq\Omega_{X_{2},f_{2},\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,3r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi_{2})
,
\zeta_{2}\coloneqq\zeta_{X_{2}}(x,3r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi_{2})
and
\eta_{2}\coloneqq\eta_{X_{2}}(x,3r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)
. Let us observe for later that

(3.2.20)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{2}(x)\|\leq 30r(\zeta_{2}+\eta_{2})

by (
2.3.11
). We start by constructing a map
\varphi_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X_{1}\cap B(x,r)\to B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)
as follows. For each
p\in X_{1}\cap B(x,r)
, let
\tilde{p}\in X_{2}
be a point in
X_{2}
such that

(3.2.21)

\displaystyle d(p,\tilde{p})\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(p,X_{2})+\varepsilon r\leq rI_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x,r)+\varepsilon r\leq 2r,

so that
\tilde{p}\in X_{2}\cap B(x,3r).
Using (
3.2.20
) and (
3.2.21
) with the fact that
p\in X_{1}\cap B(x,r)
we have

(3.2.22)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{2}(\tilde{p})\|

\displaystyle=\|\varphi(x)-\varphi(\tilde{p})\|+Cr(\zeta_{2}+\eta_{2})\leq d(x,\tilde{p})+Cr(\zeta_{2}+\eta_{2})

(3.2.23)

\displaystyle\leq d(x,p)+d(p,\tilde{p})+Cr(\zeta_{2}+\eta_{2})

(3.2.24)

\displaystyle\leq r+rI_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x,r)+Cr(\zeta_{2}+\eta_{2})+\varepsilon r.

By Lemma
2.3.3
we can find a point
\varphi_{1}(p)\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)
such that

(3.2.25)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{1}(p)-\varphi_{2}(\tilde{p})\|\leq rI_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x,r)+Cr(\zeta_{2}+\eta_{2})+\varepsilon r.

Let us estimate
\zeta_{X_{1}}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi_{1}).
Let
y,z\in X_{1}\cap B(x,r).
Since
\tilde{y},\tilde{z}\in X_{2}\cap B(x,3r)
we know by the definition of
\zeta_{2}
that
\|\varphi_{2}(\tilde{y})-\varphi_{2}(\tilde{z})\|-d(\tilde{y},\tilde{z})\lesssim\zeta_{2}.
Using this with (
3.2.21
) and (
3.2.25
), we have

(3.2.26)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{1}(y)-\varphi_{1}(z)\|-d(y,z)

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi_{1}(y)-\varphi_{2}(\tilde{y})\|+\|\varphi_{2}(\tilde{y})-\varphi_{2}(\tilde{z})\|

(3.2.27)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|\varphi_{2}(\tilde{z})-\varphi_{1}(z)\|-d(\tilde{y},\tilde{z})+d(y,\tilde{y})+d(z,\tilde{z})

(3.2.28)

\displaystyle\lesssim rI_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x,r)+r\zeta_{2}+r\eta_{2}+\varepsilon r.

Similarly,

(3.2.29)

\displaystyle d(y,z)-\|\varphi_{1}(y)-\varphi_{1}(z)\|

\displaystyle\leq rI_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x,r)+r\zeta_{2}+r\eta_{2}+\varepsilon r.

Combining the above two inequality, we have

(3.2.30)

\displaystyle\zeta_{X_{1}}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi_{1})\lesssim\zeta_{2}+\eta_{2}+I_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x,r)+\varepsilon.

Now we estimate
\eta_{X_{1}}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi_{1}).
Let
u\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)
and let
\tilde{u}=\lambda u
where
0<\lambda\leq 1
is maximal such that

(3.2.31)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{u}\|\leq r-r(33\zeta_{2}+33\eta_{2}+3I_{X_{2},X_{1}}(x,3r))-\varepsilon r.

This choice of
\tilde{u}
will become more clear shortly, observe for the moment that

(3.2.32)

\displaystyle\|u-\tilde{u}\|\leq r(33\zeta_{2}+33\eta_{2}+3I_{X_{2},X_{1}}(x,3r))+\varepsilon r.

By definition of
\eta_{2}
, there exists
q\in X_{2}\cap B(x,3r)
such that
\|\tilde{u}-\varphi_{2}(q)\|\leq 3r\eta_{2}.
Let
p\in X_{1}
be a point in
X_{1}
such that
d(q,p)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(q,X_{1})+\varepsilon r
and let
\tilde{p}
be as in (
3.2.21
). Observe that

(3.2.33)

\displaystyle d(p,x)

\displaystyle\leq d(p,q)+d(q,x)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(q,X_{1})+\varepsilon r+\|\varphi_{2}(q)-\varphi_{2}(x)\|+3r\zeta_{2}

(3.2.34)

\displaystyle\leq 3rI_{X_{2},X_{1}}(x,3r)+\varepsilon r+\|\tilde{u}\|+\|\tilde{u}-\varphi_{2}(q)\|+\|\varphi_{2}(x)\|+3r\zeta_{2}

(3.2.35)

\displaystyle\leq 3rI_{X_{2},X_{1}}(x,3r)+\varepsilon r+\|\tilde{u}\|+33r\eta_{2}+33r\zeta_{2}\leq r,

so that
p\in X_{1}\cap B(x,r).
This is why we chose
\tilde{u}
as in (
3.2.31
). Notice also, since
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(p,X_{2})\leq d(p,q),
we have

(3.2.36)

\displaystyle d(q,\tilde{p})

\displaystyle\leq d(q,p)+d(p,\tilde{p})\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(q,p)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(p.X_{2})+\varepsilon r\leq 2d(q,p)+\varepsilon r

(3.2.37)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(q,X_{1})+3\varepsilon r\leq 6rI_{X_{2},X_{1}}(x,3r)+3\varepsilon r.

Using this estimate with the definition of
\zeta_{2}
, the definition of
q
after (
3.2.32
) and (
3.2.25
), it follows that

(3.2.38)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{u}-\varphi_{1}(p)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{u}-\varphi_{2}(q)\|+\|\varphi_{2}(q)-\varphi_{2}(\tilde{p})\|+\|\varphi_{2}(\tilde{p})-\varphi_{1}(p)\|

(3.2.39)

\displaystyle\lesssim r(\zeta_{2}+\eta_{2}+I_{X_{2},X_{1}}(x,3r)+\varepsilon).

With (
3.2.32
), this implies

(3.2.40)

\displaystyle\eta_{X_{1}}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi_{1})\lesssim\zeta_{2}+\eta_{2}+I_{X_{2},X_{1}}(x,3r)+\varepsilon.

Finally, we can estimate
\Omega_{X_{1},f_{1},\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi_{1}).
Let
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an affine function such that
\|A(x)-A(y)\|_{0}\leq K\|x-y\|
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

\frac{1}{3r}\sup_{y\in X_{2}\cap B(x,3r)}\|f_{2}(x)-A(\varphi_{2}(x))\|_{0}\leq\Omega_{2}+\varepsilon.

Let
y\in X_{1}\cap B(x,r)
and let
\tilde{y}\in X_{2}\cap B(x,3r)
be as in (
3.2.21
). By (
3.2.21
), (
3.2.25
) and recalling that
f_{1},f_{2}
are the restrictions of
f
to
X_{1},X_{2}
, respectively, we have

(3.2.41)

\displaystyle\|f_{1}(y)-A(\varphi_{1}(y))\|_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\|f(y)-f(\tilde{y})\|_{0}+\|f_{2}(\tilde{y})-A(\varphi_{2}(\tilde{y}))\|_{0}

(3.2.42)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|A(\varphi_{2}(\tilde{y}))-A(\varphi_{1}(y))\|_{0}

(3.2.43)

\displaystyle\leq d(y,\tilde{y})+3r(\Omega_{2}+\varepsilon)+K\|\varphi_{2}(\tilde{y})-\varphi_{1}(y)\|

(3.2.44)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{K}r(\zeta_{2}+\Omega_{2}+I_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x,r)+\varepsilon).

Thus,

(3.2.45)

\displaystyle\Omega_{f_{1},X_{1}}^{K}(x,r,\varphi_{1})\lesssim_{K}\zeta_{2}+\Omega_{2}+I_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x,r)+\varepsilon.

Combining this with (
3.2.19
), (
3.2.30
), (
3.2.40
) and taking
\varepsilon\to 0
proves the lemma.
∎

Remark 3.2.5
.

It is easy to show that
rI_{X,Y}(x,r)\leq sI_{X,Y}(y,s)
any
x,y\in X
and
0<r\leq s<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)
such that
B(x,r)\subseteq B(y,s).
In particular, the above estimate may be written as

(3.2.46)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{X_{1},f_{1},\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,r)\lesssim_{K}\gamma_{X_{2},f_{2},\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x,3r)+I_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x,3r)+I_{X_{2},X_{1}}(x,3r).

Although slightly weaker, this estimate will ease the notation in Section
4
.

4.
UR implies BWGL and affine approximations

Contents of this section

4.1.
Main result

Our goal in this section is to prove the following.

Theorem 4.1.1
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space. If
X
is UR then
X
satisfies a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients.

The proof of Theorem
4.1.1
goes roughly as follows. Suppose
\mathscr{Y}
is a collection of Ahlfors
n
-regular sets with uniform regularity constant and suppose each
Y\in\mathscr{Y}
satisfies the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients with a uniform constant
K
and uniform Carleson norms. We first show that
if

X
has big pieces of elements of
\mathscr{Y}
(in the sense of Definition
3.1.2
)
then

X
itself satisfies a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients. That is, the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients is
stable
under the big pieces functor. This result is inspired by the stability result for BWGL first shown for subsets of Euclidean space and the Heisenberg group by Rigot
[
Rig19
]
. The results in
[
Rig19
]
were inspired by the stability result for the WGL (a one-sided version of the BWGL) in Euclidean spaces in
[
DS93a
, Part IV]
.

If
X
is uniformly
n
-rectifiable then it has big pieces of spaces which are bi-Lipschitz equivalent to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(after embedding in a suitable Banach space). The second part of the proof of Theorem
4.1.1
is showing that these spaces satisfy a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients with uniform
K
and Carleson norms.

4.2.
Stability of Carleson condition for
\gamma
under big pieces

The goal now is to prove the following proposition, which states that the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients is stable under big pieces.

Proposition 4.2.1
.

Let
Z
be a metric space,
C_{1},K\geq 1
, and let

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\{Y\subseteq Z\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\

\displaystyle Y\mbox{ is Ahlfors $n$-regular with regularity constant $C_{1}$ and satisfies a }

(4.2.2)

Carelson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients with constant

(4.2.3)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{ $K$ and Carleson norms $\{C(\varepsilon)\}_{\varepsilon>0}$}\}.

If
X\subseteq Z
is Ahlfors
n
-regular with regularity constant
C_{0}
and has big pieces of
\mathcal{F}
then
X
satisfies a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients with constant
K
. The family of Carleson norms depends only on
C_{0},C_{1},\{C(\varepsilon)\},n
and the big pieces constant
\theta.

To prove Proposition
4.2.1
it will be useful to work with Christ-David cubes (Lemma
2.6.1
). Below we formulate the BWGL and the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients in terms of Christ-David cubes.

Definition 4.2.2
.

Suppose
(X,d)
is a doubling metric space and
\mathcal{D}
is a system of Christ-David cubes on
X
. For
A\geq 1,
a cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}
, a norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a map
\varphi\in\Phi(x_{Q},A\ell(Q),\|\cdot\|)
, define

(4.2.4)

\displaystyle\zeta_{X}(AQ,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\zeta_{X}(x_{Q},A\ell(Q),\|\cdot\|,\varphi);

(4.2.5)

\displaystyle\eta_{X}(AQ,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\eta_{X}(x_{Q},A\ell(Q),\|\cdot\|,\varphi);

(4.2.6)

\displaystyle\xi_{X}(AQ)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\xi_{X}(x_{Q},A\ell(Q)).

If
\|\cdot\|_{0}
is another norm and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
is 1-Lipschitz, we also define

(4.2.7)

\displaystyle\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(AQ,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Omega_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x_{Q},A\ell(Q),\|\cdot\|,\varphi);

(4.2.8)

\displaystyle\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(AQ)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x_{Q},A\ell(Q)).

If there are further metric spaces
Y,Z
such that
X,Y\subseteq Z,
define

(4.2.9)

\displaystyle I_{X,Y}(AQ)=I_{X,Y}(x_{Q},A\ell(Q)).

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.6.5
, (
3.2.3
) and (
3.2.2
) we obtain the following discretized formulations of the BWGL and the Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients. The definition of Carleson packing condition be found in Definition
2.6.3
.

Lemma 4.2.3
.

Let
A>1
,
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space and
\mathcal{D}
a system of Christ-David cubes on
X
. Then,
X
satisfies the
BWGL
if and only if for
\varepsilon>0
the set
\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\xi_{X}(AQ)>\varepsilon\}
satisfies a Carleson packing condition.

Lemma 4.2.4
.

Let
A>1,

X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space and
\mathcal{D}
a system of Christ-David cubes on
X
. Then,
X
satisfies a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients if and only if there exists
K\geq 1
and for each
\varepsilon>0
a constant
C=C(\varepsilon)
such that if
\|\cdot\|_{0}
is a norm and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
is 1-Lipschitz then
\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(AQ)>\varepsilon\}
satisfies a Carleson packing condition with Carleson norm
C
.

It will be via Lemma
4.2.4
that we prove Proposition
4.2.1
. We need to collect some more results before beginning the proof.

Lemma 4.2.5
.

Let
(X,d)
be an Ahlfors
(C_{0},n)
-regular metric space and
s>0
. Let
a>0
,
A\geq 1
and suppose
\mathcal{N}\subseteq X
is an
as
-separated net with
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(\mathcal{N})\leq As
. Then,
|\mathcal{N}|\lesssim_{C_{0},n,A,a}1.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{N}=\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I}.
For each
i\in\mathcal{N},
let
B_{i}=B(z_{i},as/4).
Pick some
i_{0}\in I
and let
B=B(z_{i_{0}},2As).
Then,
B_{i}\subseteq B
for each
i\in I
and the result now follows by Ahlfors regularity since

(4.2.10)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}|C_{0}^{-1}(as)^{n}\leq\sum_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{i})\leq\mathcal{H}^{n}(B)\leq C_{0}(2As)^{n}.

∎

Lemma 4.2.6
.

Suppose
(Z,d)
is a metric space and
X,Y\subseteq Z
are Ahlfors
n
-regular. Let
C_{0}
be the regularity constant for
X
and
\mathcal{D}
a system of Christ-David cubes for
X.
Then,
\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I_{X,Y}(3Q)>\varepsilon\}
satisfies a Carleson packing condition for each
\varepsilon>0
with Carleson norm depending only on
C_{0},n
and
\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}
. Without loss of generality we may suppose
\varepsilon<1.
For each
Q\subseteq Q_{0}
such that
I_{X,Y}(3Q)>\varepsilon
there exists a point
y_{Q}\in X\cap 3B_{Q}
such

(4.2.11)

\displaystyle 3\varepsilon\ell(Q)<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y_{Q},Y)\leq 3\ell(Q).

Choose
k=k(Q)
to be the smallest positive integer such that
5\varrho^{k}\varepsilon^{-1}\leq\ell(Q)
and let
T(Q)\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
such that
y_{Q}\in T(Q)
. In particular,
3B_{Q}\cap T(Q)\neq\emptyset.
Let

C_{*}=1+\tfrac{3}{\varepsilon\varrho}.

By maximality and using the fact that
\varepsilon<1,
we have

(4.2.12)

\displaystyle\ell(T(Q))\leq\frac{\ell(T(Q))}{\varepsilon}\leq\ell(Q)<\frac{\ell(T(Q))}{\varepsilon\varrho}\leq C_{*}\ell(T(Q)).

Since
3B_{Q}\cap T(Q)\neq\emptyset,
the first two inequalities imply

(4.2.13)

\displaystyle T(Q)\subseteq 6B_{Q}.

Furthermore, using that
y_{Q}\in T(Q)
with (
4.2.11
) and the second and third inequalities in (
4.2.12
), we have

(4.2.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\ell(T(Q))&\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y_{Q},Y)-\ell(T(Q))\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{T(Q)},Y)\\
&\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(y_{Q},Y)+\ell(T(Q))\leq C_{*}\ell(T(Q)).\end{split}

We claim

(4.2.15)

\displaystyle|\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T(Q)=T\}|\lesssim_{C_{0},n}1.

Indeed, suppose
T\in\mathcal{D}
. By (
4.2.12
), if
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
are distinct cubes such that
T(Q)=T(Q^{\prime})=T
, then
\ell(Q)=\ell(Q^{\prime})=5\varrho^{k_{0}}
for some
k_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}.
Recall from Lemma
2.6.1
that the centred of the cubes in
\mathcal{D}_{k}
are chosen from a
\varrho^{k}
-separated net, for such cubes we have
d(x_{Q},x_{Q^{\prime}})\geq\varrho^{k_{0}}
. Furthermore, since
3B_{Q}\cap T\neq\emptyset
and
3B_{Q^{\prime}}\cap T\neq\emptyset
, the triangle inequality and the second inequality in (
4.2.12
) implies
d(x_{Q},x_{Q^{\prime}})\lesssim\varrho^{k_{0}}
. Applying Lemma
4.2.5
now gives (
4.2.15
).

Let
\mathcal{G}
be the set of
T\in\mathcal{D}
such that
T\subseteq 6B_{Q_{0}}
and
2\ell(T)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{T},Y)\leq C_{*}\ell(T)
. If
Q\subseteq Q_{0}
then
6B_{Q}\subseteq 6B_{Q_{0}}
by Remark
2.6.2
. Thus, it follows from (
4.2.13
) and (
4.2.14
) that
T(Q)\in\mathcal{G}
for each
Q
such that
I_{X,Y}(3Q)>\varepsilon.
This, the third inequality in (
4.2.12
) and (
4.2.15
) imply

(4.2.16)

\displaystyle\sum\{\ell(Q)^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\subseteq Q_{0},\ I_{X,Y}(3Q)>\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\sum\{\ell(T(Q))^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\subseteq Q_{0},\ I_{X,Y}(3Q)>\varepsilon\}

(4.2.17)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C_{0},n}\sum\{\ell(T)^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T\in\mathcal{G},\ T\subseteq 6B_{Q_{0}}\}.

We observe now that
\mathcal{G}
has bounded overlap. Indeed, let
p\in X
and let
\mathcal{G}(p)=\{T\in\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}p\in T\}
. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\cdot,Y)
is 1-Lipschitz, if
T\in\mathcal{G}(p)
then

(4.2.18)

\displaystyle\ell(T)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{T},Y)-d(x_{T},p)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(p,Y)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{T},Y)+d(x_{T},p)\leq 2C_{*}\ell(T)

This, and the dependency of
C_{*}
on
\varepsilon
, imply
|\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{G}(p)\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}\neq\emptyset\}|\lesssim_{\varepsilon}1.
Since
\mathcal{D}_{k}
is disjoint for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
|\mathcal{G}(p)\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}|\leq 1
. Thus,
|\mathcal{G}(p)|\lesssim_{\varepsilon}1
as required.

We use bounded overlap and Ahlfors regularity to conclude the lemma since

(4.2.19)

\displaystyle\sum\{\ell(T)^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T\in\mathcal{G},\ T\subseteq 6B_{Q_{0}}\}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C_{0}}\sum\{\mathcal{H}^{n}(T)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T\in\mathcal{G},\ T\subseteq 6B_{Q_{0}}\}

(4.2.20)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{H}^{n}(X\cap 6B_{Q_{0}})\lesssim_{C_{0}}\ell(Q_{0})^{n}.

∎

By Lemma
2.6.5
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 4.2.7
.

Let
Z
be a metric space and suppose
X,Y\subseteq Z
are Ahlfors
n
-regular. Let
C_{0}
be the regularity constant for
X
. For all
\varepsilon>0
the set
\{(x,r)\in X\cap\mathbb{R}^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I_{X,Y}(x,r)>\varepsilon\}
is a Carleson set with Carleson norm depending only on
C_{0},n
and
\varepsilon.

To conclude the proof of Proposition
4.2.1
we require the following.

Lemma 4.2.8

(
[
DS93a
, Lemma IV.1.12]
)
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space and
\mathcal{D}
a system of dyadic cubes for
X
. Let
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{D}\to[0,\infty)
be given and suppose there are
N>0
and
\eta>0
such that

\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\left\{x\in R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subseteq R\\
x\in Q\end{subarray}}\alpha(Q)\leq N\right\}\right)\geq\eta\ell(R)^{n}.

for all
R\in\mathcal{D}.
Then,

\sum_{Q\subseteq R}\alpha(Q)\ell(Q)^{n}\lesssim_{N,\eta}\ell(R)^{n}

for all
R\in\mathcal{D}.

Proof of Proposition
4.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a system of Christ-David cubes for
X
and
\varepsilon>0
. Suppose
\|\cdot\|
is a norm and
f\colon X\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)
is a 1-Lipschitz mapping. Set
\mathcal{B}=\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|}^{K}(3Q)>\varepsilon\}.
By Lemma
4.2.4
and Lemma
4.2.8
, it suffices to find constants
N,\eta>0
depending only on
C_{0},C_{1},C(\varepsilon),n
and
\theta
such that

(4.2.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\left\{x\in R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subseteq R\\
x\in Q\end{subarray}}\chi_{\mathcal{B}}(Q)\leq N\right\}\right)\geq\eta\ell(R)^{n}

for all
R\in\mathcal{D}.

Fix a cube
R\in\mathcal{D}
and recall the constant
c_{0}
from Lemma
2.6.1
. By assumption we can find an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space
Y=Y_{R}\subseteq Z
with regularity constant
C_{1}
satisfying a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients with constant
K
and Carleson norms
\{C(\varepsilon)\}_{\varepsilon>0}
and such that

(4.2.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(R\cap Y)\geq\mathcal{H}^{n}(X\cap Y\cap c_{0}B_{R})\geq\theta(c_{0}\ell(R))^{n}.

For
\delta>0
let

(4.2.23)

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}^{1}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\{(x,t)\in X\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|}^{K}(x,t)>\delta\};

(4.2.24)

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}^{2}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\{(x,t)\in X\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I_{X,Y}(x,t)>\delta\},

(4.2.25)

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{\delta}^{3}

\displaystyle=\{(x,t)\in Y\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma_{Y,f,\|\cdot\|}^{K}(x,t)>\delta\},

(4.2.26)

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{\delta}^{4}

\displaystyle=\{(x,t)\in Y\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I_{Y,X}(x,t)>\delta\}.

Let
C_{2}
(resp.
C_{3}
) be the implicit constant in (
3.2.3
) (resp. (
3.2.46
)). Then, let

\varepsilon_{1}=\frac{3\varepsilon}{4C_{2}},\ \varepsilon_{2}=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{3C_{3}}\mbox{ and }\varepsilon_{3}=\frac{\varepsilon_{2}}{2C_{2}}.

Suppose
x\in R\cap Y
and
Q\in\mathcal{B}
is such that
x\in Q\subseteq R
. Observe first that
3B_{Q}\subseteq B(x,4\ell(Q))
, hence,
(x,4\ell(Q))\in\mathscr{B}_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{1}
by (
3.2.3
). Now, applying (
3.2.46
) we find
(x,12\ell(Q))\in\mathscr{B}^{i}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
for some
i\in\{2,3,4\}.
Finally, if
(x,12\ell(Q))\in\mathscr{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}^{i}
and
12\ell(Q)\leq r<24\ell(Q)
then
(x,r)\in\mathscr{B}_{\varepsilon_{3}}^{i}
by (
3.2.3
). Combining these observations we get

(4.2.27)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subseteq R\\
x\in Q\end{subarray}}\chi_{\mathcal{B}}(Q)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subseteq R\\
x\in Q\end{subarray}}\chi_{\mathscr{B}_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{1}}(x,4\ell(Q))\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subseteq R\\
x\in Q\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=2}^{4}\chi_{\mathscr{B}_{\varepsilon_{2}}^{i}}(x,12\ell(Q))

(4.2.28)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subseteq R\\
x\in Q\end{subarray}}\int_{12\ell(Q)}^{24\ell(Q)}\sum_{i=2}^{4}\chi_{\mathscr{B}_{\varepsilon_{3}}^{i}}(x,r)\,\frac{dr}{12\ell(Q)}\leq 2\sum_{i=2}^{4}\int_{0}^{24\ell(R)}\chi_{\mathscr{B}_{\varepsilon_{3}}^{i}}(x,r)\,\frac{dr}{r}

Fix some arbitrary
z\in R\cap Y.
Then,
R\cap Y\subseteq B_{Y}(z,24\ell(Q)).
Now, using the above estimate with Corollary
4.2.7
along with the fact that
Y
satisfies a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients, we have

(4.2.29)

\displaystyle\int_{R\cap Y}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subseteq R\\
x\in Q\end{subarray}}\chi_{\mathcal{B}}(Q)\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=2}^{4}\int_{B_{Y}(z,24\ell(R))}\int_{0}^{24\ell(R)}\chi_{\mathscr{B}_{\varepsilon_{3}}^{i}}(x,r)\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mathcal{H}^{n}(x)

(4.2.30)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C_{0},C_{1},C(\varepsilon_{3}),\varepsilon,n}\ell(R)^{n}.

Thus, for
N\geq 1
large enough depending on each of
C_{0},C_{1},C(\varepsilon_{3}),\varepsilon,n
and
\theta
, we have

\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\left\{x\in R\cap Y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subseteq R\\
x\in Q\end{subarray}}\chi_{\mathcal{B}}(Q)>N\right\}\right)\leq\frac{\theta(c_{0}\ell(R))^{n}}{2}.

If we set
\eta=\theta(c_{0}\ell(R))^{n}/2
, then (
4.2.21
) now follows from the above inequality and (
4.2.22
).
∎

4.3.
UR implies big pieces of bi-Lipschitz images of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in Banach spaces

In this section we prove the following.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Suppose
X
is UR with constants
C_{0},L
and
\theta
. Then there exists
L^{\prime}
, depending on
L,n
and
\theta
, a Banach space
\mathcal{B}
and an isometric embedding
j\colon X\to\mathcal{B}
such that
j(X)
has big pieces of

(4.3.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\{g(\mathbb{R}^{n})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}g\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}\mbox{ is }L^{\prime}\mbox{-bi-Lipschitz}\}

in
\mathcal{B}.
The big pieces constant depends only on
\theta
.

For the proof of Lemma
4.3.1
require the following result.

Lemma 4.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a Banach space,
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
L\geq 1
and suppose
f\colon A\to\mathcal{B}
is
L
-bi-Lipschitz onto its image. Then there exist constants
m
and
L^{\prime}
, depending only on
L
and
n,
such that the following holds. Let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
and equip
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
with the metric
d_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}=\sqrt{d_{\mathcal{B}}^{2}+d_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}^{2}}.
Then, identifying
\mathcal{B}
with a subset of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, there exists an extension
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
of
f
such that
g
is
L^{\prime}
-bi-Lipschitz onto its image.

This result is proved in the case
\mathcal{B}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
in
[
DS91
, Proposition 17.4]
(note, the roles of
d
and
n
are reversed there). In this case we simply have
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{d+m}
with
d_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
the Euclidean metric. The essential properties of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(the codomain) used in the proof of
[
DS91
, Proposition 17.4]
are the triangle inequality and the fact that
L
-Lipschitz functions from subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
into
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be extended to
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz functions from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
into
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
L^{\prime}
depending only on
L
and
n
. The analogous extension property for Lipschitz functions taking values in Banach spaces was proven in
[
JLS86
]
. Thus, the exact same proof gives Lemma
4.3.2
.

Proof of Lemma
4.3.1
.

Let
m
be the constant from Lemma
4.3.2
, let
i\colon X\to\ell^{\infty}(X)
be the Kuratowski embedding and set
\mathcal{B}=\ell^{\infty}(X)\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
. From now on we will identify
\ell^{\infty}(X)
with a subset of
\mathcal{B}
and
i
with an isometric embedding of
X
in
\mathcal{B}
in the obvious way.

Let
Y=i(X)
and let us show that
Y
has big pieces of
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{B}.
Fix
x\in Y
and
0<r<\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(Y)
. Notice that
Y
is UR since
X
is UR and
i
is an isometry. In particular, by
[
Sch09
, Corollary 1.2]
, it has BPBI in the sense of Definition
1.2.2
with constants
L_{*}
(depending on
L,n
and
\theta
) and
\theta_{*}
(depending only on
\theta
). Thus, there exists a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and an
L_{*}
-bi-Lipschitz map
f\colon A\to Y\subseteq\ell^{\infty}(X)
such that

(4.3.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(f(A)\cap B_{Y}(x,r))\geq\theta_{*}r^{n}.

By Lemma
4.3.2
we can find an
L^{\prime}
-bi-Lipschitz extension
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}
of
f
with
L^{\prime}
depending only on
L_{*}
and
n.
It now follows that (
4.3.2
) that

(4.3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(g(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap B_{Y}(x,r))\geq\mathcal{H}^{n}(f(A)\cap B_{Y}(x,r))\geq\theta_{*}r^{n}

as required.
∎

4.4.
A Carleson condition for
\gamma
on bi-Lipschitz images of
\mathbb{R}^{n}

The main goal of this section is to prove the following.

Proposition 4.4.1
.

Let
(\Sigma,d)
be a metric space and suppose there exists
L\geq 1
and an
L
-bi-Lipschitz map
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\Sigma
such that
\Sigma=g(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Then
\Sigma
satisfies a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
coefficients with constant
K=K(L,n).
The Carleson norm for the set in (
3.1.6
) depends only on
\varepsilon,L
and
n.

For the proof of Proposition
4.4.1
we begin by setting some notation and recalling some important results. For a cubes
I
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
let
x_{I}
denote its centre and
\ell(I)
denote its side-length. Let
\Delta
be the set of dyadic cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Let
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}
be the collection of all semi-norms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mathcal{N}
the collection of all norms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{N}_{L}
the collection of norms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

L^{-1}\|x\|\leq|x|\leq L\|x\|\mbox{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

where
|\cdot|
denotes the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
The following is a consequence of Dorronsoro’s Theorem
[
Dor85
]
.

Theorem 4.4.2
.

For each
L\geq 1
there exists
K=K(L,n)
such that the following holds. Suppose
\|\cdot\|_{0}\in\mathcal{N}
,
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
is
L
-Lipschitz and
\varepsilon>0.
For each
J\in\Delta,
we have

(4.4.1)

\displaystyle\sum\{\ell(I)^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in\Delta,\ I\subseteq J,\ \Omega_{\mathbb{R}^{n},f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(x_{I},3\ell(I),|\cdot|,\emph{Id})>\varepsilon\}\lesssim_{\varepsilon,L,n}\ell(J)^{n}.

Remark 4.4.3
.

Dorronsoro’s Theorem implies Theorem
4.4.2
immediately for
L
-Lipschitz functions
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the coefficients
\Omega_{\mathbb{R}^{n},g,|\cdot|}^{K}(\cdot,\cdot,|\cdot|,\text{Id})
. In the general case we proceed as follows. Let
T\colon(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a
\sqrt{n}
-bi-Lipschitz linear isomorphism (whose existence is guaranteed by Remark
2.2.1
) and set
g\coloneqq T\circ f.
It is easy to show

\Omega_{\mathbb{R}^{n},f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{Kn^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{n}\Omega_{\mathbb{R}^{n},g,|\cdot|}^{K}

so that Theorem
4.4.2
with
f
and
\|\cdot\|_{0}
follows from the same result with
g
and
|\cdot|.

For a metric space
(X,d),
a Lipschitz function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow X
and a cube
I
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(not necessarily dyadic), define

\text{md}_{f}(I)=\frac{1}{\ell(I)}\inf_{\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}}\sup_{x,y\in I}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d(f(x),f(y))-\|x-y\|\right\rvert}

and

\text{md}_{f}^{L}(I)=\frac{1}{\ell(I)}\inf_{\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}_{L}}\sup_{x,y\in I}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d(f(x),f(y))-\|x-y\|\right\rvert}

The following is proved in
[
AS14
]
.

Theorem 4.4.4
.

Let
L\geq 1,

f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow X
be
L
-Lipschitz function and
\varepsilon>0
. Then, for each
J\in\Delta
,

\sum\{\ell(I)^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in\Delta,\ I\subseteq J,\ \emph{md}_{f}(3I)>\varepsilon\}\lesssim_{d,\varepsilon,L}\ell(J)^{n}.

We record the following corollary.

Corollary 4.4.5
.

Let
L\geq 1,

f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow X
be an
L
-bi-Lipschitz function and
\varepsilon>0
. Then, for each
J\in\Delta
,

\sum\{\ell(I)^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\in\Delta,\ I\subseteq J,\ \emph{md}^{2L}_{f}(3I)>\varepsilon\}\lesssim_{d,\varepsilon,L}\ell(J)^{n}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we may suppose
0<\varepsilon<(4L)^{-1}
(larger values for
\varepsilon
are immediate from this case). Suppose
I\in\Delta
is such that
\text{md}_{f}(3I)\leq\varepsilon.
It suffices to show
\text{md}_{f}^{2L}(3I)\leq\varepsilon
. To see that this holds, let
0<\delta<(4L)^{-1}
and
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}
such that

(4.4.2)

\displaystyle\sup_{x,y\in 3I}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d(f(x),f(y))-\|x-y\|\right\rvert}\leq(\text{md}_{f}(3I)+\delta)\ell(3I)\leq(\varepsilon+\delta)\ell(3I).

Since
\delta
is arbitrary, if we show
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}_{2L}
then we are done. Let
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and choose
x,y\in 3I
such that
|x-y|=\ell(3I)
and
z=\lambda(y-x)
for some
\lambda>0.
By (
4.4.2
), our choice of
\varepsilon
and
\delta
, and the fact that
f
is
L
-bi-Lipschitz, we have

(4.4.3)

\displaystyle L^{-1}|z|

\displaystyle=L\lambda|y-x|\leq\lambda d(f(x),f(y))\leq\lambda\|x-y\|+(\varepsilon+\delta)\lambda\ell(3I)

(4.4.4)

\displaystyle=\|z\|+(2L)^{-1}|z|.

After rearranging, the above inequality implies
|z|\leq 2L\|z\|.
For the reverse inequality, we use (
4.4.2
) again to get

(4.4.5)

\displaystyle\|z\|=\lambda\|y-x\|\leq\lambda d(f(x),f(y))+2\varepsilon\lambda\ell(3I)\leq L\lambda|y-x|+2\varepsilon\lambda\ell(3I)\leq 2L|z|.

Thus,
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}_{2L}
.
∎

For the remainder of the section we fix a metric space
(\Sigma,d)
and suppose there exists an
L
-bi-Lipschitz function
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\Sigma
such that
\Sigma=g(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Let
\mathcal{D}
be a system of Christ-David cubes for
\Sigma.

Lemma 4.4.6
.

For each
Q\in\mathcal{D}
there exists
I_{Q}\in\Delta
such that
\ell(I_{Q})\sim_{L}\ell(Q)
and
3B_{Q}\subseteq g(3I_{Q}).
For each
I\in\Delta
, we have

(4.4.6)

\displaystyle|\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I_{Q}=I\}|\lesssim_{L,n}1.

Proof.

Let
k_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
2^{k_{0}-1}\leq L<2^{k_{0}}.
For
Q\in\mathcal{D}
let
k(Q)\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
2^{k(Q)-1}\leq\ell(Q)<2^{k(Q)}.
Let
I_{Q}
be the unique cube such that
g^{-1}(x_{Q})\in I_{Q}
and
\ell(I_{Q})=2^{k_{0}+k(Q)}.
Clearly
\ell(I_{Q})\sim_{L}\ell(Q).
Let
x\in 3B_{Q}
and let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
x=g(y).
Then, since
g
is
L
-bi-Lipschitz,

(4.4.7)

\displaystyle|y-x_{I_{Q}}|

\displaystyle\leq|g^{-1}(x)-g^{-1}(x_{Q})|+|g^{-1}(x_{Q})-x_{I_{Q}}|

(4.4.8)

\displaystyle\leq 3L\ell(Q)<3\cdot 2^{k^{0}+k(Q)}=3\ell(I_{Q}).

It is easy to check
B(x_{I_{Q}},3\ell(I_{Q}))\subseteq 3I_{Q}
so that
y\in 3I_{Q}
and
3B_{Q}\subseteq 3I_{Q}.
We finish the proof by checking (
4.4.6
).

Let
I\in\Delta
and suppose
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
are such that
I_{Q}=I_{Q^{\prime}}=I
. Then,
\ell(Q)=\ell(Q^{\prime})=5\varrho^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\{c_{0}B_{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}\}
are disjoint this implies
d(x_{Q},x_{Q^{\prime}})\gtrsim\ell(Q).
Since
g(x_{Q})\in I,
we have
|g(x_{Q})-x_{I}|\lesssim_{n}\ell(I)\lesssim_{L}\varrho^{k}.
Thus,
d(x_{Q},g^{-1}(x_{I}))\lesssim_{n,L}\varrho^{k}.
Similarly,
d(x_{Q^{\prime}},g^{-1}(x_{I}))\lesssim_{n,L}\varrho^{k}
. Thus,
d(x_{Q},x_{Q^{\prime}})\lesssim_{n,L}\varrho^{k}.
We can now apply Lemma
4.2.5
to conclude (
4.4.6
).
∎

Lemma 4.4.7
.

Suppose
\|\cdot\|_{0}
is a norm and
f\colon\Sigma\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
is a map. Set
h\coloneqq f\circ g.
Then, for
Q\in\mathcal{D}
and
K\geq 1,

\gamma_{\Sigma,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(3Q)\lesssim_{K,L}\Omega_{\mathbb{R}^{n},h,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{\frac{K}{2L}}(x_{I_{Q}},3\ell(I_{Q}),|\cdot|,\emph{Id})+\emph{md}_{g}^{2L}(3I_{Q}),

with
I_{Q}
as in Lemma
4.4.6
.

Proof.

Let
\delta>0
and suppose
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}_{2L}
is a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

(4.4.9)

\displaystyle\sup_{x,y\in 3I_{Q}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d(g(x),g(y))-\|x-y\|\right\rvert}\leq\ell(3I_{Q})(\text{md}_{g}^{2L}(3I_{Q})+\delta).

Let
y_{Q}=g^{-1}(x_{Q})
and set
\varphi=p_{y_{Q},3\ell(Q)}\circ g^{-1},
where
p_{z,\lambda}
is the map from Lemma
2.3.3
. Then
\varphi
maps
\Sigma\cap 3B_{Q}
to
B_{\|\cdot\|}(g^{-1}(x_{Q}),3\ell(Q))
and

(4.4.10)

\displaystyle\|\varphi(x)-g^{-1}(x)\|\leq\max\{0,\|g^{-1}(x)-g^{-1}(x_{Q})\|-3\ell(Q)\}.

We begin by estimating
\zeta_{\Sigma}(3Q,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)
. If
x\in\Sigma\cap 3B_{Q}\subseteq g(3I_{Q})
, then (
4.4.9
) and (
4.4.10
) imply

(4.4.11)

\displaystyle\|\varphi(x)-g^{-1}(x)\|

\displaystyle\leq\max\{0,\|g^{-1}(x)-g^{-1}(x_{Q})\|-3\ell(Q)\}

(4.4.12)

\displaystyle\leq\max\{0,d(x,y)+\ell(3I_{Q})({\mathop{\mathrm{}}{md}}^{2L}_{g}(3I_{Q})+\delta)-3\ell(Q)\}

(4.4.13)

\displaystyle\leq\ell(3I_{Q})({\mathop{\mathrm{}}{md}}^{2L}_{g}(3I_{Q})+\delta).

By (
4.4.9
) and (
4.4.11
), if
x,y\in\Sigma\cap 3B_{Q}
then

(4.4.14)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d(x,y)-\|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\|\right\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq\big|d(x,y)-\|g^{-1}(x)-g^{-1}(y)\|\big|+\|\varphi(x)-g^{-1}(x)\|

(4.4.15)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|\varphi(y)-g^{-1}(y)\|

(4.4.16)

\displaystyle\leq 3\ell(3I_{Q})({\mathop{\mathrm{}}{md}}^{2L}_{g}(3I_{Q})+\delta).

Taking the supremum over all
x,y\in\Sigma\cap 3B_{Q}
and recalling that
\ell(I_{Q})\sim\ell(Q),
we have

(4.4.17)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\Sigma}(3Q,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)\lesssim_{L}{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{md}}^{2L}_{g}(3I_{Q})+\delta.

Now for
\eta_{\Sigma}(3Q,\|\cdot\|,\varphi).
Let
u\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(y_{Q},3\ell(Q))
,
\lambda=\ell(3I_{Q})({\mathop{\mathrm{}}{md}}^{2L}_{g}(3I_{Q})+\delta)
and choose a point
\tilde{u}\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(y_{Q},3\ell(Q)-2\lambda)
such that
\|u-\tilde{u}\|\leq 2\lambda.
Such a point can be found by Lemma
2.3.3
. By (
4.4.9
) we have
x=g(\tilde{u})\in\Sigma\cap 3B_{Q}
since

d(x_{Q},x)=d(g(y_{Q}),g(\tilde{u}))\leq\|y_{Q}-\tilde{u}\|+\lambda\leq 3\ell(Q)-\lambda.

This, along with (
4.4.11
), gives

(4.4.18)

\displaystyle{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\|\cdot\|}(u,\varphi(\Sigma\cap 3B_{Q}))

\displaystyle\leq{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\|\cdot\|}(u,\varphi(x))\leq\|u-g^{-1}(x)\|+\|g^{-1}(x)-\varphi(x)\|

(4.4.19)

\displaystyle=\|u-\tilde{u}\|+\|g^{-1}(x)-\varphi(x)\|\leq 3\lambda.

Since
u\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(y_{Q},3\ell(Q))
was arbitrary, this implies

(4.4.20)

\displaystyle\eta_{\Sigma}(3Q,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)\lesssim_{L}{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{md}}^{2L}_{g}(3I_{Q})+\delta.

It remains to estimate
\Omega_{\Sigma,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(3Q,\|\cdot\|,\varphi).
Recall that
h=f\circ g.
Let
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
be an affine function such that
\text{Lip}(A)\leq\tfrac{K}{2L}
and

\sup_{y\in 3I_{Q}}\|h(y)-A(y)\|_{0}\leq\ell(3I_{Q})[\Omega_{\mathbb{R}^{n},h,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{\frac{K}{2L}}(x_{I_{Q}},3\ell(I_{Q}),|\cdot|,\text{Id})+\delta].

Since
\|\cdot\|\in\mathcal{N}_{2L},
we have
\text{Lip}(A)\leq K
when viewed as a function from
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)
. Let
x\in 3B_{Q}
and set
y=g^{-1}(x)\in 3I_{Q}.
By the definition of
A
and (
4.4.11
) we have

(4.4.21)

\displaystyle\|f(x)-A(\varphi(x))\|_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\|f(x)-A(g^{-1}(x))\|_{0}+\|A(g^{-1}(x))-A(\varphi(x))\|_{0}

(4.4.22)

\displaystyle\leq\|h(y)-A(y)\|_{0}+K\|g^{-1}(y)-\varphi(y)\|

(4.4.23)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{K}\ell(3I_{Q})[\Omega_{\mathbb{R}^{n},h,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{\frac{K}{2L}}(x_{I_{Q}},3\ell(I_{Q}),|\cdot|,\text{Id})+\text{md}^{2L}_{g}(3I_{Q})+2\delta].

Taking the supremum over all
x\in 3B_{Q}
and recalling that
\ell(I_{Q})\sim_{L}\ell(Q)
we have

(4.4.24)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Sigma,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(3Q,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)\lesssim\Omega_{\mathbb{R}^{n},h,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{\frac{K}{2L}}(x_{I_{Q}},3\ell(I_{Q}),|\cdot|,\text{Id})+{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{md}}^{2L}_{g}(3I_{Q})+2\delta.

Combining this with (
4.4.17
) and (
4.4.20
) then taking
\delta\to 0
finishes the proof of the lemma.

∎

Lemma 4.4.8
.

There exists
K=K(L,n)\geq 1
and for each
\varepsilon>0
a constant
C=C(\varepsilon)
such that if
\|\cdot\|_{0}
is a norm and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Sigma\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0})
is 1-Lipschitz then
\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(3Q)>\varepsilon\}
satisfies a Carleson packing condition with Carleson norm
C.

Proof.

Let
h=f\circ g
so that
h
is
L
-Lipschitz from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
from
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{0}).
Let
\tilde{K}=\tilde{K}(L,n)
be the constant from Theorem
4.4.2
and set
K=2\tilde{K}L.
By Lemma
4.4.7
there exists a constant
C=C(K,L)\geq 1
such that

(4.4.25)

\displaystyle\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma_{\Sigma,f}^{K}(3Q)>\varepsilon\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Omega_{\mathbb{R}^{n},h,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{\tilde{K}}(x_{I_{Q}},3\ell(I_{Q}),|\cdot|,\text{Id})>\varepsilon/C\}

(4.4.26)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\cup\{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{md}_{g}^{2L}(3I_{Q})>\varepsilon/C\}.

Then, by Theorem
4.4.2
, Corollary
4.4.5
and Lemma
4.4.6
, we have

(4.4.27)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\{\ell(Q)^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma_{\Sigma,f,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{K}(3Q)>\varepsilon\}

(4.4.28)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\lesssim_{L,n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\Delta\\
I\subseteq I_{Q_{0}}\end{subarray}}\{\ell(I)^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Omega_{\mathbb{R}^{n},h,\|\cdot\|_{0}}^{\tilde{K}}(x_{I},3\ell(I),|\cdot|,\text{Id})>\varepsilon/C\}

(4.4.29)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\Delta\\
I\subseteq I_{Q_{0}}\end{subarray}}\{\ell(I)^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\text{md}^{2L}_{g}(3I)>\varepsilon/C\}\lesssim_{L,\varepsilon,n}\ell(I_{Q_{0}})^{n}\lesssim_{L,n}\ell(Q_{0})^{n}.

∎

We conclude the proof of Proposition
4.4.1
(hence, the proof of Theorem
4.1.1
) by combining Lemma
4.2.4
with Lemma
4.4.8
.

5.
Metric Reifenberg Flat (RF) parametrizations with varying tangents

Contents of this section

5.1.
Main result

In this section we prove our generalized Reifenberg theorem. The argument is inspired by the scheme of
[
CC97
]
and its presentation in
[
Vio21
]
. The following is our main result.

Theorem 5.1.1
.

Let
(X,d)
a metric space,
x_{0}\in X
and
\delta,\varepsilon>0
. For each
\ell\geq 0
, let
r_{\ell}=10^{-\ell},

\{x_{j,\ell}\}_{j\in J_{\ell}}
a collection of points in
B_{X}(x_{0},1)
and
\{\|\cdot\|_{j,\ell}\}_{j\in J_{\ell}}
a collection of norms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
For
j\in J_{\ell}
let

B^{j,\ell}=B_{X}(x_{j,\ell},r_{\ell})\mbox{ and }B_{j,\ell}=B_{\|\cdot\|_{j,\ell}}(0,r_{\ell}).

Suppose each of the following conditions is satisfied.

(1)

At the top scale
J_{0}=\{j_{0}\}
and
x_{j_{0},0}=x_{0}.

(2)

For each
\ell\geq 0
and
i,j\in J_{\ell}
with
i\neq j,
we have

(5.1.1)

\displaystyle d_{X}(x_{i,\ell},x_{j,\ell})\geq r_{\ell}.

(3)

For each
\ell\geq 1
and
j\in J_{\ell}
there exists
i\in J_{\ell-1}
such that

(5.1.2)

\displaystyle x_{j,\ell}\in 2B^{i,\ell-1}.

(4)

For each
\ell\geq 0
and
j\in J_{\ell}
there exist maps
\alpha_{j,\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}100B_{j,\ell}\to 100B^{j,\ell}
and
\beta_{j,\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}100B^{j,\ell}\to 100B_{j,\ell}
each of which is a
\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA and such that

(5.1.3)

\displaystyle\alpha_{j,\ell}(0)=x_{j,\ell}\mbox{ and }\beta_{j,\ell}(x_{j,\ell})=0;

(5.1.4)

\displaystyle\|\beta_{j,\ell}\circ\alpha_{j,\ell}-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\delta r_{\ell}\mbox{ and }\|\alpha_{j,\ell}\circ\beta_{j,\ell}-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\delta r_{\ell}.

If
\delta
is chosen small enough, depending on
\varepsilon
and
n
, there exists a metric
\rho
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
which is

(5.1.5)

\displaystyle\mbox{bi-H\"{o}lder equivalent to $\|\cdot\|_{j_{0},0}$ with exponent $1-\varepsilon$},

as in (
2.1.4
), such that

(5.1.6)

(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
is
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat

and

(5.1.7)

\displaystyle|\rho(x,y)-\|x-y\|_{j_{0},0}|\leq\varepsilon\mbox{ for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.$ }

Furthermore, there are maps
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}6B_{j_{0},0}\to 6B^{j_{0},0}
and
\bar{g}\colon 6B^{j_{0},0}\to 6B_{j_{0},0}
satisfying the following conditions. Suppose
\ell\geq 0
,
j\in J_{\ell}
and let
x,y\in 6B^{j,\ell}
and
w,z\in 6B_{j_{0},\ell}
such that
g(w),g(z)\in 6B^{j,\ell}.
Then,

(5.1.8)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g(\bar{g}(x)),x)\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell},

(5.1.9)

\displaystyle\rho(\bar{g}(g(w)),w)\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell},

(5.1.10)

\displaystyle|d_{X}(g(w),g(z))-\rho(w,z)|\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell},

(5.1.11)

\displaystyle|\rho(\bar{g}(x),\bar{g}(y))-d_{X}(x,y)|\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Additionally,

(5.1.12)

if
X
is Ahlfors
n
-regular, then
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
is Ahlfors
n
-regular.

In (
5.1.12
), the regularity constant depends on
n
and the regularity constant for
X
.

After some preparation in Sections
5.2
-
5.5
, the proof of Theorem
5.1.1
begins in Section
5.6
and concludes in Section
5.9
.

Remark 5.1.2
.

Conditions (1)-(3) impose a sort of tree like structure on our collection of balls
B^{j,\ell},
with
B^{j_{0},0}
forming the root of the tree. These conditions appear naturally in stopping-time arguments, as we shall see in Section
7
. Condition (4) is really saying that
\xi_{X}(100B^{j,\ell})\leq\delta/100
(recall the definition from Definition
3.1.3
). We state this condition with two mapping because it will be useful for us to have a GHA defined on the whole of the ball in
X
and a GHA defined on the whole of the tangent ball. Equation (
5.1.4
) is just saying that the
\alpha
is roughly the inverse of
\beta
and vice-versa. It is easy to construct the maps
\alpha
and
\beta
satisfying these conditions if one knows that
\xi_{X}(100B^{j,\ell})\leq\delta/100
for each ball. Thus, one should interpret the hypotheses of Theorem
5.1.1
as saying that
X
is
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat in a tree of balls (outside of this tree we assume nothing).

With this structure, we construct a metric
\rho
which is
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat and bi-Hölder equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{j_{0},0}
. Moreover, (
5.1.7
) says that
\rho
is only a small perturbation of
\|\cdot\|_{j_{0},0}
relative to the top scale. The maps
g
and
\bar{g}
act as a kind of multi-scale Gromov-Hausdorff approximation between
X
and
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
. In particular, the existence of these maps tells us that balls in the tree are bi-laterally well-approximated by
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho).

Remark 5.1.3
.

Combining (
5.1.7
) - (
5.1.11
) we also have

(5.1.13)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g(0),x)\leq 10\varepsilon\mbox{ and }\rho(\bar{g}(x),0)\leq 10\varepsilon.

Indeed, suppose towards a contradiction that
\rho(\bar{g}(x),0)\geq 5\varepsilon.
By (
5.1.7
) this implies
\|\bar{g}(x)\|_{j_{0},0}\geq 4\varepsilon.
Let
z=-6\bar{g}(x)/\|\bar{g}(x)\|_{j_{0},0}
so that
\|\bar{g}(x)-z\|\geq 6+4\varepsilon.
Combining (
5.1.7
), (
5.1.8
) and (
5.1.10
), we now have
\rho(\bar{g}(x),z)\geq 6+3\varepsilon
and

(5.1.14)

\displaystyle d_{X}(x,g(z))\geq d_{X}(g(\bar{g}(x)),g(z))-\varepsilon\geq\rho(\bar{g}(x),z)-2\varepsilon\geq 6+\varepsilon.

Since
g
takes values in
6B^{j_{0},0},
this is a contradiction and proves the second inequality in (
5.1.13
) with the slightly better constant
5\varepsilon.
To prove the first inequality in (
5.1.13
), we combine this improved estimate with (
5.1.9
) and (
5.1.11
) to get

(5.1.15)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g(0),x)\leq\rho(\bar{g}(g(0)),\bar{g}(x))+\varepsilon\leq\rho(\bar{g}(g(0)),0)+\rho(\bar{g}(x),0)+\varepsilon\leq 10\varepsilon.

Remark 5.1.4
.

For our application it is desirable that
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
is linearly locally contractible (see Definition
6.3.1
). It is possible to show this directly via the construction however we chose to prove more generally that all
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat sets are linearly locally contractible for
\delta
small enough (see Proposition
6.3.4
). We actually use Theorem
5.1.1
to prove this fact.

We prove the conclusions of Theorem
5.1.1
with
\varepsilon
replaced by
C\varepsilon
for some
C
depending only on
n
, which is sufficient. The conclusions of Theorem
5.1.1
are stronger as
\varepsilon
becomes smaller, so we restrict our attention to
\varepsilon
sufficiently small. Let

0<\varepsilon\leq\delta<1/100.

Throughout the remainder of this section we let
(X,d)
be a fixed metric space,
\{x_{j,\ell}\}_{\ell\geq 0,j\in J_{\ell}}
be a collection of points in
B_{X}(0,1)
,
\{\|\cdot\|_{j,\ell}\}_{\ell\geq 0,j\in J_{\ell}}
be a collection of norms on
\mathbb{R}^{n},
and we assume conditions (1)-(4) of Theorem
5.1.1
hold with parameter
\delta.

5.2.
Constructing smooth transition maps

One can think of
\beta_{j,\ell}\colon 100B^{j,\ell}\to 100B_{j,\ell}
as a rough coordinate patch in
X
. If
\beta_{i,\ell},\beta_{j,\ell}
are nearby coordinate patches (in the sense that
15B^{i,\ell}\cap 15B^{j,\ell}\neq\emptyset
), their ‘transition map’ is given by
\beta_{j,\ell}\circ\alpha_{i,\ell}
(Lemma
5.2.2
). As mentioned in Section
1.7
, we prove Theorem
5.1.1
by first constructing a sequence of approximating smooth manifolds for
X
. Keeping this in mind, Proposition
5.2.1
below produces, for each nearby pair
i,j\in J_{\ell}
, a smooth perturbation
\tilde{I}_{i,j,\ell}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j,\ell}\to\mathbb{R}_{i,\ell}^{n}
of the map
\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}
satisfying a necessary cyclic condition (see Remark
2.5.2
). A suitable restriction of these maps will be the transition maps for the manifolds we construct in Section
5.3
.

It may be useful to recall from (
2.2.1
) that
\|\cdot\|_{C^{m}(U),r}
denotes the uniform norm of the first
m
Fréchet derivatives.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

There exists constants
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(n)
and
\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)
such that the following holds. Suppose
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}
and
0<\delta<\delta_{1}
. For
\ell\geq 0
, let

(5.2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\ell}=\{(i,j)\in J_{\ell}\times J_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}15B^{i,\ell}\cap 15B^{j,\ell}\neq\emptyset\}.

Then, for each
(i,j)\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
there exists a smooth diffeomorphism
\tilde{I}_{i,j,\ell}\colon\mathbb{R}_{j,\ell}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{i,\ell}^{n}
and an affine map
T_{i,j,\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j,\ell}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{i,\ell}
satisfying the following conditions. First, both
\tilde{I}_{i,j,\ell}
and
T_{i,j,\ell}
are
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz and

(5.2.2)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{I}_{i,j,\ell}-T_{i,j,\ell}\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j,\ell}),r_{\ell}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

Second, we have

(5.2.3)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{I}_{i,j,\ell}(x)-\beta_{i,\ell}\circ\alpha_{j,\ell}(x)\|_{i,\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}

for all
x\in 45B_{j,\ell}.
Finally, for any triple
(i,j),(j,k),(i,k)\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell},
the maps
\tilde{I}_{i,j,\ell},\tilde{I}_{j,k,\ell}
and
\tilde{I}_{i,k,\ell}
are
9r_{\ell}
-cyclic in the sense of Definition
2.5.1
.

Fix
\ell\geq 0
for the remainder of this subsection. When the context is clear, we will write
J=J_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
. For
j\in J
, we will also write

x_{j}=x_{j,\ell},\ \|\cdot\|_{j}=\|\cdot\|_{j,\ell},\ B^{j}=B^{j,\ell},\ B_{j}=B_{j,\ell}\mbox{ and }\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}=(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{j}).

Lemma 5.2.2
.

Let
(i,j)\in\mathcal{F}
. Then,
\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}
is well-defined on
45B_{i}
and

(5.2.4)

\displaystyle\|\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}(x)-x\|_{i}\lesssim\delta r_{\ell}

for all
x\in 45B_{i}.

Proof.

Let
x\in 45B_{i}.
First, by (
2.3.12
) we have
d(\alpha_{i}(x),x_{i})\leq 45r_{\ell}+11\delta r_{\ell}\leq 46r_{\ell}
(recalling that
\delta<1/100
). In particular,
\alpha_{i}(x)\in 46B^{i}\subseteq\text{dom}(\beta_{j})
. It now follows from (
5.1.4
) that
\alpha_{j}(\beta_{j}(\alpha_{i}(x)))\in 47B^{i}\subseteq\text{dom}(\beta_{i})
. Thus,
\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}
is well-defined. Then, using (
5.1.4
) with the fact that
\alpha_{j},\beta_{i}
are
\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA, we get

(5.2.5)

\displaystyle\|\beta_{i}(\alpha_{j}(\beta_{j}(\alpha_{i}(x))))-x\|_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\|\beta_{i}(\alpha_{j}(\beta_{j}(\alpha_{i}(x))))-\beta_{i}(\alpha_{i}(x))\|_{i}+\|\beta_{i}(\alpha_{i}(x))-x\|_{i}

(5.2.6)

\displaystyle\leq\|\alpha_{j}(\beta_{j}(\alpha_{i}(x)))-\alpha_{i}(x)\|_{i}+\delta r_{\ell}\lesssim\delta r_{\ell}.

∎

Lemma 5.2.3
.

There exists
\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}(\varepsilon)
such that the following holds. Suppose
0<\delta\leq\delta_{1}.
Then, for each
(i,j)\in\mathcal{F}
there exists an affine
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz map
T_{i,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}
such that

(5.2.7)

\displaystyle\|T_{i,j}(x)-\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}(x)\|_{i}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}

for all
x\in 45B_{j}
. Additionally, if we let
T_{j,i}=T_{i,j}^{-1},
then

(5.2.8)

\displaystyle\|T_{j,i}(x)-\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}(x)\|_{j}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}

for all
x\in 45B_{i}.

Proof.

Let
(i,j)\in\mathcal{F}.
We start by constructing
T_{i,j}.
Using the final part of Lemma
2.3.6
, there exists a
C\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA
\phi_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}80B_{j}\to 80B^{j}
such that
d(\alpha_{j}(x),\phi_{j}(x))\leq C\delta r_{\ell}
for all
x\in 80B_{j}
(recall that
\alpha_{j}(0)=x_{j}
by (
5.1.3
)). Letting
z=\beta_{i}(x_{j})
and applying Lemma
2.3.6
again gives a
C\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA
\phi_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}80B^{j}\to B_{\|\cdot\|_{i}}(z,80r_{\ell})
such that
\|\beta_{i}(x)-\phi_{i}(x)\|_{i}\leq C\delta r_{\ell}
for all
x\in 80B^{j}.
By Lemma
2.3.8
,

\phi_{i,j}\coloneqq\phi_{i}\circ\phi_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}80B_{j}\to B_{\|\cdot\|_{i}}(z,80r_{\ell})

defines a
C\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA. By Lemma
2.3.14
, as long as
\delta
is chosen small enough depending on
\varepsilon,
there exists an affine
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz map
T_{i,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{i}
such that

\|T_{i,j}(x)-\phi_{i,j}(x)\|_{i}\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell}

for all
x\in 80B_{j}.

We now consider (
5.2.7
), which we prove for a slightly larger ball. Suppose
x\in 79B_{j}.
By (
2.3.12
),
\alpha_{j}(x)\in 80B^{j}
and by construction
\phi_{j}(x)\in 80B^{j}.
Taking
\delta
small enough depending on
\varepsilon
, and using the relation between
\alpha_{j},\phi_{j}
and
\beta_{i},\phi_{i}
on
80B^{j},
we have

(5.2.9)

\displaystyle\|T_{i,j}(x)-\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}(x)\|_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\|T_{i,j}(x)-\phi_{i,j}(x)\|_{i}+\|\phi_{i}\circ\phi_{j}(x)-\beta_{i}\circ\phi_{j}(x)\|_{i}

(5.2.10)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|\beta_{i}\circ\phi_{j}(x)-\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}(x)\|_{i}

(5.2.11)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell}+C\delta r_{\ell}+d(\phi_{j}(x),\alpha_{j}(x))+C\delta r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

We move on to (
5.2.8
). Suppose
x\in 45B_{i}.
Using (
2.3.12
) again, we have
\alpha_{i}(x)\in 46B^{i}\subseteq 76B^{j}
, where the final inclusion follows from the triangle inequality since
15B^{i}\cap 15B^{j}\neq\emptyset
. Another application of (
2.3.12
) then gives
\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}(x)\in 77B^{j}\subseteq 79B^{j}.
Finally, by Lemma
5.2.2
, (
5.2.7
) and since
T_{j,i}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, we get

(5.2.12)

\displaystyle\|T_{j,i}(x)-\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}(x)\|_{j}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|x-T_{i,j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}(x)\|_{i}

(5.2.13)

\displaystyle\leq\|x-\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}(x)\|_{i}

(5.2.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}(x)-T_{i,j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}(x)\|_{i}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

∎

Remark 5.2.4
.

For the remainder of this subsection we will assume
\delta<\delta_{1}
so that the maps
T_{i,j}
are well defined.

Lemma 5.2.5
.

Let
(i,j),(j,k),(i,k)\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mathrm{Id}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the identity map. Then,

(5.2.15)

\displaystyle\|T_{k,i}\circ T_{i,j}\circ T_{j,k}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{C^{2}(12B_{k}),r_{\ell}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
H\coloneqq T_{k,i}\circ T_{i,j}\circ T_{j,k}
and suppose
x\in 12B_{k}.
Since
T_{k,i}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, the same is true of its linear part,
L_{k,i}.
Hence,

(5.2.16)

\displaystyle\|T_{k,i}\circ T_{i,j}\circ T_{j,k}(x)-x\|_{k}

\displaystyle=\|L_{k,i}\left(T_{i,j}\circ T_{j,k}(x)-T_{i,k}(x)\right)\|_{k}

(5.2.17)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|T_{i,j}\circ T_{j,k}(x)-T_{i,k}(x)\|_{i}.

Applying (
2.3.12
) twice we have
\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{k}(x)\in 45B_{j}
so we can apply Lemma
5.2.3
to the map
T_{i,j}
at this point. Using also that
T_{i,j}
is bi-Lipschitz, and applying Lemma
5.2.3
to the maps
T_{j,k}
and
T_{i,k}
at
x
, we get

(5.2.18)

\displaystyle\|T_{i,j}\circ T_{j,k}(x)-T_{i,k}(x)\|_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\|T_{i,j}(T_{j,k}(x))-T_{i,j}(\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{k}(x))\|_{i}

(5.2.19)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|T_{i,j}(\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{k}(x))-\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{k}(x)\|_{i}

(5.2.20)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\|\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{k}(x)-T_{i,k}(x)\|_{i}

(5.2.21)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{k}(x)-\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{k}(x)\|_{i}+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Now, using (
5.1.4
), the fact that
\beta_{i}
is a
\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA and
\delta\leq\varepsilon
, we have

(5.2.22)

\displaystyle\|\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{k}(x)-\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{k}(x)\|_{i}

\displaystyle\leq d(\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{k}(x),\alpha_{k}(x))+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}

(5.2.23)

\displaystyle\leq C\delta r_{\ell}+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Combining the above three estimates gives

(5.2.24)

\displaystyle\|H(x)-x\|_{k}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Since
H
is affine, this and Lemma
2.4.5
now imply

(5.2.25)

\displaystyle\|DH(x)\cdot y-y\|_{k}\lesssim\varepsilon\|y\|

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
The estimates on the second derivatives are trivial since
H
is affine and
\mathrm{Id}
is linear so that
D^{2}H
and
D^{2}\mathrm{Id}
are zero. This observation together with (
5.2.24
) and (
5.2.25
) completes the proof.
∎

If
0<\lambda<12
and
\varepsilon\ll\lambda
then Lemma
5.2.5
says the maps
T_{i,j}
are almost
\lambda r_{\ell}
-cyclic in the sense of Definition
2.5.1
. Suppose there exist maps
I_{i,j}
which well approximate the
T_{i,j}
(so that the
I_{i,j}
are also almost
\lambda r_{\ell}
-cyclic), the following lemma finds smooth perturbations
\hat{I}_{i,j}
of the
I_{i,j}
which are exactly
\lambda^{\prime}r_{\ell}
-cyclic for some parameter
\lambda^{\prime}
which is slightly smaller than
\lambda
. The
\hat{I}_{i,j}
still well approximate the
T_{i,j}
so it is possible to apply the lemma again. We will prove Proposition
5.2.1
by carefully iterating this lemma.

Lemma 5.2.6
.

The exists a constant
C_{1}\geq 1
depending only on
n
and for each
C_{2}\geq 1
and
N\geq 1
a constant
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(C_{2},N)>0
such that the following holds. Suppose
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, let
1/N<\lambda\leq 10,

(i,j),(j,k),(i,k)\in\mathcal{F}
and suppose for each distinct pair
a,b\in\{i,j,k\}
that there is a smooth diffeomorphism
I_{a,b}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{b}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{a}
such that
I_{a,b}^{-1}=I_{b,a}
and

(5.2.26)

\displaystyle\|I_{a,b}-T_{a,b}\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{b}),r_{\ell}}\leq C_{2}\varepsilon.

Then there exists a smooth diffeomorphism
\hat{I}_{k,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
such that

(5.2.27)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{I}_{k,j}=I_{k,j}\mbox{ outside }I_{j,i}(\lambda B_{i});

(5.2.28)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{I}_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(x))=I_{k,i}(x)\mbox{ for all }x\in\lambda B_{i}\mbox{ such that }I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(x))=I_{k,i}(x);

(5.2.29)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{I}_{k,j},I_{j,i},I_{k,i}\mbox{ are }(\lambda-\tfrac{1}{N})r_{\ell}\mbox{-cyclic}.

Furthermore, letting
\hat{I}_{j,k}=\hat{I}^{-1}_{k,j},
we have

(5.2.30)

\displaystyle\|I_{j,k}-\hat{I}_{j,k}\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}),r_{\ell}}\leq C_{1}C_{2}\varepsilon\mbox{ and }\|I_{k,j}-\hat{I}_{k,j}\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}),r_{\ell}}\leq C_{1}C_{2}\varepsilon.

Proof.

Let
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda-\tfrac{1}{2N}
,
\lambda^{\prime\prime}=\lambda-\tfrac{1}{N}
and set

(5.2.31)

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle=I_{k,j}\circ I_{j,i}\left(B_{i}(0,\lambda^{\prime}r_{\ell})\right)\cap B_{k}(0,\lambda^{\prime}r_{\ell});

(5.2.32)

\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle=I_{k,j}\circ I_{j,i}\left(B_{i}(0,\lambda r_{\ell})\right)\cap B_{k}(0,\lambda r_{\ell}).

Suppose
(a,b)=(i,j)
or
(a,b)=(j,i).
Since
U_{2}\subseteq 12B_{k}
, we can combine Lemma
2.4.3
with Lemma
5.2.5
and (
5.2.26
) to get,

(5.2.33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|I_{k,a}\circ I_{a,b}\circ I_{b,k}-\mbox{Id}\|_{C^{2}(U_{2}),r_{\ell}}&\leq\|I_{k,a}\circ I_{a,b}\circ I_{b,k}-T_{k,a}\circ T_{a,b}\circ T_{b,k}\|_{C^{2}(U_{2}),r_{\ell}}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\|T_{k,a}\circ T_{a,b}\circ T_{b,k}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{C^{2}(U_{2}),r_{\ell}}\\
&\lesssim C_{2}\varepsilon.\end{split}

If
U_{1}=\emptyset
, it turns out the functions
I_{k,j},I_{j,i}
and
I_{k,i}
are already
\lambda^{\prime\prime}r_{\ell}
-cyclic and we can just take
\hat{I}_{a,b}=I_{a,b}
for each distinct
a,b\in\{i,j,k\}
. To see that the maps are
\lambda^{\prime\prime}r_{\ell}
-cyclic it suffices to check, by Remark
2.5.3
, that (
2.5.2
) holds for the permutations
(i,j,k),(k,i,j)
and
(i,k,j).
We will show (
2.5.2
) is vacuous in each case i.e. there are no points satisfying the hypotheses of (
2.5.2
). For
(i,j,k)
we notice immediately, using the definition of
U_{1}
in (
5.2.31
) and the fact that
\lambda^{\prime\prime}<\lambda^{\prime}
, that there is no
x\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{i}
such that
I_{j,i}(x)\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{j}
and
I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(x))\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{k}.
For
(k,i,j),
suppose there exists
x\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{k}
such that
y=I_{i,k}(x)\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{i}
and
z=I_{j,i}(y)\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{j}.
Since
x\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{k},
(
5.2.33
) implies
I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(y))=I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(I_{i,k}(x)))\in\lambda^{\prime}B_{k}
as long as
\varepsilon
is small enough depending on
C_{2}.
This gives
U_{1}\neq\emptyset
which is a contradiction. Finally, for
(i,k,j),
suppose there exists
x\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{i}
such that
w=I_{k,i}(x)\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{k}
and
I_{j,k}(I_{k,i}(x))\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{j}.
Since
w\in\lambda^{\prime\prime}B_{k}
, (
5.2.33
) implies
I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(x))=I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(I_{i,k}(w)))\in\lambda^{\prime}B_{k}
so that again
U_{1}\neq\emptyset,
a contradiction.

Suppose instead that
U_{1}\neq\emptyset.
We’d like to apply Lemma
2.4.2
to the map
H=I_{k,i}\circ I_{i,j}\circ I_{j,k}
and the sets
U_{1}
and
U_{2}.
Equation (
2.4.12
) follows immediately from (
5.2.33
). We will now verify that

(5.2.34)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(U_{1},U_{2}^{c})\geq\frac{r_{\ell}}{10N}.

Let
w\in U_{1}
and
z\in U_{2}^{c}.
If
z\not\in B_{k}(0,\lambda r_{\ell})
then
\|w-z\|_{k}\geq r_{\ell}/2N\geq r_{\ell}/10N
since
U_{1}\subseteq B_{k}(0,\lambda^{\prime}r_{\ell})
. Suppose instead that
z\in B_{k}(0,\lambda r_{\ell}).
Let
x\in B_{i}(0,\lambda^{\prime}r_{\ell})
such that
w=I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(x))
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
z=I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(y)).
It must be that
y\notin B_{i}(0,\lambda r_{\ell})
, otherwise
z\in U_{2}
by the definition (
5.2.32
). Thus,
\|x-y\|_{i}\geq r_{\ell}/2N
. Applying (
5.2.26
), using that each
T_{ab}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, and choosing
\varepsilon
small enough depending on
C_{2}
and
N
, we have

(5.2.35)

\displaystyle\|w-z\|_{k}

\displaystyle=\|I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(x))-I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(y))\|_{k}

(5.2.36)

\displaystyle\geq\|T_{k,j}(T_{j,i}(x))-T_{k,j}(T_{j,i}(y))\|_{k}-CC_{2}\varepsilon r_{\ell}

(5.2.37)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\|x-y\|_{i}}{2}-CC_{2}\varepsilon r_{\ell}\geq\frac{r_{\ell}}{10N}.

In either case
\|w-z\|_{k}\geq r_{\ell}/10N
. Since
w,z
were arbitrary this completes the proof of (
5.2.34
)

Choosing again
\varepsilon
small enough depending on
C_{2}
and
N
, we can apply Lemma
2.4.2
. Let
\hat{H}
be the resulting modification of
H
satisfying (
2.4.13
)-(
2.4.16
). Define

\hat{I}_{k,j}=\hat{H}\circ I_{k,j}\mbox{ and }\hat{I}_{j,k}=\hat{I}_{k,j}^{-1}.

Let us check (
5.2.27
)-(
5.2.30
), beginning with (
5.2.27
). By definition of
U_{2}
(see (
5.2.32
)) if
x\in I_{j,i}(\lambda B_{i})^{c}
then
I_{k,j}(x)\in U_{2}^{c}.
Equation (
5.2.27
) now follows from (
2.4.13
) and the definition of
\hat{I}_{k,j}
above. For (
5.2.28
), suppose that
y\in\lambda B_{i}
is such that
I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(y))=I_{k,i}(y).
Applying
H
to both sides, and recalling that
I_{a,b}^{-1}=I_{b,a},
we get
H(I_{k,i}(y))=I_{k,i}(y)
so that
\hat{H}(I_{k,i}(y))=I_{k,i}(y)
by (
2.4.15
). This gives

(5.2.38)

\displaystyle I_{k,i}(y)=\hat{H}\circ I_{k,i}(y)=\hat{H}\circ I_{k,j}\circ I_{j,i}(y)=\hat{I}_{k,j}\circ I_{j,i}.

Equation (
5.2.30
) follows immediately from (
2.4.16
), Lemma
2.4.1
and Lemma
2.4.3
as long as
C_{1}
is chosen sufficiently large. For (
5.2.29
), let
\lambda^{\prime\prime\prime}=\lambda-\tfrac{3}{4N}
and let
z\in B_{i}(0,\lambda^{\prime\prime\prime}r_{\ell})
such that
I_{j,i}(z)\in B_{j}(0,\lambda^{\prime\prime\prime}r_{\ell})
and
\hat{I}_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(z))\in B_{k}(0,\lambda^{\prime\prime\prime}r_{\ell}).
By (
5.2.30
),
I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(z))\in B_{k}(0,\lambda^{\prime}r_{\ell})
so in fact
I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(z))\in U_{1}.
Then, (
2.4.14
) implies

(5.2.39)

\displaystyle\hat{I}_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(z))=\hat{H}(I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(z)))=H(I_{k,j}(I_{j,i}(z)))=I_{k,i}(z).

We now get (
5.2.29
) from Lemma
2.5.4
since
\lambda^{\prime\prime\prime}-\tfrac{1}{4N}=\lambda-\tfrac{1}{N}.

∎

Before iterating Lemma
5.2.6
, we need one more result and a little more notation.

Lemma 5.2.7
.

There exists
N_{0}=N_{0}(n)
and a partition of
J_{\ell}
into families
\{J^{m}_{\ell}\}_{m=1}^{N_{0}}
such that

(5.2.40)

\displaystyle d(x_{i},x_{j})\geq 100r_{\ell}

for all distinct
i,j\in J^{m}_{\ell}
. For
i\in J_{\ell},
let
m(i)
be such that
i\in J_{\ell}^{m(i)}.
If
(i,j),(i,k)\in\mathcal{F}
then
m(i)\neq m(j)
and
m(j)\neq m(k).

Proof.

We start by proving the following claim. Let
N\geq 1
and suppose we have defined sets
J_{\ell}^{1},\dots,J_{\ell}^{N}
satisfying (
5.2.40
) and

(5.2.41)

\displaystyle J_{\ell}\setminus\bigcup_{m=1}^{N}J_{\ell}^{m}\neq\emptyset.

Then, there is
N_{0}=N_{0}(n)
such that

(5.2.42)

\displaystyle N\leq N_{0}.

To prove the claim, pick some
j\in J_{\ell}\setminus\bigcup_{m=1}^{N}J_{\ell}^{m}.
By maximality, for each
1\leq m\leq N
there exists
j_{m}\in J_{\ell}^{m}
such that
d(x_{j},x_{j_{m}})\leq 100r_{\ell},
in particular,
x_{j_{m}}\in 100B^{j}.
Let
z_{j_{m}}=\beta_{j}(x_{j_{m}})\in 100B_{j}
. Since
\beta_{j}
is a
\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA, Theorem
5.1.1
(2) implies

\|z_{j_{m}}-z_{j_{m^{\prime}}}\|_{j}\geq r_{\ell}/2

for all
1\leq m,m^{\prime}\leq N.
A standard volume argument in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}
then implies (
5.2.42
).

We return now to the construction of the partition, which we define iteratively. First, let
J_{\ell}^{1}
be a maximal collection of indices in
J_{\ell}
such that (
5.2.40
) holds for all distinct
i,j\in J_{\ell}^{1}
. Let
N\geq 1
and suppose we have defined sets
J_{\ell}^{1},\dots,J_{\ell}^{N}
each satisfying (
5.2.40
). If (
5.2.41
) holds then we let
J_{\ell}^{N+1}
be a maximal collection of indices in
J_{\ell}\setminus\bigcup_{m=1}^{N}J_{\ell}^{m}
such that (
5.2.40
) holds for all distinct
i,j\in J_{\ell}^{N+1}
. Otherwise, we stop. Equation (
5.2.42
) guarantees the process terminates with the correct bound on the numbers of sets in our partition.
∎

Remark 5.2.8
.

We will write
J^{m}
for
J_{\ell}^{m}
when the context is clear.

Remark 5.2.9
.

Let

(5.2.43)

\displaystyle N_{1}\coloneqq{N_{0}\choose 3}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\varepsilon_{1}\coloneqq\sup\{\varepsilon_{0}(pC_{1}^{p},N_{0})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}p\in\mathbb{N},\ 1\leq p\leq N_{0}\}

with
N_{0}
as in Lemma
5.2.7
and
C_{1},\varepsilon_{0}
as in Lemma
5.2.6
. Tracking the dependencies, it follows that
N_{1}=N_{1}(n)
and
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(n).

Consider a finite sequence
\{D_{p}\}_{p=0}^{N_{1}}
