of 3-tuples defined as follows. First, let
D_{0}=(3,2,1).
Then, if
D_{p}=(a,b,c)
has been defined for some
p\geq 0,
let

(5.2.44)

\displaystyle D_{p+1}=\begin{cases}(a,b,c+1)&\mbox{ if }c<b-1;\\
(a,b+1,1)&\mbox{ if }c=b-1\mbox{ and }b<a-1;\\
(a+1,2,1)&\mbox{ if }c=b-1\mbox{ and }b=a-1.\end{cases}

Now we can iterate Lemma
5.2.6
.

Lemma 5.2.10
.

Suppose
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
. Then, for each
0\leq p\leq N_{1}
and
(i,j)\in\mathcal{F}
there are smooth diffeomorphisms
I_{i,j}^{p}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{i}
such that
(I_{i,j}^{p})^{-1}=I_{j,i}^{p}
and

(5.2.45)

\displaystyle\|I_{i,j}^{p}-T_{i,j}\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}),r_{\ell}}\leq pC_{1}^{p}\varepsilon.

Furthermore, suppose
(i,j),(j,k),(i,k)\in\mathcal{F}
are such that
m(i)<m(j)<m(k)
. If
(m(k),m(j),m(i))=D_{p^{\prime}}
for some
1\leq p^{\prime}\leq p<N_{1}
then
I_{i,j}^{p},I_{j,k}^{p},I_{i,k}^{p}
are
\lambda_{p}r_{\ell}
-cyclic, where
\lambda_{p}=10-\tfrac{p}{N_{1}}.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction. For each
(j,k)\in\mathcal{F}
let

I_{j,k}^{0}=T_{j,k}.

The case
p=0
is then immediate. For the inductive step, suppose that we have established the result for some
0\leq p<N_{1}.
Let

D_{p+1}=(a_{3},a_{2},a_{1}).

We will produce the maps
I_{j,k}^{p+1}
by modifying the maps
I_{j,k}^{p}.
However, we will only modify a map
I_{j,k}^{p}
if
m(j)=a_{2}
,
m(k)=a_{3}
and there exists
i\in J
with
m(i)=a_{1}
such that
(i,j),(i,k)\in\mathcal{F}.
Otherwise we simply set
I_{j,k}^{p+1}=I_{j,k}^{p}.
Let us describe how we perform the modification in this first case. Fix indices
i,j,k\in J
as above.
By (
5.2.45
) in the case
p
, the maps
I_{i,j}^{p},I_{j,k}^{p},I_{i,k}^{p}
satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
5.2.6
with
C_{2}=pC_{1}^{p}
and
\lambda=\lambda_{p}.
By assumption we have
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
which by Remark
5.2.9
implies
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}(C_{2},N_{1}).
We can now apply Lemma
5.2.6
with the constant
C_{2}
and
\lambda
as above to get maps
I_{j,k}^{p+1}
and
I_{k,j}^{p+1}
.

By construction, for each
(i,j)\in\mathcal{F}
we have
(I_{i,j}^{p+1})^{-1}=I_{j,i}^{p+1}
and, by (
5.2.30
), (
5.2.45
) and the triangle inequality,

(5.2.46)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|I_{i,j}^{p+1}-I^{p}_{i,j}\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}),r_{\ell}}&\leq pC_{1}^{p+1}\varepsilon;\\
\|I_{i,j}^{p+1}-T_{i,j}\|_{C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}),r_{\ell}}&\leq(p+1)C_{1}^{p+1}\varepsilon.\end{split}

That is, (
5.2.45
) holds in the case
p+1.
We are left to check the cyclic condition. Let
(i,j),(j,k),(i,k)\in\mathcal{F}
such that
m(i)<m(j)<m(k)
and
(m(k),m(j),m(i))=D_{p^{\prime}}
for some
1\leq p^{\prime}\leq p+1.
Since we only modified maps
I_{j,k}^{p}
and
I_{k,j}^{p}
with
m(j)=a_{2}
and
m(k)=a_{3}
we only need to consider
p^{\prime}
with
D_{p^{\prime}}=(a_{3},a_{2},b_{1})
for some
1\leq b_{1}\leq a_{1}.
The remaining cyclic relations are true by hypothesis. If
b_{1}=a_{1}
then
I_{i,j}^{p+1},I_{j,k}^{p+1},I_{i,k}^{p+1}
are
\lambda_{p+1}r_{\ell}
-cyclic by Lemma
5.2.6
.

It only remains to consider the case
b_{1}<a_{1}.
Let
\lambda=\lambda_{p}-1/(2{N_{1}})
and suppose
x\in\lambda B_{i}
is such that
I_{j,i}^{p+1}(x)\in\lambda B_{j}
and
I_{k,j}^{p+1}(I_{j,i}^{p+1}(x))\in\lambda B_{k}.
Since
\lambda<\lambda_{p}
and
I_{j,i}^{p+1}=I_{j,i}^{p}
(we didn’t modify these maps at this stage), we have

(5.2.47)

\displaystyle x\in\lambda_{p}B_{i}\mbox{ and }I_{j,i}^{p}(x)\in\lambda_{p}B_{j}.

Additionally, if
\varepsilon
small enough depending on
C_{1}
and
{N_{1}},
(
5.2.46
) gives

(5.2.48)

\displaystyle I_{k,j}^{p}(I_{j,i}^{p}(x))=I_{k,j}^{p}(I_{j,i}^{p+1}(x))\in\lambda_{p}B_{k}.

The inductive hypothesis implies
I_{k,j}^{p}(I_{j,i}^{p}(x))=I_{k,i}^{p}(x),
so, if we can show

(5.2.49)

\displaystyle I_{k,j}^{p+1}(I_{j,i}^{p}(x))=I_{k,j}^{p}(I_{j,i}^{p}(x)),

then using again that
I_{j,i}^{p+1}=I_{j,i}^{p}
and
I_{i,k}^{p+1}=I_{i,k}^{p}
(we also didn’t modify this map), we have

(5.2.50)

\displaystyle I_{k,j}^{p+1}(I_{j,i}^{p+1}(x))=I_{k,j}^{p+1}(I_{j,i}^{p}(x))=I_{k,j}^{p}(I_{j,i}^{p}(x))=I_{k,i}^{p}(x)=I_{k,i}^{p+1}(x).

The full cyclic relation follows from this and Lemma
2.5.4
.

Thus, our goal for the rest of the proof is to show (
5.2.49
). By construction, the map
I_{k,j}^{p+1}
differs from
I_{k,j}^{p}
only if there exists
i^{\prime}\in J
with
m(i^{\prime})=a_{1}
such that
(i^{\prime},j),(i^{\prime},k)\in\mathcal{F}.
We restrict our attention to this case. Let
y=I_{j,i}^{p}(x)
. If
y\in I_{j,i^{\prime}}^{p}(\lambda B_{i^{\prime}})^{c}\subseteq I_{j,i^{\prime}}^{p}(\lambda_{p+1}B_{i^{\prime}})^{c},
then
I_{k,j}^{p+1}(y)=I_{k,j}^{p}(y)
by (
5.2.27
). This gives exactly (
5.2.49
). Suppose instead that there exists
z\in\lambda B_{i^{\prime}}
such that
I_{j,i^{\prime}}^{p}(z)=y=I_{j,i}^{p}(x).
Since
\lambda<\lambda_{p},
we have
z\in\lambda_{p}B_{i^{\prime}}
. By (
5.2.47
) and (
5.2.48
) we also have
I_{j,i^{\prime}}(z)=y\in\lambda_{p}B_{j}
and
I_{k,j}^{p}(I_{j,i^{\prime}}(z))\in\lambda_{p}B_{k}.
It follows from the induction hypothesis that

(5.2.51)

\displaystyle I_{k,j}^{p}(I_{j,i^{\prime}}^{p}(z))=I_{k,i^{\prime}}^{p}(z).

Then, by (
5.2.28
),

(5.2.52)

\displaystyle I_{k,j}^{p+1}(I_{j,i}^{p}(x))=I_{k,j}^{p+1}(I_{j,i^{\prime}}^{p}(z))=I_{k,j}^{p}(I_{j,i^{\prime}}(z))=I_{k,j}^{p}(I_{j,i}(x)).

This establishes (
5.2.49
) and closes the induction.

∎

Proof of Proposition
5.2.1
.

Let
\delta_{1}
be as in Lemma
5.2.3
and
\varepsilon_{1}
be as in Remark
5.2.9
. For
(i,j)\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
we set
\tilde{I}_{i,j,\ell}=I_{i,j,\ell}^{N_{1}}.
Equation (
5.2.2
) and the cyclic properties are immediate from Lemma
5.2.10
, using the fact that
N_{1}=N_{1}(n).
Equation (
5.2.3
) follows from (
5.2.2
) and (
5.2.7
).
∎

5.3.
Construction of the (possibly) non-connected manifolds
W_{\ell}

In this subsection we construct a sequence of Finsler manifolds
W_{\ell}
whose transition maps will be a suitable restriction of those constructed in the previous section. Let
\varepsilon_{1},\delta_{1}>0
be the constants appearing in Proposition
5.2.1
and suppose for the remainder of this subsection that
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}
and
0<\delta<\delta_{1}
. Additionally, fix some
\ell\geq 0.
As in the previous section we will write
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
when the context is clear.

For
(i,j)\in\mathcal{F},
let

(5.3.1)

\displaystyle\Omega_{i,j}=\tilde{I}^{-1}_{i,j}(8B_{i})\cap 8B_{j},\mbox{ and }I_{i,j}=\tilde{I}_{i,j}|_{\Omega_{i,j}},

where
\tilde{I}_{i,j}=\tilde{I}_{i,j,\ell}
is the map from Proposition
5.2.1
. Since
\tilde{I}_{i,j}^{-1}
is an open mapping (since it is a diffeomorphism) and both
8B_{i}
and
8B_{j}
are open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have that
\Omega_{i,j}
is open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Definition 5.3.1
.

For
j\in J_{\ell}
let
\text{in}_{j}\colon 8B_{j}\to\bigsqcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}8B_{j}
be the natural injection. Define a relation
\sim
on
\bigsqcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}8B_{j}
by declaring
x\sim y
if
x=y
or if there exists
i,j\in J_{\ell}
with
i\neq j
and
x^{\prime}\in 8B_{i},y^{\prime}\in 8B_{j}
such that
\text{in}_{i}(x^{\prime})=x,\ \text{in}_{j}(y^{\prime})=y
and
y^{\prime}=I_{j,i}(x^{\prime}).

Lemma 5.3.2
.

The relation
\sim
is an equivalence relation.

Proof.

Reflexivity and symmetry are immediate, so we only need to verify transitivity. Suppose
x,y,z\in\bigsqcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}8B_{j}
are such that
x\sim y
and
y\sim z.
Without loss of generality, we may assume
x,y,z
are distinct. Then, there exists distinct indices
i,j,k\in J_{\ell}
and points
x^{\prime}\in 8B_{i},\ y^{\prime}\in 8B_{j},\ z^{\prime}\in 8B_{k}
such that
x=\text{in}_{i}(x^{\prime}),\ y=\text{in}_{j}(y^{\prime})
and
z=\text{in}_{k}(z^{\prime}).
By definition, we have

y^{\prime}=I_{j,i}(x^{\prime})\in 8B_{j}\mbox{ and }z^{\prime}=I_{k,j}(y^{\prime})=I_{k,j}\circ I_{j,i}(x^{\prime})\in 8B^{i}.

By Proposition
5.2.1
, the maps
\tilde{I}_{i,j},\tilde{I}_{j,k},\tilde{I}_{i,k}
are
9r_{\ell}
-cyclic, so that
z=\tilde{I}_{k,i}(x)
. Since
z^{\prime}\in 8B^{i}
and
x^{\prime}\in 8B^{i}
this implies
x\in\Omega_{k,i}
, hence,
z^{\prime}=I_{k,i}(x^{\prime}).
By definition this means
x\sim z,
as required.
∎

Let

(5.3.2)

\displaystyle W_{\ell}=\bigsqcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}8B_{j}/\sim.

Let
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bigsqcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}8B_{j}\to W_{\ell}
be the quotient map induced by the equivalence relation
\sim
and for each
j\in J_{\ell}
let

q_{j}\coloneqq Q\circ\text{in}_{j}\colon 8B_{j}\to W_{\ell}.

Let
\tau_{\ell}
be the coarsest topology on
W_{\ell}
such that each
q_{j}
is continuous i.e.

\tau_{\ell}=\{V\subseteq W_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}q_{j}^{-1}(V)\mbox{ is an open subset of }\mathbb{R}^{n}\mbox{ for all }j\in J_{\ell}\}.

By definition
\text{in}_{j}
is injective and by construction
Q
is injective on
\text{in}_{j}(8B_{j})
. Thus,
q_{j}
is injective and hence invertible. For
j\in J_{\ell}
and
0<\lambda\leq 8,
let

(5.3.3)

\displaystyle\lambda U^{j}=q_{j}(\lambda B_{j})\mbox{ and }\varphi_{j}=q_{j}^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}8U^{j}\to 8B_{j}.

\Omega_{i,j}
\Omega_{j,i}
8B_{i,\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
8B_{j,\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
(\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i})^{-1}
I_{i,j}
(
5.2.3
)
8U^{i,\ell}{}\subset W_{\ell}
\approx
8U^{j,\ell}\subset W_{\ell}
\varphi_{j}^{-1}
\varphi_{i}
8B^{j,\ell}\subset X
8B^{i,\ell}\subset X
\beta_{j}
\alpha_{i}

Figure 5.3.1.
The manifold
W_{\ell}

Remark 5.3.3
.

Recalling from Theorem
5.1.1
(1) that
J_{0}=\{j_{0}\},
we simply have
W_{0}=8B_{j_{0}}
and
q_{j_{0}}=\text{Id}.

Remark 5.3.4
.

It follows by construction that if
i,j\in J_{\ell}
and
x=\varphi_{j}(w)
for some
w\in 8U^{i}\cap 8U^{j}
then
x\in\Omega_{j,i}.
Furthermore, for each
x\in\Omega_{i,j}
we have

(5.3.4)

\displaystyle I_{i,j}(x)=q_{i}^{-1}(q_{j}(x))=\varphi_{i}(\varphi_{j}^{-1}(x)).

Lemma 5.3.5
.

The space
(W_{\ell},\tau_{\ell})
is a topological manifold i.e. a locally Euclidean Hausdorff space. The collection
\{(8U^{j},\varphi_{j})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j\in J\}
is a smooth atlas for
W_{\ell}
.

Proof.

To prove
W_{\ell}
is locally Euclidean, it suffices to check
8U^{j}
is open in
W_{\ell}
and
q_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}8B_{j}\to 8U^{j}
is a homeomorphism for each
j\in J_{\ell}
. This follows if we show
q_{j}(V)\in\tau_{\ell}
for each open
V\subseteq 8B_{j}
, which is equivalent to showing that
q_{i}^{-1}(q_{j}(V))
is open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for all
i\in J
. Fix such an open
V
and
i\in J.
If
i=j,
then
q_{j}^{-1}(q_{j}(V))=V
which is open by assumption. If
i\neq j,
by (
5.3.1
) and (
5.3.4
) we have

(5.3.5)

\displaystyle q_{i}^{-1}(q_{j}(V))=I_{i,j}(V)=\tilde{I}_{i,j}(V\cap\Omega_{i,j}).

Since
V
and
\Omega_{i,j}
are open in
\mathbb{R}^{n},
and
\tilde{I}_{i,j}
is an open mapping (since it is a diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
onto itself), we have that
q_{i}^{-1}(q_{j}(V))
is open.

Now, let us check that
W_{\ell}
is Hausdorff. Suppose
x,y\in W_{\ell}.
If
x,y\in 8U^{j}
for some
j\in J_{\ell}
then it is easy to find disjoint open sets
V_{x}\ni x
,
V_{y}\ni y
in
W_{\ell}
by using the Hausdorff property of
8B_{j}.
Suppose then that there exist
i,j\in J_{\ell}
such that
x\in 8U^{i}\setminus 8U^{j}
and
y\in 8U^{j}\setminus 8U^{i}.
Since
8U^{i}
and
8U^{i}\setminus\overline{8U^{i}}
are open in
W_{\ell}
, if
y\not\in\partial(8U^{i})
then again there exist open sets
V_{x}\ni x
,
V_{y}\ni y
in
W_{\ell}
, so we may support further that
y\in 8U^{j}\cap\partial(8U^{i}).
In this case, let
\tilde{x}=\psi_{i}(x),\ \tilde{y}=\psi_{j}(y),

\eta=\max\{{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{i}(\tilde{x},\partial(8B_{i})),{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{i}(\tilde{y},\partial(8B_{i}))\}/4,

and set

(5.3.6)

\displaystyle V_{x}=\psi_{i}^{-1}(B_{\|\cdot\|_{i}}(\tilde{x},\eta))\quad\mbox{ and }\quad V_{y}=\psi_{j}^{-1}(B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(\tilde{y},\eta)).

It is clear that
V_{x}\ni x
,
V_{y}\ni
and that both sets are open in
W_{\ell}.
It only remains to check that they are disjoint. Suppose towards a contradiction that there exists
z\in V_{x}\cap V_{y}.
By Remark
5.3.4
we have
\psi_{j}(z)\in B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(\tilde{y},\eta)\cap\Omega_{i,j}
and
\tilde{I}_{i,j}(\psi_{j}(z))=I_{i,j}(\psi_{j}(z))\in B_{\|\cdot\|_{i}}(\tilde{x},\eta)
. Since
\tilde{I}_{i,j}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, this implies

(5.3.7)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{x}-\tilde{I}_{i,j}(\tilde{y})\|_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\eta+\|\tilde{I}_{i,j}(\psi_{j}(z))-\tilde{I}_{i,j}(\tilde{y})\|_{i}

(5.3.8)

\displaystyle\leq\eta+(1+C\varepsilon)\|\psi_{j}(z)-\tilde{y}\|_{j}\leq 3\eta\leq 3\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\tilde{x},\partial(8B_{i}))/4.

However, since
y\in 8U^{j}\cap\partial(8U^{i})
and
\psi_{j}
is continuous we have
\tilde{y}\in 8B_{j}\cap\partial(\Omega_{i,j}).
Moreover, since
\tilde{I}_{i,j}
is continuous, we have
\tilde{I}_{i,j}(\tilde{y})\in\partial(8B_{i})
so that
\|\tilde{x}-\tilde{I}_{i,j}(\tilde{y})\|_{i}\geq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\tilde{x},\partial(8B_{i})).
This contradicts (
5.3.7
).

The fact that
\{(8U^{j},\varphi_{j})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j\in J\}
is a smooth atlas for
W_{\ell}
is a smooth atlas for
W_{\ell}
follows immediately from (
5.3.4
) and the fact that
I_{i,j}
is smooth by Proposition
5.2.1
.
∎

From now on we view
W_{\ell}
as equipped with the above smooth structure. In particular, if
x\in W_{\ell}
then the tangent space
T_{x}W_{\ell}
is well-defined. We equip
W_{\ell}
with a Finsler metric by defining, for each
x\in W_{\ell},
a norm
\|\cdot\|_{W_{\ell},x}
on
T_{x}W_{\ell}
as follows. Let
\{\theta_{j,\ell}\}_{j\in J_{\ell}}
be a partition of unity subordinate to
\{8U^{j}\}_{j\in J_{\ell}}.
Then, for each
v\in T_{x}W_{\ell},
let

(5.3.9)

\displaystyle\|v\|_{W_{\ell},x}=\sum_{j\in J_{\ell}}\theta_{j,\ell}(x)\|D\varphi_{j}(x)\cdot v\|_{j}.

We equip
T_{x}W_{\ell}
with this norm so that the following is true.

Lemma 5.3.6
.

Let
j\in J_{\ell}
and
x\in 8U^{j}.
The map
D\varphi_{j}(x)\colon T_{x}W_{\ell}\to T_{\varphi_{j}(x)}\mathbb{R}_{j}^{n}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz.

Proof.

Let
v\in T_{x}W_{\ell}.
Suppose
i\in J_{\ell}
is such that
\theta_{i,\ell}(x)\neq 0
. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{supp}(\theta_{i,\ell})\subseteq 8U^{j}
we have
x\in 8U^{i}\cap 8U^{j}
so that
\varphi_{i}(x)=I_{i,j}(\varphi_{j}(x))
by (
5.3.4
). Then,

(5.3.10)

\displaystyle D\varphi_{j}(x)=D[I_{i,j}\circ\varphi_{j}](x)=DI_{i,j}(\varphi_{j}(x))\circ D\varphi_{j}(x).

Since
I_{i,j}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, this gives

(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\|D\varphi_{j}(x)\cdot v\|_{j}\leq\|D\varphi_{i}(x)\cdot v\|_{i}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|D\varphi_{j}(x)\cdot v\|_{j}.

Since
\sum_{j\in J_{\ell}}\theta_{j,\ell}(x)=1,
this implies

(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\|v\|_{W_{\ell},x}\leq\|D\varphi_{j}(x)\cdot v\|_{j}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|v\|_{W_{\ell},x},

which completes the proof.
∎

5.4.
Smooth maps between manifolds

In this section we construct a sequence of smooth mappings
f_{\ell}\colon E_{\ell}\to W_{\ell+1}
for some suitable subset
E_{\ell}\subseteq W_{\ell}.
Let’s give a rough description of how this is done. Suppose
j\in J_{\ell+1}
. Similar to Lemma
5.2.3
, the flatness condition implies the tangent spaces corresponding to the indices
j
and
i(j)
(as in Theorem
5.1.1
(3)) are roughly compatible i.e. there exists a smooth
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz map
K_{i(j),j}
between them which well-approximates the rough transition maps given by
X
(Lemma
5.4.1
). Pushing
8U^{j,\ell+1}\subseteq W_{\ell+1}
through the map

(5.4.1)

\displaystyle\varphi^{-1}_{i(j),\ell}\circ K_{i(j),j}\circ\varphi_{j,\ell+1}

we find a corresponding region
C^{j,\ell+1}_{0}
in
W_{\ell}
. We contract each of these regions slightly and take
E_{\ell}
to be their union (see (
5.4.14
)). Let
h_{j,\ell+1}\colon C^{j,\ell+1}_{0}\to 8U^{j,\ell+1}
be the inverse of the mapping in (
5.4.1
). For nearby patches
C^{i,\ell+1}_{0}
and
C^{j,\ell+1}_{0}
, these mappings are small perturbations of each other (Lemma
5.4.7
). A modification procedure similar to that in the previous section then produces a smooth mapping
h_{\ell}\colon E_{\ell}\to W_{\ell+1}
so that, locally,
h_{\ell}
is small perturbation of the preliminary map
h_{j,\ell+1}
(Lemma
5.4.8
, see in particular (3)).

Before stating the main proposition, we state some preliminary results and notation. For each
\ell\geq 0
and
j\in J_{\ell+1}
choose some
i(j)\in J_{\ell}
as in Theorem
5.1.1
(3). We recall that this means

(5.4.2)

\displaystyle x_{j,\ell+1}\in 2B^{i(j),\ell}.

The first result is a slight modification of Lemma
5.2.3
.

Lemma 5.4.1
.

Let
\ell\geq 0
and
j\in J_{\ell+1}
. Then there exists an affine
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz map
K_{i(j),j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{i(j)}
such that

(5.4.3)

\displaystyle\|K_{i(j),j}-\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}(x)\|_{i(j)}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}

for all
x\in 45B_{j}
. Additionally, if we let
K_{j,i(j)}=K_{i(j),j}^{-1},
then

(5.4.4)

\displaystyle\|K_{j,i(j)}(x)-\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i(j)}(x)\|_{j}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}

for all
x\in\beta_{i(j)}(99B^{j}).

Proof.

The proof is very similar to Lemma
5.2.3
. By Theorem
5.1.1
(3) we have
100B^{j}\subseteq 100B^{i(j)}.
Let
z=\beta_{i(j)}(x_{j}).
By the final part of Lemma
2.3.6
, there exists a
C\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA
\phi_{i(j)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}100B^{j}\to B_{\|\cdot\|_{i(j)}}(z,100r_{\ell+1})
such that
\|\beta_{i(j)}(x)-\phi_{i(j)}(x)\|_{i(j)}\leq C\delta r_{\ell}
for all
x\in 100B^{j}.
By Lemma
2.3.8
,

\phi_{i(j),j}\coloneqq\phi_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}100B_{j}\to B_{\|\cdot\|_{i(j)}}(z,100r_{\ell+1})

defines a
C\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA. By Lemma
2.3.14
, as long as
\delta
is chosen small enough depending on
\varepsilon,
there exists an affine
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz map
K_{i(j),j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{i(j)}
such that

\|K_{i(j),j}(x)-\phi_{i(j),j}(x)\|_{i}\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell}

for all
x\in 100B_{j}.

We prove (
5.4.3
) for a slightly larger ball. Suppose
x\in 100B_{j}.
By definition,
\alpha_{j}(x)\in 100B^{j}
. Taking
\delta
small enough depending on
\varepsilon
, and using the above relation between
\beta_{i(j)},\phi_{i(j)}
on
100B^{j},
we have

(5.4.5)

\displaystyle\|K_{i(j),j}(x)-\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}(x)\|_{i(j)}

\displaystyle\leq\|K_{i(j),j}(x)-\phi_{i(j),j}(x)\|_{i(j)}

(5.4.6)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|\phi_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}(x)-\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}(x)\|_{i(j)}

(5.4.7)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell}+C\delta r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Let us take a look at (
5.4.4
). Suppose
x\in\beta_{j}(99B^{j}).
By (
5.1.4
) we have
\alpha_{i(j)}(x)\in 100B^{j}.
By definition, we have
\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i(j)}(x)\in 100B_{j}.
Then, using Lemma
5.2.2
, (
5.4.3
) and since
K_{j,i(j)}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, the left-hand side of (
5.4.4
) is at most

(5.4.8)

\displaystyle\|x-K_{i(j),j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i(j)}(x)\|_{i(j)}

(5.4.9)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt\leq\|x-\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i(j)}(x)\|_{i(j)}

(5.4.10)

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt+\|\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i(j)}(x)-K_{{i(j)},j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i(j)}(x)\|_{i(j)}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

∎

(
5.4.12
)
8C^{j,\ell+1}\subset W_{\ell}
8B_{i(j),\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

h_{j,\ell+1}
(

)

Figure 5.4.1.

For each

and

, let

\displaystyle\Phi_{j,\ell+1}=\varphi_{i(j),\ell}^{-1}\circ K_{i(j),j}|_{8B_{j,\ell+1}},

and note that

Proposition 5.4.2
.

For each
\ell\geq 0
there exists a diffeomorphism
h_{\ell}\colon E_{\ell}\to W_{\ell+1}
such that
Dh_{\ell}(x)\colon T_{x}W_{\ell}\to T_{h_{\ell}(x)}W_{\ell+1}

is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz for each
x\in E_{\ell}.
If

for some
j\in J_{\ell+1}
, then

(5.4.15)

\displaystyle h_{\ell}(x)=\varphi_{j,\ell+1}^{-1}\circ\hat{H}_{j}\circ\Phi_{j,\ell+1}^{-1}(x)=\varphi_{j,\ell+1}^{-1}\circ\hat{H}_{j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ\varphi_{i(j),\ell}(x)

for some diffeomorphism
\hat{H}_{j}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}
satisfying

(5.4.16)

\displaystyle\|\hat{H}_{j}-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{1},r_{\ell}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

If
j\in J_{\ell+1}
and
A\in\mathbb{N}
such that
1\leq A\leq 6
then

(5.4.17)

\displaystyle AU^{j,\ell+1}\subseteq h_{\ell}\left(C^{j,\ell+1}_{(7-A)N_{0}}\right)\subseteq h_{\ell}(C^{j,\ell+1}_{N_{0}})\subseteq h_{\ell}(E_{\ell}\cap 3U^{i(j)}).

In particular,

(5.4.18)

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell+1}}6U^{j,\ell+1}\subseteq W_{\ell+1}^{*}\coloneqq h_{\ell}(E_{\ell}).

Finally, we have

(5.4.19)

\displaystyle E_{\ell+1}\subseteq\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell+1}}3U^{j,\ell+1}.

Fix
\ell\geq 0
for the remainder of the subsection. When it is clear we will drop the subscript
\ell
from our notation. Before proving the above proposition, we need some preliminary results.

Lemma 5.4.3
.

For each
1\leq m\leq N_{0}
and
j,k\in J_{\ell+1}^{m}
we have
C_{0}^{j}\cap C_{0}^{k}=\emptyset
and
C^{j}_{0}\subseteq 3U^{i(j)}.

Proof.

Fix
1\leq m\leq N_{0}
and
j,k\in J_{\ell+1}^{m}
and suppose towards a contradiction that there exists
x\in C^{j}_{0}\cap C^{k}_{0}.
Let
y\in 8B_{j}
and
z\in 8B_{k}
such that
x=\Phi_{j}(y)=\Phi_{k}(z)
. Recalling the definition of
\Phi
from (
5.4.11
), this implies
\varphi_{i(j)}^{-1}\circ K_{i(j),j}(y)=\varphi_{i(k)}^{-1}\circ K_{i(k),k}(z).
Applying
\varphi_{i(j)}
to both sides and recalling (
5.3.4
), we get

(5.4.20)

\displaystyle K_{i(j),j}(y)=I_{i(j),i(k)}\circ K_{i(k),k}.

Using this with (
5.1.4
), Lemma
5.2.3
, Lemma
5.4.1
, and the fact that
\beta_{i(j)}
is a
\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA and
I_{i(k)}
is bi-Lipschitz, it is not difficult to show that

(5.4.21)

\displaystyle d(\alpha_{j}(y),\alpha_{k}(z))

\displaystyle\leq d(\alpha_{j}(y),\alpha_{i(k)}\circ\beta_{i(k)}\circ\alpha_{k}(z))+d(\alpha_{i(k)}\circ\beta_{i(k)}\circ\alpha_{k}(z),\alpha_{k}(z))

(5.4.22)

\displaystyle\leq\|\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}(y)-\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{i(k)}\circ\beta_{i(k)}\circ\alpha_{k}(z)\|_{i(j)}+\delta r_{\ell}

(5.4.23)

\displaystyle\leq\|K_{i(j)}-I_{i(j),i(k)}\circ K_{i(k),k}\|_{i(j)}+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}

Since
\alpha_{j}(y)\in 9B^{j}
and
\alpha_{k}(z)\in 9B^{k}
by (
2.3.12
), it follows from above estimate and the triangle inequality that
d(x_{j},x_{k})\leq 20r_{\ell}.
This contradicts the separation condition (
5.2.40
).

Let us now show that
C^{j}_{0}\subseteq 3U^{i(j)}.
Recall from (
5.4.11
) and (
5.4.12
) that
C_{0}^{j}=\varphi_{i(j)}^{-1}\circ K_{i(j),j}(8B_{j}).
For
x\in 8B_{j}
we have by Theorem
5.1.1
(3), Lemma
5.4.1
, (
2.3.12
) and (
5.1.3
) that

(5.4.24)

\displaystyle\|K_{i(j),j}(x)\|_{i(j)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}(x)-\beta_{i(j)}(x_{i(j)})\|_{i(j)}+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\leq d(\alpha_{j}(x),x_{i(j)})+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}

(5.4.25)

\displaystyle\leq d(\alpha_{j}(x),x_{j})+d(x_{j},x_{i(j)})+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\leq 9r_{\ell+1}+2r_{\ell}+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\leq 3r_{\ell}.

Thus,
K_{i(j),j}(x)\in 3B_{i(j)}.
By (
5.3.3
) this implies
\varphi_{i(j)}^{-1}\circ K_{i(j),j}(x)\in 3U^{i(j)}
. Since
x
was arbitrary, we conclude
C_{0}^{j}\subseteq 3U^{i(j)},
as required.
∎

Lemma 5.4.4
.

Let
i,j\in J_{\ell}
and suppose
w\in 8U^{i}\cap 8U^{j}.
Then,

(5.4.26)

\displaystyle d_{X}(\alpha_{i}\circ\varphi_{i}(w),\alpha_{j}\circ\varphi_{j}(w))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Proof.

Recalling (
5.3.4
) we have
\varphi_{j}(w)=I_{j,i}(\varphi_{i}(w))=\tilde{I}_{j,i}(\varphi_{i}(w))\in 8B_{j}.
By (
5.1.4
) and Proposition
5.2.1
we have
d(\alpha_{j}(\beta_{j}(x)),x)\leq\delta r_{\ell}\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell}
for all
x\in 10B^{j}
and
\|\tilde{I}_{j,i}(x)-\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}(x)\|_{j}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}
for all
x\in 8B_{i}.
Since
\varphi_{i}(w)\in 8B_{i},
combing the above we get

(5.4.27)

\displaystyle d(\alpha_{i}(\varphi_{i}(w)),\alpha_{j}(\varphi_{j}(w)))

\displaystyle=d(\alpha_{i}(\varphi_{i}(w)),\alpha_{j}(\tilde{I}_{j,i}(\varphi_{i}(w))))

(5.4.28)

\displaystyle\leq d(\alpha_{i}(\varphi_{i}(w)),\alpha_{j}(\beta_{j}(\alpha_{i}(\varphi_{i}(w)))))+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

∎

Lemma 5.4.5
.

Let
j,k\in J_{\ell+1}
and set
V_{m}=\Phi_{j}^{-1}(C_{m}^{j}\cap C_{m}^{k})
for
1\leq m\leq N_{0}.
Suppose
x\in V_{m}
for some
m\geq 2
and suppose
y\in 8B_{j,\ell+1}
is such that
\|x-y\|_{j}\leq r_{\ell}/(4N_{0}).
Then,
y\in V_{m-1}

Proof.

It is enough to show
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(z,V_{m})>r_{\ell}/(4N_{0})
for all
z\in 8B_{j,\ell+1}\setminus V_{m-1}.
Pick such a
z
and let
w\in V_{m}
such that
\|w-z\|_{j}\leq 2\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(z,V_{m}).
Furthermore, choose a point
p\in\partial V_{m-1}
on the line segment connecting
w
with
z
. We have

(5.4.29)

\displaystyle{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}(p,V_{m})\leq\|p-w\|=\|w-z\|-\|p-z\|\leq 2{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}(z,V_{m})

so in particular it suffices to prove
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(p,V_{m})>r_{\ell}/(2N_{0}).
This is our goal for the remainder of the proof. First notice, since
\Phi_{j}
and
\Phi_{k}
are homeomorphisms, we have

\partial V_{m-1}\subseteq\partial(\lambda_{m-1}B_{j})\cup\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{k}(\partial(\lambda_{m-1}B_{k}))

and

(5.4.30)

\displaystyle V_{m}\subseteq\lambda_{m}B_{j}\cap\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{k}(\lambda_{m}B_{k}).

If
p\in\partial(\lambda_{m-1}B_{j}),
then immediately
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(p,V_{m})\geq r_{\ell}/{N_{0}}.
Suppose instead that
p\in\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{k}(\partial(\lambda_{m-1}B_{k}))
and let
q\in\lambda_{m-1}B_{k}
such that
p=\Phi_{j}^{-1}(\Phi_{k}(q))\in\lambda_{m-1}B_{k}
. Since
q\in\lambda_{m-1}B_{k}
we have
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(q,\lambda_{m}B_{k})\geq r_{\ell}/{N_{0}}
. If
z^{\prime}\in\Phi_{k}^{-1}(C^{j}_{0}\cap C^{k}_{0})
it follows from the definition in (
5.4.11
) and (
5.3.4
) that

\Phi_{j}^{-1}(\Phi_{k}(z^{\prime}))=K_{j,i(j)}\circ\tilde{I}_{i(j),i(k)}\circ K_{i(k),k}(z^{\prime}).

By (
5.4.30
) and since by Lemma
5.2.3
and Lemma
5.4.1
each of the
K_{a,b}
and
\tilde{I}_{a,b}
are
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, we then have

(5.4.31)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(p,V_{m})

\displaystyle\geq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\Phi_{j}^{-1}(\Phi_{k}(q)),\Phi_{j}^{-1}(\Phi_{k}(\lambda_{m}B_{k})))

(5.4.32)

\displaystyle\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(q,\lambda_{m}B_{k})\geq r_{\ell}/(2N_{0})

for
\varepsilon
small enough. This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.4.6
.

Let
j,k\in J_{\ell+1}
and suppose there exists
x\in\emph{dom}(\Phi_{k}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{j})\subseteq 8B_{j}.
Then,
\tilde{I}_{k,j}
is well-defined and

(5.4.33)

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{k}^{-1}(\Phi_{j}(x))-\tilde{I}_{k,j}(x)\|_{k}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Proof.

To prove that
\tilde{I}_{k,j}
is well-defined, it suffices to show
d(x_{j},x_{k})\leq 18r_{\ell+1}
(in this case
15B^{j}\cap 15B^{k}\neq\emptyset
and so
(j,k)\in\mathcal{F}
, recall the definition in (
5.2.1
)). Since
x\in\text{dom}(\Phi_{k}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{j})
, there exists
y\in 8B_{k}
such that
\Phi_{j}(x)=\Phi_{k}(y).
By (
2.3.12
) we have

(5.4.34)

\displaystyle d(x_{j},x_{k})

\displaystyle\leq d(x_{j},\alpha_{j}(x))+d(\alpha_{j}(x),\alpha_{k}(y))+d(\alpha_{k}(y),x_{k})

(5.4.35)

\displaystyle\leq d(\alpha_{j}(x),\alpha_{k}(y))+17r_{\ell+1},

so we only need to show
d(\alpha_{j}(x),\alpha_{k}(y))\leq r_{\ell+1}.
To see that this, we apply (
5.1.4
), Lemma
5.4.1
, Lemma
5.4.4
and (
5.4.11
) to get

(5.4.36)

\displaystyle d(\alpha_{j}(x),\alpha_{k}(y))

\displaystyle\leq d(\alpha_{i(j)}\circ\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}(x),\alpha_{i(k)}\circ\beta_{i(k)}\circ\alpha_{k}(x))+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}

(5.4.37)

\displaystyle\leq d_{X}(\alpha_{i(j)}\circ K_{i(j),j}(x),\alpha_{i(k)}\circ K_{i(k),k}(y))+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}

(5.4.38)

\displaystyle=d_{X}(\alpha_{i(j)}\circ\varphi_{i(j)}\circ\Phi_{j}(x),\alpha_{i(k)}\circ\varphi_{i(k)}\circ\Phi_{k}(y))+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Taking
\varepsilon
small enough we get the desired estimate. We move onto proving (
5.4.33
). Using the definition in (
5.4.11
) and applying (
5.3.4
), we have

\Phi_{k}^{-1}(\Phi_{j}(x))=K_{k,i(k)}\circ\tilde{I}_{i(k),i(j)}\circ K_{i(j),j}(x).

Then, by combination of (
5.1.4
), Lemma
5.2.3
, Lemma
5.4.1
and Proposition
5.2.1
, the left-hand side of (
5.4.33
) is at most

(5.4.39)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|K_{k,i(k)}\circ\tilde{I}_{i(k),i(j)}\circ K_{i(j),j}(x)-\tilde{I}_{k,j}(x)\|_{k}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\|\beta_{k}\circ\alpha_{i(k)}\circ\beta_{i(k)}\circ\alpha_{i(j)}\circ\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}(x)-\tilde{I}_{k,j}(x)\|_{k}+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\|\beta_{k}\circ\alpha_{j}(x)-\tilde{I}_{k,j}(x)\|_{k}+C\varepsilon r_{\ell+1}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}\leq 18r_{\ell}.\end{split}

∎

For the following lemma, recall the definition of
h_{j}
from (
5.4.13
).

Lemma 5.4.7
.

Let
j,k\in J_{\ell+1}
,
1\leq m\leq{N_{0}}
and suppose
C_{m-1}^{j}\cap C_{m-1}^{k}\neq\emptyset
. Then,

(5.4.40)

\displaystyle h_{j}(C_{m-2}^{j}\cap C_{m-2}^{k})\subseteq h_{k}(C_{0}^{k}).

In particular, the map
\Phi_{k}^{-1}\circ h_{k}^{-1}\circ h_{j}\circ\Phi_{k}
is well-defined on
\Phi^{-1}_{k}(C_{m-2}^{j}\cap C_{m-2}^{k})
. Furthermore, we have

(5.4.41)

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{k}^{-1}\circ h_{k}^{-1}\circ h_{j}\circ\Phi_{k}-\mathrm{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\Phi^{-1}_{k}(C_{m-2}^{j}\cap C_{m-2}^{k})),r_{\ell}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

Proof.

We start with (
5.4.40
). Let
x\in C_{m-2}^{j}\cap C_{m-2}^{k}
and let
y=\Phi^{-1}_{j}(x).
By definition
y\in\text{dom}(\Phi_{k}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{j})
and so
\tilde{I}_{k,j}
is well-defined by Lemma
5.4.6
. Recall the definition of
\Omega_{k,j}
and
I_{k,j}
from (
5.3.1
). Since
\Phi_{k}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{j}(y)\in\lambda_{m-2}B_{k}
, (
5.4.33
) implies
\tilde{I}_{k,j}(y)\in 8B_{k}.
In particular
y\in\Omega_{k,j}
and
I_{k,j}(y)=\tilde{I}_{k,j}(y).
Now let
w=I_{k,j}(y)\in 8B_{k}
and
v=\Phi_{j}(w)\in C^{k}_{0}.
Then, using (
5.3.4
) with (
5.4.13
) and the above definitions, we get

(5.4.42)

\displaystyle h_{j}(x)=\varphi^{-1}_{j}\circ\Phi_{j}^{-1}(x)=\varphi_{k}^{-1}(I_{k,j}(y))=\varphi_{k}^{-1}(w)=h_{k}(v)\in h_{k}(C_{0}^{k}).

Let us prove (
5.4.41
). Let
U\coloneqq\Phi^{-1}_{k}(C_{m-2}^{j}\cap C_{m-2}^{k})
. Unravelling the definition (
5.4.11
) and (
5.4.13
) while making use of (
5.3.4
) we have

(5.4.43)

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}^{-1}\circ h_{k}^{-1}\circ h_{j}\circ\Phi_{k}|_{U}

\displaystyle=I_{k,j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ I_{i(j),i(k)}\circ K_{i(k),k}|_{U}.

Using this with Lemma
2.4.3
and (
5.2.2
), the left-hand side of (
5.4.41
) is at most

(5.4.44)

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{k}^{-1}\circ h_{k}^{-1}\circ h_{j}\circ\Phi_{k}-T_{k,j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ T_{i(j),i(k)}\circ K_{i(k),k}\|_{C^{1}(U),r_{\ell}}

(5.4.45)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\|T_{k,j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ T_{i(j),i(k)}\circ K_{i(k),k}-\text{Id}\|_{C^{1}(U),r_{\ell}}

(5.4.46)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq C\varepsilon+\|T_{k,j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ T_{i(j),i(k)}\circ K_{i(k),k}-\text{Id}\|_{C^{1}(U),r_{\ell}}.

Applying (
5.4.39
) with Lemma
2.4.3
and (
5.2.2
), we have
\|T_{k,j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ T_{i(j),i(k)}\circ K_{i(k),k}-x\|_{k}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}
for all
x\in U.
Then, after applying Lemma
2.4.5
, we obtain
\|T_{k,j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ T_{i(j),i(k)}\circ K_{i(k),k}-\text{Id}\|_{C^{1}(U),r_{\ell}}\lesssim\varepsilon.
This finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.4.8
.

There exists a sequence of maps
g_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell+1}^{k}}C_{m}^{j}\to W_{\ell+1},

1\leq m\leq N_{0},
such that the following holds:

(1)

If
1\leq m<m^{\prime}\leq{N_{0}}
and
i\in J_{\ell+1}^{m},\ j\in J_{\ell+1}^{m^{\prime}}
are such that
C^{i}_{m^{\prime}-1}\cap C^{j}_{m^{\prime}-1}\neq\emptyset,
then

(5.4.47)

\displaystyle g_{m}(C^{i}_{m^{\prime}-1}\cap C^{j}_{m^{\prime}-1})\subseteq h_{j}(C_{0}^{j})=B^{j,\ell+1}.

(2)

If
1\leq m<m^{\prime}\leq{N_{0}}
and
i\in J_{\ell+1}^{m},\ j\in J_{\ell+1}^{m^{\prime}}
are such that
C^{i}_{m^{\prime}-1}\cap C^{j}_{m^{\prime}-1}\neq\emptyset,
then

(5.4.48)

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ h^{-1}_{j}\circ g_{m}\circ\Phi_{j}-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\Phi_{j}^{-1}(C^{i}_{m^{\prime}-1}\cap C^{j}_{m^{\prime}-1})),r_{j,k+1}}\lesssim_{m}\varepsilon.

(3)

If
1\leq m\leq N_{0}
and
j\in J^{m}_{\ell+1}
then exists a diffeomorphism
\hat{H}_{j}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}
such that
\|\hat{H}_{j}-\emph{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),r_{\ell}}\lesssim\varepsilon
and

g_{m^{\prime}}(x)=\varphi_{j}^{-1}\circ\hat{H}_{j}\circ\Phi_{j}^{-1}(x)

for all
m<m^{\prime}\leq N_{0}
and
x\in C^{j}_{m^{\prime}}.

Proof.

We construct the maps by induction. First, define
g_{1}\colon\bigcup_{j\in J^{1}}C_{1}^{j}\to W_{\ell+1}
by setting

g_{1}(x)=h_{j}(x)\mbox{ whenver }x\in C_{1}^{j}.

This is well-defined since
C^{i}_{1}\cap C^{j}_{1}=\emptyset
for all
i,j\in J^{1}
such that
i\neq j
by Lemma
5.4.3
. Conditions (1) and (2) follow immediately from Lemma
5.4.7
. Condition (3) follows if we set
\hat{H}_{j}=\text{Id}
for each
j\in J^{1}.
This completes the base case.

Assume now that we have defined the maps up to some integer
1\leq m\leq N_{0}-1
and let us describe how to construct
g_{m+1}.
For
j\in J^{m+1}
, set

(5.4.49)

\displaystyle H_{j}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ h_{j}^{-1}\circ g_{m}\circ\Phi_{j},

(5.4.50)

\displaystyle U_{1,j}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{j}^{-1}\left(\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}\bigcup_{i\in J^{k}}C^{i}_{m+1}\right)\cap C^{j}_{m+1}\right);

(5.4.51)

\displaystyle U_{2,j}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{j}^{-1}\left(\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}\bigcup_{i\in J^{k}}C^{i}_{m}\right)\cap C^{j}_{m}\right).

We want to apply Lemma
2.4.2
with the map
H_{j}
and the sets
U_{1,j}
and
U_{2,j}.
Clearly,
U_{1,j}\subseteq U_{2,j}.
Combining conditions (1) and (2) for the map
g_{m}
implies
H_{j}
is well-defined on
U_{2,j}
and satisfies

(5.4.52)

\displaystyle\|H_{j}-\text{Id}\|_{C^{1}(U_{2,j}),r_{\ell}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

It only remains to check

(5.4.53)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(U_{1,j},U_{2,j}^{c})\gtrsim\frac{r_{\ell}}{N_{0}}.

To see that (
5.4.53
) holds, let
x\in U_{1,j}
and
y\in U_{2,j}^{c}.
By definition
x\in\lambda_{m+1}B_{j,\ell+1}.
If
y\not\in\lambda_{m}B_{j,\ell+1}
then immediately we get
\|x-y\|_{j}\geq r_{\ell}/N_{0}.
Suppose instead
y\in\lambda_{m}B_{j,\ell+1}
so that
\Phi_{j}(y)\in C^{j}_{m}.
By (
5.4.50
), since
x\in U_{1,j}
there exists
i\in\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}J^{k}
such that
\Phi_{j}(x)\in C^{i}_{m+1}\cap C_{m+1}^{j}
i.e.
x\in\Phi_{j}(C_{m+1}^{i}\cap C_{m+1}^{j})
. By (
5.4.51
), since
y\in U_{2,j}^{c}
and
\Phi_{j}(y)\in C^{j}_{m}
it must be that
\Phi_{j}(y)\not\in C_{m}^{i}
. Hence,
y\not\in\Phi_{j}(C_{m}^{i}\cap C_{m}^{j}).
Lemma
5.4.5
now implies
\|y-x\|_{j}>r_{\ell}/(4N_{0})
. This completes the proof of (
5.4.53
).

Now, let
\hat{H}_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the map obtained from Lemma
2.4.2
by modifying
H_{j}.
Let us recall here that by (
2.4.16
) and (
5.4.52
) we have

(5.4.54)

\displaystyle\|\hat{H}_{i}-\text{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{i}),r_{\ell+1}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

Now, define

(5.4.55)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{j}=h_{j}\circ\Phi_{j}\circ\hat{H}_{j}\circ\Phi_{j}^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C_{0}^{j}\to W_{\ell+1}

and let

(5.4.56)

\displaystyle g_{m+1}=\begin{cases}g_{m}&\mbox{ on }\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{m}\bigcup_{i\in J^{\ell}}C_{m+1}^{i}\\
\tilde{h}_{j}&\mbox{ on }C_{m+1}^{j}\mbox{ for each }j\in J^{m+1}.\end{cases}

Using (
2.4.13
) with (
5.4.49
), if
z\in\Phi_{j}(U_{1,j})
then
h_{j}\circ\Phi_{j}\circ\hat{H}_{j}\circ\Phi_{j}^{-1}(z)=g_{m}(z)
so that
g_{m+1}
is well-defined. Now that we have defined
g_{m+1},
we are left to check conditions (1) – (3).

Condition (1):
Suppose
m+1<m^{\prime}\leq N_{0}
and
i\in J^{m+1},j\in J^{m^{\prime}}
are such that
C^{i}_{m^{\prime}-1}\cap C^{j}_{m^{\prime}-1}\neq\emptyset
and fix some
x\in C^{i}_{m^{\prime}-1}\cap C^{j}_{m^{\prime}-1}
. Since
m+1<m^{\prime}
we have
m+2\leq m^{\prime}
so that
m+1\geq m^{\prime}-1.
This gives
x\in C_{m+1}^{i}
and (
5.4.56
) now implies

(5.4.57)

\displaystyle g_{m+1}(x)=\tilde{h}_{i}(x).

By Lemma
5.4.7
and (
5.4.57
), it suffices to show

(5.4.58)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{i}(x)\in h_{i}(C_{m^{\prime}-2}^{i}\cap C_{m^{\prime}-2}^{j}).

To see (
5.4.58
), we first observe by (
5.4.12
) and (
5.4.52
) that
\Phi^{-1}_{i}(x)\in\lambda_{m^{\prime}-1}B_{i}
and

(5.4.59)

\displaystyle\|\hat{H}_{i}(\Phi_{i}^{-1}(x))-\Phi_{i}^{-1}(x)\|_{i}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Hence,
\hat{H}_{i}(\Phi^{-1}_{i}(x))\in\lambda_{m^{\prime}-2}B_{i}
, so that

(5.4.60)

\displaystyle\Phi_{i}\circ\hat{H}_{i}\circ\Phi_{i}^{-1}(x)\in C_{m^{\prime}-2}^{i}

by (
5.4.12
). Since
x\in C_{m^{\prime}-1}^{i}\cap C_{m^{\prime}-1}^{j},
we have
\Phi_{i}^{-1}(x)\in\text{dom}(\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{i}).
Combining this with Lemma
5.4.5
and (
5.4.59
) gives
\hat{H}_{i}\circ\Phi_{i}^{-1}(x)\in\text{dom}(\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{i})
. Lemma
5.4.6
now implies
\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{i}\circ\hat{H}_{i}\circ\Phi_{i}^{-1}(x)\in\lambda_{m^{\prime}-2}B_{j}
so that

(5.4.61)

\displaystyle\Phi_{i}\circ\hat{H}_{i}\circ\Phi_{i}^{-1}(x)\in C_{m^{\prime}-2}^{j}.

Equation (
5.4.58
) follows from (
5.4.60
), (
5.4.61
), and the definition of
\tilde{h}_{i}
in (
5.4.55
).

Condition (2):
Suppose
m+1<m^{\prime}\leq N_{0}
and
i\in J^{m+1},j\in J^{m^{\prime}}
are such that
C^{i}_{m^{\prime}-1}\cap C^{j}_{m^{\prime}-1}\neq\emptyset
. By (
5.4.61
), if
y\in\Phi_{j}^{-1}(C^{i}_{m^{\prime}-1}\cap C^{j}_{m^{\prime}-1})
, then
\Phi_{i}\circ\hat{H}_{i}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{i}\circ\Phi_{j}(y)\in\text{dom}(\Phi_{j}^{-1}).
Furthermore, by the same reason as for (
5.4.57
), we have

g_{m+1}(\Phi_{j}(y))=\tilde{h}_{i}(\Phi_{j}(y)).

Combining this with (
5.4.55
) gives

(5.4.62)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ h_{j}^{-1}\circ g_{m+1}\circ\Phi_{j}(y)&=\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ h_{j}^{-1}\circ h_{i}\circ\Phi_{i}\circ\hat{H}_{i}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{i}\circ\Phi_{j}(y)\\
&=\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ h_{j}^{-1}\circ h_{i}\circ\Phi_{j}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{j}\circ\Phi_{i}\circ\hat{H}_{i}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{i}\circ\Phi_{j}(y)\\
&=f\circ g\circ\hat{H}_{i}\circ g^{-1}(y),\end{split}

where,
f=\Phi_{j}^{-1}\circ h_{j}^{-1}\circ h_{i}\circ\Phi_{j}
and
g=\Phi^{-1}_{j}\circ\Phi_{i}=K_{j,i(j)}\circ I_{i(j),i(i)}\circ K_{i(i),i}.
By Lemma
5.4.7
, we have

(5.4.63)

\displaystyle\|f-\text{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\Phi_{j}^{-1}(C^{i}_{m^{\prime}-1}\cap C^{j}_{m^{\prime}-1})),r_{\ell+1}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

Clearly
g
is invertible with
g,g^{-1}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Furthermore, using (
5.2.2
) along with the fact that
T_{i(j),i(i)}
,
K_{j,i(j)}
,
K_{i(i),i}
are affine and
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, we have

(5.4.64)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\|Dg(x)\|+\|D[g^{-1}](x)\|+r_{\ell+1}\|D^{2}g(x)\|+r_{\ell+1}\|D^{2}[g^{-1}](x)\|\right)\lesssim 1.

Then, Lemma
2.4.4
and (
5.4.52
) give

(5.4.65)

\displaystyle\|g\circ\tilde{H}_{i}\circ g^{-1}-\text{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}),r_{\ell+1}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

Condition (2) follows from (
5.4.62
), (
5.4.63
), (
5.4.65
) and Lemma
2.4.3
.

Condition (3):
Suppose
1\leq m+1<m^{\prime}\leq N_{0}
and
j\in J^{m}.
Iterating (
5.4.56
), gives
g_{m^{\prime}}|_{C^{j}_{m^{\prime}}}=g_{m}|_{C^{j}_{m^{\prime}}}=\tilde{h}_{j}|_{C^{j}_{m^{\prime}}}.
Condition (3) now follows from (
5.4.13
), (
5.4.55
), and (
5.4.52
).

∎

Proof of Proposition
5.4.2
.

Recall the definition of
E_{\ell}
from (
5.4.14
). We set

h_{\ell}\coloneqq g_{N_{0}}\colon E_{\ell}\to W_{\ell+1}.

Both (
5.4.15
) and (
5.4.16
) are then immediate from Lemma
5.4.8
(3). For the first inclusion in (
5.4.17
), we notice that for each
j\in J_{\ell+1}
and
A\in\mathbb{N}
such that
1\leq A\leq 6
, by Lemma
2.3.9
and (
5.4.16
), that
AB_{j}\subseteq\hat{H}_{j}((A+1)B_{j}).
Using this with (
5.3.3
), (
5.4.12
) and (
5.4.15
), gives

(5.4.66)

\displaystyle AU^{j}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{j}^{-1}(AB_{j})\subseteq\varphi_{j}^{-1}\circ\hat{H}_{j}((A+1)B_{j})

(5.4.67)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{j}^{-1}\circ\hat{H}_{j}\circ\Phi_{j}^{-1}\left(C^{j}_{(7-A)N_{0}}\right)=h_{\ell}\left(C^{j}_{(7-A)N_{0}}\right).

The second inclusion in (
5.4.17
) is immediate from (
5.4.12
). The final inclusion in (
5.4.17
) follows from the definition of
E_{\ell}
and Lemma
5.4.3
. Equation (
5.4.18
) is immediate from (
5.4.17
). For (
5.4.19
), suppose
x\in E_{\ell+1}.
By definition there exists
1\leq m\leq N_{0}
and
j\in J^{m}_{\ell+2}
such that
x\in C^{j,\ell+2}_{N_{0}}.
If
i(j)\in J_{\ell+1}
is as in (
5.4.2
), it follows from Lemma
5.4.3
that
x\in C^{j,\ell+2}_{N_{0}}\subseteq 3U^{i(j)}\subseteq 6U^{i(j)}
and we are done.
∎

5.5.
Construction of the connected manifolds
M_{\ell}

In this section we construct a sequence of connected Finsler manifolds
M_{\ell}
by filling between the connected components of
W_{\ell}
using charts from
W_{1},\dots,W_{\ell-1}.
We define the
M_{\ell}
(as smooth manifolds) inductively, together with topologies
\tau_{\ell}
and diffeomorphisms
p_{\ell}\colon W_{\ell}^{*}\to p_{\ell}(W_{\ell}^{*})\subseteq M_{\ell}
, where
W_{\ell}^{*}
is as in (
5.4.18
). Note that we have only defined
W_{\ell}^{*}
for
\ell\geq 1
so let us declare now that

(5.5.1)

\displaystyle W_{0}^{*}\coloneqq W_{0}=8B_{j_{0}},

where the last equality follows from Remark
5.3.3
. After constructing the
M_{\ell}
as smooth manifolds, we equip them with a suitable Finsler metric (see after Corollary
5.5.4
).

We being by letting
\tau_{0}
be the usual topology on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(5.5.2)

\displaystyle M_{0}=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j_{0}}\mbox{ and }p_{0}\coloneqq\text{Id}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W_{0}^{*}\to M_{0}

Equipping
M_{0}
with
\tau_{0},
it is clear that
(M_{0},\tau_{0})
is a smooth connected manifold and
p_{0}
is a diffeomorphism. Let
\ell\geq 0
and suppose we have constructed
M_{\ell},

\tau_{\ell}
and
p_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W_{\ell}^{*}\to p_{\ell}(W_{\ell}^{*}).
Let us define
M_{\ell+1},\tau_{\ell+1}
and
p_{\ell+1},
starting with
M_{\ell+1}.
Recall the definition of
E_{\ell}
from (
5.4.14
). Define open subsets
E^{\prime}_{\ell}\subseteq E^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}\subseteq E_{\ell}
by

(5.5.3)

\displaystyle E_{\ell}^{\prime}\coloneqq\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell+1}}C_{N_{0}+2}^{j,\ell+1}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell+1}}C_{N_{0}+1}^{j,\ell+1}

and let

(5.5.4)

\displaystyle G_{\ell}=p_{\ell}(E_{\ell})\mbox{ and }G_{\ell}^{\prime}=p_{\ell}(E_{\ell}^{\prime}).

It follows from Proposition
5.4.2
that
E_{\ell}\subseteq W_{\ell}^{*}
so that
G_{\ell}
and
G_{\ell}^{\prime}
are well-defined. Consider a punctured version of
M_{\ell},

(5.5.5)

\displaystyle M_{\ell}^{*}=M_{\ell}\setminus\overline{G_{\ell}^{\prime}}.

Similar to Section
5.3
, the manifold
M_{\ell+1}
will be define by taking the quotient of
M_{\ell}^{*}\sqcup W_{\ell+1}^{*}
with respect to some equivalence relation
\sim
, which we now define. Let

\text{in}_{1}\colon M_{\ell}^{*}\to M_{\ell}^{*}\sqcup W_{\ell+1}^{*}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\text{in}_{2}\colon W_{\ell+1}^{*}\to M_{\ell}^{*}\sqcup W_{\ell+1}^{*}

be the natural injections. Suppose
x,y\in M_{\ell}^{*}\sqcup W_{\ell+1}^{*}
. We declare
x\sim y
if either

(i)

x=y;

(ii)

there are
x^{\prime}\in M_{\ell}^{*}\cap G_{\ell}
and
y^{\prime}\in W_{\ell+1}^{*}
such that
x=\text{in}_{1}(x^{\prime}),\ y=\text{in}_{2}(y^{\prime})
and
y^{\prime}=h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(x^{\prime});

(iii)

there are
x^{\prime}\in W_{\ell+1}^{*}
and
y^{\prime}\in M_{\ell}^{*}\cap G_{\ell}
such that
x=\text{in}_{2}(x^{\prime}),\ y=\text{in}_{1}(y^{\prime})
and
x^{\prime}=h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(y^{\prime}).

It is easy to check that the relation
\sim
defines an equivalence relation on
M_{\ell}^{*}\sqcup W_{\ell+1}^{*}
. Now let

M_{\ell+1}=\left(M_{\ell}^{*}\sqcup W_{\ell+1}^{*}\right)/\sim

and

\Pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}M_{\ell}^{*}\sqcup W_{\ell+1}^{*}\to M_{\ell+1}

be the quotient map induced by the equivalence relation
\sim
. Then, define

\pi_{\ell+1}\coloneqq\Pi\circ\text{in}_{1}\colon M_{\ell}^{*}\to M_{\ell+1},\ p_{\ell+1}\coloneqq\Pi\circ\text{in}_{2}\colon W_{\ell+1}^{*}\to M_{\ell+1}.

Since
\text{in}_{1}
(resp.
\text{in}_{2}
) is injective and
\Pi
is injective when restricted to
\text{in}(M_{\ell}^{*})
(resp.
\text{in}_{2}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})
), we have that
\pi_{\ell+1}
(resp.
p_{\ell+1}
) is invertible. Let
\tau_{\ell+1}
be the coarsest topology on
M_{\ell+1}
such that
\pi_{\ell+1}
and
p_{\ell+1}
are continuous i.e.

(5.5.6)

\displaystyle\tau_{\ell+1}=\{U\subseteq M_{\ell+1}\colon

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1}(U)\mbox{ is an open subset of }M_{\ell}^{*},

(5.5.7)

\displaystyle p_{\ell+1}^{-1}(U)\mbox{ is an open subset of }W_{\ell+1}^{*}\}.

Remark 5.5.1
.

It follows from construction that if
x\in M_{\ell}^{*}
and
y\in W_{\ell+1}^{*}
are such that
\pi_{\ell+1}(x)=p_{\ell+1}(y)
then

(5.5.8)

\displaystyle p_{\ell+1}^{-1}(\pi_{\ell+1}(x))=h_{\ell}(p_{\ell}^{-1}(x))\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1}(p_{\ell+1}(y))=p_{\ell}(h_{\ell}^{-1}(y)).

Lemma 5.5.2
.

The space
(M_{\ell+1},\tau_{\ell+1})
is a smooth manifold and the maps
\pi_{\ell+1}
and
\ p_{\ell+1}
are diffeomorphisms onto their image.

Proof.

We first check that
M_{\ell+1}
is a topological manifold. Both
M_{\ell}^{*}
and
W_{\ell+1}^{*}
are smooth manifolds (being open subsets of
M_{\ell}
and
W_{\ell+1},
respectively). Let
\{(V_{1}^{i},\sigma_{1}^{i})\}_{i\in I_{1}}
be a smooth atlas for
M_{\ell}^{*}
and
\{(V_{2}^{i},\sigma_{2}^{i})\}_{i\in I_{2}}
a smooth atlas for
W_{\ell+1}^{*}.
For each
i_{1}\in I_{1}
and
i_{2}\in I_{2}
the sets
\pi_{\ell+1}(V_{1}^{i})
,
p_{\ell+1}(V_{2}^{i_{2}})
are open in
M_{\ell+1}
and by construction

M_{\ell+1}\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I_{1}}\pi_{\ell+1}(V_{1}^{i})\cup\bigcup_{i\in I_{2}}p_{\ell+1}(V_{2}^{i}).

To see that
M_{\ell+1}
is locally Euclidean it suffices to show

\pi_{\ell+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}M_{\ell}^{*}\to\pi_{\ell+1}(M_{\ell}^{*})\mbox{ and }p_{\ell+1}\colon W_{\ell+1}^{*}\to p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})\mbox{ are homeomorphisms.}

We will show all the details in the case of
\pi_{\ell+1},
the arguments for
p_{\ell+1}
are similar and we omit them. Let
U\subseteq M_{\ell}^{*}
open. It suffices to show
\pi_{\ell+1}(U)\in\tau_{\ell+1}
. Clearly,
\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1}(\pi_{\ell+1}(U))=U
is open in
M_{\ell}^{*}
by definition. By Remark
5.5.1
we have

p_{\ell+1}^{-1}(\pi_{\ell+1}(U))=h_{\ell}(p_{\ell}^{-1}(U)).

By Proposition
5.4.2
and the induction hypothesis we have that
h_{\ell}\colon E_{\ell}\to W_{\ell+1}^{*}
and
p_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}W_{\ell}^{*}\to p_{\ell}(W_{\ell}^{*})
are homeomorphism. Thus,
p_{\ell+1}^{-1}(\pi_{\ell+1}(U))
is open. Since both
\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1}(\pi_{\ell+1}(U))
and
p_{\ell+1}^{-1}(\pi_{\ell+1}(U))
are open, we conclude by definition that
\pi_{\ell+1}(U)\in\tau_{\ell+1}.

Let us now check that
M_{\ell+1}
is Hausdorff. Let
x,y\in M_{\ell+1}.
If
x,y\in\pi_{\ell+1}(M_{\ell}^{*})
or
x,y\in p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})
then we can find disjoint open sets
V_{x}\ni x
,
V_{y}\ni y
in
M_{\ell+1}
by using the Hausdorff properties in
M_{\ell}^{*}
and
W_{\ell+1}^{*}
, respectively. Suppose then that

x\in\pi_{\ell+1}(M_{\ell^{*}})\setminus p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})\mbox{ and }y\in p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})\setminus\pi_{\ell+1}(M_{\ell}^{*}).

We claim that

(5.5.9)

\displaystyle x\in M_{\ell+1}\setminus\overline{p_{\ell+1}(h_{\ell}(E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}))}\mbox{ and }y\in p_{\ell+1}(h_{\ell}(E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime})).

Observing that
E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}
is open in
W_{\ell}
and
h_{\ell},\ p_{\ell+1}
are homeomorphism, the sets in (
5.5.9
) are disjoint open sets in
M_{\ell+1}
. Thus, (
5.5.9
) is sufficient to prove
M_{\ell+1}
is Hausdorff. The first part of (
5.5.9
) follow since

\overline{p_{\ell+1}(h_{\ell}(E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}))}=p_{\ell+1}(h_{\ell}(\overline{E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}}))\subseteq p_{\ell+1}(h_{\ell}(E_{\ell}))=p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}).

To see that the second part holds, suppose towards a contradiction that
y=p_{\ell+1}(h_{\ell}(z))
for some
z\in E_{\ell}\setminus E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq E_{\ell}\setminus\overline{E_{\ell}^{\prime}}.
By (
5.5.4
) and (
5.5.5
), we have
p_{\ell}(z)\in M_{\ell}^{*}.
Notice also that

(5.5.10)

\displaystyle h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(p_{\ell}(z))=h_{\ell}(z)=p_{\ell+1}^{-1}(y).

It follows from the definition of
\sim
that

y=\pi_{\ell+1}(p_{\ell}(z))\in\pi_{\ell+1}(M_{\ell}^{*}),

which is a contradiction. This concludes the proof that
M_{\ell+1}
is a topological manifold.

We now show that
M_{\ell+1}
is smooth by showing the atlas

(5.5.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\{(\pi_{\ell+1}(V_{1}^{i}),\sigma_{1}^{i}\circ\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i\in I_{1}\}\cup\{(p_{\ell+1}(V_{2}^{i}),\sigma_{2}^{i}\circ p_{\ell+1}^{-1})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i\in I_{2}\}

is a smooth. Consider charts
(B,\phi),(B^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}
with
B\cap B^{\prime}\neq\emptyset.
If
B=\pi_{\ell+1}(V_{1}^{i})
and
B^{\prime}=\pi_{\ell+1}(V_{1}^{i^{\prime}})
for some
i,i^{\prime}\in I_{1}
, the associated transition maps are smooth using by the smoothness of transition maps for
M_{\ell}^{*}
. Similarly, if
B=p_{\ell+1}(V_{2}^{i})
and
B^{\prime}=p_{\ell+1}(V_{2}^{i^{\prime}})
for some
i,i^{\prime}\in I_{2},
the associated transition maps are smooth by using the smooth of transition maps for
W_{\ell+1}^{*}.
Suppose then that
B=\pi_{\ell+1}(V_{1}^{i})
and
B=p_{\ell+1}(V_{2}^{i^{\prime}})
for some
i\in I_{1}
and
i^{\prime}\in I_{2}.
By assumption the maps
p_{\ell},\sigma_{1}^{i}
and
\sigma_{2}^{i^{\prime}}
are smooth. By Proposition
5.4.2
the same is true of
h_{\ell}.
Thus, we have transition maps

(5.5.12)

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}^{i^{\prime}}\circ p_{\ell+1}^{-1}\circ\pi_{\ell+1}\circ\sigma_{1}^{i}|_{\sigma_{1}^{i}(V_{1}^{i})}=\sigma_{2}^{i^{\prime}}\circ h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}\circ\sigma_{1}^{i}|_{\sigma_{1}(V_{1}^{i})}

and

(5.5.13)

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}^{i}\circ\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1}\circ p_{\ell+1}\circ\sigma_{2}^{i^{\prime}}|_{\sigma_{2}^{i^{\prime}}(V_{2}^{i^{\prime}})}=\sigma_{1}^{i}\circ p_{\ell}\circ h_{\ell}^{-1}\circ\sigma_{2}^{i^{\prime}}|_{\sigma_{2}^{i^{\prime}}(V_{2}^{i^{\prime}})}

which are all smooth. It now follows that
\mathcal{A}
is a smooth atlas.
∎

Lemma 5.5.3
.

The map
f_{\ell}\colon M_{\ell}\to M_{\ell+1}
defined by

(5.5.14)

\displaystyle f_{\ell}(x)=\begin{cases}p_{\ell+1}\circ h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)&\mbox{ if }x\in G_{\ell}\\
\pi_{\ell+1}(x)&\mbox{ if }x\in M_{\ell}^{*}\end{cases}

is a well-defined diffeomorphism and satisfies

(5.5.15)

\displaystyle f(G_{\ell})=p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*}).

Proof.

It is immediate from the definition of
\sim
that if
x\in M_{\ell}^{*}\cap G_{\ell}
then
\text{in}_{1}(x)\sim\text{in}_{2}(h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)).
Recalling the definition of
\Pi
,
\pi_{\ell+1}
and
p_{\ell+1},
this implies
\pi_{\ell+1}(x)=p_{\ell+1}\circ h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)
so that
f_{\ell}
is well-defined. It follows from (
5.4.18
), (
5.5.4
) and (
5.5.14
) that

f_{\ell}(G_{\ell})=p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*}).

It only remains to check that
f_{\ell}
is a diffeomorphism. Since each of the maps
\pi_{\ell+1},p_{\ell+1},h_{\ell}
and
p_{\ell}
are diffeomorphisms on their images,
f_{\ell}
is a local diffeomorphism. To prove
f_{\ell}
is a global diffeomorphism we just need to show that it is bijective. To see that
f_{\ell}
is injective, suppose
x,y\in M_{\ell}
are distinct. If
x,y\in G_{\ell}
or
x,y\in M_{\ell}^{*}
then
f_{\ell}(x)\neq f_{\ell}(y)
since
p_{\ell+1}\circ h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}
and
\pi_{\ell+1}
are injective. Thus, it suffices to consider the case
x\in G_{\ell}\setminus M_{\ell}^{*}=\bar{G_{\ell}^{\prime}}
and
y\in M_{\ell}^{*}\setminus G_{\ell}.
In this case, if
f_{\ell}(x)=f_{\ell}(y)
then by Remark
5.5.1
we have

(5.5.16)

\displaystyle x=p_{\ell}\circ h_{\ell}^{-1}\circ h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)=\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1}\circ p_{\ell+1}\circ h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)=y,

a contradiction. To see that
f_{\ell}
is surjective, we have by (
5.5.14
) and (
5.5.15
) that

f_{\ell}(M_{\ell})\supseteq f_{\ell}(G_{\ell})\cup f_{\ell}(M_{\ell}^{*})=p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})\cup\pi_{\ell+1}(M_{\ell}^{*})=M_{\ell+1}.

∎

By assumption
M_{\ell}
is connected and by Lemma
5.5.3
,
M_{\ell+1}
is homeomorphic to
M_{\ell}.
In particular, we obtain the following which closes the inductive step.

Corollary 5.5.4
.

The space
M_{\ell+1}
is connected.

From now on we will view
M_{\ell}
as equipped with the above smooth structure. In particular, if
x\in M_{\ell}
, the tangent space
T_{x}M_{\ell}
is well-defined. We equip
M_{\ell}
with a Finsler metric by defining, for each
x\in M_{\ell},
a norm
\|\cdot\|_{M_{\ell},x}
on
T_{x}M_{\ell}
as follows. If
\ell=0
and
x\in M_{0}
we simply let

(5.5.17)

\displaystyle\|\cdot\|_{M_{0},x}=\|\cdot\|_{j_{0}}.

For
\ell\geq 1,
let
\{\theta_{1}^{\ell},\theta_{2}^{\ell}\}
be a partition of unity subordinate to
\{p_{\ell}(W_{\ell}^{*}),\pi_{\ell}(M_{\ell-1}^{*})\}.
Then, for
x\in M_{\ell}
and
v\in T_{x}M_{\ell},
define

(5.5.18)

\displaystyle\hskip 21.49997pt\|v\|_{M_{\ell},x}\coloneqq\theta_{1}^{\ell}(x)\|D[p_{\ell}^{-1}](x)\cdot v\|_{W_{\ell},p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)}+\theta_{2}^{\ell}(x)\|D[\pi_{\ell}^{-1}](x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell-1},\pi_{\ell}^{-1}(x)}.

By Lemma
5.5.2
both
\pi_{\ell}
and
p_{\ell}
are diffeomorphisms so the metric is well-defined. With this metric we have the following.

Lemma 5.5.5
.

Let
\ell\geq 0.
For each
x\in W_{\ell}^{*},
the map
Dp_{\ell}(x)\colon T_{x}W_{\ell}\to T_{p_{\ell}(x)}M_{\ell}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz. Similarly, for each
x\in M_{\ell-1}^{*},
the map
D\pi_{\ell}(x)\colon T_{x}M_{\ell-1}\to T_{\pi_{\ell}(x)}M_{\ell}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz.

Proof.

Let
x\in W_{\ell}^{*}
and
v\in T_{x}W_{\ell}.
If
p_{\ell}(x)\not\in\pi_{\ell}(M_{\ell-1}^{*})
then
\theta_{2}^{\ell}(p_{\ell}(x))=0
and
\theta_{1}^{\ell}(p_{\ell}(x))=1.
Hence
\|Dp_{\ell}(x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell},p_{\ell}(x)}=\|v\|_{W_{\ell},x}
and there is nothing to show. Thus, we may suppose
p_{\ell}(x)\in\pi_{\ell}(M_{\ell-1}^{*}).
Let
y\in M_{\ell-1}^{*}
such that
p_{\ell}(x)=\pi_{\ell}(y).
Then, by (
5.5.18
),

(5.5.19)

\displaystyle\|Dp_{\ell}(x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell},p_{\ell}(x)}

\displaystyle=\theta_{1}^{\ell}(p_{\ell}(x))\|v\|_{W_{\ell},x}

(5.5.20)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\theta_{2}^{\ell}(p_{\ell}(x))\|D[\pi_{\ell}^{-1}\circ p_{\ell}](x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell-1},y}.

Let us focus on the second term. From Remark
5.5.1
we have that
\pi_{\ell}^{-1}\circ p_{\ell}(x)=p_{\ell-1}\circ h_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x),
hence,

(5.5.21)

\displaystyle D[\pi_{\ell}^{-1}\circ p_{\ell}](x)=D[p_{\ell-1}\circ h_{\ell-1}^{-1}](x)=Dp_{\ell-1}(h_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x))\circ D[h_{\ell-1}^{-1}](x).

We claim that

p_{\ell-1}(h_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x))\not\in\pi_{\ell-1}(M_{\ell-2}^{*}).

Indeed, suppose towards a contradiction that there exists
y\in M_{\ell-2}^{*}
such that
p_{\ell-1}(h_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x))=\pi_{\ell-1}(y).
By Remark
5.5.1
and the definition of
W_{\ell}^{*}
in (
5.4.18
), it follows that

(5.5.22)

\displaystyle h_{\ell-2}\circ p_{\ell-2}^{-1}(y)=p_{\ell-1}^{-1}\circ\pi_{\ell-1}(y)=h_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x)\in E_{\ell-1}.

In particular, by (
5.4.14
) and Lemma
5.4.3
, there exists an index
j\in J_{\ell}
such that
h_{\ell-2}\circ p_{\ell-2}^{-1}(y)\in C^{j,\ell}_{N_{0}}\subseteq 3U^{i(j)},
with
i(j)\in J_{\ell-1}
as in (
5.4.2
). Then, (
5.4.14
) and the first inclusion in (
5.4.17
) imply

p_{\ell-2}^{-1}(y)\in C^{i(j),\ell-1}_{4N_{0}}\subseteq\overline{E_{\ell-2}^{\prime}},

which gives
y\in\overline{G_{\ell-2}^{\prime}}
by (
5.5.4
). This contradicts the definition of
M_{\ell-2}^{*}
in (
5.5.5
), and proves the claim.

The claim gives that
\theta_{2}^{\ell-1}(p_{\ell-1}\circ h_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x))=0.
From this and the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{M_{\ell-1},\cdot}
in (
5.5.18
), it follows that
Dp_{\ell-1}(h_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x))
is an isometry. Returning to (
5.5.21
), we now have

(5.5.23)

\displaystyle\|D[\pi_{\ell}^{-1}\circ p_{\ell}](x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell-1},y}=\|D[h_{\ell-1}^{-1}](x)\cdot v\|_{W_{\ell-1},h_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x)}.

Applying Proposition
5.4.2
gives

(5.5.24)

\displaystyle(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\|v\|_{W_{\ell},x}\leq\|D[\pi_{\ell}^{-1}\circ p_{\ell}](x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell-1},y}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|v\|_{W_{\ell},x}.

The fact that
Dp_{\ell}(x)
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz follows by plugging this estimate into (
5.5.19
) and using the fact that
\theta_{1}^{\ell}+\theta_{2}^{\ell}=1
. The proof for
D\pi_{\ell}(x)
is virtually identical, we omit the details.
∎

Since
M_{\ell}
is a connected manifold, it is path connected. In particular, we can define the path metric. For
x,y\in M_{\ell},
let
\Gamma_{x,y,\ell}
be the collection of all differentiable curves
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M_{\ell}
such that
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(1)=y.
Let
d_{\ell}
be the path metric on
M_{\ell}
defined by

(5.5.25)

\displaystyle d_{\ell}(x,y)=\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{x,y,\ell}}\int_{0}^{1}\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\,dt

Lemma 5.5.6
.

If
x\in M_{\ell}
and
v\in T_{x}M_{\ell}
then

(5.5.26)

\displaystyle(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\|v\|_{M_{\ell},x}\leq\|Df_{\ell}(x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell+1},f_{\ell}(x)}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|v\|_{M_{\ell},x}.

In particular,
f_{\ell}\colon M_{\ell}\to M_{\ell+1}
is bi-Lipschitz. Assume further that
f_{\ell}(x)\in M_{\ell+1}\setminus\overline{p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})}
. Then,

(5.5.27)

\displaystyle\|Df_{\ell}(x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell+1},f_{\ell}(x)}=\|v\|_{M_{\ell},x}

Proof.

Fix a pair
x\in M_{\ell}
and
v\in T_{x}M_{\ell}.
We start with (
5.5.26
). Suppose first
x\in G_{\ell}
so that
f_{\ell}(x)=p_{\ell+1}\circ h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)
by (
5.5.14
). By Proposition
5.4.2
, Lemma
5.5.5
and (
5.5.18
) we have

(5.5.28)

\displaystyle\|Df_{\ell}(x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell+1},f_{\ell}(x)}

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|D[h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}](x)\cdot v\|_{W_{\ell+1},h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)}

(5.5.29)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|D[p_{\ell}^{-1}](x)\cdot v\|_{W_{\ell},p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|v\|_{M_{\ell},x}.

The argument for the lower estimate is similar. If instead
x\in M_{\ell}^{*},
so that
f_{\ell}(x)=\pi_{\ell+1}(x)
by (
5.5.14
), we can apply Lemma
5.5.5
to
D\pi_{\ell+1}
which gives (
5.5.26
) immediately.

We now prove (
5.5.27
). Suppose
f_{\ell}(x)\in M_{\ell+1}\setminus\overline{p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})}
and let
V
be an open neighbourhood of
x
such that
f_{\ell}(V)\subseteq M_{\ell+1}\setminus\overline{p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})}.
By (
5.5.15
) we have
f_{\ell}(G_{\ell})=p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*}).
It follows that
V\subseteq M_{\ell}^{*}
and
f_{\ell}|_{V}=\pi_{\ell+1}|_{V}
. Thus,

(5.5.30)

\displaystyle I_{n}=D[\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1}\circ f_{\ell}](x)=D[\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1}](f_{\ell}(x))Df_{\ell}(x).

Recall the definitions of
\theta_{i}^{\ell+1}
from above (
5.5.18
). Since
\theta^{\ell+1}_{1}
is supported in
p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})
and
\{\theta_{1}^{\ell+1},\theta^{\ell+1}_{2}\}
is a partition of unity, we have
\theta_{1}^{\ell+1}(f_{\ell}(x))=0
and
\theta_{2}^{\ell+1}(f_{\ell}(x))=1.
In particular, by (
5.5.18
) and (
5.5.30
), we have

(5.5.31)

\displaystyle\|Df_{\ell}(x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell+1},f_{\ell}(x)}=\|D[\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1}](f_{\ell}(x))Df_{\ell}(x)\cdot v\|_{M_{\ell},\pi_{\ell+1}^{-1}(f_{\ell}(x))}=\|v\|_{M_{\ell},x}.

∎

We introduce an important collection of charts on
M_{\ell}
(not covering
M_{\ell}
) which roughly speaking correspond to the location of
W_{\ell}
in
M_{\ell}.
Recall the definitions of
\lambda U^{j,\ell}
and
\varphi_{j,\ell}
from Section
5.3
. For
0\leq\lambda\leq 6
,
\ell\geq 0
and
j\in J_{\ell}
let

(5.5.32)

\displaystyle\lambda D^{j}=p_{\ell}(\lambda U^{j}).

Furthermore, let

(5.5.33)

\displaystyle\psi_{j}\coloneqq\varphi_{j}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}\colon 6D^{j}\to 6B_{j}.

We prove some basic properties concerning the maps
\psi_{j}
and the sets
\lambda D^{j}.
The first lemma tells us that the
I_{i,j}
act as transitions maps these coordinate charts in
M_{\ell}.

Lemma 5.5.7
.

Let
\ell\geq 1,

i,j\in J_{\ell}
and suppose
x\in 6B_{i}
is such that
\psi_{i}^{-1}(x)\in 6D^{j}.
Then
x\in\Omega_{j,i}
and

I_{j,i}(x)=\psi_{j}(\psi_{i}^{-1}(x)).

Proof.

Since
\psi_{i}^{-1}(x)\in 6D^{j}
, it follows from (
5.5.32
) and (
5.5.33
) that
\varphi_{i}^{-1}(x)\in 6U^{j}.
In particular
x\in\Omega_{j,i}
and
\varphi_{j}(\varphi_{i}^{-1}(x))=I_{j,i}(x)
by Remark
5.3.4
. Using this and again (
5.5.33
), we get

(5.5.34)

\displaystyle\psi_{j}(\psi_{i}(x))=\varphi_{j}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}\circ p_{\ell}\circ\varphi_{i}^{-1}(x)=\varphi_{j}(\varphi_{i}^{-1}(x))=I_{j,i}(x).

∎

Lemma 5.5.8
.

Let
\ell\geq 0
,
j\in J_{\ell+1}
and suppose
x\in M_{\ell}
is such that
f_{\ell}(x)\in 6D^{j}.
Then,

(5.5.35)

\displaystyle f_{\ell}(x)=\psi_{j}^{-1}(x)\circ\hat{H}_{j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ\psi_{i(j)}(x)

with
i(j)
as in (
5.4.2
),
K_{i(j),j}
as in Lemma
5.4.1
and
\hat{H}_{j}
as in Proposition
5.4.2
.

Proof.

By (
5.5.32
) and (
5.4.18
) we have
f_{\ell}(x)\in 6D^{j}=p_{\ell+1}(6U^{j})\subseteq p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})
. Equation (
5.5.15
) then implies
x\in G_{\ell}
so that
f_{\ell}(x)=p_{\ell+1}\circ h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(x).
Since
h_{\ell}(p_{\ell}^{-1}(x))=p_{\ell+1}^{-1}(f_{\ell}(x))\in 6U^{j},
It follows from the first inclusion in (
5.4.17
) that there exists
p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)\in C_{N_{0}}^{j}
. Now, using (
5.4.15
) and (
5.5.33
), we get

(5.5.36)

\displaystyle f_{\ell}(x)=p_{\ell+1}\circ\varphi_{j}^{-1}\circ\hat{H}_{j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ\varphi_{i(j)}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(x)=\psi_{j}^{-1}\circ\hat{H}_{j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ\psi_{i(j)}(x)

∎

If
j\in J_{\ell+1}
and
i(j)\in J_{\ell}
is as in (
5.4.2
), it follows from the triangle inequality that
6B^{j}\subseteq 3B^{i(j)}.
The follow lemma tells us that the corresponding charts in
M_{\ell}
satisfy a similar nesting property (up to pushing
3D^{i(j)}
forward by
f_{\ell}
).

Lemma 5.5.9
.

If
\ell\geq 0
and
j\in J_{\ell+1}
then
6D^{j}\subseteq f_{\ell}(3D^{i(j)})
. Moreover,

(5.5.37)

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell+1}}6D^{j}\subseteq p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})\subseteq f_{\ell}\left(\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}3D^{j}\right)\subseteq f_{\ell}(p_{\ell}(W_{\ell}^{*})).

Proof.

Let
\ell\geq 0
and
j\in J_{\ell+1}.
Recalling the definitions of
G_{\ell}
and
f_{\ell}
from (
5.5.4
) and (
5.5.14
), respectively, and applying (
5.5.32
) and (
5.4.17
) we have

(5.5.38)

\displaystyle 6D^{j}

\displaystyle=p_{\ell+1}(6U^{j})\subseteq p_{\ell+1}\circ h_{\ell}(E_{\ell}\cap 3U^{i(j)})=p_{\ell+1}\circ h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}\circ p_{\ell}(E_{\ell}\cap 3U^{i(j)})

(5.5.39)

\displaystyle=p_{\ell+1}\circ h_{\ell}\circ p_{\ell}^{-1}(G_{\ell}\cap 3D^{i(j)})=f_{\ell}(G_{\ell}\cap 3D^{i(j)})\subseteq f_{\ell}(3D^{i(j)}).

We now check (
5.5.37
). Recall from (
5.5.15
) that
f_{\ell}(G_{\ell})=p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*}).
Using this with (
5.4.18
) and (
5.4.19
) we get

(5.5.40)

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell+1}}6D^{j}

\displaystyle=p_{\ell+1}\left(\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell+1}}6U^{j}\right)\subseteq p_{\ell+1}(W_{\ell+1}^{*})=f_{\ell}(G_{\ell})=f_{\ell}(p_{\ell}(E_{\ell}))

(5.5.41)

\displaystyle\subseteq f_{\ell}\left(p_{\ell}\left(\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}3U^{j}\right)\right)=f_{\ell}\left(\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}3D^{j}\right).

This take care of the first two inequality in (
5.5.37
). The final inequality follows from (
5.4.18
) since

f_{\ell}\left(\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}3D^{j}\right)=f_{\ell}\left(p_{\ell}\left(\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}3U^{j}\right)\right)\subseteq f_{\ell}(p_{\ell}(W_{\ell}^{*})).

∎

The following lemma will be useful in showing that each
\psi_{j}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz.

Lemma 5.5.10
.

Let
\ell\geq 0,

j\in J_{\ell}
and suppose
\gamma\colon[t_{1},t_{2}]\to 6D^{j}
is a smooth curve. Then,

(5.5.42)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\,dt\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\|\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{2}))-\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{1}))\|_{j}.

Proof.

Since
\gamma(t)\in 6D^{j}
for all
t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
we have that
\psi_{j}\circ\gamma\colon[0,1]\to 6B_{j}
defines a smooth path from
\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{1}))
to
\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{2})).
Using this with Lemma
5.3.6
and Lemma
5.5.5
gives

(5.5.43)

\displaystyle\|\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{2}))-\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{1}))\|_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\|(\psi_{j}\circ\gamma)^{\prime}(t)\|_{j}\,dt

(5.5.44)

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\|D\varphi_{j}(p_{\ell}^{-1}(\gamma(t)))D[p_{\ell}^{-1}](\gamma(t))\cdot\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{j}\,dt

(5.5.45)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\,dt.

∎

Lemma 5.5.11
.

For each
\ell\geq 0
and
j\in J_{\ell},
the map
\psi_{j}\colon 6D^{j}\to 6B_{j}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz when
6D^{j}
is equipped with
d_{\ell}
.

Proof.

We start by showing the following.

Claim 1:
For each
\ell\geq 0
,
j\in J_{\ell}
and
x,y\in 6D^{j}
we have

(5.5.46)

\displaystyle d_{\ell}(x,y)\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|\psi_{j}(x)-\psi_{j}(y)\|_{j}.

Indeed, fix some
\ell\geq 0
,
j\in J_{\ell}
,
x,y\in 6D^{j}
and let
\tilde{\gamma}\colon[0,1]\to 6B_{j}
defined by
\tilde{\gamma}(t)=\psi_{j}(x)+t(\psi_{j}(y)-\psi_{j}(x))
be the straight line connecting
\psi_{j}(x)
with
\psi_{j}(y).
Define a curve
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M_{\ell}
by

\gamma=\psi_{j}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma}=p_{\ell}\circ\varphi_{j}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma}.

Applying Lemma
5.3.6
and Lemma
5.5.5
we get

(5.5.47)

\displaystyle d_{\ell}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\,dt\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}\|\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(t)\|_{j}\,dt

(5.5.48)

\displaystyle=(1+C\varepsilon)\|\psi_{j}(y)-\psi_{j}(x)\|_{j}.

For the reverse estimate, we start by showing a weaker statement.

Claim 2:
For each
\ell\geq 0
,
j\in J_{\ell}
and
x,y\in 3D^{j}
we have

(5.5.49)

\displaystyle\|\psi_{j}(x)-\psi_{j}(y)\|_{j}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)d_{\ell}(x,y).

Indeed, fix some
\ell\geq 0
,
j\in J_{\ell}
and
x,y\in 3D^{j}.
If
x=y
then
\psi_{j}(x)=\psi_{j}(y)
and there is nothing to show so we may suppose
x
and
y
are distinct. Now, let
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M_{\ell}
a smooth curve with
\gamma(0)=x,\ \gamma(1)=y
such that

(5.5.50)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\,dt\leq(1+\varepsilon)d_{\ell}(x,y).

If
\gamma(t)\in 6D^{j}
for all
t\in[0,1]
then the estimate (
5.5.49
) follows immediately from (
5.5.50
) and Lemma
5.5.10
. Suppose instead that there exists
t\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(t)\in\partial(6D^{j}).
In this case we can find
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,1]
such that

(5.5.51)

\displaystyle\gamma(t_{1}),\gamma(t_{2})\in\partial((6-\varepsilon)D^{j})\subseteq 6D^{j}

and

\gamma(t)\in(6-\varepsilon)D^{j}\subseteq 6D^{j}\mbox{ for all }0\leq t<t_{1}\mbox{ and }t_{2}<t\leq 1.

Recalling
\gamma(0)=x\in 3D^{j}
and using (
5.5.51
), we have
\psi_{j}(\gamma(0))\in 3B_{j}
and
\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{1}))\in\partial((6-\varepsilon)B_{j})
. This implies
\|\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{1}))-\psi_{j}(\gamma(0))\|_{j}\geq(3-\varepsilon)r_{\ell}.
Similarly,
\|\psi_{j}(\gamma(1))-\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{2}))\|_{j}\geq(3-\varepsilon)r_{\ell}.
Using these estimates along with Lemma
5.5.10
and (
5.5.50
) gives

(5.5.52)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(1+\varepsilon)d_{\ell}(x,y)&\geq\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\,dt+\int_{t_{2}}^{1}\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\,dt\\
&\geq\frac{1}{1+C\varepsilon}\left(\|\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{1}))-\psi_{j}(\gamma(0))\|_{j}+\|\psi_{j}(\gamma(1))-\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{2}))\|_{j}\right)\\
&\geq\frac{6r_{\ell}}{1+C\varepsilon}.\end{split}

Since
x,y\in 3D^{j}
we have
\psi_{j}(x),\psi_{j}(y)\in 3B_{j}
so that
\|\psi_{j}(x)-\psi_{j}(y)\|_{j}\leq 6r_{\ell}.
This observation and (
5.5.52
) finish the proof of Claim 2 since

(5.5.53)

\displaystyle\|\psi_{j}(x)-\psi_{j}(y)\|\leq 6r_{\ell}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)d_{\ell}(x,y).

We use Claim 2 to prove the following, which completes the proof of the lemma.

Claim 3:
For each
\ell\geq 0
,
j\in J_{\ell}
and
x,y\in 6D^{j}
we have

(5.5.54)

\displaystyle\|\psi_{j}(x)-\psi_{j}(y)\|_{j}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)d_{\ell}(x,y).

The case
\ell=0
is immediate from construction, but let us outline where to find the relevant information. Recall from Theorem
5.1.1
that
J_{0}=\{j_{0}\}.
By (
5.5.17
) and (
5.5.25
) the path metric
d_{0}
is just the metric induced by
\|\cdot\|_{j_{0}}.
By Remark
5.3.3
, (
5.5.2
) and (
5.5.33
) we have
\psi_{j_{0}}=\text{Id}
. Combining these facts gives (
5.5.54
).

Suppose instead that
\ell\geq 1
and fix some
j\in J_{\ell}
and
x,y\in 6D^{j}.
By Lemma
5.5.9
we have
f_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x),f_{\ell-1}^{-1}(y)\in 3D^{i(j)}
so that, by Lemma
5.5.6
and Claim 2,

(5.5.55)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\|\psi_{i(j)}(f_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x))-\psi_{i(j)}(f_{\ell-1}^{-1}(y))\|_{i(j)}

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)d_{\ell-1}(f_{\ell-1}(x),f_{\ell-1}(y))

(5.5.56)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)d_{\ell}(x,y).

By Lemma
5.5.8
,

(5.5.57)

\displaystyle\|\psi_{i(j)}(f_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x))-\psi_{i(j)}(f_{\ell-1}^{-1}(y))\|_{i(j)}

(5.5.58)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt=\|K_{i(j),j}\circ\hat{H}_{j}^{-1}\circ\psi_{j}(x)-K_{i(j),j}\circ\hat{H}_{j}^{-1}\circ\psi_{j}(y)\|_{i(j)},

with
K_{i(j),j}
as in Lemma
5.4.1
and
\hat{H}_{j}
as in Proposition
5.4.2
. Since both
K_{i(j),j}
and
\hat{H}_{j}
are
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, this implies

(5.5.59)

\displaystyle\|\psi_{j}(x)-\psi_{j}(y)\|_{j}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|\psi_{i(j)}(f_{\ell-1}^{-1}(x))-\psi_{i(j)}(f_{\ell-1}^{-1}(y))\|_{i(j)}.

Equations (
5.5.55
) and (
5.5.59
) finish the proof of Claim 3 and hence the proof of the lemma.

∎

Lemma 5.5.12
.

Let
C_{*}
be the constant appearing Lemma
5.5.10
and let
0<\lambda,\mu<6
be real numbers such that
\nu\coloneqq\lambda+(1+C_{*}\varepsilon)\mu<6.
Let
\ell\geq 0,\ j\in J_{\ell}
and
x\in\lambda D^{j}.
If
y\in M_{\ell}
is such that
d_{\ell}(x,y)<\mu r_{\ell}
then

(5.5.60)

\displaystyle y\in\nu D^{j}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the contrapositive, that is, if
y\in M_{\ell}\setminus\nu D^{j}
then
d_{\ell}(x,y)\geq\mu r_{\ell}.
Fix some
y\in M_{\ell}\setminus\nu D^{j}.
Let
\eta>0
and a choose smooth path
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M_{\ell}
with
\gamma(0)=x
,
\gamma(1)=y
and such that

(5.5.61)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\,dt\leq d_{\ell}(x,y)+\eta.

Since
y\not\in\nu D^{j}
there exists
t\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(t)\in\partial(\nu D^{j}).
Let
t_{0}
be the infimum over all such
t.
In this way we have
\gamma(t_{0})\in\partial(\nu B_{j})\mbox{ and }\gamma(t)\in\nu D^{j}\mbox{ for all }0\leq t<t_{0}.
By the definition of
\psi_{j}
and
\nu D^{j}
, we have
x\in\lambda B_{j}
and
\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{0}))\in\partial(\nu B_{j})
so that

(5.5.62)

\displaystyle\|\psi_{j}(x)-\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{0}))\|_{j}\geq(1+C_{*}\varepsilon)\mu r_{\ell}.

Applying Lemma
5.5.10
gives

(5.5.63)

\displaystyle\mu r_{\ell}\leq(1+C_{*}\varepsilon)^{-1}\|\psi_{j}(x)-\psi_{j}(\gamma(t_{0}))\|_{j}\leq\int_{0}^{1}\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\,dt\leq d_{\ell}(x,y)+\eta.

Since
\eta
was arbitrary, this implies
d_{\ell}(x,y)\geq\mu r_{\ell}
and finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In the next section we will need to track the trajectory of points
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
under the maps
f_{\ell}
from (
5.5.14
). To do this we define a sequence of maps
F_{\ell}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M_{\ell}
inductively by setting

(5.5.64)

\displaystyle F_{0}=\text{Id}\mbox{ and }F_{\ell}=f_{\ell-1}\circ F_{\ell-1}\mbox{ for }\ell\geq 1.

Lemma 5.5.13
.

Let
k\geq 0
and let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
F_{k}(x)\in M_{k}\setminus\bigcup_{j\in J_{k}}5D^{j}.
Then,

{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\ell}\left(F_{\ell}(x),\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6D^{j}\right)\geq r_{k}/2

for all
\ell\geq k+1,
where
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}_{\ell}
is the distance in
M_{\ell}
with respect to the path metric
d_{\ell}.

Proof.

Let
B_{k}=B(F_{k}(x),r_{k}/2)
(the ball taken with respect to
d_{k}
) and, for
\ell\geq k+1
, let
B_{\ell}=f_{\ell-1}\circ\dots\circ f_{k+1}(B_{k}).
Since
F_{k}(x)\not\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{k}}5D^{j}
, we have by Lemma
5.5.12
that
d_{k}(F_{k}(x),\bigcup_{j\in J_{k}}4D^{j})\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}r_{k}.
It follows from the definition of
B_{k}
that

(5.5.65)

\displaystyle\overline{B_{k}}\cap\bigcup_{j\in J_{k}}4D^{j}=\emptyset.

Thus, for
\ell\geq k+1,
this and (
5.5.37
) imply

(5.5.66)

\displaystyle\overline{B_{\ell}}\cap\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6D^{j}\subseteq\overline{B_{\ell}}\cap p_{\ell}(W_{\ell}^{*})=\emptyset

so that

(5.5.67)

\displaystyle{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\ell}\left(F_{\ell}(x),\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6D^{j}\right)\geq{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\ell}\left(F_{\ell}(x),\partial(B_{\ell})\right).

Let us estimate
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\ell}\left(F_{\ell}(x),\partial(B_{\ell})\right)
from below. Suppose
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to B_{\ell}
is a smooth curve connecting
F_{\ell}(x)
with
\partial(B_{\ell})
. It follows that

\tilde{\gamma}\coloneqq f_{k}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{\ell-1}^{-1}\circ\gamma\colon[0,1]\to B_{k}

is a smooth curve from
F_{k}(x)
to
\partial(B_{k}).
By (
5.5.66
), for each
k\leq m\leq\ell-1
and each
t\in[0,1]
we have

(5.5.68)

\displaystyle f_{m}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{\ell-1}^{-1}(\gamma(t))\in B_{m}\subseteq M_{m}\setminus p_{m}(W_{m}^{*}).

This and (
5.5.27
) imply

\|\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{k},\tilde{\gamma}(t)}=\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,1].

Hence,

(5.5.69)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{\ell},\gamma(t)}\,dt=\int_{0}^{1}\|\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(t)\|_{M_{k},\tilde{\gamma}(t)}\,dt\geq{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{k}(F_{k}(x),\partial(B_{k}))=r_{k}/2.

Taking the infimum over all such paths implies
{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\ell}\left(F_{\ell}(x),\partial(B_{\ell})\right)\geq r_{k}/2
which, when coupled with (
5.5.67
), finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.5.14
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and suppose
k\geq 0
is the largest integer such
F_{k}(x),F_{k}(y)\in 6D^{j}
for some
j\in J_{k}.
If
d_{k}(F_{k}(x),F_{k}(y))\leq r_{k+1}/2
then

(5.5.70)

\displaystyle F_{k+1}(x),F_{k+1}(y)\not\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{k+1}}5D^{j}

and

(5.5.71)

\displaystyle{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\ell}\left(F_{\ell}(x),\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6D^{j}\right),\ {\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\ell}\left(F_{\ell}(y),\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6D^{j}\right)\geq r_{k+1}/2

for all
\ell\geq k+2,
where
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}_{\ell}
is the distance in
M_{\ell}
with respect to the path metric
d_{\ell}.

Proof.

The inequalities in (
5.5.71
) follow immediately from Lemma
5.5.13
and (
5.5.70
). Let us see how to prove (
5.5.70
). By symmetry we need only show that
F_{k+1}(x)\not\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{k+1}}5D^{j}.
Suppose towards a contradiction that there exists
j\in J_{\ell+1}
such that
F_{k+1}(x)\in 5D^{j}
. By Lemma
5.5.6
we have

(5.5.72)

\displaystyle d_{k+1}(F_{k+1}(x),F_{k+1}(y))\leq(1+C\varepsilon)d_{k}(F_{k}(x),F_{k}(y))\leq 3r_{k+1}/4.

Since
F_{k+1}(x)\in 5D^{j},
Lemma
5.5.12
implies
F_{k+1}(y)\in 6D^{j}
which contradicts the maximality of
k
and proves (
5.5.70
).
∎

5.6.
Construction of the metric
\rho
and the map
g

We turn our attention now to proving Theorem
5.1.1
.

We obtain
g
as a limit of maps
g_{\ell}
defined as follows. Recall the definition of
F_{\ell}
from (
5.5.64
). For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\ell\geq 0
we will denote

x_{\ell}=F_{\ell}(x).

For each
x\in 50B_{j_{0}}
, let

(5.6.1)

\displaystyle\ell(x)=\sup\left\{\ell\geq 0\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x_{\ell}\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6D^{j}\right\},

where we set the supremum over the empty set to be zero. Set
j_{0}(x)=j_{0}
and for each
0\leq\ell\leq\ell(x)
choose an arbitrary index
j_{\ell}(x)
such that
x_{\ell}\in 6D^{j_{\ell}(x)}.
Then, for
\ell\geq 0
, set

(5.6.2)

\displaystyle g_{\ell}(x)=\begin{cases}\alpha_{j_{\ell}(x)}\circ\psi_{j_{\ell}(x)}\circ F_{\ell}(x)&\mbox{ if }\ell\leq\ell(x);\\
\alpha_{j_{\ell(x)}(x)}\circ\psi_{j_{\ell(x)}(x)}\circ F_{\ell(x)}(x)&\mbox{ if }\ell>\ell(x).\end{cases}

See Figure
5.6.1
.

...
...
(
5.5.2
)
M_{0}
M_{1}
M_{2}
M_{\ell}
6B^{j,\ell}\subset X
6B_{j,\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
\alpha_{j}
g_{\ell}
(
5.6.2
)
F_{\ell}
(
5.5.64
)
f_{1}
f_{2}
(
5.5.14
)
f_{3}
6D_{j}
\psi_{j}\hskip 5.0pt6D_{j}\to 6B_{j,\ell}
(
5.5.33
)
.
.

Figure 5.6.1.
The map
g_{\ell}

Lemma 5.6.1
.

Let
x\in 6B_{j_{0}}
,
0\leq\ell\leq\ell(x)
and
i\in J_{\ell}
such that
x_{\ell}\in 6D^{i}
. Then,

(5.6.3)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g_{\ell}(x),\alpha_{i}\circ\psi_{i}(x_{\ell}))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Proof.

Let
j=j_{\ell}(x).
By definition
\psi_{j}(x_{\ell})=I_{j,i}(\psi_{i}(x_{\ell}))=\tilde{I}_{j,i}(\psi_{j}(x_{\ell}))\in 6B_{j}.
By (
5.1.4
), and Proposition
5.2.1
we have
d_{X}(\alpha_{j}(\beta_{j}(x)),x)\leq\delta r_{\ell}\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell}
for all
x\in 10B^{j}
and
\|\tilde{I}_{j,i}(x)-\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i}(x)\|_{j}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}
for all
x\in 8B_{i}.
Also, by definition we have
\psi_{i}(x_{\ell})\in 8B_{i}.
Combing the above, we get

(5.6.4)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g_{\ell}(x),\alpha_{i}\circ\psi_{i}(x_{\ell}))

\displaystyle=d_{X}(\alpha_{j}(\psi_{j}(x_{\ell})),\alpha_{i}(\psi_{i}(x_{\ell})))

(5.6.5)

\displaystyle=d_{X}(\alpha_{j}(\tilde{I}_{j,i}(\psi_{i}(x_{\ell}))),\alpha_{i}(\psi_{i}(x_{\ell})))

(5.6.6)

\displaystyle\leq d_{X}(\alpha_{j}(\beta_{j}(\alpha_{i}(\psi_{i}(x_{\ell})))),\alpha_{i}(\psi_{i}(x_{\ell})))+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

∎

Lemma 5.6.2
.

For each
\ell\geq 0
and
x\in 50B_{j_{0}}
we have

(5.6.7)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g_{\ell}(x),g_{\ell+1}(x))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

In particular, the limit
g\coloneqq\lim_{\ell\to\infty}g_{\ell}\colon 50B_{j_{0}}\to X
exists, takes values in
50B^{j_{0}},
and satisfies

d_{X}(g(x),g_{\ell}(x))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Proof.

If
x\in 50B_{j_{0}}\setminus 6B_{j_{0}}
then
g_{\ell}(x)=g_{0}(x)
for all
\ell\geq 0
and there is nothing to show. Suppose then that
x\in 6B_{j_{0}}
and fix some
\ell\geq 0
. If
\ell+1>\ell(x)
then
g_{\ell+1}(x)=g_{\ell}(x)
and there is nothing to show. Let us suppose instead that
\ell+1\leq\ell(x)
so that
f_{\ell}(x_{\ell})=x_{\ell+1}\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell+1}}6D^{j}.
Let
j=j_{\ell+1}(x)
as above (
5.6.2
). It follows from Lemma
5.5.8
and (
5.6.2
) that

(5.6.8)

\displaystyle g_{\ell+1}(x)=\alpha_{j}\circ\psi_{j}\circ f_{\ell}(x_{\ell})=\alpha_{j}\circ\hat{H}_{j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ\psi_{i(j)}(x_{\ell}).

Using this with (
5.1.4
), (
5.4.16
) and Lemma
5.4.1
, we have

(5.6.9)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g_{\ell+1}(x),\alpha_{i(j)}(\psi_{i(j)}(x_{\ell})))

\displaystyle\leq d_{X}(g_{\ell+1}(x),\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}\circ\alpha_{i(j)}\circ\psi_{i(j)}(x_{\ell}))+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}

(5.6.10)

\displaystyle\leq d_{X}(g_{\ell+1}(x),\alpha_{j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ\psi_{i(j)}(x_{\ell}))+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}

(5.6.11)

\displaystyle\leq d_{X}(g_{\ell+1}(x),\alpha_{j}\circ H_{j}\circ K_{j,i(j)}\circ\psi_{i(j)}(x_{\ell}))+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}

(5.6.12)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

By Lemma
5.6.1
,
d_{X}(g_{\ell}(x),\alpha_{i(j)}(\psi_{i(j)}(x_{\ell}))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}
and the result now follow from the triangle inequality.
∎

We now construct the semi-metric
\phi_{\infty}
and the metric
\rho.
We begin by defining a sequence of semi-metrics
\phi_{\ell}
on
M_{\ell}
as follows. For
\ell=0
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
set

(5.6.13)

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x,y)\coloneqq\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}.

Let
\ell\geq 1
and suppose we have defined
\phi_{\ell}
on
M_{\ell}
. We define
\phi_{\ell+1}
on
M_{\ell+1}
by setting

(5.6.14)

\displaystyle\phi_{\ell+1}(x,y)=\begin{cases}d_{{\ell+1}}(x,y)&\mbox{ if }x,y\in 6D^{j}\mbox{ for some }j\in J_{\ell+1};\\
\phi_{\ell}(f_{\ell}^{-1}(x),f_{\ell}^{-1}(y))&\mbox{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Recall the definition of
F_{\ell}
in (
5.5.64
) and let
\bar{\phi}_{\ell}
denote the pull-back of
\phi_{\ell}
by
F_{\ell}
, that is,

(5.6.15)

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}_{\ell}=F^{*}_{\ell}\phi_{\ell}.

Lemma 5.6.3
.

If
\ell\geq 0
and
x,y\in M_{\ell}
then

(5.6.16)

\displaystyle|\phi_{\ell+1}(f_{\ell}(x),f_{\ell}(y))-\phi_{\ell}(x,y)|\lesssim\varepsilon\min\{\phi_{\ell}(x,y),r_{\ell}\}.

Furthermore, the limit
\phi_{\infty}\coloneqq\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\bar{\phi}_{\ell}
exists and for each
\ell\geq 0
