and
w,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have

(5.6.17)

\displaystyle|\phi_{\infty}(x,y)-\phi_{\ell}(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Proof.

If there does not exist
j\in J_{\ell+1}
such that
f_{\ell}(x),f_{\ell}(y)\in 6D^{j}
then (
5.6.16
) is immediate from (
5.6.14
). Suppose instead that there exists
j\in J_{\ell+1}
such that
f_{\ell}(x),f_{\ell}(y)\in 6D^{j}
. Lemma
5.5.9
gives
x,y\in 3D^{i(j)}
so that, using (
5.6.14
) and Lemma
5.5.6
, we have

(5.6.18)

\displaystyle|\phi_{\ell+1}(f_{\ell}(x),f_{\ell}(y))-\phi_{\ell}(x,y)|

\displaystyle=|d_{\ell+1}(f_{\ell}(x),f_{\ell}(y))-d_{\ell}(x,y)|

(5.6.19)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon d_{\ell}(x,y)=\varepsilon\phi_{\ell}(x,y).

Since
x,y\in 3D^{i(j)}
we also have
d_{\ell}(x,y)\lesssim r_{\ell}
. This and the above estimate complete the proof of (
5.6.16
). To see that
\phi_{\infty}
exists, we observe from (
5.6.15
) and (
5.6.16
) that

(5.6.20)

\displaystyle|\bar{\phi}_{\ell+1}(x,y)-\bar{\phi}_{\ell}(w,z)|=|\phi_{\ell+1}(f_{\ell}(F_{\ell}(x)),f_{\ell}(F_{\ell}(y)))-\phi_{\ell}(F_{\ell}(x),F_{\ell}(y))|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

From this we also get (
5.6.17
), noting that
\sum_{m=\ell}^{\infty}r_{m}\lesssim r_{\ell}.

∎

Lemma 5.6.4
.

For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
let

k(x,y)=\sup\{\ell\geq 0\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x_{k},y_{k}\in 6D^{j}\mbox{ for some }j\in J_{\ell}\}.

For each
\ell\geq k(x,y)
we have

(5.6.21)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(x,y)=\bar{\phi}_{\ell}(x,y)=\phi_{\ell}(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})=d_{\ell}(x_{\ell},y_{\ell}).

Proof.

This is immediate from (
5.6.14
)
∎

Lemma 5.6.5
.

Let
x,y\in 50B_{j_{0}}
and
k(x,y)
as in Lemma
5.6.4
. Then,

(5.6.22)

\displaystyle|d_{X}(g(x),g(y))-\phi_{\infty}(x,y)|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k(x,y)}

Furthermore, if
x_{\ell}\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6D^{j}
for some
\ell\geq 0
then

(5.6.23)

\displaystyle|d_{X}(g(x),g(y))-\phi_{\infty}(x,y)|\lesssim\varepsilon\left(\phi_{\infty}(x,y)+r_{\ell}\right).

Proof.

We start with (
5.6.22
). If
k(x,y)=0
then
g_{0}(x,y)=\alpha_{j_{0}}(x,y)
by definition and
\phi_{\infty}(x,y)=\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}
by (
5.6.13
) and Lemma
5.6.4
. Equation (
5.6.22
) now follows since
\alpha_{j_{0}}\colon 100B_{j_{0}}\to 100B^{j_{0}}
is a
\delta r_{0}
-GHA. Suppose instead that
k=k(x,y)\geq 1.
Let
j\in J_{k}
is such that
x_{k},y_{k}\in 6D^{j}.
By Lemma
5.6.1
we have

d_{X}(g_{k}(x),\alpha_{j}\circ\psi_{j}(x_{k}))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad d_{X}(g_{k}(y),\alpha_{j}\circ\psi_{j}(y_{k}))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

Using this with Lemma
5.5.11
, Lemma
5.6.2
, (
5.6.21
) and the fact that
\alpha_{j}
is a
\delta r_{k}
isometry, the left-hand side of (
5.6.22
) is at most

(5.6.24)

\displaystyle|d_{X}(g(x),g(y))-d_{X}(g_{k}(x),g_{k}(y))|

(5.6.25)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+|d_{X}(g_{k}(x),g_{k}(y))-d_{X}(\alpha_{j}\circ\psi_{j}(x_{k}),\alpha_{j}\circ\psi_{j}(y_{k}))|

(5.6.26)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+|d_{X}(\alpha_{j}\circ\psi_{j}(x_{k}),\alpha_{j}\circ\psi_{j}(y_{k}))-d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

Let us move onto (
5.6.23
). If
\ell\leq k+1
then since
r_{k}\lesssim r_{\ell}.
Suppose then that
\ell\geq k+2.
Since
x_{\ell}\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6D^{j},
Lemma
5.5.14
implies

(5.6.27)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(x,y)=d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})>r_{k+1}/2\gtrsim r_{k}.

Using this with (
5.6.22
) gives (
5.6.23
).
∎

Lemma 5.6.6
.

Let
\ell\geq 0
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\phi_{\ell}(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\leq r_{\ell}/2
. Suppose also that there exists
j\in J_{\ell}
satisfying
x_{\ell}\in 3D^{j,\ell}.
Then,
y_{\ell}\in 4D^{j,\ell}.

Proof.

Let
k
be the largest integer such that
x_{k},y_{k}\in 6D^{i}
for some
i\in J_{k}.
Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
k\leq\ell-1.
Since
x_{\ell}\in 3D^{j}
we must have
d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})>r_{k+1}/2
by Lemma
5.5.14
. Lemma
5.6.5
then gives

(5.6.28)

\displaystyle r_{\ell}/2\geq\phi_{\ell}(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})=\phi_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})=d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})>r_{k+1}/2\geq r_{\ell}/2

which is a contradiction. Thus, we have
k\geq\ell
, so there exists
i\in J_{\ell}
satisfying
x_{\ell},y_{\ell}\in 6D^{i}.
It follows from (
5.6.14
) that
d_{\ell}(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})=\phi_{\ell}(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})\leq r_{\ell}/2
so that
y_{\ell}\in 4D^{j,\ell}
by Lemma
5.5.12
.
∎

We turn our attention to the metric
\rho,
which we define below.

Definition 5.6.7
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
A
chain
from
x
to
y
is a sequence of points
\{z^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
z^{1}=x
and
z^{N}=y.
Let

\rho(x,y)=\inf\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\phi_{\infty}(x_{i},x_{i+1}),

where the infimum is taken over all chains
\{z^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
from
x
to
y.

The following says that
\rho
is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to
\phi_{\infty}
with constant close to 1.

Lemma 5.6.8
.

Let
\eta>0.
For
\varepsilon
small enough depending on
\eta
we have

(5.6.29)

\displaystyle(1+C\eta)^{-1}\phi_{\infty}(x,y)\leq\rho(x,y)\leq\phi_{\infty}(x,y)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Proof.

The second inequality is true by definition (consider the chain
z^{1}=x
,
z^{2}=y
) so let us prove the first. Before proving (
5.6.29
) for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we will prove it for all
x,y\in 6B_{j_{0}}.
We will show how to get (
5.6.29
) for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
at the end of the proof. Let
\eta>0
, assume
x,y\in 6B_{j_{0}}
are distinct and let

k=k(x,y)

Let
\{z^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
a chain from
x
to
y
such that

(5.6.30)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\rho(x,y)\leq 13r_{k},

where the last inequality follows from Lemma
5.5.11
. For each
\ell\geq 0
and
1\leq i\leq N
let

(5.6.31)

\displaystyle k(i)=k(z^{i},z^{i+1})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad z^{i}_{\ell}=F_{\ell}(z^{i}).

It follows from Lemma
5.6.5
that

(5.6.32)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(x,y)=d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})=d_{k(i)}(z_{k(i)}^{i},z_{k(i)}^{i+1}).

Claim:
We have

(5.6.33)

\displaystyle z^{i}\in 20B_{j_{0}}\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{ for all }1\leq i\leq N;

(5.6.34)

\displaystyle k(i)\geq k-2\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{ for all }1\leq i\leq N-1.

Indeed, suppose (
5.6.33
) is false and let
1<i_{0}<N
be the smallest index such that
z^{i}\not\in 20B_{j_{0}}.
Let
1\leq i_{1}<i_{0}
be the largest integer such that
z^{i_{0}}\in 6B_{j_{0}}.
It follows that
k(i)=0
for all
1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{0}.
Then, by (
5.6.30
) and (
5.6.32
),

(5.6.35)

\displaystyle 14r_{0}\leq d_{0}(z^{i_{0}},z^{i_{1}})\leq\sum_{i=i_{1}}^{i_{0}-1}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})\leq 13r_{k},

which is a contradiction. We now consider (
5.6.34
). If
k\leq 2
then this is immediate. Suppose then that
k\geq 3
and suppose towards a contradiction that there exists
1\leq i\leq N-1
such that
k(i)\leq k-3
. Let
i_{2}
be the smallest such index so that

k(i_{2})\leq k-3\mbox{ and }k(i)\geq k-2\mbox{ for all }1\leq i<i_{2}.

We have

(5.6.36)

\displaystyle d_{k(i_{2})}(z^{i_{2}}_{k(i_{2})},z^{i_{2}+1}_{k(i_{2})})=\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{2}},z^{i_{2}+1})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\rho(x,y)\leq 13r_{k}\leq\frac{13r_{k(i_{2})}}{1000}.

It follows from Lemma
5.5.14
that

{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{k}\left(z^{i_{2}}_{k},\bigcup_{j\in J_{k}}6D^{j}\right)\geq r_{k(i_{2})+1}/2\geq 50r_{k}.

Since
k=k(x,y)
and
z^{1}=x,
there exists
j\in J_{k}
such that
z^{1}\in 6D^{j}.
In particular
i_{2}\geq 2.
Let
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}
be the largest integer such that
z^{i_{3}}_{k}\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{k}}6D^{j}.
It follows that if
i_{3}\leq i<i_{2}
then

(5.6.37)

\displaystyle k-2\leq k(i)\leq k-1.

Hence,

(5.6.38)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=i_{3}}^{i_{2}-1}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\sum_{i=i_{3}}^{i_{2}-1}d_{k}(z^{i}_{k},z^{i+1}_{k})\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}d_{k}(z^{i_{2}}_{k},z^{i_{3}}_{k})\geq 49r_{k}.

This is a contradiction.

Let
\eta>0
to be chosen small enough. We split the proof of (
5.6.29
) into two cases.

Case 1:
Suppose first that
d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})\leq\eta r_{k}.
We claim that

(5.6.39)

\displaystyle k(i)-1\leq k\leq k(i)+2

for all
1\leq i\leq N-1.
Assuming (
5.6.39
) for the moment, by applying (
5.6.32
), Lemma
5.5.6
and Lemma
5.6.5
we get

(5.6.40)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(x,y)

\displaystyle=d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}d_{k}(z_{k}^{i},z_{k}^{i+1})\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}d_{k(i)}(z_{k(i)}^{i},z_{k(i)}^{i+1})

(5.6.41)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)(\tau r_{k}+\rho(x,y)).

Since
\tau
was arbitrary this finishes the proof of (
5.6.29
) in Case 1 up to showing (
5.6.39
). We will prove (
5.6.39
) below.

We get the second inequality from (
5.6.34
), so let us prove the first. Suppose towards a contradiction that there exists
1\leq i\leq N-1
such that
k(i)\geq k+2.
Let
i_{0}
be the smallest such index. We have

(5.6.42)

\displaystyle z_{k+2}^{i_{0}}\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{k+2}}6D^{j}.

Furthermore, for any
1\leq i<i_{0}
, since
k-1\leq k(i)\leq k+1
and
\phi_{k(i)}(z^{i}_{k(i)},z^{i+1}_{k(i)})=d_{k(i)}(z^{i}_{k(i)},z^{i+1}_{k(i)})
, we have by Lemma
5.5.6
that

(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\phi_{k(i)}(z^{i}_{k(i)},z^{i+1}_{k(i)})\leq d_{k+2}(z_{k+2}^{i},z_{k+2}^{i+1})\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\phi_{k(i)}(z^{i}_{k(i)},z^{i+1}_{k(i)})

Using this with (
5.6.42
) and Lemma
5.5.14
gives

(5.6.43)

\displaystyle(1+C\varepsilon)\phi_{k(i_{0}-1)}(z_{k(i_{0}-1)}^{i_{0}-1},z_{k(i_{0}-1)}^{i_{0}})

\displaystyle\geq d_{k+2}(z_{k+2}^{i_{0}-1},z_{k+2}^{i_{0}})

(5.6.44)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{k+2}\left(z_{k+2}^{i_{0}-1},\bigcup_{j\in J_{k+2}}6D^{j}\right)

(5.6.45)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{k+2}\left(x_{k+2},\bigcup_{j\in J_{k+2}}6D^{j}\right)

(5.6.46)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt-\sum_{i=1}^{i_{0}-2}d_{k+2}(z_{k+2}^{i},z_{k+2}^{i+1})

(5.6.47)

\displaystyle\geq r_{k+1}/2-(1+C\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{i_{0}-2}\phi_{k(i)}(z_{k(i)}^{i},z_{k(i)}^{i+1})

(5.6.48)

\displaystyle\geq r_{k+2}/2.

For
\varepsilon
small enough, this implies

(5.6.49)

\displaystyle(1+\varepsilon)\eta r_{k}\geq(1+\varepsilon)\rho(x,y)\geq\phi_{k(i_{0}-1)}(z_{k(i_{0}-1)}^{i_{0}-1},z_{k(i_{0}-1)}^{i_{0}})\geq r_{k}/4\geq r_{k},

which is a contradiction for
\eta
small enough. This finishes the proof of the claim.

Case 2:
Suppose that
d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})>\eta r_{k}.
We will find a new chain
\{w^{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}\subseteq 50B_{j_{0}}
from
x
to
y
such that the following holds.

(1)

We have

(5.6.50)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{K-1}\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})\leq(1+C\eta)\rho(x,y)+C\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y).

(2)

For
1\leq i\leq K-1
let
\tilde{k}(i)
be the largest integer such that there exists
j\in J_{\tilde{k}(i)}
satisfying
w^{i}_{\tilde{k}(i)},w^{i+1}_{\tilde{k}(i)}\in 6D^{j}.
If

I=\{1\leq i\leq K-1\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})\leq\eta^{2}r_{\tilde{k}(i)}\}

then

(5.6.51)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}\varepsilon r_{\tilde{k}(i)}\lesssim\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y).

Before seeing how to construct
\{w^{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}
, let use it to complete the proof of (
5.6.29
) in Case 2. Recall the map
g\colon 50B_{j_{0}}\to X
constructed in Lemma
5.6.2
. If
C^{\prime}
is the constant appearing in (
5.6.22
) and
i\in I^{c}
then by taking
\varepsilon
small enough with respect to
\eta
we have

(5.6.52)

\displaystyle(1+\eta)\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})

\displaystyle\geq(1+C^{\prime}\varepsilon\eta^{-2})\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})\geq\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})+C^{\prime}\varepsilon r_{\tilde{k}(i)}

(5.6.53)

\displaystyle\geq d_{X}(g(w^{i}),g(w^{i+1})).

Combine this estimate with (
5.6.22
), (
5.6.32
), (
5.6.51
), using that
\phi_{\infty}(x,y)=d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})>\eta r_{k}
and taking
\varepsilon
small enough depending on
\eta,
we have

(5.6.54)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{K-1}\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in I}\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})+\sum_{i\in I^{c}}\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})

(5.6.55)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in I}[d_{X}(g(w^{i}),g(w^{i+1}))-C\varepsilon r_{\tilde{k}(i)}]

(5.6.56)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\sum_{i\in I^{c}}(1+C\varepsilon\eta^{-2})^{-1}d_{X}(g(w^{i}),g(w^{i+1}))

(5.6.57)

\displaystyle\geq(1+C\eta)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{K-1}d_{X}(g(w^{i}),g(w^{i+1}))-C\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y)

(5.6.58)

\displaystyle\geq(1+C\eta)^{-1}d_{X}(g(x),g(y))-C\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y)

(5.6.59)

\displaystyle\geq(1+C\eta)^{-1}(\phi_{\infty}(x,y)-C\varepsilon r_{k})-C\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y)

(5.6.60)

\displaystyle\geq(1+C\eta)^{-1}\phi_{\infty}(x,y).

After rearranging, this and (
5.6.50
) gives (
5.6.29
).

For the remainder of the proof we describe how to construct such a chain. If
\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})>\eta^{2}r_{k(i)}
for all
1\leq i\leq N-1
then we simply take
w^{i}=z^{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq N-1
(in this case
I=\emptyset).
Otherwise, we construct
\{w^{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}
by removing certain links from the original chain. Let

(5.6.61)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=\{1\leq i\leq N-1\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})\leq\eta r_{k(i)}\}\neq\emptyset

and for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
let
i+1\leq\bar{i}\leq N
be the maximal index such that

(5.6.62)

\displaystyle d_{k(i)}(z_{k(i)}^{i},z^{i^{\prime}}_{k(i)})\leq r_{k(i)+2}\mbox{ for all }i\leq i^{\prime}\leq\bar{i}.

Let
i_{1}
be the smallest index in
\mathcal{I}.
Then, supposing
i_{m}
has been defined for some
m\geq 1,
if

\{i\in\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i\geq\bar{i}_{m}\}\neq\emptyset

let
i_{m+1}
be the smallest such index. Otherwise, we stop. Let
\{i_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\Lambda}
,
\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, be the resulting sequence. For each
1\leq m\leq\Lambda
we remove from
\{z^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
the links in the chain lying between
i_{m}
and
\bar{i}_{m}
. More precisely, let

(5.6.63)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{0}=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{ if }i_{1}=1;\\
\{1,\cdots,i_{1}-1\}&\mbox{ otherwise},\end{cases}

and for
1\leq m\leq\Lambda,
let

(5.6.64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{m}=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{ if }m<\Lambda\mbox{ and }\bar{i}_{m}=i_{m+1};\\
\{\bar{i}_{m},\dots,i_{m+1}-1\}&\mbox{ if }m<\Lambda\mbox{ and }\bar{i}_{m}<i_{m+1};\\
\emptyset&\mbox{ if }m=\Lambda\mbox{ and }\bar{i}_{m}=N;\\
\{\bar{i}_{m},\dots,N\}&\mbox{ if }m=\Lambda\mbox{ and }\bar{i}_{m}<N.\end{cases}

Then, set

(5.6.65)

\displaystyle Z=\{z^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}\setminus\bigcup_{m=1}^{\Lambda}\bigcup_{i_{m}<i<\bar{i}_{m}}\{z^{i}\}=\bigcup_{m=1}^{\Lambda}\{z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}}\}\cup\bigcup_{m=0}^{\Lambda}\bigcup_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{m}}\{z^{i}\}.

We let
\{w^{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}
be an enumeration of
Z
with order inherited from
\{z^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
i.e. if
1\leq i\leq j\leq K
and
1\leq k,\ell\leq N
are such that
w^{i}=z^{k}
and
w^{j}=z^{\ell}
, then
k\leq\ell.

Before proving conditions (1) and (2) we make and prove several claims.
For
1\leq m\leq\Lambda
such that
k(i_{m})\geq 1,
choose
j(m)\in J_{k(i_{m})-1}
such that

(5.6.66)

\displaystyle z^{i}_{k(i)-1}\in 3D^{j(m)},

which exists by Lemma
5.5.9
.

Claim 1:
If
1\leq m\leq\Lambda
is such that
k(i_{m})\geq 1
and
i_{m}\leq i\leq\bar{i}_{m}
then

(5.6.67)

\displaystyle z^{i}_{k(i_{m})-1}\in 4D^{j(m)}.

If
1\leq m\leq\Lambda
and
i_{m}\leq i<\bar{i}_{m}
then

(5.6.68)

\displaystyle k(i_{m})-1\leq k(i)\leq k(i_{m})+1.

If
\bar{k}(i_{m})=k(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})
then

(5.6.69)

\displaystyle k(i_{m})-1\leq\bar{k}(i_{m})\leq k(i_{m})+1.

Equation (
5.6.67
) follows from (
5.6.62
), (
5.6.66
) and Lemma
5.5.12
. Suppose now that
1\leq m\leq\Lambda
and
i_{m}\leq i<i_{m}
. If
k(i_{m})=0
then the first inequality in (
5.6.68
) is clear. If
k(i_{m})\geq 1,
since (
5.6.67
) holds for
i
and
i+1
, the first inequality in (
5.6.68
) follows from this and the definition of
k(i)
. For the second inequality, note that, by Lemma
5.5.14
and (
5.6.62
) we have

(5.6.70)

\displaystyle{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{k(i_{m})+2}\left(z_{k(i_{m})+2}^{i_{m}},\bigcup_{j\in J_{k+2}}6D^{j}\right)\geq r_{k(i_{m})+1}/2.

Also, by Lemma
5.5.6
and (
5.6.62
), we have

(5.6.71)

\displaystyle d_{k(i_{m})+2}(z^{i_{m}}_{k(i_{m})+2},z^{i}_{k(i_{m})+2})\leq(1+C\varepsilon)r_{k(i_{m})+2}\leq r_{k(i_{m})+1}/4.

These two inequalities imply

z_{k(i_{m})+2}^{i}\not\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{k(i_{m})+2}}6D^{j}

and (
5.6.68
) now follows from the definition of
k(i).
The first inequality in (
5.6.69
) holds by essentially the same argument used to establish the first inequality in (
5.6.68
). The second inequality follows from the definition of
\bar{k}(i_{m})
and (
5.6.70
).

Let

(5.6.72)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\{1\leq m\leq\Lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})>10\eta^{2}r_{k(i_{m})}\right\};

(5.6.73)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{1\leq m\leq\Lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})\leq 10\eta^{2}r_{k(i_{m})}\mbox{ and }\bar{i}_{m}<N\right\};

(5.6.74)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{3}

\displaystyle=\left\{1\leq m\leq\Lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})\leq 10\eta^{2}r_{k(i_{m})}\mbox{ and }\bar{i}_{m}=N\right\}\subseteq\{\Lambda\}.

Claim 2:
We have

(5.6.75)

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathcal{S}_{1}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\sum_{m\in\mathcal{S}_{1}}\sum_{i=i_{m}}^{\bar{i}_{m}-1}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1}).

Suppose
m\in S_{1}.
By (
5.6.21
) and the definitions of
\bar{k}(i_{m})
and
k(i),
we have

(5.6.76)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})=d_{\bar{k}(i_{m})}(z_{\bar{k}(i_{m})}^{i_{m}},z_{\bar{k}(i_{m})}^{\bar{i}_{m}})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})=d_{k(i)}(z^{i}_{k(i)},z^{i+1}_{k(i)}).

Using this with Lemma
5.5.6
, (
5.6.68
) and (
5.6.69
), we have

(5.6.77)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)d_{k(i_{m})}(z^{i_{m}}_{k(i_{m})},z^{\bar{i}_{m}}_{k(i_{m})})

(5.6.78)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\sum_{i=i_{m}}^{\bar{i}_{m}-1}d_{k(i_{m})}(z^{i}_{k(i_{m})},z^{i+1}_{k(i_{m})})\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\sum_{i=i_{m}}^{\bar{i}_{m}-1}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1}).

The claim now follows by taking the sum over
\mathcal{S}_{1}.

Claim 3:
We have

(5.6.79)

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathcal{S}_{2}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})\lesssim\eta\sum_{m\in\mathcal{S}_{2}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{\bar{i}_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1})\leq\eta\sum_{m=1}^{\Lambda}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{m}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1}).

Let
m\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
. The first inequality follows once we show

(5.6.80)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})\leq\eta\phi_{\infty}(z^{\bar{i}_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1})

and the second inequality follows once we show

(5.6.81)

\displaystyle\bar{i}_{m}\in\mathcal{I}_{m}.

Crucial to both (
5.6.80
) and (
5.6.81
) are the inequalities

(5.6.82)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(z^{\bar{i}_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1})>r_{k(\bar{i}_{m})+4}/2>\eta r_{k(\bar{i}_{m})}

and

(5.6.83)

\displaystyle k(\bar{i}_{m})\leq k(i_{m})+4.

Before proving these, lets see how they imply (
5.6.80
) and (
5.6.81
). First, (
5.6.82
) and (
5.6.83
) give

\phi_{\infty}(z^{\bar{i}_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1})>\eta r_{k(\bar{i}_{m})}\gtrsim\eta r_{k(i_{m})}.

Using this with Lemma
5.5.6
, (
5.6.69
) and the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{2},
we get

(5.6.84)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})\leq 10\eta^{2}r_{k(i_{m})}\lesssim\eta\phi_{\infty}(z^{\bar{i}_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1})

which implies (
5.6.80
). Now for (
5.6.81
). If
m=\Lambda
, the (
5.6.81
) follows immediately from the definitions of
\mathcal{S}_{2}
and
\mathcal{I}_{m}.
Suppose then that
m<\Lambda.
If (
5.6.82
) holds then, recalling the definition of
\mathcal{I}
from (
5.6.61
), we have
\bar{i}_{m}\not\in\mathcal{I}.

Since
i_{m+1}
is the smallest index in
\{i\in\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i\geq\bar{i}_{m}\}
this gives
\bar{i}_{m}<i_{m+1}
. Equation (
5.6.81
) then follows from the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{m}.

We now prove (
5.6.82
) and (
5.6.83
), starting with (
5.6.83
). By maximality of
\bar{i}_{m}
we have

(5.6.85)

\displaystyle d_{k(i_{m})}(z_{k(i_{m})}^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1}_{k(i_{m})})>r_{k(i_{m})+2}.

Using Lemma
5.5.6
with (
5.6.69
) and the fact that
m\in\mathcal{S}_{2},
we also have

(5.6.86)

\displaystyle d_{k(i_{m})}(z^{i_{m}}_{k(i_{m})},z^{\bar{i}_{m}}_{k(i_{m})})

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)d_{\bar{k}(i_{m})}(z^{i_{m}}_{\bar{k}(i_{m})},z^{\bar{i}_{m}}_{\bar{k}(i_{m})})

(5.6.87)

\displaystyle=(1+C\varepsilon)\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})\lesssim\eta^{2}r_{k(i_{m})}.

Combining this with (
5.6.85
) gives

(5.6.88)

\displaystyle d_{k(i_{m})}(z_{k(i_{m})}^{\bar{i}_{m}},z_{k(i_{m})}^{\bar{i}_{m}+1})\geq r_{k(i_{m})+3},

which, after applying Lemma
5.5.6
, implies

d_{k(i_{m})+5}(z_{k(i_{m})+5}^{\bar{i}_{m}},z_{k(i_{m})+5}^{\bar{i}_{m}+1})\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}r_{k(i_{m})+3}\geq 50r_{k(i_{m})+5}.

By Lemma
5.5.11
we have

{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}}_{k(i_{m})+5}(6D^{j})\leq 13r_{k(i_{m})+5}

for all
j\in J_{k(i_{m})+5}
, and the above two inequalities imply (
5.6.83
). Let us prove (
5.6.82
). The second inequality is immediate if
\eta
is chosen small enough, so let us focus on the first inequality. Suppose first that
k(i_{m})\geq k(\bar{i}_{m})+2
(so that
k(i_{m})\geq 2)
. By (
5.6.67
) we have
z_{k(i_{m})-1}^{\bar{i}_{m}}\in 4D^{j(i_{m})}
so (the contrapositive of) Lemma
5.5.14
gives the first inequality in (
5.6.82
). Suppose instead that

(5.6.89)

\displaystyle k(i_{m})\leq k(\bar{i}_{m})+1.

Then, using Lemma
5.5.6
with each of (
5.6.83
), (
5.6.88
) and (
5.6.89
), we get

(5.6.90)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(z^{\bar{i}_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1})

\displaystyle=d_{k(\bar{i}_{m})}(z^{\bar{i}_{m}}_{k(\bar{i}_{m})},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1}_{k(\bar{i}_{m})})

(5.6.91)

\displaystyle\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}d_{k(i_{m})}(z^{\bar{i}_{m}}_{k(i_{m})},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1}_{k(i_{m})})\geq r_{k(\bar{i}_{m})+4}/2

and we are done.

Claim 4:
If
m\in\mathcal{S}_{3},
then

\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})\lesssim\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y).

Indeed, recall in Case 2 that
\phi_{\infty}(x,y)=d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})>\eta r_{k}.
Then, by (
5.6.34
) and the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{3}
, we have

\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})\leq 10\eta^{2}r_{k(i_{m})}\lesssim\eta^{2}r_{k}\leq\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y).

We now have everything we need to verify conditions (1) and (2). First, if we combine (
5.6.65
) with Claim 2, Claim 3 and Claim 4, then

(5.6.92)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{K-1}\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{\Lambda}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}})+\sum_{m=0}^{\Lambda}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{m}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})

(5.6.93)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\sum_{m=1}^{\Lambda}\sum_{i=i_{m}}^{\bar{i}_{m}-1}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})

(5.6.94)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+(1+C\eta)\sum_{m=0}^{\Lambda}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{m}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})+C\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y)

(5.6.95)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\eta)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{\infty}(z^{i},z^{i+1})+\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y)

(5.6.96)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\eta)\rho(x,y)+C\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y)

which gives (1). Let us focus our attention on (2). Recall the definition of
I
from the statement of (2). If
I=\emptyset
there is nothing to show, so let us assume
I\neq\emptyset
and let
i\in I.
We now show that

(5.6.97)

\displaystyle w^{i}=z^{i_{m}}\mbox{ for some }m\in\mathcal{S}_{2}\cup\mathcal{S}_{3}.

Suppose this is false and let
1\leq j\leq N-1
such that
w^{i}=z^{j}.
By (
5.6.65
) we have either
j=i_{m}
for some
m\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
or
j\in\mathcal{I}_{m}
for some
m\geq 0.
In the first case
w^{i+1}=z^{\bar{j}}
. The definition of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
implies then Lemma
5.5.6
, (
5.6.69
) and the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
imply

(5.6.98)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})=\phi_{\infty}(z^{j},z^{\bar{j}})>10\eta^{2}r_{k(j)}=10\eta^{2}r_{\tilde{k}(i)},

which is a contradiction since
i\in I.
In the second case,
w^{i+1}=z^{j+1},
so that
\phi_{\infty}(w^{i},w^{i+1})=\phi_{\infty}(z^{j},z^{j+1})>\eta r_{k(j)}=\eta r_{\tilde{k}(i)}
since
j\not\in\mathcal{I}.
This is again a contradiction.

We now use (
5.6.97
) to finish the proof of (2). Recalling the definition of
\tilde{k}(i)
and
\bar{k}(i_{m}),
we see that
\tilde{k}(i)=\bar{k}(i_{m}).
If
m\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
then by taking
\varepsilon
small enough and using (
5.6.69
), (
5.6.82
) and (
5.6.83
) we have

\varepsilon r_{\tilde{k}(i)}=\varepsilon r_{\bar{k}(i_{m})}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k(\bar{i}_{m})}\leq\eta\phi_{\infty}(z^{\bar{i}_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1}).

If
m\in\mathcal{S}_{3},
then by taking
\varepsilon
small enough and using (
5.6.34
) and (
5.6.69
) we have

(5.6.99)

\displaystyle\varepsilon r_{\tilde{k}(i)}=\varepsilon r_{\bar{k}(i_{m})}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k(i_{m})}\leq\varepsilon r_{k}\leq\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y).

Combing these estimates with (
5.6.30
) and the fact that
\rho(x,y)\leq\phi_{\infty}(x,y)
we have

(5.6.100)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}\varepsilon r_{\tilde{k}(i)}\lesssim\eta\left(\sum_{m\in\mathcal{S}_{2}}\phi_{\infty}(z^{\bar{i}_{m}},z^{\bar{i}_{m}+1})+\phi_{\infty}(x,y)\right)\lesssim\eta\phi_{\infty}(x,y),

which completes the proof of (2) and the construction of
\{w^{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}.

∎

Corollary 5.6.9
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\ell\geq 0
. Then,

(5.6.101)

\displaystyle|\rho(x,y)-\phi_{\ell}(x_{\ell},y_{\ell})|=|\rho(x,y)-\tilde{\phi}_{\ell}(x,y)|\lesssim\varepsilon(r_{\ell}+\rho(x,y)).

If
x_{\ell}\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6D^{j}
then

(5.6.102)

\displaystyle|d_{X}(g(x),g(y))-\rho(x,y)|\lesssim\varepsilon(r_{\ell}+\rho(x,y))

Proof.

The equality in (
5.6.101
) is immediate from (
5.6.21
). For the inequality, we apply Lemma
5.6.3
and Lemma
5.6.8
to get

(5.6.103)

\displaystyle|\rho(x,y)-\tilde{\phi}_{\ell}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq|\rho(x,y)-\phi_{\infty}(x,y)|+|\phi_{\infty}(x,y)-\tilde{\phi}_{\ell}(x,y)|

(5.6.104)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\rho(x,y)+\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

For (
5.6.102
), we apply Lemma
5.6.5
and Lemma
5.6.8
to get

(5.6.105)

\displaystyle|d_{X}(g(x),g(y))-\rho(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq|d_{X}(g(x),g(y))-\phi_{\infty}(x,y)|+|\phi_{\infty}(x,y)-\rho(x,y)

(5.6.106)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\left(r_{\ell}+\rho(x,y)\right).

∎

5.7.
Bi-Hölder, quasi-isometry, and Reifenberg Flatness

In the following two lemmas, we establish the properties of
\rho
stated in Theorem
5.1.1
. First, it is bi-Hölder equivalent to and a small perturbation of
\|\cdot\|_{j_{0}}
as in (
5.1.5
) and (
5.1.7
) respectively. Second, that
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
is Reifenberg flat as in (
5.1.6
).

Lemma 5.7.1
.

The metric
\rho
is bi-Hölder equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{j_{0}}
with exponent
\alpha=1-C\varepsilon
and

(5.7.1)

\displaystyle|\rho(x,y)-\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}|\leq C\varepsilon

for all
x,y\in 10B_{j_{0}}.

Proof.

For
x,y\in 10B_{j_{0}}
we have by (
5.6.17
) and Lemma
5.6.8
that

\rho(x,y)\lesssim\phi_{\infty}(x,y)\leq\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}+C\varepsilon s_{0}\lesssim s_{0}\lesssim 1.

Recalling from (
5.6.13
) that
\phi_{0}(x,y)=\|x-y\|_{j_{0}},
(
5.7.1
) is then immediate from (
5.6.101
). By Lemma
5.6.8
, to show that
\rho
is bi-Hölder equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{j_{0}}
with exponent
\alpha
, it is enough to show
\phi_{\infty}
is bi-Hölder equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{j_{0}}
with the same exponent. This is our goal for the rest of the proof. Let
C^{\prime}
be the implicit constant appearing in (
5.6.17
) and let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
\|x-y\|>r_{0}
then choosing
\varepsilon
small enough depending on
C^{\prime}
, we have

(5.7.2)

\displaystyle|\phi_{\infty}(x,y)-\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}|\leq C^{\prime}\varepsilon r_{0}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}.

Suppose instead that
\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}\leq r_{0}.
By Lemma
5.6.3
, we have

(5.7.3)

\displaystyle\phi_{\ell}(x,y)\leq(1+C\varepsilon)^{\ell}\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}

for all
\ell\geq 0.
If
m\geq 0
is such that
\phi_{\ell}(x,y)\leq r_{\ell}
for all
0\leq\ell\leq m
then Lemma
5.6.3
also gives

(5.7.4)

\displaystyle 10^{m}=r_{m}\geq\phi_{m}(x,y)\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-m}\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}.

Since the left hand-side decays more rapidly than the right, there is a maximal such
m\geq 0
, which we do not relabel. By maximality, we have

(5.7.5)

\displaystyle\phi_{m}(x,y)\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\phi_{m+1}(x,y)\geq\frac{r_{m+1}}{2}.

Using this estimate with (
5.7.3
) and (
5.7.4
), gives

(5.7.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi_{\infty}(x,y)&\leq\phi_{m}(x,y)+C\varepsilon r_{m}\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\phi_{m}(x,y)\\
&\leq(1+C\varepsilon)^{m+1}\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}\end{split}

and

(5.7.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi_{\infty}(x,y)&\geq\phi_{m}(x,y)-C\varepsilon r_{m}\geq(1+C\varepsilon)\phi_{m}(x,y)\\
&\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-m-1}\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}.\end{split}

After taking logarithms in (
5.7.4
) and rearranging, we obtain

(5.7.8)

\displaystyle m\leq\frac{1}{\log\left(\frac{10}{1+C\varepsilon}\right)}\log\left(\frac{1}{\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}}\right)\leq C\log\left(\frac{1}{\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}}\right).

Combing this estimate for
m
with (
5.7.6
) and (
5.7.7
), we see

(5.7.9)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\log\left(\frac{\phi_{\infty}(x,y)}{\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}}\right)\right\rvert}\leq(m+1)\log(1+C\varepsilon)\leq C\varepsilon\log\left(\frac{1}{\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}}\right).

After rearranging, it follows that

(5.7.10)

\displaystyle\|x-y\|^{1+C\varepsilon}_{j_{0}}\leq\phi_{\infty}(x,y)\leq\|x-y\|_{j_{0}}^{1-C\varepsilon}.

∎

Lemma 5.7.2
.

The space
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
is
(C\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
0<r<\infty
. We begin by defining a norm
\|\cdot\|
and a mapping
\phi\colon B_{\rho}(x,r)\to B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r).
We will check afterwards that
\phi
defines a
C\varepsilon r
-GHA. For
\ell\geq 0
set
B_{\ell}=F_{\ell}(B_{\rho}(x,r))\subseteq M_{\ell}
and note that
B_{0}=B_{\rho}(x,r)
by (
5.5.64
). Furthermore, let

(5.7.11)

\displaystyle k=\max\{\ell\geq 0\colon\mbox{there exists }j\in J_{\ell}\mbox{ such that }B_{\ell}\subseteq 6D^{j}\}.

We define the maximum of the empty set to be zero. If
k\geq 1
fix some
i\in J_{k}
satisfying
B_{k}\subseteq 6D^{i,k}
, let
\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|_{i,k}
and
\psi=\psi_{i,k}\colon 6D^{i,k}\to 6B_{i,k}
. Otherwise, set
\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|_{j_{0}}
and
\psi=\text{Id}.
In either case we have that
\psi
is a
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz map by Lemma
5.5.11
. By translation, we may assume

(5.7.12)

\displaystyle\psi(F_{k}(x))=0,

noting that this does not affect the bi-Lipschitz constant. Let
p_{0,r}
be the map from Lemma
2.3.3
and set

(5.7.13)

\displaystyle\phi\coloneqq p_{0,r}\circ\psi\circ F_{k}\colon B_{0}\to B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r).

Let
\eta>0
to be chosen sufficiently small. We now check that
\phi
defines a
C\varepsilon r
-GHA, the proof splits into two cases.

Case 1:
Suppose that
d_{k}(x_{k},p_{k})\leq\eta r_{k}
for all
p\in B_{0}.
We begin by showing

(5.7.14)

\displaystyle F_{k}|_{B_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(B_{0},\rho)\to(B_{k},d_{k})\mbox{ is a }(1+C\varepsilon)\mbox{-bi-Lipschitz equivalence.}

Indeed, by maximality of
k
there exists a pair of points
p,q\in B_{0}
such that if
\ell>k
and
j\in J_{\ell}
then either
p_{\ell}\not\in 6D^{j}
or
q_{\ell}\not\in 6D^{j}.
By assumption,
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(B_{k})\leq 2\eta r_{k},
so that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(B_{k+2})\leq 3\eta r_{k}
by Lemma
5.5.6
. Furthermore, Lemma
5.5.14
implies
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}_{k+2}(p_{k+2},\bigcup_{j\in J_{k+2}}6D^{j})\geq r_{k+1}/2.
Combining the previous two estimates with the triangle inequality gives

(5.7.15)

\displaystyle B_{k+2}\cap\bigcup_{j\in J_{k+2}}6D^{j}=\emptyset.

Let us see how (
5.7.15
) implies (
5.7.14
). Let
a,b\in B_{0}
and as above let us write
a_{\ell}=F_{\ell}(a)
and
b_{\ell}=F_{\ell}(b)
for
\ell\geq 0.
Combining (
5.7.15
) with (
5.6.21
) we have
\phi_{\infty}(a,b)=d_{k+2}(a_{k+2},b_{k+2}).
Applying Lemma
5.6.8
and Lemma
5.5.6
then gives

(5.7.16)

\displaystyle|\rho(a,b)-d_{k}(a_{k},b_{k})|

\displaystyle\leq|\rho(a,b)-d_{k+2}(a_{k+2},b_{k+2})|+|d_{k+2}(a_{k+2},b_{k+2})-d_{k}(a_{k},b_{k})|

(5.7.17)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon(d_{k+2}(a_{k+2},b_{k+2})+d_{k}(a_{k},b_{k}))\lesssim\varepsilon d_{k}(a_{k},b_{k})

and we conclude (
5.7.14
).

Now we check that
\phi
defines a
C\varepsilon r
-isometry. Let
y,z\in B_{0}.
Combining (
5.7.12
) with that fact that
\psi
and
F_{k}
are both
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, we know
\psi(F_{k}(y))\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,(1+C\varepsilon)r)
so that
\|\phi(y)-\psi(F_{k}(y))\|\leq C\varepsilon r
by (
5.7.13
) and Lemma
2.3.3
. The same argument also gives
\|\phi(z)-\psi(F_{k}(z))\|\leq C\varepsilon r.
Using this, and again the fact that
\psi
and
F_{k}
are
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, we have

(5.7.18)

\displaystyle|\|\phi(y)-\phi(z)\|-\rho(y,z)|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon r+|\|\psi(F_{k}(y))-\psi(F_{k}(z))\|-\rho(y,z)|

(5.7.19)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon r+C\varepsilon\rho(y,z)\leq C\varepsilon r.

Since
y,z\in B_{0}
were arbitrary, we are done.

It remains to check that
\phi
is
C\varepsilon r
-dense. Let
u\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)
and set
v=\tfrac{u}{1+C\varepsilon}
and
w=F_{k}^{-1}(\psi^{-1}(v)).
Then
\|u-v\|\leq C\varepsilon r
and
\|v\|\leq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}r
. Recalling (
5.7.12
) and using that
F_{k}
and
\psi
are
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz we have

(5.7.20)

\displaystyle\rho(w,x)=\rho(F_{k}^{-1}(\psi^{-1}(v)),F_{k}^{-1}(\psi^{-1}(0)))\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\|v\|\leq r

so that
w\in B_{0}.
Since
v\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r),
Lemma
2.3.3
implies
p_{0,r}(v)=v
. The triangle inequality, (
5.7.13
) and the definition of
w
then gives

(5.7.21)

\displaystyle\hskip 25.00003pt\|\phi(w)-u\|\leq\|\phi(w)-v\|+\|v-u\|=\|\psi(F_{k}(w))-v\|+\|v-u\|\leq C\varepsilon r.

Since
u
was arbitrary, this completes the proof in this case.

Case 2:
Suppose now that there exists
p\in B_{0}
such that
d_{k}(x_{k},p_{k})>\eta r_{k}.
For
\varepsilon
small enough depending on
\eta
, it follows from Lemma
5.6.8
and (
5.6.17
) that
\phi_{k}(x_{k},p_{k})\leq(1+C\varepsilon\eta^{-1})\rho(x,p)\leq 2r
for such a point. Note that

(5.7.22)

\displaystyle d_{k}(y_{k},z_{k})=\phi_{k}(y_{k},z_{k})\mbox{ for all }y,z\in B_{0}.

This follows from (
5.6.13
) if
k=0
and from (
5.6.14
) if
k\geq 1,
recalling that
B_{k}\subseteq 6D^{i,k}.
Combing the above two observations with our assumption on
d_{k}(x_{k},p_{k})
gives

r\geq\eta r_{k}/2.

Let us check that
\phi
defines a
C\varepsilon r
-isometry. Let
y,z\in B_{0}.
By Corollary
5.6.9
and (
5.7.22
) we have

(5.7.23)

\displaystyle d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\rho(x,y)+C\varepsilon r_{k}\leq(1+C\varepsilon\eta^{-1})r.

Using (
5.7.12
) and the fact that
\psi
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, this gives
\psi(F_{k}(y))\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,(1+C\varepsilon\eta^{-1})r).
Lemma
2.3.3
then implies
\|\phi(y)-\psi(F_{k}(y))\|\leq C\varepsilon\eta^{-1}r.
Similarly, we have
\|\phi(z)-\psi(F_{k}(z))\|\leq C\varepsilon\eta^{-1}r.
Using these estimates with (
5.7.22
) and Corollary
5.6.9
, we get

(5.7.24)

\displaystyle|\|\phi(y)-\phi(z)\|-\rho(y,z)|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\eta^{-1}r+|\|\psi(F_{k}(y))-\psi(F_{k}(z))\|-\rho(y,z)|

(5.7.25)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\eta^{-1}r

(5.7.26)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+|\|\psi(F_{k}(y))-\psi(F_{k}(z))\|-d_{k}(F_{k}(y),F_{k}(z))|

(5.7.27)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+|d_{k}(F_{k}(y),F_{k}(z))-\rho(y,z)|

(5.7.28)

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\eta^{-1}r+\varepsilon\rho(y,z)+\varepsilon r_{k}\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon r}{\eta}.

Let us now show that
\phi
is
C\varepsilon r
-dense. Let
u\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)
and let
r^{\prime}=\tfrac{(r-C\varepsilon r_{k})}{1+C\varepsilon}
,
v=p_{z,r^{\prime}}(u)
and
w=\psi^{-1}(F_{k}^{-1}(v)).
Notice that
\|v\|\leq r^{\prime}
and by Lemma
2.3.3
that
\|u-v\|\leq C\varepsilon(r+r_{k})\leq C\varepsilon\eta^{-1}r
. Then, by (
5.7.12
), Corollary
5.6.9
, and the fact that
\psi
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, we have

(5.7.29)

\displaystyle\rho(w,z)

\displaystyle=\rho(F_{k}^{-1}(\psi^{-1}(v)),F_{k}^{-1}(\psi^{-1}(0)))\leq C\varepsilon r_{k}+(1+C\varepsilon)d_{k}(\psi^{-1}(v),\psi^{-1}(0))

(5.7.30)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon r_{k}+(1+C\varepsilon)\|v\|\leq r,

so that
w\in B_{0}.
By exactly the same estimate as in (
5.7.21
), we get

(5.7.31)

\displaystyle\|\phi(w)-u\|\leq C\varepsilon r.

Since
u
was arbitrary, this finishes the proof.
∎

5.8.
The map
\bar{g}

For each
\ell\geq 0
and
j\in J_{\ell}
it is possible, by Lemma
2.3.6
, to find a
C\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA
\tilde{\beta}_{j,\ell}\colon 100B^{j,\ell}\to 100B_{j,\ell}
which satisfies (
5.1.3
), (
5.1.4
) (with constant
C\delta r_{\ell}
) and
\tilde{\beta}_{j,\ell}(6B^{j,\ell})\subseteq 6B_{j,\ell}
. Thus, we might as well (and will) assume that

(5.8.1)

\displaystyle\beta_{j,\ell}(6B^{j,\ell})\subseteq 6B_{j,\ell}.

This is needed for example in (
5.8.3
) below, since
\text{dom}(\psi_{j,\ell}^{-1})=6B_{j,\ell}.

Let us now define the map
\bar{g}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}6B^{j_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}.
For
x\in 6B^{j_{0}},
let

(5.8.2)

\displaystyle k(x)=\sup\left\{\ell\geq 0\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6B^{j}\right\},

where again we set the supremum over the empty set to be zero. For each
0\leq k\leq k(x)
choose an arbitrary index
i_{k}(x)
such that
x\in 6B^{i_{k}(x)}.
Then, for
k\geq 0
, set

(5.8.3)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{k}(x)=\begin{cases}F_{k}^{-1}\circ\psi^{-1}_{i_{k}(x)}\circ\beta_{i_{k}(x)}(x)&\mbox{ if }\ell\leq k(x);\\
F_{k(x)}^{-1}\circ\psi^{-1}_{i_{k(x)}(x)}\circ\beta_{i_{k(x)}(x)}(x)&\mbox{ if }k>k(x).\end{cases}

Note that
\bar{g}_{k}
is well-defined by (
5.8.1
).

Lemma 5.8.1
.

Let
k\geq 0
and
i,j\in J_{k}
such that
x\in 6B^{i}\cap 6B^{j}.
Then,

(5.8.4)

\displaystyle\phi_{k}(\psi_{i}^{-1}\circ\beta_{i}(x),\psi_{j}^{-1}\circ\beta_{j}(x))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

Proof.

By assumption,
\beta_{i}
is a
\delta r_{i}
-GHA and by Lemma
5.5.11
,
\psi_{i}
is bi-Lipschitz. Using this with (
5.1.4
) and Lemma
5.5.7
gives

(5.8.5)

\displaystyle\phi_{k}(\psi_{i}^{-1}\circ\beta_{i}(x),\psi_{j}^{-1}\circ\beta_{j}(x))

\displaystyle=\phi_{k}(\psi_{i}^{-1}\circ\beta_{i}(x),\psi_{i}^{-1}\circ I_{i,j}\circ\beta_{j}(x))

(5.8.6)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\beta_{i}(x)-I_{i,j}\circ\beta_{j}(x)\|_{i}

(5.8.7)

\displaystyle\leq\|\beta_{i}(x)-\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}(x)\|_{i}+C\varepsilon r_{k}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

∎

Lemma 5.8.2
.

For each
k\geq 0
and
x\in 6B^{j_{0}}
we have

(5.8.8)

\displaystyle\rho(\bar{g}_{k}(x),\bar{g}_{k+1}(x))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

In particular, the limit
\bar{g}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\bar{g}_{k}
exists, takes values in
6B_{j_{0}}
and satisfies

\rho(\bar{g}_{k}(x),\bar{g}(x))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

Proof.

Let
k\geq 0
and
x\in 6B^{j_{0}}.
If
k+1>k(x)
then
\bar{g}_{k}(x)=\bar{g}_{k+1}(x)
and there is nothing to show. Suppose then, that
k+1\leq k(x).
Let
i=i_{k}(x)
and
j=i_{k+1}(x)
as above (
5.8.3
). By (
5.8.1
), we have
\beta_{j}(x)\in 6B_{j}
and by (
5.5.32
) we have
\psi_{j}^{-1}\circ\beta_{j}(x)\in 6D^{j}.
It follows from Lemma
5.5.8
that

(5.8.9)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{k+1}(x)=F_{k}^{-1}\circ\psi_{i(j)}^{-1}\circ K_{i(j),j}\circ\hat{H}_{j}^{-1}\circ\beta_{j}(x),

where
i(j)\in J_{k}
and
K_{i(j),j}
are as in (
5.4.2
) and Lemma
5.4.1
, respectively, and
\hat{H}_{j}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies

(5.8.10)

\displaystyle\|\hat{H}_{j}-\text{Id}\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),r_{k+1}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

Let us now estimate the left-hand side of (
5.8.8
). Of course, we have

(5.8.11)

\displaystyle\rho(\bar{g}_{k}(x),\bar{g}_{k+1}(x))\leq\rho(\bar{g}_{k}(x),F_{k}^{-1}\circ\psi_{i(j)}^{-1}\circ\beta_{i(j)}(x))+\rho(F_{k}^{-1}\circ\psi_{i(j)}^{-1}\circ\beta_{i(j)}(x),\bar{g}_{k+1}(x)).

To estimate the first term we apply (
5.6.101
) and Lemma
5.8.1
which gives

(5.8.12)

\displaystyle\rho(\bar{g}_{k}(x),F_{k}^{-1}\circ\psi_{i(j)}^{-1}\circ\beta_{i(j)}(x))\lesssim\phi_{k}(\psi_{i(j)}\circ\beta_{i(j)}(x),\psi_{i}\circ\beta_{i}(x))+\varepsilon r_{k}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

By Corollary
5.6.9
, (
5.1.4
) and Lemma
5.4.1
, the second term is at most

(5.8.13)

\displaystyle\phi_{k}(\psi_{i(j)}^{-1}\circ\beta_{i(j)}(x),F_{k}\circ\bar{g}_{k+1}(x))+\varepsilon r_{k}

(5.8.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\lesssim\phi_{k}(\psi_{i(j)}^{-1}\circ\beta_{i(j)}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}(x),F_{k}\circ\bar{g}_{k+1}(x))+\varepsilon r_{k}

(5.8.15)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\lesssim\phi_{k}(\psi_{i(j)}^{-1}\circ K_{i(j),j}\circ\beta_{j}(x),F_{k}\circ\bar{g}_{k+1}(x))+\varepsilon r_{k}.

Making using of the definition of
\bar{g}_{k+1}(x)
in (
5.8.9
) and (
5.8.10
), the last term is at most a constant multiple of
\varepsilon r_{k}.
This now finishes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The following proves (
5.1.11
).

Lemma 5.8.3
.

Let
k\geq 0,\ j\in J_{k}
and
x,y\in 6B^{j}.
Then,

(5.8.16)

\displaystyle|\rho(\bar{g}(x),\bar{g}(y))-d_{X}(x,y)|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

Proof.

It suffices to show

(5.8.17)

\displaystyle|\rho(\bar{g}(x),\bar{g}(y))-d_{X}(x,y)|\lesssim\varepsilon(\rho(\bar{g}(x),\bar{g}(y))+r_{k}).

Indeed, applying the above estimate twice and using that
x,y\in 6B^{j}
(which implies
d_{X}(x,y)\lesssim r_{k})
, we get

(5.8.18)

\displaystyle|\rho(\bar{g}(x),\bar{g}(y))-d_{X}(x,y)|\lesssim\varepsilon(d_{X}(x,y)+\varepsilon r_{k}+r_{k})\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

To obtain (
5.8.17
), we first apply Corollary
5.6.9
, Lemma
5.8.2
and the triangle inequality to get

(5.8.19)

\displaystyle|\rho(\bar{g}(x),\bar{g}(y))-d_{X}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon(\rho(\bar{g}(x),\bar{g}(y))+r_{k})

(5.8.20)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+|\phi_{k}(F_{k}(\bar{g}_{k}(x)),F_{k}(\bar{g}_{k}(y)))-d_{X}(x,y)|.

Since
x\in 6B^{j}
, it follows from (
5.8.3
) and Lemma
5.8.1
that

\phi_{k}(F_{k}(\bar{g}_{k}(x)),\psi^{-1}_{j}(\beta_{j}(x)))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}.

Similarly,
\phi_{k}(F_{k}(\bar{g}_{k}(y)),\psi^{-1}_{j}(\beta_{j}(y))\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}
. Using this and the fact that
\psi_{j}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz and
\beta_{j}
is a
C\delta r_{k}
-GHA, the final term in (
5.8.19
) is at most

(5.8.21)

\displaystyle C\varepsilon r_{k}+|\phi_{k}(\psi_{j}^{-1}(\beta_{j}(x)),\psi_{j}^{-1}(\beta_{j}(y)))-d_{X}(x,y)|+C\varepsilon r_{k}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{k}

which proves (
5.8.17
).
∎

The following proves (
5.1.8
).

Lemma 5.8.4
.

Let
\ell\geq 0
and suppose
x\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6B^{j}
. Then,

(5.8.22)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x)),x)\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

From this, it follows that

(5.8.23)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g(\bar{g}(x)),x)\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Proof.

Let us start with (
5.8.22
). If
\ell=0
then
g_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x))=\alpha_{j_{0}}(\beta_{j_{0}}(x))
and (
5.8.22
) follows from (
5.1.4
). Suppose now that
\ell\geq 1.
Since
x\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6B^{j}
we have
\ell\leq k(x)
(recall (
5.8.2
)). It follows from (
5.8.3
) that there exists
j\in J_{\ell}
such that
\bar{g}_{\ell}(x)=F_{\ell}^{-1}\circ\psi_{j}^{-1}\circ\beta_{j}(x)
. Since

(5.8.24)

\displaystyle F_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x))=\psi_{j}^{-1}\circ\beta_{j}(x)\in 6D^{j}

we have
\ell\leq\ell(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x))
, recall the definition of
\ell(z)
from above (
5.6.2
). In particular, by (
5.6.2
), there exists
i\in J_{\ell}
such that
g_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x))=\alpha_{i}\circ\psi_{i}\circ F_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x)).
Using this with Lemma
5.5.7
we get

(5.8.25)

\displaystyle g_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x))=\alpha_{i}\circ\psi_{i}\circ\psi_{j}^{-1}\circ\beta_{j}(x)=\alpha_{i}\circ I_{i,j}\circ\beta_{j}(x).

Using (
5.1.4
), (
5.2.3
) and the fact that
\alpha_{j}
is a
\delta r_{\ell}
-GHA now gives (
5.8.22
) since

(5.8.26)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x)),x)

\displaystyle\leq C\delta r_{\ell}+d_{X}(\alpha_{i}\circ I_{i,j}\circ\beta_{j}(x),\alpha_{i}\circ\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}(x))

(5.8.27)

\displaystyle\leq C\delta r_{\ell}+\|I_{i,j}\circ\beta_{j}(x)-\beta_{i}\circ\alpha_{j}\circ\beta_{j}(x)\|_{i}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Let us see how to get (
5.8.23
) from (
5.8.22
). Since
F_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x))\in 6D^{j},
we have by Corollary
5.6.9
and Lemma
5.8.2
that

(5.8.28)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g(\bar{g}(x)),g(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x)))\lesssim\rho(\bar{g}(x),\bar{g}_{\ell}(x))+\varepsilon r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Using this with Lemma
5.6.2
and (
5.8.22
), the left-hand side of (
5.8.23
) is at most

(5.8.29)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g(\bar{g}(x)),g(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x))+d_{X}(g(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x)),g_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x)))+d_{X}(g_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(x)),x)\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell},

as required.
∎

The following proves (
5.1.9
).

Lemma 5.8.5
.

Let
\ell\geq 0
,
x\in 6B_{j_{0}}
and suppose
g(x)\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6B^{j}.
Then,

(5.8.30)

\displaystyle\rho(\bar{g}(g(x)),x)\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.8.2
it suffices to show

(5.8.31)

\displaystyle\rho(\bar{g}_{\ell}(g(x)),x)\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

By construction
F_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(g(x)))\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{\ell}}6D^{j}.
It then follows from Lemma
5.6.2
, Corollary
5.6.9
and Lemma
5.8.4
that

(5.8.32)

\displaystyle\rho(\bar{g}_{\ell}(g(x)),x)

\displaystyle\lesssim d_{X}(g(\bar{g}_{\ell}(g(x))),g(x))+\varepsilon r_{\ell}

(5.8.33)

\displaystyle\lesssim d_{X}(g_{\ell}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(g(x))),g(x))+\varepsilon r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

∎

Equation (
5.1.10
) follows from (
5.1.9
) and (
5.1.11
). Indeed, setting
x=g(w)
and
y=g(z)
gives

|d_{X}(g(w),g(z))-\rho(w,z)|\lesssim\varepsilon r_{\ell}.

5.9.
The Ahlfors-regular case

We now prove (
5.1.12
).

Lemma 5.9.1
.

If
X
is Ahlfors
n
-regular then
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
is Ahlfors
n
-regular. Moreover, the regularity constant for
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
depends only on
n
and the regularity constant for
X
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r>0
. Let
B_{0}=B_{\rho}(x,r)
and for
\ell\geq 1
set
B_{\ell}=F_{\ell}(B_{0})\subseteq M_{\ell}
. Let

(5.9.1)

\displaystyle k=\max\{\ell\geq 0\colon\mbox{there exists }j\in J_{\ell}\mbox{ such that }B_{\ell}\subseteq 6D^{j}\},

where the maximum of the empty set is defined to be zero. If
k\geq 1
let
j\in J_{k}
such that
B_{k}\subseteq 6D^{i,k}
. Let
\eta>0.
We split into two cases.

Case 1:
Suppose that
d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})\leq\eta r_{k}
for all
y\in B_{0}
. The setup in this case is exactly the same as in Case 1 of the proof of Lemma
5.7.2
. In particular, (
5.7.14
) and (
5.7.15
) hold for
B_{k}
. We will see that

(5.9.2)

\displaystyle B(x_{k},r/2)\subseteq B_{k}.

This is immediate for
k=0
so let us suppose
k\geq 1.
We begin by showing

r\leq 2\eta r_{k}.

Indeed, suppose
y\in B_{0}
is such that
\rho(x,y)=r
and let
k(x,y)
be as in Lemma
5.6.4
. By (
5.7.15
) we know
k\leq k(x,y)\leq k+1
. It then follows from Corollary
5.6.9
and Lemma
5.5.6
that

(5.9.3)

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\rho(x,y)\leq(1+C\varepsilon)[d_{k(x,y)}(x,y)+C\varepsilon r_{k(x,y)}]

(5.9.4)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C\varepsilon)[d_{k}(x,y)+C\varepsilon r_{k}]\leq 2\eta r_{k}.

We return to (
5.9.2
). Let
w\in B(x_{k},r/2)
and let
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
w=z_{k}.
Since
d_{k}(x_{k},z_{k})\leq r/2\leq\eta r_{k}
and
x_{k}\in 6D^{j}
we have
k(x,z)\geq k-1.
Using (
5.7.15
) again, we have
k(x,z)\leq k+1.
Thus,

(5.9.5)

\displaystyle\rho(x,z)\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\phi_{\infty}(x,z)\leq(1+C\varepsilon)d_{k}(x,z)\leq(1+C\varepsilon)r/2\leq r.

If follows that
z\in B_{0}
and
w=z_{k}\in B_{k}.
The measure estimates for
B_{0}
now follow from (
5.7.14
) since

(5.9.6)

\displaystyle r^{n}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{n}(6D^{j})\gtrsim(1+C\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{k})\geq\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{0})\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{k})

(5.9.7)

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{H}^{n}(B(x_{k},r/2))\gtrsim r^{n}.

Case 2:
Suppose instead that there exists
y\in B_{0}
such that
d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})>\eta r_{k}.
We begin by estimating
\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{0})
from above. Let

E_{\infty}=\{x\in B_{0}\colon\mbox{for every }\ell\geq 0\mbox{ there exists }j\in J_{\ell}\mbox{ such that }x_{\ell}\in 6D^{j}\}

and, for each
\ell\geq k
and
j\in J_{\ell},
let

(5.9.8)

\displaystyle R^{j,\ell}=6D^{j,\ell}\setminus f_{\ell}^{-1}\left(\bigcup_{i\in J_{\ell+1}}6D^{i,\ell+1}\right)\mbox{ and }R^{j,\ell}_{\infty}=F_{\ell}^{-1}(R^{j,\ell}).

Set

(5.9.9)

\displaystyle J^{\prime}_{\ell}=\{j\in J_{\ell}\colon R^{j,\ell}_{\infty}\cap B_{0}\neq\emptyset\}\mbox{ and }J^{\prime}=\bigcup_{\ell\geq k}J_{\ell}^{\prime}.

It follows that

(5.9.10)

\displaystyle B_{0}\subseteq E_{\infty}\cup\bigcup_{\ell\geq k}\bigcup_{j\in J^{\prime}_{\ell}}R^{j,\ell}_{\infty}.

Let us bound the measure of
E_{\infty}
from above. Observe that
g|_{E_{\infty}}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz. Indeed, suppose
w,z\in E_{\infty}
and let
k^{\prime}=k(w,z)
as in Lemma
5.6.4
. Since
w,z\in E_{\infty},
we have by Lemma
5.5.14
that
\phi_{\infty}(w,z)=d_{k^{\prime}}(w_{k^{\prime}},z_{k^{\prime}})\gtrsim r_{k^{\prime}}
so that
\rho(w,z)\gtrsim r_{k^{\prime}}
by Lemma
5.6.8
. Since
w_{k^{\prime}},z_{k^{\prime}}\in 6D^{j}
for some
j\in J_{k^{\prime}},
it follows from Corollary
5.6.9
that

(5.9.11)

\displaystyle|d_{X}(g(x),g(y))-\rho(x,y)|\lesssim\varepsilon(r_{k^{\prime}}+\rho(x,y))\lesssim\varepsilon\rho(x,y).

Now that we know
g|_{E_{\infty}}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz, we have that
g(E_{\infty})
is contained in a ball of radius
2r
in
X
. Using Ahlfors regularity of
X
and the bi-Lipschitz estimates of
g|_{E_{\infty}}
again, we get

(5.9.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(E_{\infty})\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{n}(g(E_{\infty}))\lesssim r^{n}.

Let us bound from above the measure of the second set in (
5.9.10
). For each
j\in J_{\ell}^{\prime},
fix
z^{j,\ell}\in R^{j,\ell}_{\infty}\cap B_{0}
and let

w^{j,\ell}=\alpha_{j,\ell}(\psi_{j,\ell}(z^{j,\ell}_{\ell}))\in X.

Since
d_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})>\eta r_{k}
for some
y\in B_{0},
we have
r\gtrsim\eta r_{k}
by Lemma
5.6.3
and Lemma
5.6.8
(see Case 2 in the proof of Lemma
5.7.2
). By taking
\varepsilon
small enough, this, Lemma
5.6.1
, Corollary
5.6.9
(noting that
z^{j,\ell}_{\ell}\in 6D^{j,\ell}
) and the fact that
\ell\geq k
, give

(5.9.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{X}(g_{k}(x),w^{j,\ell})&\leq d_{X}(g_{k}(x),g_{\ell}(z^{j,\ell}))+C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\leq d_{X}(g_{k}(x),g_{k}(z^{j,\ell}))+C\varepsilon r_{k}\\
&\leq(1+C\varepsilon)\rho(x,z^{j,\ell})+C\varepsilon r_{k}\leq(1+C\varepsilon\eta^{-1})\rho(x,z^{j,\ell})\\
&\leq 2r.\end{split}

Define

(5.9.14)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{j,\ell}\coloneqq B_{X}(w^{j,\ell},\eta r_{\ell})\subseteq B_{X}(g_{k}(x),3r),

where the final inclusion follows from (
5.9.13
). We claim that
\{\mathfrak{B}^{j}\}_{\ell\geq k,\ j\in J_{\ell}^{\prime}}
has bounded overlap i.e. there exists a constant
C\geq 1
such that, for all
q\in X,
we have

(5.9.15)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\geq k}\sum_{j\in J_{\ell}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{B}^{j,\ell}}(q)\leq C.

Indeed, fix
q\in X
and suppose the left-hand side of (
5.9.15
) is non-zero (otherwise there is nothing to show). We first show that

(5.9.16)

\displaystyle|\{\ell\geq k\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{j\in J_{\ell}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{B}^{j,\ell}}(q)\neq\emptyset\}|\leq 2.

Let
\ell\geq k
the smallest integer such that
\sum_{j\in J_{\ell}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{B}^{j,\ell}}(q)\neq\emptyset
and let
i\in J_{\ell}^{\prime}
such that
q\in\mathfrak{B}^{i,\ell}.
Suppose further that
m\geq\ell+2
and
j\in J_{m}^{\prime}.
Recalling the definition of
k(\cdot,\cdot)
from Lemma
5.6.4
, we have
k^{\prime\prime}=k(z^{i,\ell},z^{j,\ell})\leq\ell.
Since
z^{j,m}\in\bigcup_{j\in J_{m}}6D^{j}
and
m\geq\ell+2\geq k^{\prime\prime}+2
we have by Lemma
5.5.14
that

(5.9.17)

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}(z^{i,\ell},z^{j,m})=d_{k^{\prime\prime}}(z^{i,\ell}_{k^{\prime\prime}},z^{j,m}_{k^{\prime\prime}})\geq r_{k^{\prime\prime}+1}/2\geq r_{\ell+1}/2.

Lemma
5.6.1
, Lemma
5.6.2
and Lemma
5.6.5
then give

(5.9.18)

\displaystyle d_{X}(y^{i,\ell},y^{j,m})

\displaystyle\geq d_{X}(\bar{g}_{\ell}(z^{i,\ell}),\bar{g}_{m}(z^{j,m}))-C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\geq d_{X}(\bar{g}(z^{i,\ell}),\bar{g}(z^{j,m}))-C\varepsilon r_{\ell}

(5.9.19)

\displaystyle\geq\phi_{\infty}(z^{i,\ell},z^{j,m})-C\varepsilon r_{\ell}\geq r_{\ell+1}/8\geq\eta r_{\ell}+\eta r_{m}.

This implies
\mathfrak{B}^{i,\ell}\cap\mathfrak{B}^{j,m}=\emptyset
so that
x\not\in\mathfrak{B}^{j,m}.
Since
m\geq\ell+2
and
j\in J_{m}^{\prime}
are arbitrary, we get (
5.9.16
).

Next, we show that

(5.9.20)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in J_{m}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{B}^{j,m}}(q)\lesssim 1\mbox{ for all }m\in\{\ell,\ell+1\}.

Fix
i\in J_{m}^{\prime}
such that
q\in\mathfrak{B}^{i,m}
(we may assume such an index exists otherwise there is nothing to show). Suppose
j\in J_{m}^{\prime}
is such that
q\in\mathfrak{B}^{i,m}\cap\mathfrak{B}^{j,m}
. By definition
w^{i,m}\in 6B^{i,m}
and
w^{j,m}\in 6B^{j,m}
. It follows that
q\in 7B^{i,m}\cap 7B^{j,m}
and
x_{j,m}\in 15B^{i,m}.
Thus,

\{x_{j,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j\in J_{m}^{\prime},\ x\in\mathfrak{B}^{j,m}\}

is an
r_{m}
-separated collection of points contained in
15B^{i,m}.
Since
X
is Ahlfors
n
-regular, this implies (
5.9.20
).

Using (
5.9.15
) and (
5.9.14
) we have

(5.9.21)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\geq k}\sum_{j\in J_{\ell}^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{n}(R^{j,\ell}_{\infty})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\geq k}\sum_{j\in J_{\ell}^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{n}(R^{j,\ell})\leq\sum_{\ell\geq k}\sum_{j\in J_{\ell}^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{n}(6D^{j,\ell})

(5.9.22)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\eta}\sum_{\ell\geq k}\sum_{j\in J_{\ell}^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathfrak{B}^{j,\ell})\lesssim r^{n}.

Combining (
5.9.10
), (
5.9.12
) and (
5.9.21
) gives

(5.9.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{0})\lesssim r^{n}.

Let us now prove the lower Ahlfors estimate. Let
z=\psi_{j}(x_{k}).
We construct a continuous mapping
\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4)\to B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4)
such that
\sigma=\sigma_{2}\circ\sigma_{1}
for some continuous
\sigma_{1}\colon B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4)\to B_{0}
and some
C(n)
-Lipschitz
\sigma_{2}\colon B_{0}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}
. We will do this in such a way that

(5.9.24)

\displaystyle\|\sigma(w)-w\|_{j}\lesssim\varepsilon\eta^{-1}r\mbox{ for all }w\in B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4).

Before constructing
\sigma
, let us see how the existence of such a map gives the lower Ahlfors bound. First, Lemma
2.3.9
implies
B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/8)\subseteq\sigma(B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4)).
Then, since
\sigma(B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4))\subseteq\sigma_{2}(B_{0})
and
\sigma_{2}
is
C(n)
-Lipschitz, we have

(5.9.25)

\displaystyle r^{n}\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/8))\leq\mathcal{H}^{n}(\sigma_{2}(B_{0}))\lesssim_{n}\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{0}).

To define the mapping we first need to know

(5.9.26)

\displaystyle B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4)\subseteq\psi_{j}(B_{k}).

Since
\psi_{j}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz it is enough to show
B(x_{k},r/2)\subseteq B_{k}\subseteq 6D^{j}.
If
k=0
then this is immediate. Suppose instead that
k\geq 1.
Recall from above (
5.9.13
) that
r\gtrsim\eta r_{k}
. Thus, if
p\in B_{0}
is such that
\rho(x,p)=r,
then for
\varepsilon
small enough, Corollary
5.6.9
gives

(5.9.27)

\displaystyle 13r_{k}\geq d_{k}(x_{k},p_{k})\geq(1+C\varepsilon)^{-1}\rho(x,p)-C\varepsilon r_{k}\geq r/2.

In the first inequality we used that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(6D^{j})\leq 13r_{k}.
Now suppose
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
z_{k}\in B(x_{k},r/2)
and let us prove (
5.9.26
). By the above inequality and Lemma
5.5.6
we have
d_{k-1}(z_{k-1},x_{k-1})\leq 14r_{k}\leq 2r_{k-1}.
By Lemma
5.5.9
we have
x_{k-1}\in 3D^{i(j)}
so that
z_{k-1}\in 4D^{i(j)}
by Lemma
5.5.12
. All of this is to say that
k(x,z)\geq k-1
and so
\phi_{k}(x_{k},z_{k})\leq(1+C\varepsilon)d_{k}(x_{k},z_{k})\leq r/4
by Lemma
5.5.6
. Now, using Corollary
5.6.9
and choosing
\varepsilon
small enough depending on
\eta,
we have

\rho(x,z)\leq(1+C\varepsilon)(\phi_{k}(x_{k},z_{k})+C\varepsilon\eta^{-1}r)\leq r.

This gives
z\in B_{0}
which implies
z_{k}\in B_{k},
and we are done.

Now let us define the function
\sigma.
Set
\sigma_{1}=F_{k}^{-1}\circ\psi_{j}^{-1}\colon B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Then, by Lemma
5.6.3
, Lemma
5.6.8
and since
\psi_{j}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-Lipschitz, we have

(5.9.28)

\displaystyle|\rho(\sigma_{1}(p),\sigma_{1}(q))-\|p-q\|_{j}|\leq C\varepsilon r_{k}\leq C_{1}\varepsilon\eta^{-1}r.

for all
p,q\in B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(x_{k},r/4).
Thus,

(5.9.29)

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4))\subseteq B_{0}.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a maximal
\eta^{2}r_{k}
-separated net in
B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4)
such that
z\in\mathcal{N}.
By (
5.9.28
),
\sigma_{1}|_{\mathcal{N}}
is
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz. Let
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=\sigma_{1}(\mathcal{N}),
which is contained in
B_{0}
by (
5.9.29
), and extend
(\sigma_{1}|_{\mathcal{N}})^{-1}
to a
C(n)
-Lipschitz map
\tilde{\sigma}_{2}\colon B_{0}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}.
Finally, let

\sigma_{2}=p_{z,r/4}\circ\tilde{\sigma}_{2},

where
p_{z,\lambda}
is the map defined in Lemma
2.3.3
. Since
\tilde{F}_{2}
is
C(n)
-Lipschitz and
p_{z,r/4}
is 2-Lipschitz, it only remains to show (
5.9.24
). Fix
w\in B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4)
and let
q\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\|q-w\|_{j}\leq\eta^{2}r_{k}\lesssim\eta r.
Since
q\in\mathcal{N}
we have
q=\tilde{\sigma}_{2}\circ\sigma_{1}(p).
Hence,

(5.9.30)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(\sigma_{1}(w))-w\|_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(\sigma_{1}(w))-\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(\sigma_{1}(q))\|+\|q-w\|_{j}\lesssim_{n}d(\sigma_{1}(w),\sigma_{1}(q))+\eta r

(5.9.31)

\displaystyle\leq\|q-w\|_{j}+\eta r\lesssim\eta r.

Since
w\in B_{\|\cdot\|_{j}}(z,r/4),
this implies
\|\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(\sigma_{1}(w))-z\|\lesssim\eta r.
Now, Lemma
2.3.3
and (
5.9.30
) gives

(5.9.32)

\displaystyle\|\sigma(w)-w\|_{j}\leq\|p_{z,r/4}(\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(\sigma_{1}(w)))-\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(\sigma_{1}(w))\|_{j}+\|\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(\sigma_{1}(w))-w\|_{j}\lesssim\varepsilon r.

∎

5.10.
Proof of Theorem
C

In this section we see how Theorem
5.1.1
implies Theorem
C
. So, fix
\varepsilon>0
and suppose
X
is
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat up to scale
s_{0}>0
and let
x\in X
and
0<s_{0}<r.
By scaling we may suppose
r=1.
As in Theorem
5.1.1
, we let
r_{\ell}=10^{-\ell}
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(5.10.1)

\displaystyle r_{k+1}\leq s_{0}<r_{k}.

For
\ell=0
, define the index set
J_{0}=\{j_{0}\}
the point
x_{j_{0},0}=x.
Then, for
1\leq\ell\leq k
, let
\{x_{j,\ell}\}_{j\in J_{\ell}}
be a maximal
r_{\ell}
-separated net in
B_{X}(x,1)
. Conditions (1) - (3) are immediate to verify. Since
X
is
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat up to scale
s_{0}
and
100r_{\ell}\geq s_{0}
for all
0\leq\ell\leq k,
we can find norms
\|\cdot\|_{j,\ell}
satisfying (4) with constant
C\delta
(note, the maps obtained from the Reifenberg condition do not automatically satisfy (
5.1.3
), however, at the cost of using the slightly worse constant
C\delta
, we can modify these maps so that (
5.1.3
) holds by Lemma
2.3.6
).

Choose
\delta
small enough so that the conclusion of Theorem
5.1.1
holds with constant
\varepsilon/20
and let
\rho
be the metric and
g,\bar{g}
the maps we obtain. The statement concerning Ahlfors regularity is immediate from the corresponding statement in Theorem
5.1.1
. The fact that
\bar{g}
maps
B_{X}(x,r)
into
B_{\rho}(0,2r)
follows from (
5.1.7
) and (
5.1.11
). To verify (
1.5.1
) we proceed as follows. Let
y,z\in B_{X}(x,r)
and let
0\leq\ell\leq k
be the largest integer such that there exists
j\in J_{\ell}
with
y,z\in 6B^{j,\ell}.
Suppose to begin with that
\ell<k.
By maximality there exists
i\in J_{\ell+1}
such that
d(y,x_{i,\ell+1})\leq r_{\ell+1}
, in particular,
y\in 6B^{i,\ell+1}.
By maximality of
\ell
we have
z\not\in 6B^{i,\ell+1}.
It now follows that
d(y,z)\geq 5r_{\ell+1}.
Using this with (
5.1.11
) gives

(5.10.2)

\displaystyle|\rho(\bar{g}(y),\bar{g}(z))-d_{X}(y,z)|\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell}/20\leq\varepsilon d(y,z)\leq\varepsilon(d_{X}(y,z)+s_{0}).

Suppose instead that
\ell=k.
Using (
5.1.11
) again, with (
5.10.1
), we have

(5.10.3)

\displaystyle|\rho(\bar{g}(y),\bar{g}(z))-d_{X}(y,z)|\leq\varepsilon r_{k}/20\leq\varepsilon s_{0}\leq\varepsilon(d_{X}(y,z)+s_{0}).

Let’s now verify (
1.5.2
). Pick some
u\in B_{\rho}(0,1-\varepsilon).
By (
5.1.13
) we have
d_{X}(g(0),x)\leq\varepsilon/2
(recall we specified the conclusion of Theorem
5.1.1
to hold with constant
\varepsilon/20
). By (
5.1.7
) we have
u\in B_{j_{0},0}
so that
g(u)\in 6B^{j_{0},0}
. Furthermore, by (
5.1.13
) we have
d_{X}(g(0),x)\leq\varepsilon/2
(recall we specified the conclusion of Theorem
5.1.1
to hold with constant
\varepsilon/20
) so that
g(0)\in 6B^{j_{0},0}.
Applying (
5.1.10
) now gives

(5.10.4)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g(u),x)\leq d_{X}(g(u),g(0))+d_{X}(g(0),x)\leq\rho(u,0)+\varepsilon/20+\varepsilon/20\leq 1.

This implies
g(u)\in B_{X}(x,1)
, so, by maximality, there exists
j\in J_{k}
such that
g(u)\in 6B^{j,k}.
Using (
5.1.9
) and (
5.10.1
) gives now

(5.10.5)

\displaystyle{\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}}_{\rho}(u,\bar{g}(B_{X}(x,1)))\leq\rho(u,\bar{g}(g(u)))\leq\varepsilon r_{k}/20\leq\varepsilon s_{0}.

6.
RF metric spaces and Corona Decompositions (CD)

Contents of this section

6.1.
Main result

In this section we show how to use Theorem
5.1.1
to prove some interesting results concerning Reifenberg flat metric spaces, recall Definition
1.5.1
. The definition is stated in terms of
\xi_{X}
which can be found in Definition
3.1.3
.

Our main goal is the following. The notion of corona decomposition by normed space will be defined in Section
6.4
.

Theorem 6.1.1
.

Suppose
X
is a
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat and Ahlfors

(C_{0},n)
-regular metric space with a system of Christ-David cubes

\mathcal{D}.
Provided
\delta
is small enough, depending only on
n
, then

X
admits a corona decomposition by normed spaces with constants

\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
depending only on
n
. The Carleson norms in
Definition
6.4.1
depend additionally on
C_{0},n
and
\delta.

6.2.
RF metric spaces are bi-Hölder to normed spaces

The first result on Reifenberg flat metric spaces is a generalization of
[
CC97
, Theorem A.1.1]
. For Cheeger-Colding, Reifenberg flat means that every ball is flat with respect to
(\mathbb{R}^{n},|\cdot|)
i.e.
\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with the Euclidean norm. For us, we allow the norm to vary as different scales and locations. The result in
[
CC97
]
is itself a metric space generalization of Reifenberg’s topological disk theorem which appears in
[
Rei60
]
. First, let us state a version of Theorem
5.1.1
tailored to Reifenberg flat metric spaces.

Lemma 6.2.1
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta>0.
Suppose
(X,d)
is
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat,
x\in X
,
r>0
and
\|\cdot\|
is a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\xi_{X}(x,100r,\|\cdot\|)\leq 100\delta r.
If
\delta
is chosen small enough, depending on
\varepsilon
and
n
, there exists a metric
\rho
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
which is bi-Hölder equivalent to
\|\cdot\|
with exponent
1-\varepsilon
such that
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
is
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat and

(6.2.1)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\rho(y,z)-\|y-z\|\right\rvert}\leq\varepsilon r

for all
y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Furthermore, there are maps
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,6r)\to B(x,6r)
and
\bar{g}\colon B(x,6r)\to B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,6r)
such that
g|_{B_{\rho}(0,2r)}
and
\bar{g}|_{B(x,2r)}
are
(1+C\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz. We also have
g(0)\in B(x,10\varepsilon r)
and
\bar{g}(x)\in B_{\rho}(0,10\varepsilon r).
If
y\in B_{\rho}(0,2r)
and
z\in B(x,2r),
then

(6.2.2)

\displaystyle\bar{g}(g(y))=y\quad\mbox{ and }\quad g(\bar{g}(z))=z.

If
y,z\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,6r)

(
resp.
y,z\in B(x,6r))
then

(6.2.3)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d(g(y),g(z))-\rho(y,z)\right\rvert}\leq\varepsilon r\quad(\text{resp.}\ \mathinner{\!\left\lvert\rho(\bar{g}(y),\bar{g}(z))-d(y,z)\right\rvert}\leq\varepsilon r).

If additionally
X
is Ahlfors
n
-regular, then
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
is Ahlfors
n
-regular with regularity constant depending on
n
and the regularity constant for
X
.

Proof.

By scale invariance we may assume
r=1.
Let
J_{0}=\{j_{0}\}
and set
x_{j_{0},0}=x.
Then, supposing we have defined collections of points
\{x_{j,\ell}\}_{j\in J_{\ell}}
up to some
\ell\geq 0,
obtain
\{x_{j,\ell+1}\}_{j\in J_{\ell+1}}
by completing
\{x_{j,\ell}\}_{j\in J_{\ell}}
to a maximal
r_{\ell+1}
-separated net in
B_{X}(x,3).
For each
\ell\geq 0
and
j\in J_{\ell}
choose a norm
\|\cdot\|_{j,\ell}
such that
\xi(x_{j,\ell},100r_{\ell},\|\cdot\|_{j,\ell})\leq 100\delta r_{\ell}.
It is immediate that the hypotheses of Theorem
5.1.1
hold for the pair
(\{x_{j,\ell}\},\{\|\cdot\|_{j,\ell}\})_{\ell\geq 0,j\in J_{\ell}}
with constant
C\delta.
By choosing
\delta
small enough, we can find a metric
\rho
and maps
g,\bar{g}
satisfying the conclusion of Theorem
5.1.1
with constant
\varepsilon.
We immediately get that
\rho
is bi-Hölder to
\|\cdot\|
with the correct exponent and (
6.2.1
). The statement concerning Ahlfors regularity is also immediate. The fact that
g(0)\in B(x,10\varepsilon)
and
\bar{g}(x)\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,10\varepsilon)
follows from (
5.1.13
). Equation (
6.2.3
) follows by combining (
5.1.10
) and (
5.1.11
). It only remains to check the bi-Lipschitz conditions and (
6.2.2
).

Let
y,z\in B_{\rho}(0,2).
By (
5.1.13
) we know
g(0)\in B(x,10\varepsilon)
. Combing this with (
5.1.10
) implies
g(y),g(z)\in B(x,3).
Let
\ell\geq 0
such that there exists
j\in J_{\ell}
satisfying
g(y),g(z)\in 6B^{j}.
Let
i\in J_{\ell}
such that
d_{X}(x_{i,\ell+1},g(y))\leq r_{\ell+1}
(we know such a index exists by maximality because
g(y)\in B(x,3)
). By maximality of
\ell
, we know
g(z)\not\in 6B^{i},
hence,
d_{X}(g(y),g(z))\gtrsim r_{\ell}.
If now follows from (
5.1.10
) that

(6.2.4)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d_{X}(g(y),g(z))-\rho(y,z)\right\rvert}\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon d(g(y),g(z)).

Suppose now that
w,x\in B(x,1).
Let
k\geq 0
be the largest integer such that there exists
i\in J_{k}
such that
w,x\in 6B^{i}.
By the same reasoning above, we have
d_{X}(w,x)\gtrsim r_{\ell}.
Using (
5.1.11
) again we get

(6.2.5)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\rho(\bar{g}(w),\bar{g}(x))-d_{X}(w,x)\right\rvert}\leq\varepsilon r_{\ell}\lesssim\varepsilon d_{X}(w,x).

Now for (
6.2.2
). Suppose
y\in B_{\rho}(0,2)
and
z\in B_{X}(x,2).
As above, we know
g(y)\in B_{X}(x,3).
By maximality, we know for each
\ell\geq 0
that there exists
i,j\in J_{\ell}
such that
g(y)\in 6B^{i}
and
z\in 6B^{j}.
Equation (
6.2.2
) then follows from (
5.1.8
) and (
5.1.9
).
∎

Proposition 6.2.2
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta>0
. Suppose
(X,d)
is
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat,
x\in X
,
r>0
and
\|\cdot\|
is a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\xi(x,100r,\|\cdot\|)\leq 100\delta r.
If
\delta
is small enough, depending on
\varepsilon
, then there exists a map
h\colon B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)\to B_{X}(x,r)
which is bi-Hölder with exponent
\alpha=1-\varepsilon
such that
h(0)\in B_{X}(x,10\varepsilon r),

(6.2.6)

\displaystyle|d(h(y),h(z))-\|y-z\|\,|\leq\varepsilon r

for all
y,z\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)
and

(6.2.7)

\displaystyle h(B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r))\supseteq B_{X}(0,(1-\varepsilon)r).

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
r=1.
Choose
\delta
small enough so that the conclusion of Lemma
6.2.1
holds with constant
\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}.
Let
\rho
be the metric and
g\colon B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,6r)\to B(x,6r)
the map we obtain. Let
s\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the map defined by
s(x)=\tfrac{x}{1+\varepsilon}
and set
h\coloneqq g\circ s.
Let us check that
h
is bi-Hölder. Clearly
s
is
(1+\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz and
s(B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,1))\subseteq B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,1).
Thus, it suffices to check
g
is bi-Hölder on
B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,1).
Let
y,z\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,1).
By (
6.2.1
) we know
B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,1)\subseteq B_{\rho}(0,2)
. Then, since
g
is bi-Lipschitz on
B_{\rho}(0,2)
and
\rho
is bi-Hölder equivalent to
\|\cdot\|
with exponent
\alpha=1-\varepsilon,
we have

(6.2.8)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g(y),g(z))\lesssim\rho(y,z)\leq\|y-z\|^{1-\varepsilon}

and

(6.2.9)

\displaystyle d_{X}(g(y),g(z))\gtrsim\rho(y,z)\gtrsim\|y-z\|^{1+\varepsilon}.

Equation (
6.2.6
) follows from (
6.2.1
), (
6.2.3
), and the fact that

\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\|y-z\|-\|s(y)-s(z)\|\right\rvert}\lesssim\varepsilon

for all
y,z\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,1).
Finally, we check (
6.2.7
) (with a slightly worse constant).
Pick a point
u\in B_{X}(x,1-13\varepsilon)
and set
v=s^{-1}(\bar{g}(u))=(1+\varepsilon)\bar{g}(u).
By (
6.2.2
) we know
g(\bar{g}(u))=u
so that
h(v)=u.
By (
6.2.1
), (
6.2.3
) and using that fact that
\bar{g}(x)\in B_{\rho}(0,10\varepsilon),
we have

(6.2.10)

\displaystyle\|\bar{g}(u)\|

\displaystyle\leq\rho(\bar{g}(u),0)+\varepsilon\leq\rho(\bar{g}(u),\bar{g}(x))+\rho(\bar{g}(x),0)+\varepsilon

(6.2.11)

\displaystyle\leq d_{X}(u,x)+12\varepsilon\leq 1-\varepsilon\leq\tfrac{1}{1+\varepsilon}.

It now follows that
\|v\|=(1+\varepsilon)\|\bar{g}(u)\|\leq 1
so that
v\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,1).

∎

6.3.
AR+RF implies UR

One major goal of this section is to show that upper Ahlfors regular Reifenberg flat metric spaces are UR (Proposition
6.3.6
). The proof of this fact is reminiscent of the proof that
Weak Geometric Lemma
and
Big Projections
imply
Big Pieces of Lipschitz Graphs
in
[
DS93b
]
. Crucial for us is the use of Theorem
C
. It allows us to show that Reifenberg flat metric spaces have a suitable metric space analogue of the big projections property (Corollary
6.3.5
) and satisfy certain topological conditions required to apply a result of Guy C. David
[
Dav16
]
(see Theorem
6.3.2
) on the bi-Lipschitz decomposition of Lipschitz functions defined on certain metric spaces.

To state the result of Guy C. David, we need the following definition.

Definition 6.3.1
.

A metric space
X
is called
linearly locally contractible
(LLC) if there exist constant
A\geq 1
and
R>0
such that every ball
B
of radius
0<r<R
is contractible in
AB
(i.e. there exists a continuous map
H\colon B\times[0,1]\to AB
such that
H(\cdot,0)
is the identity map on
B
and
H(\cdot,1)
is a constant map).

Theorem 6.3.2

(
[
Dav16
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular, LLC, complete, oriented topological
n
-manifold and
\|\cdot\|
a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be a system of Christ-David cubes for
X
and
z\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q_{0}\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)
be Lipschitz. For each
\varepsilon>0
there exist constants
L,N\geq 1
depending only on
\varepsilon,n
, the regularity constant of
X
and the Lipschitz constant of
z,
and measurable subsets
E_{1},\dots,E_{N}
such that
z|_{E_{i}}
is
L
-bi-Lipschitz and

(6.3.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(z\left(Q_{0}\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}E_{i}\right)\right)\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{H}^{n}(Q_{0}).

Remark 6.3.3
.

The statement in
[
Dav16
]
is more general than that stated above. In particular, it allows for Ahlfors
s
-regular (i.e. non-integer) domains and Ahlfors
s
-regular targets spaces possessing
n
-
manifold weak tangents
, see
[
Dav16
, Definition 1.5]
. It is easy to check that
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)
has
n
-manifold weak tangents, see the first point in
[
Dav16
, Remark 1.7]
for an explanation in the case of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with Euclidean norm. The argument for general norms is exactly the same.

In order to make use of Theorem
6.3.2
, we of course need to know Reifenberg flat metric spaces are LLC. This is a consequence of Proposition
6.2.2
and is proven below.

Proposition 6.3.4
.

Suppose
X
is a
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat metric space. If
\delta>0
is chosen small enough then
X
is LLC with constants
A=2
and
R=\infty.

Proof.

Let
x\in X
,
r>0
and choose a norm
\|\cdot\|
such that
\xi_{X}(x,200r,\|\cdot\|)\leq 200\delta r.
Let
\varepsilon>0
and choose
\delta
small enough so the conclusion of Proposition
6.2.2
holds with constant
\varepsilon.
Let
h\colon B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,2r)\to B_{X}(x,2r)
be the map we obtain. By (
6.2.7
) we know
h^{-1}
is well-defined on
B_{X}(x,r).
Since
h(0)\in B_{X}(x,20\varepsilon r)
we know by (
6.2.6
) that
h^{-1}(x)\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,22\varepsilon r).
Using (
6.2.6
) again implies
h^{-1}(B_{X}(x,r))\subseteq B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,(1+24\varepsilon)r).
Now consider the map
H\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B(x,r)\times[0,1]\to X
defined by

(6.3.2)

\displaystyle H(z,t)=h\left(h^{-1}(x)+t(h^{-1}(z)-h^{-1}(x))\right).

We observe first that
H
takes values in
B_{X}(x,2r).
Indeed, suppose
z\in B(x,r)
and
0\leq t\leq 1.
By the discussion above we know both
h^{-1}(x),h^{-1}(z)\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,(1+24\varepsilon)r)\subseteq B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,3r/2).
By convexity, this implies
h^{-1}(x)+t(h^{-1}(z)-h^{-1}(x))\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,3r/2).
Since
h(0)\in B_{X}(0,20\varepsilon r),
equation (
6.2.6
) now implies
H(z,t)\in B_{X}(x,2r),
as required. Since
h
is bi-Hölder it is continuous and its inverse
h^{-1}
is continuous on
B_{X}(x,r).
Since the map
(y,s)\mapsto h^{-1}(x)+s(y-h^{-1}(x))
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[0,1]
, it follows that
H
is continuous
B(x,r)\times[0,1].
Finally, note that
H(z,0)=x
and
H(z,1)=z
. Recalling Definition
6.3.1
, this finishes the proof of Proposition
6.3.4
.
∎

Proposition
6.2.2
also gives us lower bounds on the Hausdorff measure of balls in Reifenberg flat sets (this is why we only need to assume upper Ahlfors regularity).

Corollary 6.3.5
.

Suppose
X
is
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat. If
\delta
is small enough then the following holds. Let
x\in X,\ r>0
and choose a norm
\|\cdot\|
and a
C(n)
-Lipschitz map
\varphi\colon B(x,100r)\to B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,100r)
such that
\xi_{X}(x,100r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)\lesssim\delta
(such objects exist by Lemma
2.3.5
). Then,

(6.3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(B(x,r))\gtrsim_{n}\mathcal{H}^{n}(\varphi(B(x,r)))\gtrsim_{n}r^{n}.

In particular,
X
is Ahlfors lower
n
-regular, with regularity constant depending only on
n
.

Proof.

The first inequality in (
6.3.3
) since we assumed
\varphi
to be
C(n)
-Lipschitz. It only remains to prove the second inequality. Let
\varepsilon>0
and choose
\delta
small enough such that Proposition
6.2.2
holds with constant
\varepsilon
. Let
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)\to B_{X}(x,r)
be the map we obtain. Now consider the map
f\coloneqq p_{0,r}\circ\varphi\circ h
which is continuous from
B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)
to itself. Recall,
p_{z,\lambda}
is the map from Lemma
2.3.3
. Suppose
y,z\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r).
By definition
h(y),h(z)\in B_{X}(x,r)
and
\varphi(g(y)),\varphi(g(z))\in B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r+100\delta r).
So, by Lemma
2.3.3
and (
6.2.6
) we have
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\|f(y)-f(z)\|-\|y-z\|\right\rvert}\leq C\varepsilon r.
It follows from this that

(6.3.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\|\cdot\|}(f(B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)))\gtrsim r^{n}

(this is a consequence of Brouwer’s Fixed Point Theorem, see
[
Bat20
, Lemma 7.1]
for a proof involving the unit Euclidean ball). Finally, using that
p_{0,r}
is 2-Lipschitz to get

(6.3.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\|\cdot\|}(\varphi(B(x,r)))\geq\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\|\cdot\|}(\varphi(h(B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r))))\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\|\cdot\|}(f(B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)))\gtrsim r^{n}.

∎

We now have everything we need to prove UR.

Proposition 6.3.6
.

Suppose
X
is
(\delta,n)
-Reifenberg flat and Ahlfors upper
(C_{0},n)
-regular. If
\delta>0
is small enough, depending only on
n
, then
X
is uniformly
n
-rectifiable with
UR
constants depending only on
\delta,C_{0}
and
n
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
(to be chosen small enough) and choose
\delta
small enough so that Corollary
6.3.5
and conclusion of Lemma
6.2.1
holds with constant
\varepsilon.
It is immediate that
X
is Ahlfors
n
-regular and it remains to check
X
has big pieces of Lipschitz images. Let
x\in X
,
R>0
and choose a norm
\|\cdot\|
such that
\xi_{X}(x,100R,\|\cdot\|)\leq 100\delta.
Now, let
\rho
be the metric and
g
the map we obtain by applying Lemma
6.2.1
. Let
r=R/2.

Claim:
There exists
A\subseteq B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)
and an
L(n)
-Lipschitz map
f\colon A\to B_{\rho}(0,r)
such that

\mathcal{H}^{n}(f(A)\cap B_{\rho}(0,r))\gtrsim r^{n}.

Before proving the claim, let us see how it finishes the proof of the lemma. Recalling Remark
2.2.1
, after rescaling we can find a
C(n)
-bi-Lipschitz map
T\colon(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)\subseteq B_{|\cdot|}(0,R).
Let
A^{\prime}=T(A)\subseteq B_{|\cdot|}(0,R).
Combing the fact that
g(0)\in B(x,10\varepsilon R)
with (
6.2.3
) we know
g(B_{\rho}(0,r))\subseteq B(x,R).
Since
g
is
(1+\varepsilon)
-bi-Lipschitz on
B_{\rho}(0,r)
, the map
F\coloneqq f\circ g\circ T^{-1}\colon A^{\prime}\to B(x,R)
is
C(n)
-Lipschitz. We now get

(6.3.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(F(A^{\prime})\cap B(x,R))\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{n}(f(A)\cap B_{\rho}(0,r))\gtrsim r^{n}\gtrsim R^{n}.

For the remainder of the proof we focus on proving the claim. Since is
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat, by applying Lemma
2.3.5
we can find a norm
\|\cdot\|
and a
C(n)
-Lipschitz mapping
\varphi\colon B_{\rho}(0,100r)\to B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,100r)
satisfying

\xi_{(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)}(0,100r,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)\leq C\varepsilon.

For
\varepsilon
small enough, Lemma
6.3.5
implies

(6.3.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\|\cdot\|}(\varphi(B_{\rho}(0,r)))\gtrsim r^{n}.

By Theorem
5.1.1
and Proposition
6.3.4
we know
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho)
satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
6.3.2
. Let
\eta>0
(to be chosen small enough) and find constant
L,N\geq 1
, depending on
\eta,n
and
C_{0}
such that
\varphi|_{E_{i}}
is
L
-Lipschitz for each
i=1,\dots,N
and

(6.3.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\varphi\left(B_{\rho}(0,r)\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}E_{i}\right)\right)\leq\eta\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{\rho}(0,r)).

Combining (
6.3.7
), (
6.3.8
) and choosing
\eta
small enough, we have

(6.3.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\varphi\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}E_{i}\right)\right)\geq\mathcal{H}^{n}(\varphi(B_{\rho}(0,r)))-\eta\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{\rho}(0,r))\gtrsim r^{n}.

In particular, there exists some
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{n}(\varphi(E_{i}))\gtrsim 1/N.
Let
A=\varphi(E_{i})\subseteq B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,r)
and set
f=\varphi^{-1}|_{A}\colon A\to B_{\rho}(0,r).
It follows that
f
is
C(n)
-bi-Lipschitz and

(6.3.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(f(F_{i}))\gtrsim_{n}\mathcal{H}^{n}(E_{i})\gtrsim r^{n},

as required.
∎

6.4.
Corona Decompositions and CD(
\mathscr{Y}
)

The corona decomposition for subsets of Euclidean space was introduced in
[
DS91
]
. Roughly speaking, a set
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
admits a corona decomposition if any system of Christ-David cubes may be partition into good and bad cubes which satisfy the following conditions. First, the number of bad cubes is controlled (in the sense that they satisfy a Carleson packing condition). Second, the good cubes may be decomposed into a controlled number of stopping-time regions such that for each of these regions there exists a Lipschitz graph which well-approximated the region. The definition of stopping-time region in given below. Again, controlled means that the top cubes satisfy a Carleson packing condition. For us, we need an alternative to the approximation by Lipschitz graphs.

We take the opportunity here to introduce a version of the corona decomposition for metric spaces. The final part of this section will be showing the existence of such a decomposition for Reifenberg flat metric spaces.

Definition 6.4.1

(Corona Decomposition by
\mathscr{Y}
)
.

Suppose
(X,d)
is an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space and
\mathcal{D}
a system of Christ-David cubes on
X
. Let
\mathscr{Y}
be a family of Ahlfors
n
-regular metric spaces with uniform regularity constant. We say
X
admits a
Corona Decomposition by
\mathscr{Y}
(CD(
\mathscr{Y}
)), with constants
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\geq 1
, if for each
\theta>0
there exists a partition
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{G}\cup\mathcal{B}
such that the following conditions hold.

(1)

There exists a partition of
\mathcal{G}
into disjoint stopping-time regions
\{S\}_{S\in\mathcal{F}}.

(2)

The families
\mathcal{B}
and
\{Q(S)\}_{S\in\mathcal{F}}
satisfy a Carleson packing condition with Carleson constants depending only on
\theta.

(3)

For each
S\in\mathcal{F}
there exists
Y_{S}\in\mathscr{Y}
, a point
y\in Y
and a map
\varphi_{S}\colon 3B_{Q(S)}\to B_{Y}(y,3\ell(Q(S)))
such that if
Q\in S
and
x,y\in 3B_{Q}
satisfy
d(x,y)\geq\theta\ell(Q),
then

(6.4.1)
