\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}}d(x,y)\leq d_{Y_{S}}(\varphi_{S}(x),\varphi_{S}(y))\leq\Lambda_{2}d(x,y).

Definition 6.4.2
.

We say
X
admits a
Corona Decomposition by normed spaces
(CD(normed spaces)) with constant
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
if it admits a corona decomposition by

\mathscr{Y}=\{(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\|\cdot\|\mbox{ is a norm on }\mathbb{R}^{n}\}

with constant
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
. We say
X
admits a
Corona Decomposition by Ahlfors
(C,n)
-regular
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat spaces
with constant
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
if
X
admits a corona decomposition by

\mathscr{Y}(C,\varepsilon)=\{Y\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Y\mbox{ is Ahlfors }(C,n)\mbox{-regular and }(\varepsilon,n)\mbox{-Reifenberg flat}\}

with constant
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}.
We say
X
admits a
Corona Decomposition by Ahlfors regular Reifenberg flat spaces
with constant
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
if there exists
C\geq 1
such that
X
admits a corona decomposition by Ahlfors
(C,n)
-regular
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat spaces with constant
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
for all
\varepsilon>0.

Remark 6.4.3
.

Let us describe the relationship between the usual corona decomposition and the corona decomposition by normed spaces. Items (1) and (2) are lifted verbatim from the definition of corona decomposition in
[
DS91
]
, with (2) quantifying the control on the number of bad cubes and stopping-times regions, respectively. For subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, item (3) is replaced by the following condition:

(3
′
)

For each
S\in\mathcal{F}
, there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma_{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with constant at most
\eta>0
such that if
Q\in S
and
x\in 3B_{Q}
then
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,\Gamma_{S})\leq\theta\ell(Q).

Condition (3) is an intrinsic condition, but it implies an extrinsic condition in the spirit of (
3^{\prime}
) in the following way. Let
\mathcal{N}
be a maximal net in
3B_{Q(S)}
such that if
Q\in S
and
x,y\in\mathcal{N}\cap Q
then
d(x,y)\geq\theta\ell(Q).
Item (3) tells us that
\mathcal{N}
is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to a subset
A\subseteq B_{\|\cdot\|_{S}}(0,3\ell(Q(S))).
Applying Lemma
4.3.2
, we can find a Banach space
\mathcal{B}
, an isometric embedding on
X
into
\mathcal{B}
and a surface
\Sigma_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
which is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that, after identifying
X
with its image in
\mathcal{B}
, we have
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\Sigma_{S}.
By maximality, if
z\in Q\in S
then
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}_{\mathcal{B}}(z,\Sigma_{S})\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}_{X}(z,\mathcal{N})\leq\theta\ell(Q).
In particular, (3) implies a version of (
3^{\prime}
) with bi-Lipschitz images of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(with possibly large constant) replacing the role of Lipschitz graphs with small constants.

Remark 6.4.4
.

Suppose
X
is an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space,
\mathcal{D}
is a system of Christ-David cubes for
X
and suppose
X
admits a corona decomposition by
\mathscr{Y}
. It is easy to obtain from this a local corona decomposition by
\mathscr{Y}
i.e. for each
Q\in\mathcal{D}
a decomposition of
\mathcal{D}(Q)
satisfying Definition
6.4.1
(1) - (3) with uniform estimates. Suppose
Q\in\mathcal{D}
and that we have such a decomposition of
\mathcal{D}(Q)
into bad cubes
\mathcal{B}
and stopping-time regions
\{S\}_{S\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}.
Viewing each cube in
\mathcal{B}
as a stopping-time region consisting of a single cube, we get a decomposition of
\mathcal{D}(Q)
into stopping-time regions
\{S\}_{S\in\mathcal{F}}
whose top cubes satisfy a Carleson packing condition. Furthermore, each
S\in\mathcal{F}
which is not a singleton satisfies Definition
6.4.1
(3). This reformulated decomposition will be used in Section
9
.

Remark 6.4.5
.

In Euclidean space, the fact that existence of a corona decomposition implies UR is a crucial step in showing BWGL implies UR. The same is true for us here. The proof of this fact in metric space is very similar to the argument given in
[
DS91
]
and the details are given in Section
9
.

6.5.
UR+RF implies CD(normed spaces)

Recalling Proposition
6.3.6
and Corollary
6.3.5
above, Theorem
6.1.1
is an easy consequence of the following proposition.

Proposition 6.5.1
.

Let
X
be uniformly
n
-rectifiable and
\mathcal{D}
a system of Christ-David cubes for
X
. Suppose for each
\varepsilon>0
and each
Q\in\mathcal{D}
with
\xi_{X}(Q)\leq\varepsilon
that there exists a norm
\|\cdot\|
and a map
\varphi\colon 3B_{Q}\to B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,3\ell(Q))
satisfying
\xi(Q,\|\cdot\|,\varphi)\leq 2\varepsilon
and
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{\|\cdot\|}(\varphi(3B_{Q}))\geq c\ell(Q)^{n}.
Then
X
admits a corona decomposition by normed spaces with constants
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
depending only on
n
. The Carleson norms in Definition
6.4.1
depend additionally2 on
n
and the constants in Definition
1.2.1
.

Let us say a few words about the proof of Proposition
6.5.1
. Let
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}
and let
\varphi
be its associate map. By modifying
\varphi
slightly we may assume it is
C(n)
-Lipschitz. Since
X
is UR it satisfies a Carleson condition for the
\gamma
-coefficients so
\varphi
is approximated by affine functions at most scales and locations. Let’s assume for simplicity that
\varphi
is approximated by affine functions at
all
scales and locations (additional considerations are needed to deal with the places where
\varphi
is not approximated by affine functions). We run a stopping-time procedure, stopping at sub-cubes
Q\subseteq Q_{0}
if there exists a pair of
\theta\ell(Q)
-separated points which are squished under
\varphi.
In this stopping-time region the upper bound in Definition
6.4.1
holds because we assume
\varphi
to be Lipschitz and the lower bound holds because of the stopping condition. We get our collection of stopping-time regions by restarting on the stopped cubes (with new
\varphi
maps). We also need to control how often we stop. Suppose
Q
is a stopped cube in the region associated to
Q_{0}
. Since there are two points in
Q
which are squished under
\varphi
and since
\varphi
is approximated by affine functions, this forces the squishing of the whole of
Q
in one direction. In particular,
\varphi(Q)
has small measure in the image. Since we are assuming large projection for
Q_{0},
this prevents there from being to many stopped cubes. A similar argument over all stopping-time regions gives the desired Carleson packing condition.

Remark 6.5.2
.

We will prove a local decomposition, namely, for each
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}
we will find a decomposition of the sub-cubes
\mathcal{D}(Q_{0})=\mathcal{G}\cup\mathcal{B}
satisfying conditions (1) - (3) with estimates independent of
Q_{0}
(recall the definition of
\mathcal{D}(Q_{0})
from (
2.6.1
)). The fact that such a local decomposition yields a global decomposition is already known and relies only on the properties of Christ-David cubes, see
[
DS91
, Page 38]
.

Now we can begin. For the remainder of this section, fix a metric space
(X,d)
satisfying the hypotheses of Proposition
6.5.1
with
\mathcal{D}
, a system of Christ-David cubes on
X
, and
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}
. Let
\theta>0
(the constant appearing in Definition
6.4.1
) and let
\varepsilon>0
, which will be chosen small enough depending on
\theta.

Recall the definitions of
\xi_{X}(AQ)
and
\gamma_{X,f,\|\cdot\|}^{K}(AQ)
from Definition
4.2.2
. We start by setting

(6.5.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=\{Q\in\mathcal{D}(Q_{0})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\xi_{X}(3Q)\leq\varepsilon\}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{D}(Q_{0})\setminus\mathcal{G}.

Remark 6.5.3
.

In the previous section we showed UR implies BWGL (recall Lemma
3.1.8
and Theorem
4.1.1
). Thus, by applying Lemma
4.2.3
we know that
\mathcal{B}
satisfies a Carleson packing condition as required for part of Definition
6.4.1
(2).

Our next step is to define the family of stopping-time regions which partition
\mathcal{G}
. By Lemma
2.3.5
, for each
Q\in\mathcal{G}
there exists a norm
\|\cdot\|_{Q}
and a
C(n)
-Lipschitz map
\pi_{Q}\colon 3B_{Q}\to B_{\|\cdot\|_{Q}}(0,3\ell(Q))
such that

\xi_{X}(3Q,\|\cdot\|_{Q},\pi_{Q})\leq\xi_{X}(3Q)+\varepsilon\leq 2\varepsilon.

Let
K\geq 1
be the constant obtained from Theorem
4.1.1
for
X.
Then set

(6.5.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\{R\in\mathcal{D}(Q)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\gamma_{X,\pi_{Q},\|\cdot\|_{Q}}^{K}(3R)\leq\varepsilon\mbox{ for all }R^{\prime}\in\text{Sibling}(R)\};

(6.5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(Q)\setminus\mathcal{G}(Q,\varepsilon).

Definition 6.5.4
.

Let
\Lambda\geq 1,
to be chosen sufficiently large. For each
Q\in\mathcal{G}
and
R\in\mathcal{G}
such that
R\subseteq Q,
define a stopping-time region
S_{Q}(R)
as follows. First, add
R
to
S_{Q}(R).
Then, inductively, add cubes
T\subset R
to
S_{Q}(R)
if each of the following conditions hold.

(1)

\text{Parent}(T)\in S_{Q}(R)
.

(2)

T\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon).

(3)

For each
T^{\prime}\in\text{Sibling}(T)
we have
\|\pi_{Q}(x)-\pi_{Q}(y)\|_{Q}\geq d(x,y)/\Lambda
for all
x,y\in 3B_{T^{\prime}}
such that
d(x,y)\geq\theta\ell(T^{\prime}).

If
T\in\min(S_{Q}(R))
then there exists
T^{\prime}\in\text{Child}(T)
such that either
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon)
or
\|\pi_{Q}(x)-\pi_{Q}(y)\|_{Q}<d(x,y)/\Lambda
for some
x,y\in 3B_{T^{\prime}}
with
d(x,y)\geq\theta\ell(T^{\prime}).
We partition
\text{min}(S_{Q}(R))
by setting

(6.5.4)

\displaystyle{\min}_{1}(S_{Q}(R))

\displaystyle=\{T\in\min(S_{Q}(R))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{ there exists }T^{\prime}\in\text{Child}(T)\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon)\};

(6.5.5)

\displaystyle{\min}_{2}(S_{Q}(R))

\displaystyle=\min(S_{Q}(R))\setminus{\min}_{1}(S_{Q}(R)).

Remark 6.5.5
.

In words, if
T\in\text{min}_{1}(S_{Q}(R))
then there exists a child
T^{\prime}
of
T
such that
\pi_{Q}
is not well-approximated by affine functions in
T^{\prime}.
If
T\in\text{min}_{2}(S_{Q}(R))
then
\pi_{Q}
is well-approximated by affine functions on each of its children, however there exists a child
T^{\prime}
such that
\pi_{Q}
contracts a pair of well-separated points in
T^{\prime}.
We will use this property later to show
\mathcal{H}^{n}(\pi_{Q}(T))
is small for such a
T
.

Before defining the family of stopping-time regions
\mathcal{F},
we first define a family
\mathcal{F}_{Q}
associated to
Q\in\mathcal{G}
and prove some properties of it. Let
\tau>0
.

Lemma 6.5.6
.

There exists a constant
N=N(C_{0},n,\varepsilon,\tau)
such that

(6.5.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\left\{x\in Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon)}\mathds{1}_{R}(x)\geq N\right\}\right)\leq\tau\ell(Q)^{n}\quad\mbox{ for all }Q\in\mathcal{G}.

Proof.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{G}.
If
R\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon)
then there exists
T\in\text{Sibling}(R)
such that
\gamma_{X,\pi_{Q},\|\cdot\|_{Q}}^{K}(T)>\varepsilon.
By Ahlfors regularity each cube in
\mathcal{D}
has a bounded number of siblings, depending on the regularity constant and
n
. Then, by Theorem
4.1.1
, Ahlfors regularity of
X
and Chebyshev’s Inequality, the left-hand side of (
6.5.6
) is at most

(6.5.7)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon)}\ell(R)^{n}\lesssim_{C_{0},n}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\subseteq Q\\
\gamma_{X,\pi_{Q},\|\cdot\|_{Q}}^{K}(T)>\varepsilon\end{subarray}}\ell(R)^{n}\lesssim_{C_{0},\varepsilon,n}\frac{\ell(Q)^{n}}{N}.

Choosing
N
large enough gives the required result.
∎

From now on we fix
N
as in the above lemma. For
Q\in\mathcal{G},
let
\text{Top}_{Q}^{0}=\{Q\}
and
\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{0}=\{S_{Q}(Q)\}
. Then, supposing
\text{Top}_{Q}^{k}
and
\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{k}
have been defined for some integer
0\leq k\leq N-2,
let

(6.5.8)

\displaystyle\text{Top}_{Q}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{R\in\text{Top}_{Q}^{k}}\bigcup_{T\in\text{min}_{1}(S_{Q}(R))}\text{Child}(T);

(6.5.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{k+1}

\displaystyle=\{S_{Q}(R)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\in\text{Top}_{Q}^{k+1}\}.

Now, let

(6.5.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{Q}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{k=1}^{N-1}\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{k}

and

(6.5.11)

\displaystyle\text{min}(\mathcal{F}_{Q})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{S_{Q}(R)\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}}{\min}_{2}(S_{Q}(R))\cup\bigcup_{S_{Q}(R)\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{N-1}}{\min}_{1}(S_{Q}(R));

Lemma 6.5.7
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{G}.
Then
\mathcal{F}_{Q}
is a disjoint collection of stopping-time regions.

Proof.

Fix distinct stopping-time regions
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}.
Let
R,R^{\prime}\subseteq Q
such that
S=S_{Q}(R)
and
S^{\prime}=S_{Q}(R^{\prime}).
Since
S
and
S^{\prime}
are distinct, we must have
R\neq R^{\prime}.
If
R\cap R^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
there is nothing to show. Suppose then that
R\cap R^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and suppose without loss of generality that
R\subseteq R^{\prime}.
By construction we have
R\subseteq R^{\prime\prime}
for some
R^{\prime\prime}\in\min(S^{\prime})
and so
S,S^{\prime}
are disjoint
∎

We will show that the image of
\min(\mathcal{F}_{Q})
under
\pi_{Q}
has small
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-measure, starting with the cubes in
\min_{1}.

Lemma 6.5.8
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{G}.
Then,

(6.5.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\pi_{Q}\left(\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{N-1}}\bigcup_{R\in\emph{min}_{1}(S)}R\right)\right)\lesssim_{C_{0},n}\tau\ell(Q)^{n}.

Proof.

Suppose
S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{N-1}
and
R\in\text{min}_{1}(S).
By construction there exists a
T_{R}\in\text{Child}(R)
which is contained in exactly
N
cubes from
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon).
Recalling that
\pi_{Q}
is
C(n)
-Lipschitz and using (
6.5.6
) with Ahlfors regularity and the fact that
\min(\mathcal{F}_{Q})
is a collection of disjoint cubes, the left-hand side of (
6.5.13
) is at most

(6.5.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{N-1}}\sum_{R\in\text{min}_{1}(S)}\mathcal{H}^{n}(\pi_{Q}(R))

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{N-1}}\sum_{R\in\text{min}_{1}(S)}\mathcal{H}^{n}(R)

(6.5.15)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C_{0},n}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{N-1}}\sum_{R\in\text{min}_{1}(S)}\mathcal{H}^{n}(T_{R})

(6.5.16)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\left\{x\in Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon)}\mathds{1}_{R}(x)\geq N\right\}\right)

(6.5.17)

\displaystyle\leq\tau\ell(Q).

∎

To show that the image of cubes in
\min_{2}
have small measure we need the following preliminary lemma.

Lemma 6.5.9
.

Let
\varepsilon,\eta>0.
Suppose
\|\cdot\|_{1}
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
are norms on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
A\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1})\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{2})
is an
L
-Lipschitz affine map such that
\|A(x)-A(y)\|_{2}\leq\eta\|x-y\|_{1}
for some
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then
\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon r_{B}}(A(B)))\lesssim_{L,n}(\varepsilon+\eta)r_{B}^{n}
for all balls
B\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1})
, where
\mathcal{N}_{\delta}(F)
denotes the
\delta
-neighbourhood of
F.

Proof.

By translating and rotating we may also assume
B
is centred at the origin and
A
is linear. It suffices to prove the case in which both the domain and image are equipped with the Euclidean norm. Indeed, suppose we have Lemma
6.5.9
for this case and suppose
\|\cdot\|_{1}
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
are norms and
A
is a map satisfying the hypotheses above with constant
\eta
and
L
. By Remark
2.2.1
there exist
C(n)
-bi-Lipschitz linear maps
T_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{i})\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},|\cdot|)
,
i=1,2.
It is immediate to check that
A^{\prime}=T_{2}\circ A\circ T_{1}^{-1}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
6.5.9
with constant
\eta^{\prime}\sim\eta
and
L^{\prime}\sim_{n}L.
If
B\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{1})
is a ball then
T_{1}(B)
is contained in a Euclidean ball
B^{\prime}
centred at the origin satisfying
r_{B}\sim r_{B}.
Hence

(6.5.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon r_{B}}(A(B)))

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon r_{B}}(A(T_{1}^{-1}(B^{\prime}))))\lesssim_{n}\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathcal{N}_{C\varepsilon r_{B^{\prime}}}(A^{\prime}(B^{\prime})))

(6.5.19)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n,L^{\prime}}(\varepsilon+\eta^{\prime})r_{B^{\prime}}^{n}\lesssim_{n,L}(\varepsilon+\eta)r_{B}^{n}

We proceed now with the proof of Lemma
6.5.9
in this special case. It is enough to show that
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon r_{B}}(A(B))
is contained in a cuboid of dimension

(6.5.20)

\displaystyle\underbrace{r_{B}\times\cdots\times r_{B}}_{\text{$(n-1)$-times}}\times(\varepsilon+\eta)r_{B}

up to some constant depending on
L
. So, let
u=x-y
and set
V
to be the
(n-1)
-dimensional subspace which is perpendicular to
u
. Let
W=A(V)
(also an
(n-1)
-dimensional subspace) and denote by
\pi_{W}
the orthogonal projection onto
W
and
\pi_{W^{\perp}}
the orthogonal projection onto
W^{\perp}.
Let
p\in\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon r_{B}}(A(B))
and suppose
z\in B
is such that
|A(z)-p|\leq\varepsilon r_{B}.
Write
z=\lambda u+\mu v
for some
v\in V
. Since
A
is
L
-Lipschitz we have

(6.5.21)

\displaystyle|\pi_{W}(p)|\leq|\pi_{W}(A(z))|+\varepsilon r_{B}\leq|A(z)|+\varepsilon r_{B}\leq L|z|+\varepsilon r_{B}\leq 2Lr_{B}.

Since
A
is linear and
A(\mu v)\in W
we know
\pi_{W^{\perp}}(A(z))=\pi_{W^{\perp}}(A(\lambda u)).
Since
\lambda u
is the orthogonal projection of
z
onto the line through
u
we know
|\lambda u|\leq|z|\leq r_{B}.
Combining these facts gives

(6.5.22)

\displaystyle|\pi_{W^{\perp}}(p)|\leq|\pi_{W^{\perp}}(A(z))|+\varepsilon r_{B}=|\pi_{W^{\perp}}(A(\lambda u))|+\varepsilon r_{B}\leq\eta|\lambda u|+\varepsilon r_{B}\leq(\eta+\varepsilon)r_{B}.

Since
p
was arbitrary, it follows that
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon r_{B}}(A(B))
is contained in a cuboid with the same dimensions as in (
6.5.20
) up to some constant depending only on
L
.
∎

Lemma 6.5.10
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{G}.
Then,

(6.5.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\pi_{Q}\left(\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}}\bigcup_{R\in\emph{min}_{2}(S)}R\right)\right)\lesssim\frac{1}{\Lambda}\ell(Q)^{n}.

Proof.

Suppose
S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}
and
R\in\min_{2}(S)
.
We claim

(6.5.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(\pi_{Q}(R))\lesssim\frac{\ell(R)^{n}}{\Lambda}.

Recalling the definition of
\text{min}_{2}(S),
there exists
T\in\text{Child}(R)\cap\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon)
and
x_{0},y_{0}\in 3B_{T}
such that
d(x_{0},y_{0})\geq\theta\ell(T)
and

(6.5.25)

\displaystyle\|\pi_{Q}(x_{0})-\pi_{Q}(y_{0})\|_{Q}<\frac{d(x_{0},y_{0})}{\Lambda}.

By definition of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(Q,\varepsilon),
there exists a norm
\|\cdot\|
, a map
\varphi\colon 3B_{T}\to B_{\|\cdot\|}(0,3\ell(T))
and a
K
-Lipschitz affine function
A\colon(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)\to(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{Q})
such that

(6.5.26)

\displaystyle\sup_{x,y\in 3B_{T}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert d(x,y)-\|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\|\right\rvert}\leq 6\varepsilon\ell(T)

and

(6.5.27)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in 3B_{T}}\|\pi_{Q}(x)-A(\varphi(x))\|_{Q}\leq 6\varepsilon\ell(T).

Let
p_{0}=\varphi(x_{0})
and
q_{0}=\varphi(y_{0}).
For
\varepsilon
small enough, depending on
\theta,
we have

(6.5.28)

\displaystyle\|p_{0}-q_{0}\|\geq d(x_{0},y_{0})-6\varepsilon\ell(T)\geq\theta\ell(T)-6\varepsilon\ell(T)\geq\frac{\theta\ell(T)}{2}.

Using this, with (
6.5.25
), (
6.5.26
) and (
6.5.27
), and choosing
\varepsilon
small enough, depending on
\theta
and
\Lambda
, we have

(6.5.29)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|A(p_{0})-A(q_{0})\|_{Q}&\leq\|A(p_{0})-\pi_{Q}(x_{0})\|_{Q}+\|\pi_{Q}(x_{0})-\pi_{Q}(y_{0})\|_{Q}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\|\pi_{Q}(y_{0})-A(q_{0})\|_{Q}\\
&\leq 12\varepsilon\ell(T)+\frac{1}{\Lambda}d(x_{0},y_{0})\\
&\leq\frac{24\varepsilon}{\theta}\|p_{0}-q_{0}\|+\frac{1}{\Lambda}\left(\|p_{0}-q_{0}\|+6\varepsilon\ell(T)\right)\\
&\leq\left(\frac{36\varepsilon}{\theta}+\frac{1}{\Lambda}\right)\|p_{0}-q_{0}\|\lesssim\frac{1}{\Lambda}\|p_{0}-q_{0}\|.\end{split}

Since
T
is a child of
R
we know
\ell(T)\sim\ell(R)
. Then, by (
6.5.27
) we have
\pi_{Q}(R)\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{C\varepsilon\ell(R)}(A(B))
for some ball
B\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|)
with radius comparable to
\ell(R).
This and (
6.5.29
) now imply (
6.5.24
) by Lemma
6.5.9
.
Using (
6.5.24
), Ahlfors regularity of
X
, and the fact that
\{R\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}R\in S,\ S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}\}
is a disjoint collection of cubes in
Q
, the left-hand side of (
6.5.23
) is at most

(6.5.30)

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}}\sum_{R\in\text{min}_{2}(S)}\mathcal{H}^{n}(\pi_{Q}(R))\lesssim\frac{1}{\Lambda}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}}\sum_{R\in\text{min}_{2}(S)}\mathcal{H}^{n}(R)\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{n}(Q)}{\Lambda}.

∎

Recalling the definition of
\text{min}(\mathcal{F}_{Q}),
the previous two lemmas combine to give the following.

Corollary 6.5.11
.

Let
Q\in\emph{Top}.
Then,

(6.5.31)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\pi_{Q}\left(\bigcup_{R\in\min(\mathcal{F}_{Q})}R\right)\right)\lesssim\left(\delta+\frac{1}{\Lambda}\right)\ell(Q)^{n}.

We can now define the family
\mathcal{F}.
Let
\text{Top}_{0}
be the set of maximal cubes in
\mathcal{G}
and

\mathcal{F}_{0}=\bigcup_{Q\in\text{Top}_{0}}\mathcal{F}_{Q}.

Then, supposing
\text{Top}_{k}
and
\mathcal{F}_{k}
have been defined for some integer
k\geq 0,
let
\text{Top}_{k+1}
be the set of maximal cubes
Q\in\mathcal{G}(Q_{0})
such that there exists
R\in\text{Top}_{k}
,
T\in\min(\mathcal{F}_{R})
and
T^{\prime}\in\text{Child}(T)
with
Q\subseteq T^{\prime}.
Let

\mathcal{F}_{k+1}=\bigcup_{Q\in\text{Top}_{k+1}}\mathcal{F}_{Q}=\bigcup_{Q\in\text{Top}}\bigcup_{i=1}^{N-1}\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{i}.

Finally, let

(6.5.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{k}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\text{Top}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\text{Top}_{k}.

Lemma 6.5.12
.

The collection
\{S\}_{S\in\mathcal{F}}
forms a disjoint partition of
\mathcal{G}.

Proof.

We begin by checking
\{S\}_{S\in\mathcal{F}}
is disjoint. Suppose
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
are distinct. Let
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\text{Top}
,
R\in\text{Top}_{Q}
and
R^{\prime}\in\text{Top}_{Q^{\prime}}
such that
S=S_{Q}(R)
and
S^{\prime}=S_{Q^{\prime}}(R^{\prime})
. If
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\emptyset
then there is nothing to show. Suppose instead that
Q\cap Q^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and suppose without loss of generality that
Q\subseteq Q^{\prime}.
By maximality,
\text{Top}_{m}
is a disjoint collection of cubes for each
m\geq 0.
Thus
Q\in\text{Top}_{k}
and
Q^{\prime}\in\text{Top}_{\ell}
for some
\ell\geq k.
By construction, if
\ell>k
then
Q\subset T
for some
T\in\min(S_{Q^{\prime}}(R^{\prime}))
. It follows that
S
is disjoint from
S^{\prime}
. If
k=\ell
then
Q=Q^{\prime}
since
\text{Top}_{k}
is disjoint. In particular,
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}.
Lemma
6.5.7
now implies
S
is disjoint from
S^{\prime}
.

Let us now check that

(6.5.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{F}}S.

The fact that
\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{F}}S\subseteq\mathcal{G}
is clear from Definition
6.5.4
(2). For the forward inclusion, fix some
T\in\mathcal{G}.
Let
k\geq 0
be the largest integer for which there exists a cube
Q\in\text{Top}_{k}
satisfying
T\subseteq Q
. Let
m\geq 0
be the largest integer for which there exists
R\in\text{Top}_{Q}^{m}
satisfying
T\subseteq R.
By maximality there exists a cube in
\text{Top}_{0}
which contains
T
and so
k
and
Q
are well-defined. Since
T\subseteq Q
and
\text{Top}_{Q}^{0}=\{Q\}
we have that
m
and
R
are also well-defined. We will show

(6.5.34)

\displaystyle T\in S_{Q}(R),

which is enough for (
6.5.33
). First, if
T\cap R^{\prime}=\emptyset
for all
R^{\prime}\in\min(S_{Q}(R))
then (
6.5.34
) is true by Definition
2.6.6
(2) (the child of
R^{\prime}
must be in
S_{Q}(R)
). Suppose then that there exists
R^{\prime}\in\min(S_{Q}(R))
such that
T\cap R^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Let
R^{\prime\prime}\in\text{Child}(R^{\prime})
such that
T\cap R^{\prime\prime}\neq\emptyset.
By Definition
2.6.6
, it is enough to show
T\supseteq R^{\prime}
or equivalently
T\supset R^{\prime\prime}.
To see this final inclusion, we proceed as follows. Suppose first that
m\leq N-2.
Then
R^{\prime\prime}\in\text{Top}_{Q}^{m+1}
and
T\supset R^{\prime\prime}
by maximality of
m
. Suppose instead that
m=N-1.
Since
T\in\mathcal{G}(Q_{0}),
if
T\subseteq R^{\prime\prime}
then by maximality we would have
T\in\text{Top}_{k+1}.
This contradicts that maximality of
k
and we conclude that
T\supset R^{\prime\prime}.

∎

Lemma 6.5.13
.

For
Q\in\emph{Top}
define

(6.5.35)

\displaystyle z(Q)=Q\setminus\bigcup_{R\in\min(\mathcal{F}_{Q})}R.

If
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\emph{Top}
are distinct then
z(Q)\cap z(Q^{\prime})=\emptyset.

Proof.

Since
z(Q)\subseteq Q
and
z(Q^{\prime})\subseteq Q^{\prime},
if
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\emptyset
then the result follows immediately. Suppose instead that
Q\cap Q^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and suppose towards a contradiction that there exists
x\in z(Q)\cap z(Q^{\prime})
. By definition, we can find
S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}
and
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}_{Q^{\prime}}
such that
x
is contained in arbitrarily small cubes from each stopping-time region. Let
k\geq 0
such that
5\varrho^{k}\leq\min\{\ell(Q(S)),\ell(Q(S^{\prime}))\}
and choose
R\in S\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}
and
R^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}
such that
x\in R\cap R^{\prime}.
Since cubes in
\mathcal{D}_{k}
are disjoint it follows that
R=R^{\prime},
however, this contradict Lemma
6.5.12
.
∎

Lemma 6.5.14
.

If
\Lambda
is chosen sufficiently large then for each
R\in\mathcal{D}(Q_{0})
we have

(6.5.36)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{F}\\
Q(S)\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\ell(Q(S))^{n}\lesssim\ell(R)^{n}.

Proof.

Fix some
R\in\mathcal{D}(Q_{0}).
Using the Ahlfors regularity of
X
and the definition of
\mathcal{F},
we have

(6.5.37)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{F}\\
Q(S)\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\ell(Q(S))^{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\text{Top}\\
Q\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{i}}\ell(Q(S))^{n}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\text{Top}\\
Q\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{i}}\mathcal{H}^{n}(Q(S)).

By the construction in (
6.5.8
), for each fixed
Q\in\text{Top}
and
i\in\{0,\dots,N\}
we know
\{Q(S)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S\in\mathcal{F}_{Q}^{i}\}
is a disjoint collection of cubes contained in
Q.
Using this fact and the Ahlfors regularity of
X
again, we get

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{F}\\
Q(S)\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\ell(Q(S))^{n}\lesssim_{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\text{Top}\\
Q\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}^{n}(Q)\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\text{Top}\\
Q\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{n}.

Thus, it suffices to show

(6.5.38)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\text{Top}\\
Q\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{n}\lesssim\ell(R)^{n}.

Suppose
Q\in\text{Top}
is such that
Q\subseteq R.
Applying Corollary
6.5.11
and choosing
\delta
small enough and
\Lambda
large enough, we have

(6.5.39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(z(Q))

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{n}\mathcal{H}^{n}(\pi_{Q}(z(Q)))\geq\mathcal{H}^{n}(\pi_{Q}(Q))-\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\pi_{Q}\left(\bigcup_{T\in\min(\mathcal{F}_{Q})}T\right)\right)

(6.5.40)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-C\left(\delta+\frac{1}{\Lambda}\right)\right)\ell(Q)^{n}\gtrsim\ell(Q)^{n}.

By Lemma
6.5.13
,
\{z(Q)\}_{Q\in\text{Top}}
forms a disjoint collection of subsets of
R.
Hence,

(6.5.41)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\text{Top}}\ell(Q)^{n}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\text{Top}}\mathcal{H}^{n}(z(Q))\leq\mathcal{H}^{n}(R)\lesssim\ell(R)^{n}.

∎

Proof of Proposition
6.5.1
.

By Remark
6.5.2
is suffices to prove a decomposition of the cubes in
\mathcal{D}(Q_{0}).
We decompose
\mathcal{D}(Q_{0})=\mathcal{G}\cup\mathcal{B}
with
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{B}
as in (
6.5.1
). Defining the family of stopping-time regions
\mathcal{F}
as in (
6.5.32
), Definition
6.4.1
(1) follows from Lemma
6.5.12
. Definition
6.4.1
(2) follows directly from Remark
6.5.3
and Lemma
6.5.14
. Finally, observe by construction that if
S\in\mathcal{F}
then
S=S_{Q}(R)
for some
Q\in\mathcal{G}
and
R\subseteq Q.
If
T\in S
then the lower bound in Definition
6.4.1
(3) follows from Definition
6.5.4
with constant
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda.
The upper bound follows with constant
\Lambda_{2}
, depending only on
n
, since we assume
\pi_{Q(S)}
to be
C(n)
-Lipschitz. This finishes the proof.
∎

7.
BWGL implies
\text{CD}(\text{RF metric})
and
CD(CD
(normed spaces))

In this section we prove the following.

Theorem 7.0.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}>1.
Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space. For
\delta>0
sufficiently small, if
X
satisfies the
BWGL(
\delta
)
then
X
admits a corona decomposition by Ahlfors regular
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat metric spaces with constants
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}.

Suppose Theorem
7.0.1
for the minute. Let
\varepsilon>0
be sufficiently small for Theorem
6.1.1
and let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small such that Theorem
7.0.1
holds for this choice of
\varepsilon.
Recalling Definition
6.4.2
, Theorem
7.0.1
implies that
X
admits a
\text{CD}(\mathscr{Y})
with
\mathscr{Y}
the family of all Ahlfors
(C,n)
-regular and
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat metric spaces. This and Theorem
6.1.1
give the following corollary.

Corollary 7.0.2
.

Let
X
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space. For
\delta>0
sufficiently small, if
X
satisfies the
BWGL
(\delta)
, then
X
admits a
\emph{CD}(\mathscr{Y})
for a family
\mathscr{Y}
of uniformly Ahlfors
n
-regular metric spaces each admitting corona decomposition by normed spaces with uniform constant.

Let’s return to the proof of Theorem
7.0.1
. Fix
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}>1
and let
\varepsilon>0
. We will find a constant
C
, independent of
\varepsilon,
and show that
X
admits a corona decomposition by the family of Ahlfors
(C,n)
-regular
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat metric spaces with constants
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
.

Let
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}.
By Remark
6.5.2
it suffices to construct a corona decomposition of the cubes
\mathcal{D}(Q_{0}).
Now, let
\theta>0
be the coarse parameter appearing in Definition
6.4.1
and we may suppose
\delta>0
is a parameter to be chosen sufficiently small depending on
\varepsilon
and
\theta.
Note that the Carleson estimates in Definition
6.4.1
(2) are allowed to depend on
\delta.
Let’s now define the families of good and bad cubes, and the family of stopping time regions. First, let

(7.0.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=\{Q\in\mathcal{D}(Q_{0})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\xi_{X}(100Q)\leq\delta\}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{D}(Q_{0})\setminus\mathcal{G}.

Definition 7.0.3
.

For
Q\in\mathcal{G}
, let
S_{Q}
be a stopping-time region defined inductively as follows. First, add
Q
to
S_{Q}.
Then, add cubes
R\subset Q
to
S_{Q}
if
\text{Parent}(R)\in S_{Q}
and
\xi_{X}(100R^{\prime})\leq\delta
for all
R^{\prime}\in\text{Sibling}(R).

Let
\text{Top}_{0}
be the maximal collection of cubes
T\in\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{F}_{0}=\{S_{Q}\}_{Q\in\text{Top}_{0}}.
Then, supposing we have define
\mathcal{F}_{k}
and
\text{Top}_{k}
up to some integer
k\geq 0,
let
\text{Top}_{k+1}
be the maximal collection of cubes
T\in\mathcal{G}
such that there exists
Q\in\text{Top}_{k}
and
R\in\min(S_{Q})
satisfying
T\subset R
. We also let

\mathcal{F}_{k+1}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{k+1}\{S_{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in\text{Top}_{i}\}.

Finally, define

\mathcal{F}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}_{k}.

One can easily verify that
\{S\}_{S\in\mathcal{F}}
defines a collection of disjoint stopping-time regions in much the same way as we did in Lemma
6.5.7
. Similarly, we can check it forms a partition of
\mathcal{G}
as in Lemma
6.5.12
. In particular, we have Definition
6.4.1
(1).

Lemma 7.0.4
.

Definition
6.4.1
(2) holds with Carleson norms depending on
\delta.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathcal{B}
satisfies a Carleson packing condition with Carleson norm depending on
\delta
follows immediately from our assumption that
X
satisfies the BWGL and Lemma
4.2.3
. It only remains to check the Carleson packing condition on
\{Q(S)\}_{S\in\mathcal{F}}
. By construction, if
S\in\mathcal{F}
then
Q(S)
is the child of some
R_{S}\in\bigcup_{Q\in\text{Top}}\min(S_{Q}).
Since children cubes are disjoint, if
R\in\bigcup_{Q\in\text{Top}}\min(S_{Q})
then
\{Q(S)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S\in\mathcal{F}\mbox{ and }R_{S}=R\}
is a disjoint collection of cubes. In particular, by Ahlfors regularity,

(7.0.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{F}\\
R_{S}=R\end{subarray}}\ell(Q(S))^{n}\lesssim\ell(R)^{n}.

For each
R\in\bigcup_{Q\in\text{Top}}\min(S_{Q})
there exists
T_{R}\in\text{Child}(R)
such that
\xi_{X}(100T_{R})>\delta.
Since
T_{R}\in\text{Child}(R)
we have
\ell(R)\sim\ell(T_{R}).
Since every cube has a unique parent, if
T\in\mathcal{D}
then

\left|\left\{R\in\bigcup_{Q\in\text{Top}}\min(S_{Q})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T_{R}=T\right\}\right|\leq 1.

Combining the above and using that
X
satisfies the BWGL, we get

(7.0.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}}\ell(Q(S))^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{Q\in\text{Top}}\sum_{R\in\min(S_{Q})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{F}\\
R_{S}=R\end{subarray}}\ell(Q(S))^{n}\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\text{Top}}\sum_{R\in\min(S_{Q})}\ell(R)^{n}

(7.0.4)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\text{Top}}\sum_{R\in\min(S_{Q})}\ell(T_{R})^{n}\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in\mathcal{D}\\
\xi_{X}(100T)>\delta\end{subarray}}\ell(T)^{n}\lesssim_{\delta}\ell(Q_{0})^{n}.

∎

The proof of Theorem
7.0.1
is concluded with the following lemma.

Lemma 7.0.5
.

If
\delta
is chosen small enough, depending on
\varepsilon
,
\theta
and
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
, then the following holds. There exists
C_{*}
independent of
\varepsilon
and for each
S\in\mathcal{F}
a metric
\rho_{S}
and a map
\varphi_{S}\colon 3B_{Q(S)}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho_{S})
is an Ahlfors
(C_{*},n)
-regular
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat metric space and if
Q\in S
and
x,y\in 3B_{Q}
are such that
d_{X}(x,y)>\theta\ell(Q)
then

(7.0.5)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}}d_{X}(x,y)\leq\rho_{S}(\varphi_{S}(x),\varphi_{S}(y))\leq\Lambda_{2}d_{X}(x,y).

Proof.

Let
S\in\mathcal{F}.
By scaling we may assume
Q(S)\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
so that
B_{Q(S)}=B(x_{Q(S)},1)
and
3\ell(Q(S))=15.
We construct
\rho_{S}
by applying Theorem
5.1.1
. First, we need to define a collection of points and norms which satisfy the hypotheses.

For each
\ell\geq 0
, let
k(\ell)\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
5\varrho^{k(\ell)}\leq r_{\ell}<5\varrho^{k(\ell)-1}.
Then, let
\{x_{j,\ell}\}_{j\in J_{\ell}}
be a maximal
r_{\ell}
-separated net in
\{x_{Q}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\in S\cap\mathcal{D}_{k(\ell)}\}
. Since
S\cap\mathcal{D}_{0}=\{Q(S)\}
we have Theorem
5.1.1
(1). The separation condition implies Theorem
5.1.1
(2).

Now we consider Theorem
5.1.1
(3). For
\ell\geq 0
and
j\in J_{\ell}
set
B^{j,\ell}=B(x_{j,\ell},r_{\ell})
. Fix
\ell\geq 1
and
j\in J_{\ell}.
Suppose first that
k(\ell)=k(\ell-1).
By maximality, there exists
i\in J_{\ell-1}
such that
d(x_{j,\ell},x_{i,\ell-1})\leq r_{\ell-1},
which implies
x_{j,\ell}\in B^{i,\ell-1}.
Suppose instead that
k(\ell)>k(\ell-1).
There exists
Q\in S\cap\mathcal{D}_{k(\ell-1)}
such that
d(x_{j,\ell},x_{Q})\leq\ell(Q)\leq r_{\ell-1}
and, by maximality,
i\in J_{\ell-1}
such that
d(x_{Q},x_{i,\ell-1})\leq r_{\ell-1}.
By the triangle inequality we have
x_{j,\ell}\in 2B^{i,\ell-1}.

Finally, let us check Theorem
5.1.1
(4). Let
\ell\geq 0
,
j\in J_{\ell}
and let
Q\in S\cap\mathcal{D}_{k(\ell)}
such that
x_{j,\ell}=x_{Q}.
Since
100B^{j,\ell}\subseteq 100B_{Q}
and
r_{\ell}\sim\ell(Q)
we have

(7.0.6)

\displaystyle\xi_{X}(100B^{j,\ell})\leq C\xi_{X}(100Q)\leq C\delta.

Choose a parameter
\gamma>0
, depending on
\theta
and
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},
such that

(7.0.7)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}^{-1}\leq(1-\gamma/\theta)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad(1+\gamma/\theta)\leq\Lambda_{2}.

Let
\delta^{\prime}=C\delta
(with
C
as in (
7.0.6
)) and choose
\delta^{\prime}
(hence
\delta
) small enough, depending on
\varepsilon
and
\gamma
, such that the conclusion of Theorem
5.1.1
holds with constant
\min\{\varepsilon,\gamma\}/10.
Let
\rho_{S}=\rho
and
\varphi_{S}=\bar{g}
be the metric and map we obtain by applying Theorem
5.1.1
with this choice of parameter.

Since
X
is Ahlfors
n
-regular,
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho_{S})
is Ahlfors
n
-regular with regularity constant
C_{*}
depending only on the regularity constant of
X
. Since
\varepsilon<\min\{\varepsilon,\gamma\}/10,
it is also
(\varepsilon,n)
-Reifenberg flat. It remains to check (
7.0.5
). Suppose
Q\in S
and
x,y\in 3B_{Q}
are such that
d_{X}(x,y)>\theta\ell(Q).
Let
\ell\geq 0
be the largest integer such that
\ell(Q)\leq r_{\ell}.
In this way, if
k\geq 0
is such that
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
then
k(\ell)=k.
Note that by maximality of
\ell
we also have
r_{\ell}\leq 10\ell(Q).
By the maximality of
\{x_{j,\ell}\}_{j\in J_{\ell}}
there exists
j\in J_{\ell}
such that
d(x_{j,\ell},x_{Q})\leq r_{\ell}
such that
3B_{Q}\subseteq 4B^{j,\ell}.
In particular, by applying (
5.1.11
), we have

(7.0.8)

\displaystyle|\rho_{S}(\varphi_{S}(x),\varphi_{S}(y))-d_{X}(x,y)|\leq\frac{\gamma r_{\ell}}{10}\leq\gamma\ell(Q)\leq\frac{\gamma d_{X}(x,y)}{\theta}.

The estimate (
7.0.5
) follows from this and the estimates on
\gamma
in (
7.0.7
).
∎

8.
\text{CD}^{2}
(normed spaces) implies CD(normed spaces)

Recall the notion of
\text{CD}(\mathscr{Y})
and corona decomposition by normed spaces from Definition
6.4.1
and Definition
6.4.2
, respectively. The main goal of this section is to prove the following, which states that a corona decomposition by metric spaces with corona decompositions implies a corona decomposition.

Proposition 8.0.1
.

Let
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{2}^{\prime}\geq 1
be given. Suppose
\mathscr{Y}
is a family of Ahlfors
n
-regular sets with uniform regularity constant each of which admits a corona decomposition by normed spaces with constants
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}.
If
X
admits a
\emph{CD}(\mathscr{Y})
with constants
\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{2}^{\prime}
, then
X
admits a corona decomposition by normed spaces with constant
\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime},\Lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, where
\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}
and
\Lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\Lambda_{2}\Lambda_{2}^{\prime}.

Proposition
8.0.1
is a variation of
[
DS93a
, Lemma 3.41]
. The proof there is said to be a variation their proof of
[
DS93a
, Lemma 3.21]
. Let us remark that what is needed is in fact a variation of
[
DS93a
, Proposition 3.32]
. Our proof below is almost identical to that of
[
DS93a
, Proposition 3.32]
, where we only deviate towards the end. At various points we will make use of
[
DS93a
, Lemmas 3.22-3.29]
which are about
coronization
(and not corona decomposition) and hold in the metric setting.

Proof of Proposition
8.0.1
.

Let
{\theta}_{1},{\theta}_{2}>0.
Let
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F})
be a coronization of
X
satisfying the assumptions in Definition
6.4.2
with constant
{\theta}_{1}
. In particular, for each
S\in\mathcal{F}
there is a
Y=Y_{S}\in\mathscr{Y}
and

\phi_{X,S}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}3B_{Q(S)}\to W
such that if
Q\in S
and
x,y\in 3B_{Q}
with

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,y)>{\theta}_{1}\ell(Q)
then

(8.0.1)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}d_{X}(x,y)\leq d_{Y}(\phi_{X,S}(x),\phi_{X,S}(y))\leq\Lambda_{2}^{\prime}d_{X}(x,y).

We also have a decomposition of
Y_{S}
into Christ-David cubes
\mathcal{D}(Y_{S})
and a coronization
(\mathcal{B}(Y_{S}),\mathcal{G}(Y_{S}),\mathcal{F}(Y_{S}))
of
Y_{S}

such that for every
T\in\mathcal{F}(Y_{S})
there is

\phi_{Y_{S},T}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}3B_{Q(T)}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\|\cdot\|_{Q(T)}}

such that if
R\in T
and
x,y\in 3B_{R}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,y)>{\theta}_{2}\ell(R)

then

\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}}d_{Y_{S}}(x,y)\leq\|\phi_{Y_{S},T}(x)-\phi_{Y_{S},T}(y)\|_{Q(T)}\leq\Lambda_{2}d_{Y_{S}}(x,y).

Note that
R
is a cube in the dyadic structure of
Y_{S}
and not in the dyadic structure of
X
.

Let
A
be a large constant (to be chosen later). We say two set
Q,R
in some common space are
A
-close
if

(8.0.2)

\displaystyle A^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(Q)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(R)\leq A\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(Q)

and

(8.0.3)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(Q,R)\leq A(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(Q)+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(R)).

For each
S\in\mathcal{F}
we introduce a new bad set
\mathcal{B}_{1}(Y_{S})
composed of all cubes in
\mathcal{D}(Y_{S})
which are
A
-close to some cube in
\mathcal{B}(Y_{S})

or
some top cube of a stopping time region in
\mathcal{T}(Y_{S})

or
are in
\mathcal{G}(Y_{S})
and are
A
-close to some other cube of
\mathcal{G}(Y_{S})
that belongs to a different stopping time region of
\mathcal{T}(Y_{S})
. It follows from the proof of
[
DS93a
, Lemma 3.26]
that
\mathcal{B}_{1}(Y_{S})
satisfies a Carleson packing condition with controlled constants.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{2}(Y_{S})
be the subset of
\mathcal{B}_{1}(Y_{S})
which are
A
-close to elements of

\phi_{X,S}(S)
.
Note that
Q\in S
implies that
\phi_{X,S}(Q)
is of comparable diameter and we may talk about cubes in
\mathcal{D}(Y_{S})
that have comparable diameter to that and are not too far (up to
A
times the diameter).

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1}(X)
be cubes in
\mathcal{D}(X)
that satisfy at least one of:

(1)

A
-close to an element of
\mathcal{B}

(2)

A
-close to an element of
Q(S)
for
S\in\mathcal{F}
;

(3)

have a
\phi_{X,S}
image that is
A
-close to a cube of

\mathcal{B}_{2}(Y_{S})
for
S\in\mathcal{F}
.

We claim that
\mathcal{B}_{1}(X)
satisfies a Carleson packing condition. Indeed, one can prove a packing condition of the cubes in
\mathcal{B}_{1}(X)
satisfying (1) and (2) by
[
DS93a
, Lemma 3.27]
which says that
A
-closeness does not ruin a packing condition. Thus, it suffices to prove a packing condition for those cubes satisfying (3). Fix
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(X)
and for
S\in\mathcal{F}
let
\mathcal{B}(S)
be those cubes in
\mathcal{B}_{1}(X)
satisfying (3). We want to show

(8.0.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{B}(S)\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{n}\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{n}.

Suppose that
S\in\mathcal{F}
contributes to the left-hand side of (
8.0.4
) and that
Q(S)\subseteq Q_{0}.
By definition of
A
-closeness, if
Q\in\mathcal{B}(S)
then there exists
R_{Q}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(Y_{S})
with
\ell(R_{Q})\sim_{A}\ell(Q)
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{Q},\phi_{X,S}(Q))\lesssim_{A}\ell(Q).
Using this and (
8.0.1
), there exists
z\in Y_{S}
and a constant
C
depending
A
and
\Lambda_{2}
such that
R_{Q}\subseteq B(z,C\ell(Q(S))).
Furthermore, the correspondence
Q\mapsto R_{Q}
is at most
C(A)
to 1. It now follows from the packing conditions on
\mathcal{B}_{2}(Y_{S})
and
\{Q(S)\}_{S\in\mathcal{F}}
that

(8.0.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{F}\\
Q(S)\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{B}(S)\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{n}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{F}\\
Q(S)\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\ell(Q(S))^{n}\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{n}.

If
S\in\mathcal{F}
contributes to the left-hand side of (
8.0.4
) but
Q_{0}\subseteq Q(S)
then we proceed as follows. Since there exists
Q\in\mathcal{B}(S)\subseteq S
such that
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\subseteq Q(S)
we have
Q_{0}\in S
by Definition
2.6.6
(2). In particular, there is at most one such
S
. As below (
8.0.4
), using the definition of
A
-closeness and (
8.0.1
), for each
Q\in\mathcal{B}(S)
there exists
R_{Q}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(Y_{S})
satisfying the same properties except
R_{Q}\subseteq B(z,C\ell(Q_{0})).
The packing condition on
\mathcal{B}_{2}(Y_{S})
then gives

(8.0.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in S\\
Q_{0}\subseteq Q(S)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{B}(S)\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)^{n}\lesssim\ell(Q_{0})^{n}.

Combining (
8.0.5
) and (
8.0.6
) finishes the proof of the claim.

By
[
DS93a
, Lemma 3.22]
there is a coronization
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(X),\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
of
X
such that
\mathcal{B}_{1}(X)\subset\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(X)
. Let
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
be given. We want to find a norm
\|\cdot\|_{Q(S^{\prime})}
and a map
\phi_{S^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}3B_{Q(S^{\prime})}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\|\cdot\|_{Q(S^{\prime})}}
such
that if
Q\in S^{\prime}
and
x,y\in 3B_{Q}
with
d_{X}(x,y)>{\theta}^{\prime}\ell(Q)

then

\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}d_{X}(x,y)\leq\|\phi_{S^{\prime}}(x)-\phi_{S^{\prime}}(y)\|\leq\Lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime}d_{X}(x,y).

By the construction of
\mathcal{B}_{1}(X)
and the procedure in
[
DS93a
, Lemma 3.22]
, we have that there is an
S\in\mathcal{F}
such that
S^{\prime}\subset S
. There is then a
Y=Y_{S}\in\mathscr{Y}
and a
\phi_{X,S}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}3B_{Q(S)}\to Y
such that if
Q\in S
and
x,y\in 3B_{Q}
satisfy

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,y)>{\theta}_{1}\ell(Q)
then

\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}}d_{X}(x,y)\leq d_{Y}(\phi_{X,S}(x),\phi_{X,S}(y))\leq\Lambda_{2}d_{X}(x,y).

Thus, for any
R\in S^{\prime}
there is
Q_{R}\in\mathcal{D}(Y_{S})
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(\phi_{X,S}(R),Q_{R})\lesssim\ell(R)\sim\ell(Q_{R})
. If
A
is large enough, then
Q_{R}\notin\mathcal{B}_{1}(Y_{S})
whenever
R\in S^{\prime}
.
Further, all the neighbours (in the stopping-time region
S^{\prime}
) are associated (by the map
R\mapsto Q_{R}
) to the same
T\in\mathcal{F}(Y_{S})
, which we now denote by
T(S^{\prime})
.

Let
\|\cdot\|_{Q(S^{\prime})}=\|\cdot\|_{Q(T(S^{\prime}))}
and let
\phi_{S^{\prime}}
be defined for
x\in 3B_{Q(S^{\prime})}
by

\phi_{S^{\prime}}(x)\coloneqq\phi_{Y_{S},T(S^{\prime})}\circ\phi_{X,S}(x).

Now, if
R\in S^{\prime}
and
x,y\in 3B_{R}
satisfy

d_{X}(x,y)>{\theta}^{\prime}\ell(R)

then,
provided

{\theta}^{\prime}>{\theta}_{1}
,
we have

d_{Y_{S}}(\phi_{X,S}(x),\phi_{X,S}(y))>\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}}d_{X}(x,y).

Since
\ell(Q_{R})\sim\ell(R)
,
if we also have

{\theta}^{\prime}/\Lambda_{1}\gtrsim{\theta}_{2}
then this gives

d_{Y_{S}}(\phi_{X,S}(x),\phi_{X,S}(y))>\theta_{2}\ell(Q_{R}).

Thus, we have

\|\phi_{S^{\prime}}(x)-\phi_{S^{\prime}}(y)\|\geq\frac{1}{\Lambda_{2}}d_{Y_{S}}(\phi_{X,S}(x),\phi_{X,S}(y))>\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2}}d_{X}(x,y).

The upper bound on
\|\phi_{S^{\prime}}(x)-\phi_{S^{\prime}}(y)\|
follows similarly.

∎

9.
CD(normed spaces) implies UR

In this section we prove the following.

Theorem 9.0.1
.

Let
(X,d)
be an Ahlfors
n
-regular metric space which admits a corona decomposition by normed spaces with constants
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
. Then
X
is UR.

This theorem is an immediate consequence of the following proposition.

Proposition 9.0.2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\Lambda\geq 1.
Suppose
(X,d)
is Ahlfors
n
-regular and admits a corona decomposition with constants
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
. Then, there is
L\geq 1,
depending only on
\varepsilon,\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
, such that for each
x\in X
and
r>0
there exists
F\subseteq B(x,r)
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{n}(B(x,r)\setminus F)\leq\varepsilon r^{n}
and an
L
-bi-Lipschitz map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Remark 9.0.3
.

This proposition is analogous to
[
DS91
, Proposition 16.1]
. Although our definition of corona decomposition differs from the one used in
[
DS91
]
, the proof of Proposition
9.0.2
follows a very similar method and only differs in our use of Definition
6.4.1
(3). We will give a brief outline of the proof and give the details of any significant changes.

Fix constants
\varepsilon,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
and fix a point
x\in X
and radius
r>0
for the remainder of the section. Our first step is to define
F
. Roughly speaking,
F
will be the parts of
B(x,r)
which are not contained in too many stopping-time regions from corona decomposition and which do not lie too near to the boundaries of certain cubes.

Let’s be more precise. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a system of Christ-David cubes on
X
. Fix
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
5\varrho^{k_{0}+1}<r\leq 5\varrho^{k_{0}}
and let
\{Q_{0,i}\}_{i\in I}
be the cubes in
\mathcal{D}_{k_{0}}
which intersect
B(x,r).
Since
X
is Ahlfors
n
-regular, we know that

(9.0.1)

\displaystyle|I|\lesssim_{C_{0},n}1.

Let

(9.0.2)

\displaystyle\theta=2\varrho\leq 2c_{0},

where
\varrho
and
c_{0}
are the constants appearing in Definition
2.6.1
. Since
X
admits a corona decomposition by normed spaces with constants
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
(recalling as well Remark
6.4.4
), for each
i\in I
there exists a collection of stopping-time regions
\mathcal{F}_{i}
such that

(9.0.3)

\displaystyle\ \{Q\in\mathcal{D}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}Q\subseteq Q_{0,i}\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}S,

(9.0.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}\ell(Q(S))^{d}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\theta}\ell(Q_{0,i})^{d},

and for each
S\in\mathcal{F}_{i}
which is not a singleton, there is a norm
\|\cdot\|_{S}
and a map
\pi_{S}\colon 3B_{Q(S)}\to B_{\|\cdot\|_{S}}(0,3\ell(Q(S)))
such that if
Q\in S
and
x,y\in 3B_{Q}
satisfy
d(x,y)\geq\theta\ell(Q),
then

(9.0.5)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}}d(x,y)\leq\|\pi_{S}(x)-\pi_{S}(y)\|_{S}\leq\Lambda_{2}d(x,y).

Let

R_{0}=\bigcup_{i\in I}Q_{0,i}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{F}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\{S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S\in\mathcal{F}_{i}\}.

Using the same terminology as in
[
DS91
]
, we call a cube
Q\subseteq R_{0}
a
transition cube
if it is the top cube, or a minimal cube of some
S\in\mathcal{F}
. Let
T
denote the set of transition cubes. Combining (
9.0.1
) with (
9.0.4
), we have

(9.0.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in T}\ell(Q)^{n}\lesssim_{\theta}\sum_{i\in I}\ell(Q_{i,0})^{n}\lesssim_{C_{0},n}r^{n}.

For
Q\in\mathcal{D},
let
N(Q)
denote the number of transition cubes strictly containing
Q
(this is the quantity
\ell(Q)
in
[
DS91
]
). Let
\eta>0
(which will be chosen small momentarily) and define the boundary of a cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
as

\sigma(Q)=\{x\in Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,E\setminus Q)\leq\eta\varrho^{k}\}.

Let
N\geq 1
(to be chosen large) and define
F\subseteq B(x,r)
by setting

F=B(x,r)\cap\left(\bigcup_{Q\in T}\sigma(Q)\right)^{c}\cap\left(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in T\\
N(Q)\geq N\end{subarray}}Q\right)^{c}.

Lemma 9.0.4
.

For
N
large enough and
\eta
small enough, both depending on
C_{0},\varepsilon
and
\theta
, we have
\mathcal{H}^{n}(B(x,r)\setminus F)\leq\varepsilon r^{n}.

Proof.

Ahlfors regularity, Lemma
2.6.1
(4) and (
9.0.6
) imply, after choosing
\eta
small enough depending on
C_{0},\varepsilon
and
\theta
, that

(9.0.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\bigcup_{Q\in T}\sigma(Q)\right)\lesssim\sum_{Q\in T}\eta^{\frac{1}{C}}\ell(Q)^{n}\lesssim_{C_{0},\theta}\eta^{\frac{1}{C}}r^{n}\leq\varepsilon r^{n}/2.

By taking
N
large enough, depending on
C_{0},\varepsilon
and
\theta,
we also get

(9.0.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\left(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in T\\
N(Q)\geq N\end{subarray}}Q\right)\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{Q\in T}\mathcal{H}^{n}(Q)\lesssim_{C_{0},n,\theta}\frac{1}{N}r^{n}\leq\varepsilon r^{n}/2.

The result now follows.
∎

From now on, we fix
N
large enough such that the above lemma holds. The next step in the proof of Proposition
9.0.2
is to construct the bi-Lipschitz map from
F
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This is done by stitching together the maps associated to each stopping-time region
S\in\mathcal{F}
. The amount of stitching is controlled by the constant
N
. We begin by defining a preliminary map
g
on the set of cubes
Q
contained in
R_{0}
.

Lemma 9.0.5
.

There exists a map
g
from the set of transition cubes
T
such that if
Q\in T
then
g(Q)
is a cube in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(g(Q))=C_{3}^{-N(Q)}\ell(Q)
for some large constant
C_{3}>1
. Furthermore, for any
Q,Q^{\prime}\in T
satisfying
Q\subseteq Q^{\prime}
we have
g(Q)\subseteq\tfrac{1}{2}g(Q^{\prime}).

Proof.

We define
g
inductively, starting with the top cubes
Q_{0,i}.
We set
\{g(Q_{0,i})\}_{i\in I}
to be a collection of cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
each with diameter
5\varrho^{k_{0}}
and with mutual distances between
5\varrho^{k_{0}}
and
5C\varrho^{k_{0}}
for some
C>1
(with
{k_{0}}
as defined after the statement of Remark
9.0.3
). Such a prescription is possible if
C
is chosen large enough by (
9.0.1
). Since
N(Q_{i,0})=0
for each
i\in I,
the cubes
g(Q_{i,0})
have the correct diameter.

We now define
g(Q)
inductively for the remaining transition cubes. Suppose
g(Q)
has been defined for some
Q\in T\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}
with
k\geq k_{0}.
If
Q
is a minimal cube of a stopping-time region, each of its children is in
T
. We want to
g
for these cubes. By definition
N(R)=N(Q)+1
for each child
R
of
Q
. Since
X
Ahlfors
n
-regular,
Q
has a bounded number of children (with constant depending only on
n
and the regularity constant). Thus, for
C_{3}>1
large enough, we can choose
g(R)
to be a collection of cubes with diameters
C_{3}^{-N(R)}\ell(R)
contained in
\tfrac{1}{2}g(Q)
such that

(9.0.9)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R),g(R^{\prime}))\geq C_{3}^{-N(R)}\ell(R).

Assume now that
Q
is the top cube of a stopping-time region
S\in\mathcal{F}
, which is not a singleton. Each minimal cube of
S
is in
T
and we want to define
g
for each one. At this point we deviate from what is written in
[
DS91
]
, we make instead use of our definition of corona decomposition.

By Remark
2.2.1
, we can find a linear isomorphism
T_{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{S})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\|T_{S}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1
and
\|T^{-1}_{S}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq n
. Set
h_{S}=T_{S}\circ\pi_{S}.
By (
9.0.5
) we have that
\pi_{S}(Q)
is contained in a ball of radius
\Lambda_{2}\ell(Q)
in
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{S}).
Since
\|T_{S}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1,
the same is true of
h_{S}(Q)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
By assumption, we have
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(g(Q))=C_{3}^{-N(Q)}\ell(Q),
so there exists an affine map
\phi_{S}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

(9.0.10)

\displaystyle\phi_{S}(h_{S}(Q))\subseteq\frac{1}{3}g(Q)

and

(9.0.11)

\displaystyle C_{4}^{-1}C_{3}^{-N(Q)}|p-q|\leq|\phi_{S}(p)-\phi_{S}(q)|\leq C_{3}^{-N(Q)}|p-q|

each
p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and some
C_{4}\geq 1
chosen large enough depending on
\Lambda_{2}
. Then, if
R\in\min(S)
, define
g(R)
to be the cube centred at
\phi_{S}(h_{S}(x_{R}))
of size
C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}\ell(R)=C_{3}^{-N(R)}\ell(R).
It is easy to see that
g(R)\subseteq\tfrac{1}{2}g(Q)
by the triangle inequality.
∎

The following lemma lets us estimate relative distance between cubes
g(R)
and
g(R^{\prime}).

Lemma 9.0.6
.

If
C_{3}
is chosen large enough depending on
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
we get the following. Let
S\in\mathcal{F}
be a stopping-time that is not a singleton and suppose
R,R^{\prime}\in\min(S).
Then,

(9.0.12)

\displaystyle C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}[\ell(R)+\ell(R^{\prime})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R,R^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R),g(R^{\prime}))

(9.0.13)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq 3C_{3}^{-N(Q)}[\ell(R)+\ell(R^{\prime})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R,R^{\prime})].

Remark 9.0.7
.

The proof of the above lemma can be found on
[
DS91
, Page 104]
or in the proof of
[
Hyd22a
, Lemma 7.6]
with some more details. In our notation, the crucial estimates for these proofs are

(9.0.14)

\displaystyle d(x_{R},x_{R^{\prime}})\geq C^{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}R+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}R^{\prime}+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R,R^{\prime}))

and

(9.0.15)

\displaystyle\|\pi_{S}(x_{R})-\pi_{S}(x_{R^{\prime}})\|_{S}\geq\frac{1}{2}d(x_{R},x_{R^{\prime}}).

See above
[
DS91
, Equation 16.11]
for the corresponding estimates. The first estimate is immediate from the construction of the Christ-David cubes. For the second estimate, David and Semmes appeal to condition (3’) in Remark
6.4.3
. For us, we get the estimate with
\tfrac{1}{\Lambda_{1}}d(x_{R},x_{R^{\prime}})
on the right-hand side, by Definition
6.4.1
(3). Indeed, let
k\geq k_{0}
be the largest integer such that there exists
Q\in S\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}
with
x_{R},x_{R^{\prime}}\in 3B_{Q}
. Suppose there exists
Q^{\prime}\in S\cap\mathcal{D}_{k+1}
such that
x_{R}\in Q^{\prime}
or
x_{R^{\prime}}\in Q
. By maximality of
k
and the triangle inequality we have
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x_{R},x_{R^{\prime}})\geq 10\varrho^{k+1}=2\varrho\ell(Q).
The estimate above then follows from (
9.0.5
) and our choice of
\theta
in (
9.0.2
). If there does not exists
Q^{\prime}\in S\cap\mathcal{D}_{k+1}
satisfying the above it must be that
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{k}.
In this case we get
d(x_{R},x_{R^{\prime}})\geq 2c_{0}\ell(Q)
and the estimate again follows from (
9.0.5
) and our choice of
\theta
. The fact that we prove (
9.0.15
) with a worse constant does not cause any significant difficulties in the proof, one just needs to take the constant
C_{3}
large enough depending on
\Lambda_{1}
.

Now that we have defined
g,
we can use it to define the mapping
f
. By definition, for each
x\in F
there is a minimal transition cube, which we denote by
Q(x),
such that
x\in Q(x).
It must be that
Q(x)
is the top cube of a stopping-time region
S(x)\in\mathcal{F}
(which is not a singleton), otherwise
x
would be contained in a smaller transition cube. Thus,
x
is contained in arbitrarily small cubes from
S(x)
. We define

f(x)=\phi_{S(x)}(h_{S(x)}(x)).

By (
9.0.10
),
f(x)\in g(Q(x)).

The proof of Theorem
9.0.1
is concluded with the following lemma.

Lemma 9.0.8
.

The map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
L
-bi-Lipschitz, where
L
depends on
C_{0},\varepsilon,\theta,\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
x,y\in F
be distinct points. We split the estimates on
|f(x)-f(y)|
into several cases. Let
Q_{1}
be the maximal cube contained in
R_{0}
such that
x\in Q_{1}
but
y\not\in Q_{1}.

Case 1:
Suppose that
Q_{1}
is contained in some stopping-time region
S\in\mathcal{F}
, but that
Q_{1}\neq Q(S)
. By maximality, any cube containing
Q_{1}
contains both
x
and
y
, in particular, this holds true for
Q(S).

Case 1(i):
Suppose further that there are cubes
R_{x},R_{y}\in\min(S)
such that
x\in R_{x}
and
y\in R_{y}.
This implies, first, that

(9.0.16)

\displaystyle d(x,y)\leq\ell(R_{x})+\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{x},R_{y}).

Secondly, since
x\not\in\sigma(R_{x})
and
y\not\in\sigma(R_{y})
(by the definition of
F
), we also have

(9.0.17)

\displaystyle d(x,y)\gtrsim\ell(R_{x})+\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{x},R_{y}).

The estimates on
f
then follow from the fact that
f(x)\in g(R_{x}),

f(y)\in g(R_{y}),

N(Q)\leq N
and the estimates (
9.0.12
), (
9.0.16
), and (
9.0.17
), since

(9.0.18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}d(x,y)&\leq\ell(R_{x})+\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{x},R_{y})\lesssim_{C_{3},N}\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R_{x}),g(R_{y})\\
&\leq|f(x)-f(y)|\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R_{x}),g(R_{y}))+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(R_{x})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(R_{y})\\
&\lesssim_{C_{3},N}\ell(R_{x})+\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(R_{x},R_{y})\lesssim d(x,y).\end{split}

Recall that
C_{3}
depends only on
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
while
N
depends only on
C_{0},\varepsilon
and
\theta.

Case 1(ii):
Suppose that
x\in R_{x}\in\min(S)
but
y
is not contained in any cubes from
\text{min}(S)
or that
y\in R_{y}\in\min(S)
but
x
is not contained in any cube from
\text{min}(S).
By symmetry, we may suppose that
x\in R_{x}
for some
R_{x}\in\text{min}(S).
If follows that
f(x)=\phi_{S}(h_{S}(x))
and by a similar argument to (
9.0.12
), it can be shown that

C_{3}^{-N(Q)-1}[\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,R_{y})]\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,g(R_{y}))\leq 3C_{3}^{-N(Q)}[\ell(R_{y})+\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,R_{y})].

Using the same estimates as in (
9.0.18
) (after replacing
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(R_{x}),g(R_{y}))
with
\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(x,g(R_{y}))
) proves the bi-Lipschitz estimates for
f
in this case.

Case 1(iii):
Suppose that neither
x
nor
y
are contained in a minimal cubes of
S
. In this case
f(x)=\phi_{S}(h_{S}(x))
and
f(y)=\phi_{S}(h_{S}(y))
and the bi-Lipschitz estimates for
f
follow from the bi-Lipschitz estimate for
T_{S}
, (
9.0.11
), and (
9.0.5
).

Case 2:
Suppose that
Q_{1}
is the top of a stopping-time region. Let
Q_{2}
be the parent of
Q_{1}.
By construction,
Q_{2}
is the minimal cube of some stopping-time region, hence, a transition cube. By maximality, it follows that
x,y\in Q_{2}.
Since
x,y\in Q_{2}
,
x\in Q_{1}\setminus\sigma(Q_{1})
and
y\not\in Q_{1}
we have
d(x,y)\sim\ell(Q_{1}).

To estimate
|f(x)-f(y)|
, first note that since
x,y\in Q_{2},
we have
f(x),f(y)\in g(Q_{2})
and so

|f(x)-f(y)|\leq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(g(Q_{2}))\lesssim\ell(Q_{2})\sim\ell(Q_{1})\sim d(x,y).

Let
Q_{1}^{\prime}
be the sibling of
Q_{1}
with
y\in Q_{1}^{\prime}.
By (
9.0.12
) we have

|f(x)-f(y)|\geq\mathop{\mathrm{}}{dist}(g(Q_{1}),g(Q_{1}^{\prime}))\gtrsim\ell(Q_{1})\sim d(x,y)

and this finishes the proof of the bi-Lipschitz estimates.

∎

10.
An example and application

We conclude by giving an example of a discrete use of Theorem
A
to construct a UR metric space
from the BWGL. Conversely this gives a condition for testing whether a discrete set has bi-Lipschitz coordinate charts.

Let
(Y,d_{Y})
be a discrete metric space and, for each
y\in Y
,
suppose that
r_{y}\in(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(Y)]
is such that
B(y,r_{y})=\{y\}
.
Let
\nu
be a measure on
Y
and suppose that for all
y\in Y
and

r\in(r_{y},\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(Y)]
we have
C^{-1}r^{n}\leq\nu(B(y,r))\leq Cr^{n}
for some fixed
C\geq 1.

For each
y\in Y,
let
D_{y,r_{y}}
be the ball of radius
r_{y}
centred at the origin in a
normed space
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{y})
and consider the metric measure space
given by taking a disjoint union of the
D_{y,r_{y}}
.
That is, let
X=\bigsqcup_{y\in Y}D_{y,r_{y}}
and for
x\in D_{y,r_{y}}
and
w\in D_{z,r_{z}}
let

d_{X}(x,w)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\|x\|_{y}+d_{Y}(z,y)+\|w\|_{z},

endowing
X
with the
n
-dimensional Hausdorff measure.
Note that
X
is Ahlfors
n
-regular (Definition
3.1.1
) with constant comparable to
C
.
For
x\in D_{y,r_{y}}
we will write
y(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=y
.
The claim is that, correctly interpreted,
Y
is uniformly rectifiable if
and only if
X
is.

One may say that
Y
is UR if there are constants
\theta,L>0
such that,
for every
y\in Y
and
r\in(r_{y},\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(Y)]
we have a set

F_{0}\subset B(0,r)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and an
L
-Lipschitz map

f_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}F_{0}\to Y
such that

\nu(B(y,r)\cap f_{0}(F_{0}))\geq\theta r^{n}
.
It is easy to see that if

X
is UR then
Y
is UR.
Conversely,
if
Y
is UR then
X
is UR.
Indeed, the main issue is (with minor abuse of notation) with
y

being the center of
D_{y,r_{y}}
and
r\in(r_{y},\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(Y)]
.
In this case consider the map
f_{0}\colon F_{0}\to Y
given by the UR of
Y

associated to
B(y,r)\subset Y
.
For each
z
in the image of
f_{0}
, every element of
f_{0}^{-1}(z)
has a ball around it
of radius of size at least
r_{z}/L
which is disjoint from
F_{0}\setminus f_{0}^{-1}(z)
.
Thus, by increasing the Lipschitz constant of
f
by a factor of
2L
, we
obtain a map
f\colon F\to X
which
covers all balls
\{D_{z,r_{z}}\colon z\in B(y,r)\}
.
Here,
F\supset F_{0}
may be taken to be

\bigcup_{a\in F_{0}}B(a,\frac{1}{2L}r_{f_{0}(a)})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}.

For
\varepsilon>0
, we may say that
Y
satisfies BWGL
(\varepsilon)

if the set

\mathcal{Y}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(y,r)\in Y\times(0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(Y))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}r>r_{y}/\varepsilon\textrm{ and }\xi_{Y}(y,r)>\varepsilon\}

is a Carleson set (see Definition
2.6.4
).
It is clear that if
X
satisfies BWGL
(\varepsilon)
then
Y
satisfies BWGL
(2\varepsilon)
.
Conversely, suppose
Y
satisfies BWGL
(\varepsilon).
We verify that
X
satisfies BWGL
(2\varepsilon)
.
Let
x\in X
and
R\in[0,\mathop{\mathrm{}}{diam}(X)]
.
Consider

\mathscr{A}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(w,r)\in B_{X}(x,R)\times(0,R)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\xi_{X}(w,r)>2\varepsilon\}.

Using the geometry of the ball
D_{y(w),r_{y(w)}}
and the
Ahlfors-regularity of
Y
and
X
, it is easy to check that

(10.0.1)

\mathscr{A}_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(w,r)\in\mathscr{A}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}r\leq r_{y(w)}\}

is a Carleson set.
Note that the condition
\xi_{X}(w,r)>2\varepsilon
prevents balls
B(w,r)
which are disjoint from the the boundary of
D_{y(w),r_{y(w)}}
from having
(w,r)\in\mathscr{A}_{1}
.

Consider now

(10.0.2)

\mathscr{A}_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(w,r)\in\mathscr{A}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}r_{y(w)}<r<\frac{1}{\varepsilon}r_{y(w)}\}.

Then the Carleson measure of
\mathscr{A}_{2}
is controlled by a constant multiple of
\log(\varepsilon)R^{n}
.
This only uses the controlled range of
r
and the Ahlfors-regularity of
X
, and does not make use of the condition
\xi>2\varepsilon
.

Finally, consider

(10.0.3)

\mathscr{A}_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(w,r)\in\mathscr{A}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}r_{y(w)}<r\}.

We consider three subsets of
\mathscr{A}_{3}
as follows.
First let

(10.0.4)

\mathscr{A}_{3.1}\coloneqq\{(w,r)\in\mathscr{A}_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{ there is no }y\in Y\mbox{ such that }0<d_{Y}(y,y(w))\leq r\}

and note that if
(w,r)\in\mathscr{A}_{3.1}
then
\xi_{Y}(w,r)>\varepsilon
as
B(y(w),r)
is a singleton.
Thus, the Carleson measure of
\mathscr{A}_{3.1}
is bounded above by the Carleson measure of
\mathcal{Y}

Second, consider

(10.0.5)

\mathscr{A}_{3.2}\coloneqq\{(w,r)\in\mathscr{A}_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{ there is }y\in Y\mbox{ such that }d_{Y}(y,y(w))\leq r,\mbox{ and }r<r_{y}/\varepsilon\}.

For
y
as in the RHS of (
10.0.5
) we have that

r\in[r_{y},r_{y}/\varepsilon]

and we may proceed as in
\mathscr{A}_{2}

to control the Carleson measure of
\mathscr{A}_{3.2}
.
Finally let

(10.0.6)

\mathscr{A}_{3.3}\coloneqq\{(w,r)\in\mathscr{A}_{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{ if }y\in Y\mbox{ such that }d_{Y}(y,y(w))\leq r,\mbox{ then }r\geq r_{y}/\varepsilon\}.

For
(w,r)\in\mathscr{A}_{3.3}

it is easy to check, since
\xi_{X}(w,r)>2\varepsilon
, that
\xi_{Y}(y(w),r)>\varepsilon.

As for
\mathscr{A}_{3.1}
, the Carleson measure of
\mathscr{A}_{3.3}
is bounded above by the Carleson measure of
\mathcal{Y}
.
Therefore, we see that
\mathcal{Y}
is a Carleson set.

Combining these definitions, we see that Theorem
A
states that
Y
is UR if and
only if it satisfies BWGL.
